# Foundations

<img src="../images/foundations.webp" alt="" style="width: 100%;" />

<!-- source: Edsger W. Dijkstra, "The Humble Programmer", ACM Turing Lecture 1972, EWD340 -->
> _"The purpose of abstracting is not to be vague, but to create a new semantic level in which one can be absolutely precise."_
>
> — Edsger W. Dijkstra, *The Humble Programmer*

---

Dijkstra's new semantic level, in this chapter, is `Kind<F, A>`: a precise way to say "some container `F`, holding an `A`" without saying which container. Java lets you write a method for a `List`, and another for an `Optional`, but not one method for any container at all; `Kind` is how Higher-Kinded-J gets there. Application code rarely needs this level, since [Effect Paths](../effect/ch_intro.md) cover it, and [How to read this chapter](#how-to-read-this-chapter) sends you to the page you came for. Here is one method written at that level. It totals an order wherever the order happens to be:

```java
  // One method for any container F: the Functor passed in knows how to map inside F
  public static <F extends WitnessArity<TypeArity.Unary>> Kind<F, BigDecimal> totals(
      Functor<F> functor, Kind<F, Order> orders) {
    return functor.map(FoundationsIntroBook::total, orders);
  }

```

The same method, run on three different containers:

```java
    Maybe<BigDecimal> maybeTotal =
        MAYBE.narrow(totals(functor(maybe()), MAYBE.widen(Maybe.just(order))));
    // Just(50.00)
    Either<String, Order> found = Either.right(order);
    Either<String, BigDecimal> eitherTotal =
        EITHER.narrow(totals(functor(either()), EITHER.widen(found)));
    // Right(50.00)
    List<BigDecimal> listTotals =
        LIST.narrow(totals(functor(list()), LIST.widen(List.of(order, refill))));
    // [50.00, 7.50]
```

Nothing in `totals` knows about `Maybe`, `Either` or `List`. The `Functor` passed in does: it is the value that knows how to map inside its own container. `functor(maybe())` asks for the one that belongs to `Maybe`, with `functor` from `Instances` and `maybe` from `Witnesses`. The bound on `F` only says that `F` holds one type, as `Maybe` does; [Type Arity](type-arity.md) explains it. The `widen` and `narrow` calls move a value between its own type and the `Kind` view of it, whose `F` is a [witness type](core-concepts.md) standing for the container.

That is the whole of Foundations, in three layers:

```mermaid
flowchart TB
    accTitle: The three layers of Foundations, and where Effect Paths sit
    accDescr: The Kind encoding is the base. Type classes such as Functor and Monad are written over it. Core types such as Maybe, Either and IO provide instances of those type classes. Effect Paths such as MaybePath and EitherPath wrap the same core types directly, without going through the type classes.
    K["Kind encoding<br/>Kind#lt;F, A#gt;, witnesses, widen and narrow"]
    TC["Type classes<br/>Functor, Applicative, Monad and more"]
    CT["Core types<br/>Maybe, Either, Validated, IO and more"]
    EP["Effect Paths<br/>MaybePath, EitherPath, IOPath and more"]
    TC -->|"written over"| K
    CT -->|"provide instances of"| TC
    EP -->|"wrap directly"| CT
    classDef step fill:#8caaee,stroke:#1e66f5,color:#232634
    classDef domain fill:#a6d189,stroke:#40a02b,color:#232634
    class K,TC step
    class CT,EP domain
```

In words: type classes are written over the `Kind` encoding, and the core types provide instances of them. Effect Paths such as `MaybePath` and `EitherPath` wrap the same core types and call them directly, without going through the type classes. `GenericPath` is the bridge the other way: it lifts any `Kind` and its `Monad` to the Path level.

~~~admonish tip title="Which level am I working at?"
- **Effect Paths** (`MaybePath`, `EitherPath`, `ValidationPath`): application code. Concrete types you call directly, with no witness, `widen` or `narrow` to write. Most code stays here, and the [Effect Paths](../effect/ch_intro.md) chapter teaches it.
- **Type classes over `Kind`** (`Functor<F>`, `Monad<F>`): one method for any container, as `totals` is. This chapter.
- **Your own `Kind`** (a witness, `widen` and `narrow` of your own): making a type of your own work with the type classes, in [Adding Your Own Type](extending-simulation.md).

When two effects must stack and no Path covers them, [Path or Transformer?](../transformers/when_to_drop_to_transformers.md) decides.
~~~

~~~admonish warning title="Before you start"
Your project builds and runs on **Java 25**. Higher-Kinded-J is built on it today, and parts of the library use preview features, which tie the build to that one release. Most of this chapter needs no preview flag: `Maybe`, `Either`, `Validated` and the type classes compile and run without one. `VTask`'s `Scope` and `Par`, and the parallel `VStream` operations, need `--enable-preview`, as [Prerequisites](../quickstart.md#prerequisites) explains. The [HKJ Gradle or Maven plugin](../tooling/gradle_plugin.md) sets the flags for you.
~~~

---

## How to read this chapter {#how-to-read-this-chapter}

You can ship with Effect Paths without reading this chapter. Start from what you came for:

| You want | Start at |
|---|---|
| One type explained, such as `Either`, `Validated` or `Maybe` | [Core Types](../monads/ch_intro.md), or [Supported Types](../monads/supported-types.md) to find the right one |
| Every error in a form or request reported at once | [ValidationPath](../effect/path_validation.md) in application code; [Validated](../monads/validated_monad.md) and [Accumulating Assembly](../monads/validated_assembly.md) for the type beneath it |
| The whole stack seen in one expression | [One Line, Six Layers](one_line_six_layers.md) |
| One method that works for every container, as `totals` does | The pages under **Ship** in the [Chapter Contents](#chapter-contents), read in order |
| A type of your own that joins the library | [Adding Your Own Type](extending-simulation.md) |
| A comparison with Vavr, Cyclops or Arrow-Kt | [How does this compare?](faq.md#how-does-this-compare-with-vavr-cyclops-or-arrow-kt), in the Foundations FAQ |
| The names you know from Scala, Haskell or Vavr | [What are the names here?](faq.md#i-know-scala-haskell-or-vavr-what-are-the-names-here), in the Foundations FAQ |
| Which laws an instance keeps, and which `hkj-test` checks | [Laws and Where HKJ Bends Them](laws.md) |
| The type class interfaces at a glance | [Quick Reference](quick_reference.md) |
| `Kind`, `Functor`, `Applicative` and `Monad` beside the Java they replace, on one page | [Foundations Journey: Cheatsheet](../tutorials/coretypes/foundations_cheatsheet.md) |
| The everyday API instead | [Effect Paths](../effect/ch_intro.md) |

# One Line, Six Layers

_One compiled Effect Path line that edits an order, taken apart into the six layers it uses._

Here is a service method that changes the email address on an order. Its body is one expression, and it comes from a compiled, tested example, so it builds as shown.

```java
  static EitherPath<OrderError, Order> changeEmail(
      OrderRepository orders, UUID id, String newEmail) {
    return orders
        .find(id)
        .toEitherPath(OrderError.notFound(id))
        .map(order -> OrderFocus.customer().email().value().set(newEmail, order))
        .via(orders::save);
  }

```

`orders` is an `OrderRepository`. Its `find` returns a `MaybePath<Order>`, and its `save` returns an `EitherPath<OrderError, Order>`, refusing an order that has already shipped. `OrderFocus` is generated by `@GenerateFocus` on `Order`, one of the order-service records the [Optics chapter](../optics/ch_intro.md) introduces. Called for a new order, an unknown id and a shipped order, the method gives three answers:

```java
    Either<OrderError, Order> changed = changeEmail(orders, placed, "ada@example.org").run();
    // Right(Order[id=..., customer=Customer[name=Ada,
    //   email=EmailAddress[value=ada@example.org]], ...])
    Either<OrderError, Order> missing = changeEmail(orders, unknown, "ada@example.org").run();
    // Left(NotFound[orderId=...])
    Either<OrderError, Order> refused = changeEmail(orders, shipped, "ada@example.org").run();
    // Left(AlreadyShipped[orderId=...])
```

A missing order becomes a `NotFound` that carries its id. The new address goes in three records deep, and you write none of the copies: the lens rebuilds `Order`, `Customer` and `EmailAddress`. A save that refuses ends the chain with its own reason. Each failure is part of the return type, a value the caller handles, rather than an exception the signature never mentions.

~~~admonish info title="What You'll Learn"
- Name the layer of the library that each step of a Path expression uses, and the page that teaches it
- Choose `map` for a step that cannot fail, and `via` for a step that can
- Match each Path method to the type class operation over `Kind` that gives the same answer
~~~

~~~admonish example title="See Example Code"
**The code on this page is [OneLinerBook.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/oneliner/OneLinerBook.java) and its [OneLinerBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/oneliner/OneLinerBookTest.java)**: the page includes them, so the build compiles and runs them.
~~~

---

## Six layers in one expression

Each step of the line belongs to a different layer of the library, and the last column links the page that teaches it.

| Step | Layer | What it does here | Where to read more |
|---|---|---|---|
| `orders.find(id)` | An effect type | Returns a `MaybePath<Order>`: an order or nothing, as a value rather than a `null` | [Maybe](../monads/maybe_monad.md) |
| `.toEitherPath(OrderError.notFound(id))` | A natural transformation | Turns the `MaybePath` into an `EitherPath`, giving the empty case a reason and leaving the order untouched | [Natural Transformation](../functional/natural_transformation.md) |
| `OrderFocus.customer().email().value()` | An optic | A lens three records deep, whose `set` returns a new `Order` and leaves the old one as it was | [Optics](../optics/ch_intro.md) |
| `.map(order -> ...)` | Functor | Applies a function that cannot fail to the order inside, and does nothing on a `Left` | [Functor](../functional/functor.md) |
| `.via(orders::save)` | Monad | Runs a step that needs the edited order and can fail for a reason of its own | [Monad](../functional/monad.md) |
| The whole expression | The algebra | The same operations exist as type classes over `Kind<F, A>`, for one method that serves any container | [Two encodings, one algebra](#two-encodings-one-algebra) |

No step tests for failure. `EitherPath` carries a failure as a `Left`, and each later step passes the `Left` along without running. The interesting code is in the middle, where the domain logic lives.

---

## Two encodings, one algebra {#two-encodings-one-algebra}

Higher-Kinded-J offers `map`, `via` and their neighbours twice. On a Path, they are methods of a concrete type, as in the line. As type classes, they are methods of interfaces such as `Functor<F>` and `Monad<F>`, which work over `Kind<F, A>`: some container `F` holding an `A`, without saying which container. One method written that way serves any container:

| What you want | On a Path | As a type class over `Kind<F, A>` |
|---|---|---|
| Change the value inside | `path.map(f)` | `functor.map(f, fa)` |
| Combine two independent values | `path.zipWith(other, f)` | `applicative.map2(fa, fb, f)` |
| Run a step that needs the value and may fail | `path.via(f)` | `monad.flatMap(f, fa)` |
| Recover from an error | `path.recover(f)` | `monadError.handleError(fa, f)` |

`EitherPath` holds an `Either` and calls it directly: no `Kind` and no type class instance sit between your call and the `Either`. The type classes do a different job, one method for any container, as the [chapter introduction](foundations_intro.md)'s `totals` is. The two agree. Run through `Functor` and `Monad` over `Kind`, the email change gives the same `Right` or `Left` as the line for each of the three ids, and the page's test checks it.

`GenericPath.of(kind, monad)` joins the two encodings: it lifts any `Kind`, with its `Monad`, to the Path level. Most application code stays with Paths, and the "Which level am I working at?" tip in the [Foundations introduction](foundations_intro.md) says when to leave them.

---

## The same skeleton, different verbs

Once the skeleton is in place, a different change is a different middle step. Marking the order paid, or taking a discount off every line, leaves the rest of the line as it was:

```java
  static EitherPath<OrderError, Order> markPaid(OrderRepository orders, UUID id) {
    return orders
        .find(id)
        .toEitherPath(OrderError.notFound(id))
        .map(order -> OrderFocus.status().set(OrderStatus.PAID, order))
        .via(orders::save);
  }

  static EitherPath<OrderError, Order> discountEveryLine(
      OrderRepository orders, UUID id, UnaryOperator<BigDecimal> discount) {
    return orders
        .find(id)
        .toEitherPath(OrderError.notFound(id))
        .map(order -> OrderFocus.lines().price().modifyAll(discount, order))
        .via(orders::save);
  }

```

The discount uses a traversal, `OrderFocus.lines().price()`, which reaches every line's price at once, and `modifyAll` applies the discount to each one. Only the middle step changes, and each is a function from `Order` to `Order`, so one method that takes that function could serve all three.

~~~admonish note title="Why `OrderError.notFound(id)`, and not `new OrderError.NotFound(id)`?"
Mid-chain, `toEitherPath` has no target type to infer from, so it takes its error type from its argument. Given a `NotFound`, the whole chain's error type becomes `NotFound`, and the method no longer returns what it declares:

<!-- verify:rejects "NotFound,?>,B>" -->
```java
static EitherPath<OrderError, Order> changeEmail(
    OrderRepository orders, UUID id, String newEmail) {
  return orders
      .find(id)
      .toEitherPath(new OrderError.NotFound(id))
      .map(order -> OrderFocus.customer().email().value().set(newEmail, order))
      .via(orders::save);
}
```

The compiler rejects the `via`: `EitherPath<OrderError,Order> cannot be converted to Chainable<? extends EitherPath<? extends NotFound,?>,B>`. The chain is typed on `NotFound`, and `save` can fail with any `OrderError`. A factory typed as the sealed `OrderError` fixes the error type at the first step, so every later step fits. Naming the type once works too, as `.<OrderError>toEitherPath(new OrderError.NotFound(id))`, which the [Quickstart](../quickstart.md) uses. [Effect compiler errors](../effect/compiler_errors.md#a-chain-typed-on-one-variant) shows how to read the message, and the other places a chain takes its error type.
~~~

The Ship pages that follow take the type-class layers in order. They start beneath them, with how `Kind<F, A>` stands for a container, in [How the Encoding Works](core-concepts.md).

~~~admonish info title="Hands-On Learning"
Write this line one layer at a time in [Tutorial 00: One Line, Six Layers](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/tutorial/coretypes/Tutorial00_OneLineSixLayers.java) (9 exercises), then grow it into a checkout step in the [Capstone: One Line, Six Layers Grows Up](../tutorials/capstone/capstone_journey.md) (7 exercises).
~~~

<!-- description: How Higher-Kinded-J writes "any container F" in Java: witness types, Kind<F, A>, widen and narrow, WitnessArity, and what each core type does with null. -->

# How the Encoding Works

_Write "any container `F`" in Java: a witness names the container, and `Kind<F, A>` carries the name._

<!-- source: Jonathan Swift, Gulliver's Travels (1726), Part III, Chapter V; text checked against Project Gutenberg eBook #829 -->
> _"since words are only names for things, it would be more convenient for all men to carry about them such things as were necessary to express a particular business they are to discourse on."_
>
> — Jonathan Swift, *Gulliver's Travels* (Part III, Chapter V)

The sages of Lagado gave up words and carried the things themselves. Java cannot carry a type constructor about, so Higher-Kinded-J carries a name for one instead: the witness.

![A head in profile, made of jigsaw pieces printed with gears and sketches, its top pieces lifting away](../images/concepts.png)

~~~admonish info title="What You'll Learn"
- Read a `Kind<F, A>` and name the container its witness stands for
- Move a value into the encoding with `widen` and back with `narrow`, and say what each costs
- Declare the `WitnessArity` bound on a generic method
- Predict what a core type does with `null`, and return `Unit` or a `Maybe` instead
- Tell an encoding failure from a domain failure
~~~

~~~admonish example title="See Example Code"
**The code on this page is [EncodingBook.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/encoding/EncodingBook.java)**: the page includes it, so the build compiles and runs it. [EncodingBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/encoding/EncodingBookTest.java) holds each claim the page makes.
~~~

---

## Why Java needs an encoding {#why-java-needs-an-encoding}

Java can abstract over a type, as `List<T>` does over `T`, but not over a type constructor such as `List` itself. A method for "any container of integers" does not compile:

<!-- verify:rejects "type parameter F" -->
```java
<F> F<Integer> doubleAll(F<Integer> numbers) {
    return numbers;
}
```

javac answers `unexpected type`, with `found: type parameter F`: a type parameter cannot take type arguments of its own. A language with higher-kinded types can name such a parameter, written `F<_>` in this book: "a container still waiting for its element type". It is not Java's `?` wildcard, which stands for an unknown type rather than a missing one. [Why Higher-Kinded Types?](hkt_introduction.md) says what this buys. Higher-Kinded-J gets there with ordinary interfaces. Each container gets a name, and generic code passes the name about in place of the container. The technique is called [defunctionalisation](../glossary/type-system.md#defunctionalisation).

## The witness and `Kind<F, A>` {#the-witness-and-kind}

A **witness** is an empty class that stands for one container. `ListKind.Witness` stands for `List`, `MaybeKind.Witness` for `Maybe`, and `EitherKind.Witness<L>` for `Either` with its left type `L` fixed. Its constructor is private, so nothing ever creates one: a witness appears only as a type argument.

**`Kind<F, A>`** is the interface that generic code works with. It reads "a container named by `F`, holding `A`", so `Kind<ListKind.Witness, String>` is the `Kind` view of a `List<String>`. A type class such as `Monad<F>` is written over `Kind<F, A>`, so one `Monad` interface serves every container that has a witness.

Two helpers cross between a value and its `Kind` view: `widen` takes the value into the encoding, and `narrow` brings it back. Here `flatMap` takes a `Kind` and gives one back, and only the `Either` instance behind `monad` looks inside:

```java
    Monad<EitherKind.Witness<String>> monad = Instances.monad(either());

    Either<String, Integer> start = Either.right(10);
    // widen: the same Either, seen as a Kind
    Kind<EitherKind.Witness<String>, Integer> kind = EITHER.widen(start);
    // flatMap takes the function first, then the Kind, and returns a Kind
    Kind<EitherKind.Witness<String>, String> result =
        monad.flatMap(i -> EITHER.widen(Either.right("v=" + i)), kind);
    // narrow: back to an Either
    Either<String, String> finalEither = EITHER.narrow(result);
    // Right(v=10)
```

`EITHER` is the helper that `EitherKindHelper` provides for `widen` and `narrow`, and each type has one, such as `MAYBE` or `LIST`. `Instances.monad(either())` is the value that knows how to `flatMap` an `Either`, and [Obtaining Instances](../functional/instances_facade.md) is the page on asking for one. [Lifting the Hood](lifting_the_hood.md) traces the same round trip call by call, with what each step allocates. The [type system glossary](../glossary/type-system.md) defines each of these terms in a line.

### The `WitnessArity` bound {#the-witnessarity-bound}

A witness also declares how many type arguments its container still needs: its *arity*. `Kind` asks for that declaration in its bound, `Kind<F extends WitnessArity<?>, A>`, so a class that is not a witness cannot stand in for `F`:

```java
// ListKind.Witness: one type argument still missing, like List<_>
final class Witness implements WitnessArity<TypeArity.Unary> {
    private Witness() {}
}

// EitherKind2.Witness: two type arguments still missing, like Either<_, _>
final class Witness implements WitnessArity<TypeArity.Binary> {
    private Witness() {}
}
```

Most type classes, `Functor` and `Monad` among them, ask for a unary witness. `Either` has two type arguments, so its `Monad` fixes the left one, and `EitherKind.Witness<L>` is unary. `Bifunctor` and `Profunctor` take a binary witness, over `Kind2`. A generic method puts the same bound on its own `F`, as `totals` does in the [Foundations](foundations_intro.md) introduction: `<F extends WitnessArity<TypeArity.Unary>>`. [Type Arity](type-arity.md) covers the rest.

## The pieces each type provides {#the-pieces-each-type-provides}

Every container that joins the encoding brings the same pieces. They differ in one respect: whether the container is its own `Kind`, or is held in a small record that is.

| Piece | A type that is its own `Kind`: `Maybe` | A type held in a record: `Optional` |
|---|---|---|
| Witness | `MaybeKind.Witness` | `OptionalKind.Witness` |
| `Kind` interface | `MaybeKind<A>`, which `Maybe` itself extends | `OptionalKind<A>`, which a holder record implements |
| Helper | `MAYBE`, on `MaybeKindHelper` | `OPTIONAL`, on `OptionalKindHelper` |
| `widen` | Returns the same object: a `Maybe` already is its `Kind` | Wraps the `Optional` in the holder, one allocation |
| `narrow` | Checks the type, then casts | Checks for the holder, then returns the `Optional` it holds |
| Type class instances | `MaybeMonad`, `MaybeTraverse` and others | `OptionalMonad`, `OptionalTraverse` and others |

The JDK's types cannot implement `Kind`, so they are held, and so are two of the library's own: `Lazy` and `Tuple2`. A type that is its own `Kind` crosses into the encoding without an allocation, and a debugger shows the `Maybe` itself, not a holder. [Adding Your Own Type](extending-simulation.md) shows how to give a type of your own these pieces.

## Values, never null {#values-never-null}

Higher-Kinded-J's own types hold a value, never `null`. They refuse it at the factory, and a `map` function that returns `null` fails them, so an absent value shows in the type. `Maybe` and `Id` treat `null` differently:

| Type | A `null` value | A `map` function that returns `null` |
|---|---|---|
| `Either`, `Try`, `Validated`, `EitherOrBoth`, `NonEmptyList`, `IO`, `VTask`, `Lazy`, `Reader`, `Context`, `State`, `Writer`, `Free` and `Trampoline` | Refused: `Either.right(null)` and `Instances.monad(either()).of(null)` throw `NullPointerException` | Fails: `Either` throws at once, `Try` gives a `Failure`, and `IO` fails when it runs |
| `Maybe` | Read as `Nothing` by `Maybe.fromNullable` and by the instance's `of`, though `Maybe.just(null)` throws | Read as `Nothing` |
| `Id` | Held | Held |

When a step has nothing to return, return [`Unit`](#unit). When a value may be absent, hold it in a `Maybe`. The JDK containers, the transformers and the tuples have rules of their own, in [Where null is read or held](#where-null-is-read-or-held). A `null` also takes a value outside the type class laws, as [Laws and Where HKJ Bends Them](laws.md) says.

[What a nullness checker sees](#what-a-nullness-checker-sees) says which of these refusals a checker reports at compile time.

## `Unit`: a value for "nothing to return" {#unit}

`void` is not a type, so it cannot fill the `A` in `Kind<F, A>`. Java's own stand-in, `Void`, has only `null` as a value, which the types on this page refuse. `org.higherkindedj.hkt.Unit` has exactly one value, `Unit.INSTANCE`. An action run for its effect gives `Unit`, and a type whose failure carries no detail takes `Unit` as its error type, as `Maybe`'s [`MonadError`](../functional/monad_error.md) does:

```java
    // run for its effect, with nothing to return, so it gives Unit
    Kind<IOKind.Witness, Unit> confirm =
        IO_OP.delay(
            () -> {
              System.out.println("Order confirmed");
              return Unit.INSTANCE;
            });
    IO_OP.unsafeRunSync(confirm);

    // Maybe's error type is Unit, so raising it gives Nothing
    MonadError<MaybeKind.Witness, Unit> maybeErrors = Instances.monadError(maybe());
    Maybe<String> absent = MAYBE.narrow(maybeErrors.raiseError(Unit.INSTANCE));
    // Nothing
```

The source is [`Unit.java`](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-api/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/hkt/Unit.java).

~~~admonish tip title="You can ship now"
You can now run any type class over a value you hold: widen it, call the instance, and narrow the result, returning `Unit` or a `Maybe` where you might have returned `null`. The rest of this page is for when a `null` turns up or `narrow` throws.
~~~

~~~admonish question title="Checkpoint: a unary witness where a binary one goes" id="check-encoding-arity"
`EitherKind.Witness<String>` names `Either` with its left type fixed. Can it stand in for `F` in `Bifunctor<F>`, which maps both sides of a two-sided type?

<!-- verify:rejects "is not within bounds of type-variable" -->
```java
Bifunctor<EitherKind.Witness<String>> bifunctor;
```
~~~

~~~admonish success title="Answer and why" collapsible=true id="check-encoding-arity-answer"
**No: javac refuses it, saying the witness "is not within bounds of type-variable" `F`.** `Bifunctor` takes a binary witness, over `Kind2`, and `EitherKind.Witness<L>` is unary, because fixing the left type leaves one argument missing. `EitherKind2.Witness`, with both missing, is the one `Bifunctor` takes.

Where this lives: [The `WitnessArity` bound](#the-witnessarity-bound).
~~~

~~~admonish question title="Checkpoint: `of(null)` in two containers" id="check-encoding-null"
What does `Instances.monad(maybe()).of(null)` give back, and what does `Instances.monad(try_()).of(null)` do?
~~~

~~~admonish success title="Answer and why" collapsible=true id="check-encoding-null-answer"
**`Nothing`, and it throws `NullPointerException`.** `Maybe`'s instance reads a `null` value as `Nothing`, and `Try` refuses one, as every library type in the table does except `Maybe` and `Id`. So code written for any `F` never hands `null` to `of`: what comes back depends on `F`.

Where this lives: [Values, never null](#values-never-null).
~~~

---

## The fine print {#the-fine-print}

### Where null is read or held {#where-null-is-read-or-held}

Some types read `null` as empty, or hold it, by design:

- **The JDK containers' instances read `null` as empty.** `of(null)` gives an empty `Optional`, `List` or `Stream`, and a `CompletableFuture` completed with `null`.
- **`MaybeT` and `OptionalT` read `null` as `Maybe` and `Optional` do.** Their `of(null)` gives an empty inner value.
- **The tuples hold whatever they are given.** `Tuple.of(null, null)` builds a `Tuple2` of two nulls.
- **`Const` ignores the value.** It never holds one, so `of(null)` gives the monoid's empty value.

So generic code never hands `null` to `of`: what comes back depends on `F`. A `flatMap` function must return a `Kind` in every type, so one that returns `null` throws `KindUnwrapException`.

### What a nullness checker sees {#what-a-nullness-checker-sees}

Every `hkj-core` package is `@NullMarked`, so a JSpecify nullness checker such as NullAway checks each call against the types the library declares. It flags a nullable value passed to `Either.right` or `VTask.succeed`, and a `map` function or a `VTask` written as a lambda that returns one.

In JSpecify mode alone, NullAway checks what a lambda returns against `Function` and the interfaces that extend it, such as `UnaryOperator`, and against no other functional interface from the JDK. So it never checks a lambda or method reference passed as a `Supplier`, a `Callable` or a `BiFunction`. A lambda that returns a `@Nullable` field compiles when passed to `VTask.of`, and the task fails only when it runs, with `callable must not return null`. `Try.of` gives a `Failure` holding a `NullPointerException`, `supplier must not return null`, and an `IO` built by `IO.delay` fails when it runs with `thunk must not return null`.

NullAway checks a lambda or method reference given to `Try.attempt` or `Lazy.defer` in JSpecify mode alone, since they take the library's own interfaces. For the rest, two NullAway options close most of the gap:

| A null returned through | In JSpecify mode alone | With both options |
|---|---|---|
| A lambda or method reference passed as a `Supplier`, `Callable` or `BiFunction`, as to `Try.of`, `VTask.of`, `IO.delay`, `Path.tryOf`, `Path.io`, `Path.vtask` or `VStream.zipWith` | Missed | Flagged |
| A `Supplier<@Nullable T>` or `Callable<@Nullable T>` variable, where the parameter is `Supplier<A>` or `Callable<A>`, as `VTask.of` and `IO.delay` take | Flagged | Flagged |
| The same variable, where the parameter is `Supplier<? extends A>`, as `Try.of` and `Path.lazyDefer` take | Missed | Flagged |
| A method reference to such a variable, as in `Try.of(s::get)` | Missed | Missed |

So pass a supplier variable itself, never as `s::get`.

Set `-XepOpt:NullAway:JSpecifyJDKModels=true` and `-XepOpt:NullAway:HandleWildcardGenerics=true` beside your build's other NullAway options. Both need JSpecify mode, `-XepOpt:NullAway:JSpecifyMode=true`. `JSpecifyJDKModels` arrived in NullAway 0.14.0, and the library checks both options with 0.14.2 today. The `net.ltgt.errorprone` Gradle plugin takes each as an `option`, as in `option("NullAway:JSpecifyJDKModels", "true")`. The models annotate much of the JDK, so your own code is held to them too: a dereferenced `System.getProperty` result is flagged.

### When `narrow` throws {#when-narrow-throws}

Two kinds of failure meet in this encoding, and they are handled differently:

- **A domain failure is a value.** A `Left`, a `Nothing`, a `Try.Failure` or a failed `CompletableFuture` travels inside its container, and the type's own methods or `MonadError`'s `handleErrorWith` recover from it.
- **An encoding failure is a bug.** `narrow` throws `KindUnwrapException`, an unchecked `IllegalArgumentException`, for `null` or another container's `Kind`, as does a `flatMap` function that returns `null`. Fix the mismatch rather than catch it.

---

~~~admonish info title="Hands-On Learning"
Practise widening and narrowing in [Tutorial 01: Kind Basics](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/tutorial/coretypes/Tutorial01_KindBasics.java) (5 exercises).
~~~

---

**Previous:** [One Line, Six Layers](one_line_six_layers.md)
**Next:** [Working with Kind](usage-guide.md)

<!-- description: Write one Java method for any container with Higher-Kinded-J: pick a witness, ask Instances for its type class, then widen, call and narrow. -->

# Working with Kind: Widen, Call, Narrow

_Five steps take a value into `Kind<F, A>`, through a type class, and back out._

![A man in a glass-walled room holds his hands together in a diamond shape in front of him](../images/oa-movements.png)

~~~admonish info title="What You'll Learn"
- Pick the witness for a container, and ask `Instances` for its type class
- Widen a value, call the type class on it, and narrow the result
- Write one method over `MonadError` that runs unchanged on a `Maybe` and an `Either`
- Recover from a failure inside the encoding, in code written for any container
~~~

~~~admonish example title="See Example Code"
**The code on this page is [WorkingWithKindBook.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/workflow/WorkingWithKindBook.java)**: the page includes it, so the build compiles and runs it. [WorkingWithKindBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/workflow/WorkingWithKindBookTest.java) holds each claim the page makes.
~~~

---

This page is for code written once for any container. Code for one container, such as a service method that returns an `EitherPath`, stays with [Effect Paths](../effect/ch_intro.md), and the [Foundations](foundations_intro.md) introduction's "Which level am I working at?" tip says how to tell which you need.

## The five steps {#the-five-steps}

1. **Pick the witness.** It names the container in your types: `MaybeKind.Witness` for `Maybe`, `EitherKind.Witness<L>` for an `Either` whose left type is `L`, `ListKind.Witness` for `List`, and `OptionalKind.Witness` for `Optional`. Every other type follows the pattern, such as `TryKind.Witness` for `Try`.
2. **Ask `Instances` for the type class.** `Instances.monadError(maybe())` gives `Maybe`'s `MonadError`, where `maybe()` names the witness. [Obtaining Instances](../functional/instances_facade.md) covers every lookup.
3. **Widen the value** with its type's helper, such as `MAYBE.widen(...)`.
4. **Call a method on the instance, passing the `Kind`**: `map`, `flatMap`, `raiseError` or `handleErrorWith`. A `Kind` has no methods of its own.
5. **Narrow the result** with the same helper, such as `MAYBE.narrow(...)`.

The code on this page uses these imports, with `Order` and `OrderStatus` from the order records the [Optics](../optics/ch_intro.md) chapter introduces:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.either.EitherKindHelper.EITHER;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.either;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.maybe;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.optional;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.maybe.MaybeKindHelper.MAYBE;

import java.math.BigDecimal;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.Order;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.OrderStatus;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Kind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.MonadError;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.TypeArity;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Unit;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.WitnessArity;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.either.Either;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.either.EitherKind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Instances;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.maybe.Maybe;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.maybe.MaybeKind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.optional.OptionalKind;
```

## Write the method once {#write-the-method-once}

`totalUnlessShipped` totals an order, and refuses one that has already shipped. It never names `Maybe`: it is written over a `MonadError` for any `F`.

```java
  // One method for any container F that can hold a failure of type E
  static <F extends WitnessArity<TypeArity.Unary>, E> Kind<F, BigDecimal> totalUnlessShipped(
      MonadError<F, E> monadError, Kind<F, Order> order, E refusal) {
    return monadError.flatMap(
        o ->
            o.status() == OrderStatus.SHIPPED
                ? monadError.raiseError(refusal)
                : monadError.of(total(o)),
        order);
  }

```

- **It asks for a `MonadError`, because refusing an order is a failure.** The introduction's `totals` needed only a `Functor`, since nothing there could fail. A [`MonadError`](../functional/monad_error.md) is a `Monad` that can also raise an error and recover from one.
- **The bound on `F` says it names a container with one type argument missing.** [The `WitnessArity` bound](core-concepts.md#the-witnessarity-bound) explains it.
- **`of` and `raiseError` come from the instance**, so the method builds results without knowing the container.
- **`flatMap` runs its function only when there is an order.** On a `Nothing`, the function never runs, and the `Nothing` comes back.
- **The caller passes the error.** The method knows nothing about `E`, so it cannot build one: the caller passes `Unit.INSTANCE` for `Maybe`, and a message for an `Either` whose left type is `String`.

## Take it through the steps {#take-it-through-the-steps}

Here the five steps total an order held in a `Maybe`:

```java
    // steps 1 and 2: Maybe's witness, named by maybe(), and the instance for it
    MonadError<MaybeKind.Witness, Unit> maybeErrors = Instances.monadError(maybe());

    // step 3, widen: the Maybe, seen as a Kind
    Kind<MaybeKind.Witness, Order> found = MAYBE.widen(Maybe.just(placed));

    // step 4, call: the method sees only the Kind and the instance
    Kind<MaybeKind.Witness, BigDecimal> totalKind =
        totalUnlessShipped(maybeErrors, found, Unit.INSTANCE);

    // step 5, narrow: back to a Maybe
    Maybe<BigDecimal> maybeTotal = MAYBE.narrow(totalKind);
    // Just(50.00)
```

The same method runs unchanged on an `Either`:

```java
    // the same method, unchanged, with Either's instance and a message as the error
    MonadError<EitherKind.Witness<String>, String> eitherErrors = Instances.monadError(either());

    Either<String, BigDecimal> refused =
        EITHER.narrow(
            totalUnlessShipped(
                eitherErrors, EITHER.widen(Either.right(shipped)), "order already shipped"));
    // Left(order already shipped)
```

~~~admonish warning title="The compiler does not check `E`"
`Instances.monadError` takes `E` from the variable you assign it to, and nothing checks it against the instance. `MonadError<MaybeKind.Witness, String>` compiles, then throws `ClassCastException` when you raise a `String`. Declare the error type the instance has, `Unit` for `Maybe`, from the table in [Obtaining Instances](../functional/instances_facade.md#zero-argument-lookups).
~~~

## Recover in code for any container {#recover-in-code-for-any-container}

Code written for any `F` cannot narrow, because it does not know which helper to use. So it recovers through the instance: `handleErrorWith` takes the failed `Kind` and a function from the error to a replacement.

```java
  // Generic code cannot narrow, so it recovers through the instance
  static <F extends WitnessArity<TypeArity.Unary>, E> Kind<F, BigDecimal> totalOrZero(
      MonadError<F, E> monadError, Kind<F, Order> order, E refusal) {
    return monadError.handleErrorWith(
        totalUnlessShipped(monadError, order, refusal), _ -> monadError.of(BigDecimal.ZERO));
  }

```

```java
    // a shipped order, recovered to zero before narrowing
    Maybe<BigDecimal> orZero =
        MAYBE.narrow(totalOrZero(maybeErrors, MAYBE.widen(Maybe.just(shipped)), Unit.INSTANCE));
    // Just(0)
```

For a chain of several dependent steps, a [for comprehension](../functional/for_comprehension.md) reads more easily than nested `flatMap` calls.

~~~admonish tip title="You can ship now"
You can now write one method over a type class and run it on any container that has an instance: widen, call, narrow. The rest of this page is for when `narrow` throws, and what widening allocates.
~~~

~~~admonish question title="Checkpoint: an error type the instance does not have" id="check-kind-error-type"
This compiles. What happens when it runs?

<!-- verify -->
```java
MonadError<OptionalKind.Witness, String> me = Instances.monadError(optional());
me.raiseError("out of stock");
```
~~~

~~~admonish success title="Answer and why" collapsible=true id="check-kind-error-type-answer"
**`raiseError` throws `ClassCastException`.** `Instances.monadError` takes `E` from the variable, and nothing checks it against the instance: `Optional`'s error type is `Unit`, not `String`. Declare `MonadError<OptionalKind.Witness, Unit>`, and raise `Unit.INSTANCE`.

Where this lives: [Take it through the steps](#take-it-through-the-steps), with each type's error type in [Obtaining Instances](../functional/instances_facade.md#zero-argument-lookups).
~~~

~~~admonish question title="Checkpoint: narrowing inside a generic method" id="check-kind-narrow"
A method written for any `F` gets back a `Kind<F, BigDecimal>`. Can it narrow that with `MAYBE.narrow` to give zero when the total failed? If not, how does it give zero?

<!-- verify:rejects "cannot be converted to" -->
```java
<F extends WitnessArity<TypeArity.Unary>> BigDecimal peekAtTotal(Kind<F, BigDecimal> total) {
    return MAYBE.narrow(total).orElse(BigDecimal.ZERO);
}
```
~~~

~~~admonish success title="Answer and why" collapsible=true id="check-kind-narrow-answer"
**No: javac refuses it, because `Kind<F, BigDecimal>` "cannot be converted to" `Maybe`'s `Kind`.** The method does not know that `F` is `Maybe`, so it cannot pick a helper, and it cannot return a plain `BigDecimal` either. It takes the `MonadError` for `F`, returns a `Kind<F, BigDecimal>` and recovers through the instance, as `totalOrZero` does with `handleErrorWith`. The caller, who knows `F`, narrows.

Where this lives: [Recover in code for any container](#recover-in-code-for-any-container).
~~~

---

## The fine print {#the-fine-print}

- **`narrow` throws `KindUnwrapException`** for a `null`, or for another container's `Kind`. It is a bug to fix, not a failure to recover. See [When `narrow` throws](core-concepts.md#when-narrow-throws).
- **Widening a JDK type allocates a holder.** Widening `Optional` or `List` allocates one, and widening `Maybe` or `Either` does not. See [The pieces each type provides](core-concepts.md#the-pieces-each-type-provides).

---

**Previous:** [How the Encoding Works](core-concepts.md)
**Next:** [Obtaining Instances](../functional/instances_facade.md)

<!-- Source: functional/instances_facade.md -->

<!-- description: Get any Higher-Kinded-J type class instance in one shape, Instances.monad(maybe()), and know which lookups the compiler checks. -->

# Obtaining Instances: The `Instances` Facade

_Ask one class for every type class instance, in one shape: `Instances.monad(maybe())`._

Each type class in Higher-Kinded-J has a standard instance for each type, and `org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Instances` is where you ask for it. Every lookup has the same shape, `Instances.x(...)`, so typing `Instances.` in an IDE lists every capability on offer.

~~~admonish info title="What You'll Learn"
- Ask `Instances` for a `Functor`, `Applicative`, `Monad` or `MonadError` with a token from `Witnesses`
- Pass the dependency an instance needs, such as a `Semigroup`, a `Monoid` or an outer `Monad`
- Tell the lookups the compiler checks from the three that can fail at run time
- Name the error type a `MonadError` really has
~~~

~~~admonish example title="See Example Code"
[InstancesFacadeBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/instances/InstancesFacadeBookTest.java) holds each claim this page makes, including that its tables match every token and every method.
~~~

---

## Zero-argument lookups

For a type whose instance needs nothing, pass its token: a method in `Witnesses`, such as `maybe()`, that names the type's witness.

<!-- verify -->
```java
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.maybe;

import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Applicative;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Functor;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Monad;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Instances;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.maybe.MaybeKind;

Monad<MaybeKind.Witness>       monad       = Instances.monad(maybe());
Applicative<MaybeKind.Witness> applicative = Instances.applicative(maybe());
Functor<MaybeKind.Witness>     functor     = Instances.functor(maybe());
```

The instance implements the whole `Functor`, `Applicative`, `Monad` chain in one object, so all three calls return that same object.

Three more lookups give a richer capability, for the types whose instance has it. `MonadError` adds raising and recovering from an error; `MonadZero` adds an empty value and `filter`; `Alternative` adds an empty value and `orElse`. For `Maybe` and `Optional`, `orElse` falls back to a second value when the first is empty; for `List`, `Stream` and `VStream`, it joins the two.

<!-- verify -->
```java
MonadError<MaybeKind.Witness, Unit> me  = Instances.monadError(maybe());
MonadZero<ListKind.Witness>         mz  = Instances.monadZero(list());
Alternative<OptionalKind.Witness>   alt = Instances.alternative(optional());
```

| Lookup | Type | Error type |
|---|---|---|
| `monadError` | `Maybe`, `Optional` | `Unit` |
| `monadError` | `Try`, `CompletableFuture`, `VTask` | `Throwable` |
| `monadError` | `Either<L, _>` | `L` |
| `monadZero` | `Maybe`, `Optional`, `List`, `Stream` | |
| `alternative` | `Maybe`, `Optional`, `List`, `Stream`, `VStream` | |

~~~admonish warning title="These three lookups compile for any token"
The compiler cannot check that a type has the capability you ask for. A type without it, as in `Instances.monadZero(either())`, throws `ClassCastException` when the call runs. A wrong error type compiles too: `MonadError<MaybeKind.Witness, String>` throws `ClassCastException` once you raise a `String`. Take `E` from the table.
~~~

### Type parameters come from your variable {#type-inference-for-phantom-typed-types}

Each token has a generic type, `Of<F>`, rather than a `Class`. Java has no generic class literals, so a `Class` would lose `Either`'s left type or `Reader`'s environment. The four tokens for types with such a parameter, `either()`, `reader()`, `context()` and `state()`, are generic methods, so the parameter comes from the variable you assign to:

<!-- verify -->
```java
// OrderError flows from the assignment target
Monad<EitherKind.Witness<OrderError>> m = Instances.monad(either());
```

### Available tokens

| Family | Tokens |
|--------|--------|
| No type parameter | `maybe()`, `io()`, `list()`, `nonEmptyList()`, `optional()`, `try_()`, `vtask()`, `vstream()`, `lazy()`, `stream()`, `completableFuture()`, `trampoline()`, `id()` |
| A type parameter | `either()`, `reader()`, `context()`, `state()` |

`try_()` ends in an underscore because `try` is a reserved word in Java.

---

## Argument-carrying instances

`Validated`, `EitherOrBoth`, `Writer` and the monad transformers each need a dependency: a `Semigroup`, which combines two values, a `Monoid`, which is a `Semigroup` with an empty value, or an outer `Monad`. None has a sensible default, so the facade asks for it in the method's parameters, where the compiler checks it and the IDE shows it:

<!-- verify -->
```java
MonadError<ValidatedKind.Witness<List<String>>, List<String>> v = Instances.validated(Semigroups.list());
Monad<EitherOrBothKind.Witness<List<String>>> eob = Instances.eitherOrBoth(Semigroups.list());
Monad<WriterKind.Witness<String>> w = Instances.writer(Monoids.string());

MonadError<EitherTKind.Witness<OptionalKind.Witness, String>, String> et =
    Instances.eitherT(Instances.monad(optional()));
MonadError<MaybeTKind.Witness<OptionalKind.Witness>, Unit> mt =
    Instances.maybeT(Instances.monad(optional()));
MonadError<OptionalTKind.Witness<MaybeKind.Witness>, Unit> ot =
    Instances.optionalT(Instances.monad(maybe()));
Monad<ReaderTKind.Witness<OptionalKind.Witness, String>> rt =
    Instances.readerT(Instances.monad(optional()));
Monad<StateTKind.Witness<Integer, OptionalKind.Witness>> st =
    Instances.stateT(Instances.monad(optional()));
MonadWriter<WriterTKind.Witness<OptionalKind.Witness, String>, String> wt =
    Instances.writerT(Instances.monad(optional()), Monoids.string());
```

A dependency in the signature cannot be missed: `Instances.validated()` with no `Semigroup` does not compile.

~~~admonish tip title="You can ship now"
You can now get any built-in instance in one shape: a token for a type that needs nothing, an argument for one that needs a dependency. The rest of this page is for what the compiler checks, what the facade covers, and the accessors behind it.
~~~

~~~admonish question title="Checkpoint: two lookups on `IO`" id="check-facade-cast"
Which of these throws, and when: `Instances.monad(io())` or `Instances.alternative(io())`?
~~~

~~~admonish success title="Answer and why" collapsible=true id="check-facade-cast-answer"
**`Instances.alternative(io())` throws `ClassCastException` when the call runs, and `Instances.monad(io())` succeeds.** The compiler cannot check that a type has the capability you ask for, and `IO`'s instance has no `Alternative`.

Where this lives: [Zero-argument lookups](#zero-argument-lookups).
~~~

~~~admonish question title="Checkpoint: a token for `Validated`" id="check-facade-argument"
`maybe()` is a token for `Instances.monad(maybe())`. Is there a `validated()` token, for `Instances.monad(validated())`?

<!-- verify:rejects "cannot find symbol" -->
```java
var monad = Instances.monad(validated());
```
~~~

~~~admonish success title="Answer and why" collapsible=true id="check-facade-argument-answer"
**No: javac reports "cannot find symbol".** `Validated` needs a `Semigroup` to join its errors, so it has no token. The facade asks for the `Semigroup` in the method's parameters instead: `Instances.validated(Semigroups.list())`.

Where this lives: [Argument-carrying instances](#argument-carrying-instances).
~~~

---

## The fine print {#the-fine-print}

### What the compiler checks {#what-the-compiler-checks}

`Instances` is a class of static methods, with no registry, no `ServiceLoader` and nothing to configure. A token lookup returns the instance its token carries, and a lookup that takes an argument builds a new instance on each call.

- **`monad`, `applicative` and `functor` are checked.** Every token carries a `Monad`, so these three always succeed.
- **`monadError`, `monadZero` and `alternative` cast, and the compiler cannot check the cast.** The warning in [Zero-argument lookups](#zero-argument-lookups) shows what fails, and when.

### The accessors behind the facade {#the-accessors-behind-the-facade}

Each class behind the facade has an accessor of its own, in one of four shapes. `Instances` is the one shape to use in new code:

<!-- verify -->
```java
Semigroup<String>            semi  = Semigroups.string();
Monad<OptionalKind.Witness>  outer = OptionalMonad.INSTANCE;

Monad<MaybeKind.Witness>             m1 = MaybeMonad.INSTANCE;            // a static field
Monad<EitherKind.Witness<String>>    m2 = EitherMonad.instance();         // a generic static method
MonadError<ValidatedKind.Witness<String>, String> v =
    ValidatedMonad.instance(semi);                                        // a factory with an argument
MonadError<EitherTKind.Witness<OptionalKind.Witness, String>, String> et =
    new EitherTMonad<>(outer);                                            // a constructor with an argument
```

### Scope

| Type class | Through `Instances` |
|------------|----------|
| `Functor`, `Applicative`, `Monad` | Yes, for every token |
| `MonadError`, `MonadZero`, `Alternative` | Yes, for the types in the table in [Zero-argument lookups](#zero-argument-lookups) |
| The instances that need an argument: `validated`, `eitherOrBoth`, `writer`, `eitherT`, `maybeT`, `optionalT`, `readerT`, `stateT`, `writerT` | Yes |
| `MonadWriter` | Only through `writerT` |
| `MonadReader`, `MonadState` | Not offered: `ReaderTMonadReader` and `StateTMonadState` are separate classes |
| `Traverse`, `Selective`, `Foldable` | Not offered: each is a separate instance class, such as `MaybeTraverse.INSTANCE` |
| `Free`, `FreeApplicative` | Not offered: use `new FreeMonad<>()` or `FreeApApplicative.instance()` |
| `Const` | Not offered: it has an `Applicative` and a `Bifunctor`, and a token carries a `Monad` |
| `Semigroup`, `Monoid` | Not offered: they are keyed by a value type, not a witness; use `Semigroups` and `Monoids` |
| `Bifunctor`, `Profunctor` | Not offered: they take a binary witness |

---

- [Monad Transformers](../transformers/transformers.md)

<!-- description: Map a function inside any container with Functor: write one method for Maybe, Either and List, and check an instance against the two laws with hkj-test. -->

# Functor: The "Mappable" Type Class

_Name the `map` every container shares, and write one method that maps inside any of them._

~~~admonish info title="What You'll Learn"
- Recognise `map` on `Optional`, `List`, `CompletableFuture` and `MaybePath` as one shape: `Functor`
- Write a method that maps inside any container, through its `Functor`
- Say what `map` promises, and what it leaves to each container
- Check an instance against the two functor laws with `hkj-test`'s `FunctorLaws`
~~~

~~~admonish example title="See Example Code"
**The code on this page is [FunctorBook.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/functor/FunctorBook.java)**, with `totals` from [FoundationsIntroBook.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/intro/FoundationsIntroBook.java): the page includes both, so the build compiles and runs them. [FunctorBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/functor/FunctorBookTest.java) holds each claim the page makes, and [FoundationsLawsBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/FoundationsLawsBookTest.java) checks the laws.
~~~

---

You have written this code:

<!-- verify -->
```java
optional.map(String::length);
list.stream().map(String::length).toList();
completableFuture.thenApply(String::length);
maybePath.map(String::length);
```

Four method calls, four containers, the same shape. `Functor` is the name of that shape: a container's `map`, keeping the two laws on this page. Most type classes in this chapter work this way, naming a capability you already use.

For one container, an [Effect Path](../effect/ch_intro.md) has its own `map`, as `maybePath.map` shows, and application code stays there. This page is for a method written once for any container, as the [Foundations](../hkts/foundations_intro.md) introduction's `totals` is.

The code on this page uses these imports, with `Order` from the order records the [Optics](../optics/ch_intro.md) chapter introduces:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.either.EitherKindHelper.EITHER;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Instances.functor;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.either;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.list;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.maybe;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.list.ListKindHelper.LIST;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.maybe.MaybeKindHelper.MAYBE;

import java.math.BigDecimal;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.function.Function;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.Order;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Functor;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Kind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.TypeArity;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.WitnessArity;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.either.Either;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.maybe.Maybe;
```

## What `map` promises {#what-map-promises}

`Functor<F>` has one method, `map`:

<!-- verify -->
```java
public interface Functor<F extends WitnessArity<TypeArity.Unary>> {
  <A, B> Kind<F, B> map(Function<? super A, ? extends B> f, Kind<F, A> fa);
}
```

Given a function from `A` to `B` and a [`Kind<F, A>`](../hkts/core-concepts.md#the-witness-and-kind), it returns a `Kind<F, B>`. It applies the function to the values inside and keeps the container's shape:

| The container holds | `map` gives | The function runs |
|---|---|---|
| Three orders, in a `List` | A `List` of three results | Once for each order |
| One order, in a `Just` or a `Right` | A `Just` or a `Right` of the result | Once |
| A deferred order, in an [`IO`](../monads/io_monad.md) | A new `IO` | Each time that `IO` runs |
| No value: a `Nothing`, an empty `Optional` or an empty `List` | The container, still empty | Never |
| A failure: a `Left` or a failed `Try` | The same failure | Never |

The function sees the values, never the container. The table assumes a function that returns a value and does not throw. [Values, never null](../hkts/core-concepts.md#values-never-null) says what each type does when it returns `null`.

## One method for any container {#one-method-for-any-container}

The introduction's `totals` is written over `Functor`. It takes the `Functor` for `F` and calls `map` on it:

```java
  // One method for any container F: the Functor passed in knows how to map inside F
  public static <F extends WitnessArity<TypeArity.Unary>> Kind<F, BigDecimal> totals(
      Functor<F> functor, Kind<F, Order> orders) {
    return functor.map(FoundationsIntroBook::total, orders);
  }

```

On containers that hold an order, it gives their totals, as [the introduction](../hkts/foundations_intro.md) shows. On containers that hold none, it keeps their shape:

```java
    Maybe<Order> noOrder = Maybe.nothing();
    Maybe<BigDecimal> noTotal = MAYBE.narrow(totals(functor(maybe()), MAYBE.widen(noOrder)));
    // Nothing
    Either<String, Order> notFound = Either.left("order not found");
    Either<String, BigDecimal> sameLeft =
        EITHER.narrow(totals(functor(either()), EITHER.widen(notFound)));
    // Left(order not found)
    List<Order> noOrders = List.of();
    List<BigDecimal> noTotals = LIST.narrow(totals(functor(list()), LIST.widen(noOrders)));
    // []
```

Nothing in `totals` checks for an empty container or a failure. Each container's `Functor` knows what mapping means there: `Maybe`'s gives back the `Nothing`, and `Either`'s gives back the `Left` as it was. That is why `totals` is written once.

## Two laws {#two-laws}

A `Functor` keeps two laws, and you have assumed both whenever you called `map`. Together they say that `map` changes the values and never the container.

~~~admonish note title="Laws"
- **Identity: mapping `a -> a` changes nothing.** `map(a -> a, fa)` equals `fa`.
- **Composition: two maps equal one map of both functions.** `map(g, map(f, fa))` equals `map(f.andThen(g), fa)`.

Two containers are equal when they hold equal values in the same shape. For a deferred container such as `IO`, they are equal when running both gives equal results. Comparing them runs both, and repeats any effect they have, so check the laws with an `IO` that gives the same result on every run, as the test's `IO.delay(() -> ORDER)` does. Both laws assume functions that never return `null`, and [Values, never null](../hkts/core-concepts.md#values-never-null) says what each type does with one.

`FunctorLaws`, in [`hkj-test`](../tooling/test_assertions.md#adding-the-dependency)'s `org.higherkindedj.hkt.laws` package, checks both laws on any instance, your own included. You pass it the equality to use. `KindEquivalence.byEqualsAfter`, in `org.higherkindedj.hkt.assertions`, builds one from a function it applies to both sides, and compares the two results with `equals`: `MAYBE::narrow` unwraps a `Maybe`, and for an `IO` the function also runs it. A test of `Maybe` and `IO` uses these imports, with JUnit's:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.io;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.maybe;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.io.IOKindHelper.IO_OP;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.maybe.MaybeKindHelper.MAYBE;

import java.util.function.BiPredicate;
import java.util.stream.Stream;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.Customer;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.Order;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Functor;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Kind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.assertions.KindEquivalence;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Instances;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.io.IO;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.io.IOKind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.laws.FunctorLaws;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.maybe.Maybe;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.maybe.MaybeKind;
```

The test checks both laws on a `Just`, a `Nothing` and an `IO`, where `ORDER` is any `Order`:

```java
  static Stream<Kind<MaybeKind.Witness, Order>> maybeOrders() {
    return Stream.of(MAYBE.widen(Maybe.just(ORDER)), MAYBE.widen(Maybe.nothing()));
  }

  @ParameterizedTest
  @MethodSource("maybeOrders")
  @DisplayName("Maybe's Functor keeps both laws, on a Just and on a Nothing")
  void maybeFunctorLaws(Kind<MaybeKind.Witness, Order> fa) {
    Functor<MaybeKind.Witness> functor = Instances.functor(maybe());
    BiPredicate<Kind<MaybeKind.Witness, ?>, Kind<MaybeKind.Witness, ?>> same =
        KindEquivalence.byEqualsAfter(MAYBE::narrow);
    FunctorLaws.assertIdentity(functor, fa, same);
    FunctorLaws.assertComposition(functor, fa, Order::customer, Customer::email, same);
  }

  @Test
  @DisplayName("IO's Functor keeps both laws, comparing what each IO gives when it runs")
  void ioFunctorLaws() {
    Functor<IOKind.Witness> functor = Instances.functor(io());
    Kind<IOKind.Witness, Order> fa = IO_OP.widen(IO.delay(() -> ORDER));
    BiPredicate<Kind<IOKind.Witness, ?>, Kind<IOKind.Witness, ?>> sameResult =
        KindEquivalence.byEqualsAfter(kind -> IO_OP.narrow(kind).unsafeRunSync());
    FunctorLaws.assertIdentity(functor, fa, sameResult);
    FunctorLaws.assertComposition(functor, fa, Order::customer, Customer::email, sameResult);
  }

```
~~~

## Back to the one-liner {#back-to-the-one-liner}

```java
  static EitherPath<OrderError, Order> changeEmail(
      OrderRepository orders, UUID id, String newEmail) {
    return orders
        .find(id)
        .toEitherPath(OrderError.notFound(id))
        .map(order -> OrderFocus.customer().email().value().set(newEmail, order))
        .via(orders::save);
  }

```

`.map(...)` is the line's Functor layer: on a `Right` it changes the order's email, and a `Left` passes through untouched. [Two encodings, one algebra](../hkts/one_line_six_layers.md#two-encodings-one-algebra) matches it to `Functor`'s `map` over `Kind`.

## Things people get wrong {#things-people-get-wrong}

~~~admonish warning title="Common Misunderstandings"
- **"Mapping iterates."** Only over a collection. `map` runs the function at most once on an `Optional`, and on an `Either` only for a `Right`.
- **"`map` runs the function now."** Not on a deferred container. `IO`'s `map` builds a new `IO`, and the function runs only when that `IO` runs. `Functor` promises the shape, not when the function runs.
- **"It is `Stream.map` with extra steps."** The aim is one method for every container that can be mapped. You pay some ceremony at the boundary, the `widen` and `narrow`, so that the method's body is written once.
- **"`map` can run a step that fails."** A function that returns an `Either` gives an `Either` inside an `Either`, such as `Right(Left(order already shipped))`. A step that can fail needs `flatMap`, which [Monad](monad.md) teaches.
~~~

~~~admonish tip title="You can ship now"
You can now write a method that maps inside any container, as `totals` does, and check an instance of your own against the two laws. The rest of this page is for which types have a `Functor`, and what builds on it.
~~~

~~~admonish question title="Checkpoint: `map` over an `Invalid`" id="check-functor-invalid"
With `Validated`'s instance, you map `LineItem::quantity` over a `Validated` that is `Invalid([SKU is blank])`. What comes back, and does `LineItem::quantity` run?
~~~

~~~admonish success title="Answer and why" collapsible=true id="check-functor-invalid-answer"
**The same `Invalid([SKU is blank])`, and the function never runs.** `map` changes the values a container holds and keeps its shape. An `Invalid` holds no line item, so there is nothing to change.

Where this lives: [What `map` promises](#what-map-promises).
~~~

~~~admonish question title="Checkpoint: the identity law on `IO`" id="check-functor-io-equality"
You check `IO`'s `Functor` against the identity law, with an equality built by `KindEquivalence.byEqualsAfter(IO_OP::narrow)`. Does `FunctorLaws.assertIdentity` pass?
~~~

~~~admonish success title="Answer and why" collapsible=true id="check-functor-io-equality-answer"
**No.** `map(a -> a, io)` builds a new `IO`, so comparing the two `IO` objects with `equals` fails, though both give the same result. For a deferred container, two values are equal when running both gives equal results, so the equality must run them, on an `IO` that gives the same result every run: `byEqualsAfter(kind -> IO_OP.narrow(kind).unsafeRunSync())`.

Where this lives: [Two laws](#two-laws).
~~~

---

## The fine print {#the-fine-print}

### Which types have a `Functor` {#which-types-have-a-functor}

| Types | Their `Functor` comes from |
|---|---|
| Every token in `Witnesses` | Their `Monad`, so [`Instances.functor(token)` always succeeds](instances_facade.md#what-the-compiler-checks) |
| `Validated`, `Writer`, `EitherOrBoth` and the transformers | Their `Monad`, from `Instances` with its argument, such as `Instances.validated(semigroup)` |
| `Free` | Its `Monad`, built with `new FreeMonad<>()` |
| `Const`, `FreeAp` and `Fetch` | An `Applicative`, with no `Monad` |
| `EitherF` | `EitherFFunctor` alone |
| `Coyoneda` | `CoyonedaFunctor`, which gives a `Functor` to a type that has none, as [Coyoneda](../monads/coyoneda.md) shows |

### What builds on `Functor` {#what-builds-on-functor}

- **`Applicative` extends `Functor`, and `Monad` extends `Applicative`.** Every instance on the next pages has this `map`.
- **`Traverse` extends both `Functor` and `Foldable`.** [Foldable and Traverse](foldable_and_traverse.md) covers it.
- **`Bifunctor` is not a `Functor`.** It maps a type with two parameters, such as both sides of an `Either`, as [Bifunctor](bifunctor.md) shows.

---

~~~admonish tip title="See Also"
- [Applicative](applicative.md): combining independent computations
- [Monad](monad.md): sequencing dependent computations with `flatMap`
- [Bifunctor](bifunctor.md): mapping over types with two parameters
- [One Line, Six Layers](../hkts/one_line_six_layers.md): where `map` sits in a whole service method
~~~

~~~admonish tip title="Further Reading"
- **Magnus Smith**, [Functors and Monads with Java and Scala](https://blog.scottlogic.com/2025/03/31/functors-monads-with-java-and-scala.html): a practical guide to functors and monads in Java
- **Bartosz Milewski**, [Functors](https://bartoszmilewski.com/2015/01/20/functors/): functors from category theory to code
- **Mark Seemann**, [Functors](https://blog.ploeh.dk/2018/03/22/functors/): a practical introduction, with examples in languages close to Java
~~~
