<!-- description: Combine independent values with Applicative: build one value from several with map2 to map5, see what each instance does, and check the four laws with hkj-test. -->

# Applicative: Combining Independent Computations

_Combine values that do not depend on one another, and let each container's instance decide what a failure does._

<!-- source: John Locke, An Essay Concerning Human Understanding, Book II, Chapter XII, section 1, a passage added in the fourth edition (1700); text checked against A. C. Fraser's edition (Clarendon Press, 1894), vol. I, p. 213 -->
> _"The acts of the mind, wherein it exerts its power over its simple ideas, are chiefly these three: (1) Combining several simple ideas into one compound one; and thus all complex ideas are made."_
>
> — John Locke, *An Essay Concerning Human Understanding* (Book II, Chapter XII)

`map3` performs Locke's first act: it combines three checked values into one line item.

~~~admonish info title="What You'll Learn"
- Build one value from several independent ones with `map2` to `map5`
- Say what `of` and `ap` add to `Functor`
- Predict what each instance's `map2` does: combine the errors, keep the first, or give every combination
- Choose between `map3` and `flatMap` by whether a step needs another's result
- Check an instance against the four applicative laws with `hkj-test`'s `ApplicativeLaws`
~~~

~~~admonish example title="See Example Code"
**The code on this page is [ApplicativeBook.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/applicative/ApplicativeBook.java)**: the page includes it, so the build compiles and runs it. [ApplicativeBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/applicative/ApplicativeBookTest.java) holds what the page says each instance does, and [FoundationsLawsBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/FoundationsLawsBookTest.java) checks the laws.
~~~

---

## When `map` is not enough {#when-map-is-not-enough}

[Functor](functor.md)'s `map` changes the value inside one container, and cannot reach a second. A line item needs three values: a SKU, a quantity and a price, each checked on its own. None of the checks needs another's result, so a line item that fails two checks could report both.

For one container, an [Effect Path](../effect/ch_intro.md) combines values with `zipWith`, and `ValidationPath`'s `zipWithAccum` keeps every error, as [Error Accumulation](../effect/path_validation.md#error-accumulation-the-point-of-it-all) shows. `ListPath`'s `zipWith` pairs by position, where `List`'s `Applicative` and `NonDetPath` give every combination. Application code stays on the Paths. This page is for a method written once for any container.

The code on this page uses these imports, with `LineItem` from the order records the [Optics](../optics/ch_intro.md) chapter introduces:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.either.EitherKindHelper.EITHER;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.either;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.validated.ValidatedKindHelper.VALIDATED;

import java.math.BigDecimal;
import java.util.List;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.LineItem;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Applicative;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Kind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Semigroups;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.TypeArity;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.WitnessArity;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.either.Either;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.either.EitherKind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Instances;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.validated.Validated;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.validated.ValidatedKind;
```

Each check returns a [`Validated`](../monads/validated_monad.md): a `Valid` holding the value, or an `Invalid` holding a list of errors.

```java
  // Three checks, each on one field, and none needs another's result
  static Validated<List<String>, String> checkSku(String sku) {
    return sku.isBlank() ? Validated.invalid(List.of("SKU is blank")) : Validated.valid(sku);
  }

  static Validated<List<String>, Integer> checkQuantity(int quantity) {
    return quantity > 0
        ? Validated.valid(quantity)
        : Validated.invalid(List.of("quantity must be positive"));
  }

  static Validated<List<String>, BigDecimal> checkPrice(BigDecimal price) {
    return price.signum() >= 0
        ? Validated.valid(price)
        : Validated.invalid(List.of("price is negative"));
  }

```

## What an `Applicative` adds {#what-an-applicative-adds}

`Applicative<F>` extends `Functor<F>` with two methods, and builds `map2` to `map5` from `map` and `ap`:

<!-- verify -->
```java
public interface Applicative<F extends WitnessArity<TypeArity.Unary>> extends Functor<F> {

  <A> Kind<F, A> of(@Nullable A value);

  <A, B> Kind<F, B> ap(Kind<F, ? extends Function<A, B>> ff, Kind<F, A> fa);

  // abridged: the null checks and a curried overload are left out,
  // and map3, map4 and map5 follow the same pattern
  default <A, B, C> Kind<F, C> map2(
      Kind<F, A> fa, Kind<F, B> fb, BiFunction<? super A, ? super B, ? extends C> f) {
    return ap(map(a -> b -> f.apply(a, b), fa), fb);
  }
}
```

`of` lifts a plain value into the container: `Validated`'s gives a `Valid`, and `Maybe`'s a `Just`. `ap` applies a function held in a container to a value held in another.

You will rarely call `ap` yourself. `map(a -> b -> f.apply(a, b), fa)` gives a `Kind<F, Function<B, C>>`: the first value inside a function that waits for the second. `ap` applies that function to `fb`. `map3` to `map5` add an input each the same way, and they are what you call.

## One method for any container {#one-method-for-any-container}

`lineItem` is written over `Applicative`. It takes the `Applicative` for `F` and calls `map3` on it:

```java
  // One method for any container F: the Applicative passed in decides what a failure does
  public static <F extends WitnessArity<TypeArity.Unary>> Kind<F, LineItem> lineItem(
      Applicative<F> applicative,
      Kind<F, String> sku,
      Kind<F, Integer> quantity,
      Kind<F, BigDecimal> price) {
    return applicative.map3(sku, quantity, price, LineItem::new);
  }

```

Run it with `Validated`'s `Applicative`, then with `Either`'s, on a blank SKU and a quantity of zero:

```java
    Applicative<ValidatedKind.Witness<List<String>>> validatedApplicative =
        Instances.validated(Semigroups.list());
    Validated<List<String>, LineItem> everyError =
        VALIDATED.narrow(
            lineItem(
                validatedApplicative,
                VALIDATED.widen(checkSku(" ")),
                VALIDATED.widen(checkQuantity(0)),
                VALIDATED.widen(checkPrice(new BigDecimal("40.00")))));
    // Invalid([SKU is blank, quantity must be positive])

    Applicative<EitherKind.Witness<List<String>>> eitherApplicative =
        Instances.applicative(either());
    Either<List<String>, LineItem> firstError =
        EITHER.narrow(
            lineItem(
                eitherApplicative,
                EITHER.widen(checkSku(" ").toEither()),
                EITHER.widen(checkQuantity(0).toEither()),
                EITHER.widen(checkPrice(new BigDecimal("40.00")).toEither())));
    // Left([SKU is blank])
```

The call is the same both times. `Instances.validated` builds `Validated`'s instance from a [semigroup](semigroup_and_monoid.md), here one that joins two lists, so it reports both errors. With `Semigroups.first()` it would report only the first. `Either`'s instance always keeps the first. `toEither()` turns each check's `Validated` into an `Either` holding the same errors, so only the instance differs.

## What each instance does {#what-each-instance-does}

`Applicative` promises that `map3` combines the three values when all are there. What it does otherwise is each instance's own:

```mermaid
flowchart LR
    accTitle: The instance decides what map3 gives
    accDescr: Three independent checks, of the SKU, the quantity and the price, each feed map3. With Validated's Applicative and a semigroup that joins lists, map3 gives every error the checks found. With Either's, it gives only the first.
    S["checkSku"] --> M["map3"]
    Q["checkQuantity"] --> M
    P["checkPrice"] --> M
    M -->|"Validated's instance"| V["Invalid: every error"]
    M -->|"Either's instance"| E["Left: the first error"]
    classDef step fill:#8caaee,stroke:#1e66f5,color:#232634
    classDef error fill:#e78284,stroke:#d20f39,color:#232634
    class S,Q,P,M step
    class V,E error
```

The diagram shows three checks that never see one another's results, meeting only at `map3`, where the instance decides what comes out. With two inputs, the instances you will meet most behave like this, and `map3` to `map5` do the same with more:

| Instance | What `map2` does with its two inputs |
|---|---|
| `Validated`, from `Instances.validated(semigroup)` | Gives the combined value if both are valid; otherwise the invalid inputs' errors, combined by the semigroup in the order of the inputs. `Semigroups.list()` keeps every error |
| `Either`, `Try` | Gives the first failure, and drops the second |
| `Maybe`, `Optional` | Is empty if either input is empty |
| `List`, `NonEmptyList` | Gives every combination: two lists of two give four values |
| [`IO`](../monads/io_monad.md), `VTask` | Builds a new deferred value that, when it runs, runs the first input and then the second. If the first fails, the second never runs |
| `CompletableFuture` | Completes when both inputs have. `map2` starts neither future, so whether their work overlaps depends on what completes each one |
| `Writer`, from `Instances.writer(monoid)` | Gives the combined value, with both logs joined by the monoid |

So `Applicative` promises neither that errors accumulate nor that inputs run at the same time. Its promise is smaller: the inputs do not depend on one another. An instance can use that, as `Validated`'s does, or not, as `Either`'s does. To run two `VTask`s at the same time, use [`Par.map2`](../monads/vtask_monad.md#parallel-composition-with-par).

## Should you use `map3` or `flatMap`? {#map3-or-flatmap}

- **Use `flatMap` when a step needs the previous result.** Finding an order's customer needs the order first. `flatMap` is [Monad](monad.md)'s. On a failure that holds no value, such as a `Left`, it stops, because the next step has nothing to run on.
- **Use `map2` to `map5` when the steps are independent.** The instance can then do more with the failures, as `Validated`'s does.

The two can differ on one instance. `Instances.validated` returns a single instance with both methods: its `map3` combines the errors of every invalid input, and its `flatMap` stops at the first. [Applicative, Selective or Monad?](abstraction_levels.md) walks through the choice.

## Four laws {#four-laws}

An `Applicative` keeps four laws. Together they say that `of` adds nothing of its own, and that `ap` gives the same result however you group the calls.

~~~admonish note title="Laws"
- **Identity: applying a lifted `a -> a` changes nothing.** `ap(of(a -> a), fa)` equals `fa`.
- **Homomorphism: a lifted function on a lifted value lifts the result.** `ap(of(f), of(x))` equals `of(f.apply(x))`.
- **Interchange: lifting the value instead of the function changes nothing.** `ap(ff, of(x))` equals `ap(of(f -> f.apply(x)), ff)`.
- **Composition: grouping the applications differently changes nothing.** `ap(ap(ap(of(compose), u), v), w)` equals `ap(u, ap(v, w))`, where `compose` is `f -> g -> x -> f.apply(g.apply(x))`.

Two containers are equal when they hold equal values, or equal errors, in the same shape. For a deferred container such as `IO`, they are equal when running both gives equal results. The laws assume functions that never return `null`, and [Values, never null](../hkts/core-concepts.md#values-never-null) says what each type does with one.

`ApplicativeLaws`, beside `FunctorLaws` in [`hkj-test`](../tooling/test_assertions.md#adding-the-dependency)'s `org.higherkindedj.hkt.laws` package, checks each law on any instance. `KindEquivalence.byEqualsAfter`, in `org.higherkindedj.hkt.assertions`, builds the equality from a function it applies to both sides before comparing them with `equals`. Here `VALIDATED::narrow` unwraps the `Kind`. Two `IO`s that give the same result are different objects, so for `IO` the function must also run it, as [Functor's laws test](functor.md#two-laws) does. The test checks `Validated`'s instance twice, once with every value valid and once with every value invalid. In the invalid case, Composition must join the three errors in the same order on both sides. `ORDER` is any `Order`, and the test uses these imports, with JUnit's:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.validated.ValidatedKindHelper.VALIDATED;

import java.util.List;
import java.util.function.Function;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.Customer;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.EmailAddress;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.Order;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Applicative;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Semigroups;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.assertions.KindEquivalence;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Instances;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.laws.ApplicativeLaws;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.validated.Validated;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.validated.ValidatedKind;
```

```java
  void assertApplicativeLaws(
      Validated<List<String>, Order> order,
      Validated<List<String>, Function<Order, Customer>> customer,
      Validated<List<String>, Function<Customer, EmailAddress>> email) {
    Applicative<ValidatedKind.Witness<List<String>>> applicative =
        Instances.validated(Semigroups.list());
    var same =
        KindEquivalence.<ValidatedKind.Witness<List<String>>>byEqualsAfter(VALIDATED::narrow);
    ApplicativeLaws.assertIdentity(applicative, VALIDATED.widen(order), same);
    ApplicativeLaws.assertHomomorphism(applicative, ORDER, Order::customer, same);
    ApplicativeLaws.assertInterchange(applicative, VALIDATED.widen(customer), ORDER, same);
    ApplicativeLaws.assertComposition(
        applicative,
        VALIDATED.widen(email),
        VALIDATED.widen(customer),
        VALIDATED.widen(order),
        same);
  }

  @Test
  @DisplayName("Validated's Applicative keeps all four laws when every value is Valid")
  void validatedApplicativeLawsWhenValid() {
    assertApplicativeLaws(
        Validated.valid(ORDER), Validated.valid(Order::customer), Validated.valid(Customer::email));
  }

  @Test
  @DisplayName("Validated's Applicative keeps all four laws when every value is Invalid")
  void validatedApplicativeLawsWhenInvalid() {
    assertApplicativeLaws(
        Validated.invalid(List.of("order not found")),
        Validated.invalid(List.of("no customer")),
        Validated.invalid(List.of("no email")));
  }

```
~~~

## Things people get wrong {#things-people-get-wrong}

~~~admonish warning title="Common Misunderstandings"
- **"`Applicative` accumulates errors."** `Validated`'s instance does. `Either`'s keeps the first error, and both are `Applicative`s.
- **"`Applicative` runs its inputs at the same time."** No instance on this page starts its inputs together: `IO` and `VTask` run them in order, and `CompletableFuture`'s waits for two futures it never started. For `VTask`s that run at once, use `Par.map2`.
- **"`Applicative` is `Monad` without `flatMap`."** Every `Monad` has an `ap`, but an instance's `ap` can do what its `flatMap` cannot, as `Validated`'s does.
- **"`map2` is only for two values."** `map3`, `map4` and `map5` take more, and stop at five. For a record with more fields, `Validated.fields()` in [Accumulating Assembly](../monads/validated_assembly.md) takes more.
~~~

~~~admonish tip title="You can ship now"
You can now build one value from several independent ones, in a method written once for any container, as `lineItem` is. You can say what each instance does with a failure, and check an instance against the four laws. The rest of this page is for which types have an `Applicative`, where `Validated` bends a rule, and what builds on `Applicative`.
~~~

~~~admonish question title="Checkpoint: `map2` with an empty list" id="check-applicative-empty-list"
With `List`'s instance, what does `map2` give for the SKUs `["LAMP", "DESK"]` and an empty list of quantities?
~~~

~~~admonish success title="Answer and why" collapsible=true id="check-applicative-empty-list-answer"
**An empty list.** `List`'s `map2` gives every combination of the two inputs, and two SKUs with no quantities make no combinations.

Where this lives: [What each instance does](#what-each-instance-does).
~~~

~~~admonish question title="Checkpoint: two slow tasks" id="check-applicative-vtask"
Two `VTask`s each take a second to run. About how long does running `Instances.applicative(vtask()).map2(first, second, f)` take, and what would take one second?
~~~

~~~admonish success title="Answer and why" collapsible=true id="check-applicative-vtask-answer"
**About two seconds.** `VTask`'s `map2` runs the first task, then the second. `Par.map2` runs the two at the same time, so it takes about one.

Where this lives: [What each instance does](#what-each-instance-does).
~~~

---

## The fine print {#the-fine-print}

### Which types have an `Applicative` {#which-types-have-an-applicative}

| Types | Their `Applicative` comes from |
|---|---|
| Every token in `Witnesses` | Their `Monad`, so [`Instances.applicative(token)` always succeeds](instances_facade.md#what-the-compiler-checks) |
| `Validated`, `Writer`, `EitherOrBoth` and the transformers | Their `Monad`, from `Instances` with its argument, such as `Instances.validated(semigroup)` |
| `Free` | Its `Monad`, built with `new FreeMonad<>()` |
| `Const`, `FreeAp` and `Fetch` | An `Applicative`, with no `Monad` |
| `EitherF` and `Coyoneda` | Nowhere: they have a `Functor` only |

### Where `Validated` bends a rule {#where-validated-bends-a-rule}

A `Monad`'s `ap` should agree with its `flatMap`: `ap(ff, fa)` should equal `flatMap(f -> map(f, fa), ff)`. `Validated`'s instance breaks this on purpose, as its javadoc says, so that `map2` to `map5` can combine the errors of every input. `hkj-test`'s `MonadLaws` checks the three monad laws, not this one. [Validated](../monads/validated_monad.md) says when you want each behaviour. [Laws and Where HKJ Bends Them](../hkts/laws.md) lists the other instances that bend a law.

### What builds on `Applicative` {#what-builds-on-applicative}

- **`Monad` extends `Applicative`.** Every monad on the next pages has `of`, `ap` and `map2`.
- **`Selective` and `Alternative` extend it too.** [Selective](selective.md) adds steps that run only when a condition holds, and [Alternative](alternative.md) adds a fallback.
- **`Traverse` takes an `Applicative`.** `traverse` runs a function on every element and combines the results with the instance it is given, so `Validated`'s combines every element's errors, as [Foldable and Traverse](foldable_and_traverse.md) shows.

---

~~~admonish tip title="See Also"
- [Functor](functor.md): the `map` that `Applicative` extends
- [Monad](monad.md): sequencing dependent computations with `flatMap`
- [Applicative, Selective or Monad?](abstraction_levels.md): choosing by whether a step needs an earlier result
- [Validated](../monads/validated_monad.md): the type built to report every error
~~~

~~~admonish tip title="Further Reading"
- **Conor McBride and Ross Paterson**, [Applicative Programming with Effects](https://www.staff.city.ac.uk/~ross/papers/Applicative.html): the paper that introduced applicative functors
- **Mark Seemann**, [Applicative Functors](https://blog.ploeh.dk/2018/10/01/applicative-functors/): a practical introduction, with examples in languages close to Java
~~~

~~~admonish info title="Hands-On Learning"
Practise Applicative combining in [Tutorial 03: Applicative Combining](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/tutorial/coretypes/Tutorial03_ApplicativeCombining.java) (8 exercises).
~~~

<!-- description: Chain steps that need the last result with Monad's flatMap: write one chain for any container, see what each instance does, and check the laws with hkj-test. -->

# Monad: Sequencing Dependent Computations

_Chain steps that each need the last result, and let each container's instance decide what a failure does._

<!-- source: Voltaire, Candide, ou l'Optimisme (1759), Chapter XXX. English text from the Boni and Liveright edition (New York, 1918), introduction by Philip Littell, checked against Project Gutenberg #19942; the French original ("Tous les événements sont enchaînés dans le meilleur des mondes possibles") checked against #4650 -->
> _"There is a concatenation of events in this best of all possible worlds: for if you had not been kicked out of a magnificent castle for love of Miss Cunegonde: if you had not been put into the Inquisition: … you would not be here eating preserved citrons and pistachio-nuts."_
>
> — Voltaire, *Candide* (Chapter XXX)

Voltaire's Pangloss tells a chain of `flatMap` calls along its happy path: each event needs the one before it. What a chain does when a link fails is left to the instance.

~~~admonish info title="What You'll Learn"
- Recognise `Optional.flatMap`, `thenCompose`, `Stream.flatMap` and a Path's `via` as one shape: `Monad`'s `flatMap`
- Write a chain once for any container, through its `Monad`
- Predict what each instance's `flatMap` does when a step has no value to work on
- Use `flatMapIfOrElse`, `as`, `asUnit`, `peek` and `flatMap2` to `flatMap5` in place of hand-written lambdas
- Check an instance against the three monad laws with `hkj-test`'s `MonadLaws`
~~~

~~~admonish example title="See Example Code"
**The code on this page is [MonadBook.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/monad/MonadBook.java)**: the page includes it, so the build compiles and runs it. [MonadBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/monad/MonadBookTest.java) holds what the page says each instance does, and [FoundationsLawsBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/FoundationsLawsBookTest.java) checks the laws.
~~~

---

## When the next step needs the last result {#when-the-next-step-needs-the-last-result}

You have written this code:

<!-- verify -->
```java
optional.flatMap(order -> findPayment(order));             // an Optional<Payment>
future.thenCompose(order -> fetchPayment(order));          // a CompletableFuture<Payment>
orders.stream().flatMap(order -> order.lines().stream());  // a Stream<LineItem>
maybePath.via(order -> paymentOf(order));                  // a MaybePath<Payment>
```

Each line runs a step that needs the order, and the step gives back a container of the same kind, which the call flattens into one. Java spells that shape two ways, and a Path a third. `Monad` is its name: a container's `flatMap`, keeping the three laws on this page.

For one container, an [Effect Path](../effect/ch_intro.md) chains with `via`, as `maybePath.via` does, and application code stays on the Paths. This page is for a method written once for any container.

~~~admonish tip title="Plain Java wins here"
A chain over one `Optional`, one `CompletableFuture` or one `Stream` needs nothing from this page: `flatMap` and `thenCompose` already chain them. `Monad` earns its place only when one method must serve any container.
~~~

The code on this page uses these imports, with `Order` from the order records the [Optics](../optics/ch_intro.md) chapter introduces:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.either.EitherKindHelper.EITHER;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.either;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.io;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.io.IOKindHelper.IO_OP;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.validated.ValidatedKindHelper.VALIDATED;

import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.function.Function;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.Order;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.OrderLenses;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.OrderStatus;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Kind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Monad;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Semigroups;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.TypeArity;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.WitnessArity;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.either.Either;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.either.EitherKind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Instances;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.io.IO;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.io.IOKind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.validated.Validated;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.validated.ValidatedKind;
```

## What a `Monad` adds {#what-a-monad-adds}

`Monad<M>` extends [`Applicative<M>`](applicative.md) with one method, and builds `flatMap2` to `flatMap5` from it. Its source names the container `M`, where this page's own code says `F`:

<!-- verify -->
```java
public interface Monad<M extends WitnessArity<TypeArity.Unary>> extends Applicative<M> {

  <A, B> Kind<M, B> flatMap(Function<? super A, ? extends Kind<M, B>> f, Kind<M, A> ma);

  // abridged: the null checks and the other default methods are left out,
  // and flatMap3, flatMap4 and flatMap5 each build on the one before
  default <A, B, R> Kind<M, R> flatMap2(
      Kind<M, A> ma, Kind<M, B> mb, BiFunction<? super A, ? super B, ? extends Kind<M, R>> f) {
    return flatMap(a -> flatMap(b -> f.apply(a, b), mb), ma);
  }
}
```

`map` takes an `A -> B`. `flatMap` takes an `A -> Kind<M, B>`: a step that gives back a container of its own. With `map`, that step would leave a container inside a container, a `Kind<M, Kind<M, B>>`. `flatMap` flattens the step's result into one `Kind<M, B>`.

When no step needs another's result, `map2` to `map5` are enough, and the less powerful tool is the one to pick. [Should you use map3 or flatMap?](applicative.md#map3-or-flatmap) says what they can do that `flatMap` cannot, and [Applicative, Selective or Monad?](abstraction_levels.md) walks through the choice.

## One chain for any container {#one-chain-for-any-container}

`markPaid` is written over `Monad`. It takes the `Monad` for `F` and calls `flatMap`: the step marks the order paid and saves it, so it needs the order `found` holds:

```java
  // One chain for any container F: save needs the order that was found
  public static <F extends WitnessArity<TypeArity.Unary>> Kind<F, Order> markPaid(
      Monad<F> monad, Kind<F, Order> found, Function<Order, Kind<F, Order>> save) {
    return monad.flatMap(
        order -> save.apply(OrderLenses.status().set(OrderStatus.PAID, order)), found);
  }

```

Run it with `Either`'s `Monad`, on an order that was found and on one that was not, then with `IO`'s. `ORDER` is any `Order` that has not been paid for, and `store` adds an order to the list `stored` and hands it back:

```java
    Monad<EitherKind.Witness<String>> eitherMonad = Instances.monad(either());
    Function<Order, Kind<EitherKind.Witness<String>, Order>> save =
        paid -> EITHER.widen(Either.right(paid));

    Either<String, Order> saved =
        EITHER.narrow(markPaid(eitherMonad, EITHER.widen(Either.right(ORDER)), save));
    // Right(Order[id=00000000-0000-0000-0000-000000000001, ..., status=PAID])

    Either<String, Order> notFound =
        EITHER.narrow(markPaid(eitherMonad, EITHER.widen(Either.left("order not found")), save));
    // Left(order not found)

    List<Order> stored = new ArrayList<>();
    Kind<IOKind.Witness, Order> program =
        markPaid(
            Instances.monad(io()),
            IO_OP.widen(IO.delay(() -> ORDER)),
            paid -> IO_OP.widen(IO.delay(() -> store(stored, paid))));
    int savedBeforeRunning = stored.size();
    // 0
    IO_OP.narrow(program).unsafeRunSync();
    int savedAfterRunning = stored.size();
    // 1
```

`Either`'s instance runs `save` on a `Right`, and passes a `Left` on without running it. `IO`'s runs nothing: `markPaid` gives back an `IO` that has not run, and the order is saved only when it runs.

## What each instance does {#what-each-instance-does}

`Monad` promises that `flatMap` hands the step the value when there is one. What it does otherwise, and when the step runs, is each instance's own. `Either`'s keeps a success track and a failure track:

<pre class="hkj-railway-diagram" role="img" aria-label="Either's two tracks through markPaid. On the Right track, the order that was found is marked paid and saved, and the result is the saved order. A Left from the lookup stays on the Left track, and save never runs. A Left from save also ends on the Left track.">
         found    mark paid, save
Right  ════●═════════════●══════▶  Right(saved order)
                          ╲  save gives a Left
Left   ────●───────────────●────▶  Left(error)
         a Left from found:
         save never runs
</pre>

The diagram shows two ways to end on the `Left` track: a `Left` from the lookup, which `save` never sees, and a `Left` from `save` itself. With one step, the instances you will meet most behave like this:

| Instance | What `flatMap` does with its input and the step |
|---|---|
| `Either`, `Validated` | Runs the step on a `Right` or a `Valid`. A `Left` or an `Invalid` passes on unchanged, and the step never runs |
| `Try` | The same with a `Success` and a `Failure`, and an exception the step throws becomes a `Failure` |
| `Maybe`, `Optional` | Runs the step on a value. An empty input stays empty, and the step never runs |
| `List`, `NonEmptyList` | Runs the step on every element and joins the results in order: two inputs that each give two results give four |
| `Stream` | The same, lazily: the step runs on each element as the result is consumed |
| [`IO`](../monads/io_monad.md), `VTask` | Builds a new deferred value and runs nothing. When it runs, it runs the input, then the step. If the input fails, the step never runs |
| `CompletableFuture` | Starts the step when the input completes. If the input fails, the result fails and the step never runs |
| `Writer`, from `Instances.writer(monoid)` | Runs the step, and joins the input's log and the step's, in that order, with the monoid |

So stopping at a failure, known as short-circuiting, comes from the instance, not from `Monad`: `Either`'s stops at a `Left`, and `List`'s runs the step on every element.

## Helpers built on `flatMap` and `map` {#helpers}

`Monad` adds default methods for chains you would otherwise write as lambdas:

| Method | What it does | Built as |
|---|---|---|
| `flatMapIfOrElse(predicate, ifTrue, ifFalse, ma)` | Chooses the next step by testing the value | `flatMap(a -> predicate.test(a) ? ifTrue.apply(a) : ifFalse.apply(a), ma)` |
| `as(b, ma)` | Keeps the outcome, and replaces the value with `b`, which must not be `null` | `map(_ -> b, ma)` |
| `asUnit(ma)` | Keeps the outcome, and replaces the value with `Unit` | `map(_ -> Unit.INSTANCE, ma)` |
| `peek(action, ma)` | Runs `action` on the value, and keeps the value | `map(a -> { action.accept(a); return a; }, ma)` |

`as`, `asUnit` and `peek` are built from `map`, so they run where `map` runs: `peek`'s action runs on a `Right` and on each element of a list, and never on a `Left`. On the order that was found, `flatMapIfOrElse` keeps an order that has not shipped, and `as` replaces it with a message:

```java
    Kind<EitherKind.Witness<String>, Order> found = EITHER.widen(Either.right(ORDER));

    Either<String, Order> unshipped =
        EITHER.narrow(
            eitherMonad.flatMapIfOrElse(
                order -> order.status() == OrderStatus.SHIPPED,
                order -> EITHER.widen(Either.left("order has shipped")),
                order -> EITHER.widen(Either.right(order)),
                found));
    // Right(Order[id=00000000-0000-0000-0000-000000000001, ..., status=NEW])

    Either<String, String> receipt = EITHER.narrow(eitherMonad.as("receipt sent", found));
    // Right(receipt sent)
```

### `flatMap2` to `flatMap5` {#flatmap2-to-flatmap5}

`flatMap2` to `flatMap5` combine two to five values with a step that gives back a container, for a combination that can itself fail. `map2` to `map5` take a plain function instead. Because they are built from `flatMap`, they take their inputs in order, so on `Validated` they stop at the first error, where its `map2` reports both:

```java
    Monad<ValidatedKind.Witness<List<String>>> validatedMonad =
        Instances.validated(Semigroups.list());
    Kind<ValidatedKind.Witness<List<String>>, String> blankSku =
        VALIDATED.widen(Validated.invalid(List.of("SKU is blank")));
    Kind<ValidatedKind.Witness<List<String>>, Integer> noQuantity =
        VALIDATED.widen(Validated.invalid(List.of("quantity must be positive")));

    Validated<List<String>, String> mapped =
        VALIDATED.narrow(
            validatedMonad.map2(blankSku, noQuantity, (sku, quantity) -> quantity + " x " + sku));
    // Invalid([SKU is blank, quantity must be positive])

    Validated<List<String>, String> flatMapped =
        VALIDATED.narrow(
            validatedMonad.flatMap2(
                blankSku,
                noQuantity,
                (sku, quantity) -> VALIDATED.widen(Validated.valid(quantity + " x " + sku))));
    // Invalid([SKU is blank])
```

The step always succeeds, so it is not why `flatMap2` stops at one error: `flatMap2` is a chain of `flatMap` calls, and `Validated`'s `flatMap` stops at the first `Invalid`. [Where Validated bends a rule](applicative.md#where-validated-bends-a-rule) says why its `map2` differs.

## Three laws {#three-laws}

A `Monad` keeps three laws. Together they say that `of` adds nothing of its own before or after a step, and that a chain gives the same result however you group its steps.

~~~admonish note title="Laws"
- **Left identity: running a step on a lifted value is the step.** `flatMap(f, of(a))` equals `f.apply(a)`.
- **Right identity: running `of` as the step changes nothing.** `flatMap(a -> of(a), ma)` equals `ma`.
- **Associativity: grouping the steps differently changes nothing.** `flatMap(g, flatMap(f, ma))` equals `flatMap(a -> flatMap(g, f.apply(a)), ma)`.

Two containers are equal when they hold equal values, or equal errors, in the same shape. For a deferred container such as `IO`, they are equal when running both gives equal results. The laws assume values that are never `null` and functions that never return one, and [Values, never null](../hkts/core-concepts.md#values-never-null) says what each type does with one.

`MonadLaws`, beside `ApplicativeLaws` in [`hkj-test`](../tooling/test_assertions.md#adding-the-dependency)'s `org.higherkindedj.hkt.laws` package, checks the three laws on any instance. It takes an equality from `KindEquivalence.byEqualsAfter`, as on the [Applicative](applicative.md#four-laws) page, which here unwraps both sides with `EITHER::narrow` and compares them with `equals`. The test checks `Either`'s instance on a `Right` and on a `Left`. `verifyEmail` always gives a `Left`, so the laws are checked when a step fails too. `ORDER` is any `Order`, and the test uses these imports, with JUnit's:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.either.EitherKindHelper.EITHER;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.either;

import java.util.function.Function;
import java.util.stream.Stream;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.Customer;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.EmailAddress;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.Order;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Kind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Monad;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.assertions.KindEquivalence;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.either.Either;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.either.EitherKind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Instances;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.laws.MonadLaws;
```

```java
  static Stream<Kind<EitherKind.Witness<String>, Order>> eitherOrders() {
    return Stream.of(
        EITHER.widen(Either.right(ORDER)), EITHER.widen(Either.left("order not found")));
  }

  @ParameterizedTest
  @MethodSource("eitherOrders")
  @DisplayName("Either's Monad keeps all three laws, on a Right and on a Left")
  void eitherMonadLaws(Kind<EitherKind.Witness<String>, Order> fa) {
    Monad<EitherKind.Witness<String>> monad = Instances.monad(either());
    var same = KindEquivalence.<EitherKind.Witness<String>>byEqualsAfter(EITHER::narrow);
    Function<Order, Kind<EitherKind.Witness<String>, Customer>> findCustomer =
        order -> EITHER.widen(Either.right(order.customer()));
    Function<Customer, Kind<EitherKind.Witness<String>, EmailAddress>> verifyEmail =
        customer -> EITHER.widen(Either.left("email not verified"));
    MonadLaws.assertLeftIdentity(monad, ORDER, findCustomer, same);
    MonadLaws.assertLeftIdentity(monad, ORDER.customer(), verifyEmail, same);
    MonadLaws.assertRightIdentity(monad, fa, same);
    MonadLaws.assertAssociativity(monad, fa, findCustomer, verifyEmail, same);
  }

```
~~~

## Back to the one-liner {#back-to-the-one-liner}

```java
  static EitherPath<OrderError, Order> changeEmail(
      OrderRepository orders, UUID id, String newEmail) {
    return orders
        .find(id)
        .toEitherPath(OrderError.notFound(id))
        .map(order -> OrderFocus.customer().email().value().set(newEmail, order))
        .via(orders::save);
  }

```

`.via(orders::save)` is the line's Monad layer: `save` needs the order the line holds so far, and on a `Left` it never runs. `via` is `EitherPath`'s own method, with no `Monad` instance involved. [Two encodings, one algebra](../hkts/one_line_six_layers.md#two-encodings-one-algebra) matches it to `Monad`'s `flatMap` over `Kind`.

## Things people get wrong {#things-people-get-wrong}

~~~admonish warning title="Common Misunderstandings"
- **"`flatMap` runs the step at once."** `Either`'s does. `IO`'s and `VTask`'s build a deferred value, and nothing runs until you run it. `Stream`'s runs the step only as the stream is consumed. The order of the steps is fixed, and the timing is the container's.
- **"Monads always short-circuit."** Instances whose input holds at most one value stop when it is missing or failed: `Either`, `Validated`, `Maybe`, `IO` and `CompletableFuture` among them. `List`'s `flatMap` runs the step on every element. The behaviour comes from the instance, not from the word "Monad".
- **"`flatMap` is `Stream.flatMap`."** `Stream.flatMap` is one instance of the shape: it flattens a stream of streams. `Monad`'s `flatMap` names the shape for any container.
- **"Nested `flatMap` calls are the cleanest you can do."** They are the most explicit, as `flatMap2`'s body shows. For more than two steps, a [For comprehension](for_comprehension.md) reads better and avoids the indentation.
- **"You can call `.flatMap` on a `Kind`."** `Kind` has no methods. Go through the `Monad<F>` instance, which knows how to flatten inside `F`.
~~~

~~~admonish tip title="You can ship now"
You can now write a chain once for any container, as `markPaid` is, and say what each instance does when a step has no value to work on. You can use the helpers in place of hand-written lambdas, and check an instance against left identity, right identity and associativity. The rest of this page is for which types have a `Monad`, and what builds on it.
~~~

~~~admonish question title="Checkpoint: `markPaid` over a list" id="check-monad-list"
`markPaid` runs with `List`'s instance on two found orders. `save` gives back an empty list for the first, and one order for the second. How many orders come back?
~~~

~~~admonish success title="Answer and why" collapsible=true id="check-monad-list-answer"
**One.** `List`'s `flatMap` runs the step on every element and joins the results, so the empty list adds nothing.

Where this lives: [What each instance does](#what-each-instance-does).
~~~

~~~admonish question title="Checkpoint: `flatMapIfOrElse` on a `Left`" id="check-monad-helpers"
On a `Left`, which branch of `flatMapIfOrElse` runs: `ifTrue` or `ifFalse`?
~~~

~~~admonish success title="Answer and why" collapsible=true id="check-monad-helpers-answer"
**Neither.** `flatMapIfOrElse` is built from `flatMap`, which passes a `Left` on without running its step, so the test and both branches never run. `ifFalse` handles a value that fails the test, not a failure.

Where this lives: [Helpers built on `flatMap` and `map`](#helpers).
~~~

---

## The fine print {#the-fine-print}

### Which types have a `Monad` {#which-types-have-a-monad}

| Types | Their `Monad` comes from |
|---|---|
| Every token in `Witnesses` | [`Instances.monad(token)`, which always succeeds](instances_facade.md#what-the-compiler-checks) |
| `Validated`, `Writer`, `EitherOrBoth` and the transformers | `Instances` with its argument, such as `Instances.writer(monoid)` |
| `Free` | `new FreeMonad<>()` |
| `Const`, `FreeAp` and `Fetch` | Nowhere: they have an `Applicative` and no `Monad` |
| `EitherF` and `Coyoneda` | Nowhere: they have a `Functor` only |

### What builds on `Monad` {#what-builds-on-monad}

- **`MonadError` extends `Monad`.** [MonadError](monad_error.md), the next page, adds raising an error and recovering from one.
- **`MonadZero` extends `Monad` and `Alternative`.** [MonadZero](monad_zero.md) adds an empty value, so a chain can filter.
- **`For` takes a `Monad`.** `For.from(monad, source)` writes a chain of `flatMap` calls as a list of steps, as [For comprehensions](for_comprehension.md) shows.
- **`GenericPath` takes a `Monad`.** `GenericPath.of(kind, monad)` turns any `Kind` that has a `Monad` into a Path, with `via`, as [GenericPath](../effect/path_generic.md) shows.

---

- [MonadError](monad_error.md): raising an error and recovering from one
- [For Comprehension](for_comprehension.md): a chain of `flatMap` calls written as steps
- [Applicative, Selective or Monad?](abstraction_levels.md): choosing by whether a step needs an earlier result
- [One Line, Six Layers](../hkts/one_line_six_layers.md): where `via` sits in the chapter's one-liner
~~~

- **Bartosz Milewski**, [Monads: Programmer's Definition](https://bartoszmilewski.com/2016/11/21/monads-programmers-definition/): monads as the composition of functions that return a container
- **Mark Seemann**, [Monads](https://blog.ploeh.dk/2022/03/28/monads/): "a functor you can flatten", the start of a series with examples in C#, F# and Haskell

<!-- description: Raise an error as a value with MonadError, recover from it in one method for any container, and see each instance's error type and laws. -->

# MonadError: Handling Errors Gracefully

_Raise an error as a value, and recover from it, in any container whose instance has an error type._

<!-- source: F. Scott Fitzgerald, "The Crack-Up", Esquire, February 1936; text checked against Esquire's online reprint of the essay -->
> _"the test of a first-rate intelligence is the ability to hold two opposed ideas in the mind at the same time, and still retain the ability to function."_
>
> — F. Scott Fitzgerald, *The Crack-Up*

A chain that can fail holds two paths in mind at once: the one where every step succeeds, and the one where a step goes wrong. `MonadError` writes both in the same code.

~~~admonish info title="What You'll Learn"
- Say why a typed error travels where an exception cannot
- Raise an error as a value with `raiseError`, in one method for any container
- Name each instance's error type, and what `raiseError` builds in it
- Choose among `handleErrorWith`, `handleError`, `recover` and `recoverWith`, and say which builds its fallback first
- State the three laws that tie raising to handling
~~~

~~~admonish example title="See Example Code"
**The code on this page is [MonadErrorBook.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/monaderror/MonadErrorBook.java)**: the page includes it, so the build compiles and runs it. [MonadErrorBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/monaderror/MonadErrorBookTest.java) holds what the page says each instance does, and [FoundationsLawsBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/FoundationsLawsBookTest.java) checks the laws.
~~~

---

## When an exception is not enough {#when-an-exception-is-not-enough}

An exception does one job well: a method throws, and a caller that can act catches. A few flat `try` blocks with early returns handle most code. The trouble starts when a failure has to travel through code that was not written for it. A checked exception cannot leave the lambda passed to `map`:

<!-- verify:rejects "unreported exception" -->
```java
List<Order> orders = ids.stream().map(id -> findOrThrow(id)).toList();
```

javac refuses it with "unreported exception OrderNotFoundException". An unchecked exception gets through, but then nothing in the signature says what can fail. A typed error is a value instead: it sits in the return type, and passes through `map` and `flatMap` as any value does. With `find` returning an `Either<String, Order>`, the same lambda compiles, and each missing order is a `Left` in the list:

<!-- verify -->
```java
List<Either<String, Order>> orders = ids.stream().map(id -> find(id)).toList();
```

You already raise and recover with values on one container:

<!-- verify -->
```java
CompletableFuture.failedFuture(new OrderNotFoundException(id));  // an error as a value
future.exceptionallyCompose(error -> fetchFromArchive(id));      // recover with another future
future.exceptionally(error -> placeholder);                       // recover with a plain value
```

`MonadError` names those three for any container: `raiseError`, `handleErrorWith` and `handleError`.

For one container, an [Effect Path](../effect/ch_intro.md) carries its error in its type, and recovers with its own methods, such as `EitherPath`'s `recover` and `recoverWith`, which take a function of the error. On `MonadError` the same two names take a fixed value, as [The same names on a Path](#the-same-names-on-a-path) shows. Application code stays on the Paths. This page is for a method written once for any container with an error type.

The code on this page uses these imports, with `Order` from the order records the [Optics](../optics/ch_intro.md) chapter introduces:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.either.EitherKindHelper.EITHER;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.either;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.maybe;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.try_;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.maybe.MaybeKindHelper.MAYBE;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.trymonad.TryKindHelper.TRY;

import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.Order;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.OrderStatus;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Kind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.MonadError;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.TypeArity;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Unit;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.WitnessArity;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.either.Either;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.either.EitherKind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Instances;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.maybe.Maybe;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.maybe.MaybeKind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.trymonad.Try;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.trymonad.TryKind;
```

## What a `MonadError` adds {#the-solution-raiseerror-and-handleerrorwith}

`MonadError<F, E>` extends [`Monad<F>`](monad.md) with two methods, where `E` is the instance's error type:

<!-- verify -->
```java
public interface MonadError<F extends WitnessArity<TypeArity.Unary>, E> extends Monad<F> {

  <A> Kind<F, A> raiseError(E error);

  <A> Kind<F, A> handleErrorWith(Kind<F, A> ma, Function<? super E, ? extends Kind<F, A>> handler);

  // abridged: handleError, recover and recoverWith are default methods
  // built on handleErrorWith
}
```

`raiseError` builds a failed value. Nothing is thrown, and every instance refuses a `null` error. `handleErrorWith` runs `handler` on a failure, and passes a success on unchanged. The handler gives back a container of its own, so a recovery can fail too.

## One method for any container {#one-method-for-any-container}

`refuseShipped` is written over `MonadError`, as `totalUnlessShipped` on [Working with Kind](../hkts/usage-guide.md) is, without the total. It takes the `MonadError` for `F` and the error to raise, so it never needs to know what an error is in `F`:

```java
  // One method for any container F whose errors are E: refuse an order that has shipped
  public static <F extends WitnessArity<TypeArity.Unary>, E> Kind<F, Order> refuseShipped(
      MonadError<F, E> monadError, Kind<F, Order> found, E shipped) {
    return monadError.flatMap(
        order ->
            order.status() == OrderStatus.SHIPPED
                ? monadError.raiseError(shipped)
                : monadError.of(order),
        found);
  }

```

```mermaid
flowchart TD
    accTitle: Where refuseShipped raises an error
    accDescr: The found value is tested. If it is already an error, flatMap passes that error on. If it holds an order that has not shipped, the order goes on as a value. A shipped order becomes the error that raiseError builds.
    F["found"] --> A{"already an error?"}
    A -->|"yes"| P["that error, passed on"]
    A -->|"no"| S{"shipped?"}
    S -->|"no"| V["of(order): a value"]
    S -->|"yes"| R["raiseError(shipped): a new error"]
    classDef step fill:#8caaee,stroke:#1e66f5,color:#232634
    classDef decision fill:#e5c890,stroke:#df8e1d,color:#232634
    classDef ok fill:#a6d189,stroke:#40a02b,color:#232634
    classDef error fill:#e78284,stroke:#d20f39,color:#232634
    class F step
    class A,S decision
    class V ok
    class P,R error
```

The diagram shows that only a shipped order becomes a new error. A caller's `handleErrorWith` can replace either error. Run it on an order that has shipped, with `Either`'s instance, then `Try`'s, then `Maybe`'s. `SHIPPED` is any `Order` whose status is `SHIPPED`:

```java
    MonadError<EitherKind.Witness<String>, String> eitherMonadError =
        Instances.monadError(either());
    Either<String, Order> refused =
        EITHER.narrow(
            refuseShipped(
                eitherMonadError, EITHER.widen(Either.right(SHIPPED)), "order has shipped"));
    // Left(order has shipped)

    MonadError<TryKind.Witness, Throwable> tryMonadError = Instances.monadError(try_());
    Try<Order> failed =
        TRY.narrow(
            refuseShipped(
                tryMonadError,
                TRY.widen(Try.success(SHIPPED)),
                new IllegalStateException("order has shipped")));
    // Failure(java.lang.IllegalStateException: order has shipped)

    MonadError<MaybeKind.Witness, Unit> maybeMonadError = Instances.monadError(maybe());
    Maybe<Order> nothing =
        MAYBE.narrow(
            refuseShipped(maybeMonadError, MAYBE.widen(Maybe.just(SHIPPED)), Unit.INSTANCE));
    // Nothing
```

The call is the same each time, and the error is the instance's: a `String` for this `Either`, a `Throwable` for `Try`, and [`Unit`](../hkts/core-concepts.md#unit) for `Maybe`. `Unit` is a type with one value, because `Nothing` carries no detail.

## What each instance does {#what-each-instance-does}

`MonadError` promises that `handleErrorWith` runs its handler on what `raiseError` built, and leaves a success alone. What an error is, and when the handler runs, is each instance's own:

| Instance | Its error type `E` | `raiseError(e)` gives | `handleErrorWith` runs the handler |
|---|---|---|---|
| `Either<L, A>` | `L` | `Left(e)` | On a `Left` |
| [`Validated<E, A>`](../monads/validated_monad.md) | `E` | `Invalid(e)` | On an `Invalid` |
| [`Try`](../monads/try_monad.md) | `Throwable` | `Failure(e)` | On a `Failure`. An exception the handler throws becomes a `Failure` too |
| `Maybe`, `Optional` | `Unit` | `Nothing`, or an empty `Optional` | On `Nothing` or an empty `Optional`, with `Unit.INSTANCE` |
| `CompletableFuture` | `Throwable` | A future that has failed with `e` | When the future fails, with the cause unwrapped from any `CompletionException` |
| `VTask` | `Throwable` | A task that fails with `e` when it runs | When the task fails, as it runs |

`IO`, `List`, `Writer`, `State` and `Reader` have no `MonadError`. An `IO` that fails throws its exception when it runs, and an `IOPath` recovers with its own `handleError`.

## Recovering {#recovering}

Four methods recover from an error, and `handleErrorWith` is the one the others are built on:

| Method | What it does | Built as |
|---|---|---|
| `handleErrorWith(ma, handler)` | Runs `handler` on the error, for a recovery that can itself fail | The instance's own |
| `handleError(ma, handler)` | Runs `handler` on the error, for a plain value | `handleErrorWith(ma, error -> of(handler.apply(error)))` |
| `recoverWith(ma, fallback)` | Swaps a failure for `fallback`, a container built before the call | `handleErrorWith(ma, error -> fallback)` |
| `recover(ma, value)` | Swaps a failure for `value` | `handleError(ma, error -> value)` |

`Store` holds one order, gives a `Left` with its own message for any other id, and counts its reads. `live` holds `ORDER`, `archive` holds `ARCHIVED`, and `MISSING` is an id neither holds. `handleErrorWith` reads the archive only when the live store fails. `handleError`'s handler gives a value of the source's own type, so `map` first turns the order into the report's `String`:

```java
    Store live = new Store(ORDER, "not in the live store");
    Store archive = new Store(ARCHIVED, "not in the archive");

    Either<String, Order> fromArchive =
        EITHER.narrow(
            eitherMonadError.handleErrorWith(
                live.find(ARCHIVED.id()), _ -> archive.find(ARCHIVED.id())));
    // Right(Order[id=00000000-0000-0000-0000-000000000003, ...])

    Either<String, Order> nowhere =
        EITHER.narrow(
            eitherMonadError.handleErrorWith(live.find(MISSING), _ -> archive.find(MISSING)));
    // Left(not in the archive)

    Either<String, String> report =
        EITHER.narrow(
            eitherMonadError.handleError(
                eitherMonadError.map(order -> "found", live.find(MISSING)),
                error -> "no order: " + error));
    // Right(no order: not in the live store)
```


### Should you use `recoverWith` or `handleErrorWith`? {#constant-fallbacks-recover-and-recoverwith}

A ticket like this one: the order service reads each order from its live store, and falls back to the archive when the order is not there. The line is `monadError.recoverWith(live.find(id), archive.find(id))`. Every request now reads the archive too, because Java evaluates `archive.find(id)` before `recoverWith` is called, whether the live store finds the order or not. The archive starts throttling the service, and its read count climbs to match the live store's. The tests stub the archive and check only the result, which is correct; none of them counts the archive's reads.

The fix gives the archive read to a handler, which runs only on a failure. For an order the live store holds, `recoverWith` reads the archive once, and the handler never does:

```java
    Store readFirst = new Store(ARCHIVED, "not in the archive");
    Kind<EitherKind.Witness<String>, Order> withFallback =
        eitherMonadError.recoverWith(live.find(ORDER.id()), readFirst.find(ORDER.id()));
    int archiveReadsWithRecoverWith = readFirst.reads();
    // 1

    Store readOnFailure = new Store(ARCHIVED, "not in the archive");
    Kind<EitherKind.Witness<String>, Order> withHandler =
        eitherMonadError.handleErrorWith(
            live.find(ORDER.id()), _ -> readOnFailure.find(ORDER.id()));
    int archiveReadsWithAHandler = readOnFailure.reads();
    // 0
```

~~~admonish warning title="recoverWith builds its fallback first"
`recoverWith` and `recover` take a value, so Java builds it before the call, whether the source fails or not. Pass them a fallback that is already built, or cheap to build. When building it costs a read, a call or any other effect, use `handleErrorWith`, whose handler runs only on a failure.
~~~

The two shortcuts treat `null` differently, as [What the shortcuts do with null](#what-the-shortcuts-do-with-null) shows.

## Three laws {#three-laws}

A `MonadError` keeps the three [monad laws](monad.md#three-laws), and three more that tie raising to handling. Together they say that a handler sees exactly the error raised and never a success, and that no step runs on an error.

~~~admonish note title="Laws"
- **Handling a raised error is the handler.** `handleErrorWith(raiseError(e), handler)` equals `handler.apply(e)`.
- **A success is never handled.** `handleErrorWith(of(a), handler)` equals `of(a)`.
- **A raised error skips every step.** `flatMap(f, raiseError(e))` equals `raiseError(e)`.

Two containers are equal when they hold equal values, or equal errors, in the same shape. For a deferred container such as `VTask`, they are equal when running both gives equal results or the same error. The laws assume values and errors that are never `null`, and functions that never return one, and [Values, never null](../hkts/core-concepts.md#values-never-null) says what each type does with one.

Checking these three laws with an [`hkj-test`](../tooling/test_assertions.md#adding-the-dependency) helper is not supported yet. The test checks them by hand on `Either`, `Try` and `Maybe`, with an equality from `KindEquivalence.byEqualsAfter` that unwraps both sides and compares them with `equals`. Its first handler gives back the error it receives, so the first law fails if an instance hands the handler a different error. `ORDER` is any `Order`, and the test uses these imports, with JUnit's:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import static org.assertj.core.api.Assertions.assertThat;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.either.EitherKindHelper.EITHER;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.either;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.maybe;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.try_;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.maybe.MaybeKindHelper.MAYBE;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.trymonad.TryKindHelper.TRY;

import java.util.function.BiPredicate;
import java.util.function.Function;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.Customer;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.Order;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Kind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.MonadError;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.TypeArity;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Unit;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.WitnessArity;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.assertions.KindEquivalence;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.either.EitherKind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Instances;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.maybe.MaybeKind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.trymonad.TryKind;
```

```java
  <F extends WitnessArity<TypeArity.Unary>, E> void assertMonadErrorLaws(
      MonadError<F, E> monadError, E error, BiPredicate<Kind<F, ?>, Kind<F, ?>> same) {
    Function<E, Kind<F, E>> giveBackTheError = monadError::of;
    Function<E, Kind<F, String>> handled = raised -> monadError.of("handled");
    Function<Order, Kind<F, Customer>> findCustomer = order -> monadError.of(order.customer());

    // handling a raised error is the handler, given that same error
    assertThat(
            same.test(
                monadError.handleErrorWith(monadError.<E>raiseError(error), giveBackTheError),
                giveBackTheError.apply(error)))
        .isTrue();
    // a success is never handled
    assertThat(
            same.test(
                monadError.handleErrorWith(monadError.of("kept"), handled), monadError.of("kept")))
        .isTrue();
    // a raised error skips every step
    assertThat(
            same.test(
                monadError.flatMap(findCustomer, monadError.<Order>raiseError(error)),
                monadError.raiseError(error)))
        .isTrue();
  }

  @Test
  @DisplayName("Either's, Try's and Maybe's MonadError keep the laws that tie raising to handling")
  void monadErrorLaws() {
    MonadError<EitherKind.Witness<String>, String> eitherErrors = Instances.monadError(either());
    MonadError<TryKind.Witness, Throwable> tryErrors = Instances.monadError(try_());
    MonadError<MaybeKind.Witness, Unit> maybeErrors = Instances.monadError(maybe());

    assertMonadErrorLaws(
        eitherErrors,
        "order not found",
        KindEquivalence.<EitherKind.Witness<String>>byEqualsAfter(EITHER::narrow));
    assertMonadErrorLaws(
        tryErrors,
        new IllegalStateException("order not found"),
        KindEquivalence.<TryKind.Witness>byEqualsAfter(TRY::narrow));
    assertMonadErrorLaws(
        maybeErrors,
        Unit.INSTANCE,
        KindEquivalence.<MaybeKind.Witness>byEqualsAfter(MAYBE::narrow));
  }

```
~~~

## Things people get wrong {#things-people-get-wrong}

~~~admonish warning title="Common Misunderstandings"
- **"`raiseError` throws an exception."** It builds a value that holds the error. Nothing is thrown, and `flatMap` passes the error on without running its step.
- **"`handleErrorWith` and a try/catch are the same."** They look alike, and work differently. A `catch` runs when the call stack unwinds; `handleErrorWith` is a method call on a value, so it composes with `map` and `flatMap`.
- **"You have to use `Either`."** Any instance with a `MonadError` works: `Either`, `Validated`, `Try`, `Maybe`, `Optional`, `CompletableFuture`, `VTask`, and the `EitherT`, `MaybeT` and `OptionalT` transformers. An Effect Path recovers through its own methods.
- **"Recovery is for the end of the chain."** It can sit anywhere. A `handleErrorWith` between two `flatMap` calls retries one step before the chain goes on.
- **"`recoverWith` runs only on a failure."** It takes a value, so Java builds its fallback first: that is how the ticket's archive was read on every request.
~~~

~~~admonish tip title="You can ship now"
You can now raise an error as a value in a method written once for any container, as `refuseShipped` is, and name each instance's error type. You can recover with the right one of four methods, keeping a costly fallback in a handler, and state the three laws that tie raising to handling. The rest of this page is for which types have a `MonadError`, the different meaning of the same names on a Path, and what builds on `MonadError`.
~~~

~~~admonish question title="Checkpoint: raising an error in a `VTask`" id="check-monaderror-vtask"
Does `Instances.monadError(vtask()).raiseError(error)` throw `error`?
~~~

~~~admonish success title="Answer and why" collapsible=true id="check-monaderror-vtask-answer"
**No.** It builds a task that fails with `error` when it runs, and nothing is thrown until then. `raiseError` builds a value in every instance; `VTask`'s value is a task.

Where this lives: [What each instance does](#what-each-instance-does).
~~~

~~~admonish question title="Checkpoint: a slow default" id="check-monaderror-recover"
`found` is a success. In `monadError.recover(found, slowDefault())`, does `slowDefault()` run?
~~~

~~~admonish success title="Answer and why" collapsible=true id="check-monaderror-recover-answer"
**Yes.** `recover` takes a value, so Java calls `slowDefault()` before `recover`, whatever `found` holds. To run it only on a failure, give it to a handler: `handleError(found, _ -> slowDefault())`.

Where this lives: [Should you use `recoverWith` or `handleErrorWith`?](#constant-fallbacks-recover-and-recoverwith)
~~~

---

## The fine print {#the-fine-print}

### Which types have a `MonadError` {#which-types-have-a-monaderror}

| Types | Their `MonadError` comes from |
|---|---|
| `Either`, `Try`, `Maybe`, `Optional`, `CompletableFuture`, `VTask` | `Instances.monadError(token)` |
| `Validated` | `Instances.validated(semigroup)` |
| `EitherT`, `MaybeT`, `OptionalT`, whose errors are `L`, `Unit` and `Unit` | `Instances.eitherT(outer)`, `Instances.maybeT(outer)` and `Instances.optionalT(outer)` |
| `IO`, `List`, `Writer`, `State`, `Reader` and the other transformers | Nowhere: they have none |

`Instances.monadError(token)` casts the token's `Monad`, and the compiler can check neither that cast nor `E`. [What the compiler checks](instances_facade.md#what-the-compiler-checks) says what fails, and when.

### The same names on a Path {#the-same-names-on-a-path}

An `EitherPath` recovers with methods that share two names with `MonadError`, and mean something else:

| What you want | On `MonadError` | On an `EitherPath` |
|---|---|---|
| A value from the error | `handleError(ma, error -> a)` | `path.recover(error -> a)` |
| Another container from the error | `handleErrorWith(ma, error -> mb)` | `path.recoverWith(error -> otherPath)` |
| A fixed value | `recover(ma, a)` | `path.recover(_ -> a)` |
| A fixed fallback, built only on a failure | `handleErrorWith(ma, _ -> mb)` | `path.orElse(() -> otherPath)` |
| A fallback already built | `recoverWith(ma, mb)` | `path.recoverWith(_ -> otherPath)` |

So on a Path, `recover` and `recoverWith` take a function of the error, which runs only on a failure. On `MonadError`, they take a fixed value, which Java builds first.

### What the shortcuts do with `null` {#what-the-shortcuts-do-with-null}

`recoverWith` refuses a `null` fallback, and a `null` source, at the call on every instance in the library, so a `null` fallback fails there and not later inside `handleErrorWith`. Each instance overrides `recoverWith` to check; an instance you write that keeps the default checks nothing. `recover` refuses a `null` source too, and leaves a `null` value to `of`:

| Instances | `recover(ma, null)` |
|---|---|
| `Maybe`, `Optional`, `MaybeT`, `OptionalT` | Gives `Nothing` or an empty `Optional` |
| `CompletableFuture` | Holds the `null` |
| `Either`, `Try`, `VTask`, `EitherT` | Refuses it at the call, whatever the source holds, because a success always holds a value |
| `Validated` | Refuses it only when the source is invalid |

### What builds on `MonadError` {#what-builds-on-monaderror}

- **`GenericPath` takes one.** `GenericPath.of(kind, monadError)` gives a Path over any `Kind` that can recover, with the Path meanings of `recover` and `recoverWith`, as [GenericPath](../effect/path_generic.md) shows.
- **Three transformers have one.** `EitherT`, `MaybeT` and `OptionalT` raise and recover in their own error type over any outer `Monad`, as [Monad Transformers](../transformers/ch_intro.md) shows.

---

~~~admonish tip title="See Also"
- [Monad](monad.md): the type class `MonadError` extends
- [Either](../monads/either_monad.md): the instance most code uses for a typed error
- [Try](../monads/try_monad.md): the instance whose error is a `Throwable`
- [EitherPath](../effect/path_either.md): recovery on a Path, for one container
- [One Line, Six Layers](../hkts/one_line_six_layers.md#two-encodings-one-algebra): `path.recover` beside `handleError`
~~~

~~~

<!-- description: Write a chain of flatMap calls as steps with For: bind, filter with when, mark independent steps with par, and traverse a collection inside the chain. -->

# For-Comprehensions

_Write a chain of `flatMap` calls as a list of steps, with filters, independent steps and traversals._

~~~admonish info title="What You'll Learn"
- Turn nested `flatMap` calls into steps with `For.from`, `from`, `let` and `yield`
- Filter a chain with `when`, given a `MonadZero`
- Mark independent steps with `par`, and say when they run at the same time
- Traverse a collection inside a chain with `traverse`, `sequence` and `flatTraverse`
- Choose between `For`, `ForPath` and `ForState`
~~~

~~~admonish example title="See Example Code"
**The code on this page is [ForBook.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/forcomprehension/ForBook.java)**: the page includes it, so the build compiles and runs it. [ForBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/forcomprehension/ForBookTest.java) holds what the page says each step does.
~~~

---

## When nested `flatMap` calls get deep {#when-nested-flatmap-calls-get-deep}

A receipt needs three things in turn: the order, its customer's verified email, and a confirmation from charging the order's total. Each step needs what an earlier step found, so [Monad](monad.md)'s `flatMap` chains them. With one `Optional` you would nest `flatMap` calls inside one another, and the same happens over `Kind`.

For one container, an [Effect Path](../effect/ch_intro.md) chains the same steps with [`ForPath`](../effect/forpath_comprehension.md), such as `ForPath.from(eitherPath)`. Application code stays on the Paths. This page is for `For`, a chain written once for any container that has a `Monad`. Written as nested calls, the receipt is:

```java
  // One receipt for any container F, as nested flatMap calls
  public static <F extends WitnessArity<TypeArity.Unary>> Kind<F, String> receiptNested(
      Monad<F> monad,
      Kind<F, Order> found,
      Function<Customer, Kind<F, EmailAddress>> verify,
      Function<BigDecimal, Kind<F, String>> charge) {
    return monad.flatMap(
        order -> {
          BigDecimal total = total(order);
          return monad.flatMap(
              email ->
                  monad.map(
                      confirmation ->
                          "receipt " + confirmation + " for " + total + " to " + email.value(),
                      charge.apply(total)),
              verify.apply(order.customer()));
        },
        found);
  }

```

The receipt is in the innermost lambda, and `total` has to be a local variable so that a later step can still see it. A `For` comprehension writes the same chain as a list of steps.

The code on this page uses these imports, with `Order` from the order records the [Optics](../optics/ch_intro.md) chapter introduces:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.either.EitherKindHelper.EITHER;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.either;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.list;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.maybe;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.list.ListKindHelper.LIST;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.maybe.MaybeKindHelper.MAYBE;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.validated.ValidatedKindHelper.VALIDATED;

import java.math.BigDecimal;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.UUID;
import java.util.function.Function;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.Customer;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.EmailAddress;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.LineItem;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.Order;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.OrderStatus;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Kind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Monad;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.MonadZero;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Semigroups;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.TypeArity;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.WitnessArity;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.either.Either;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.either.EitherKind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.expression.For;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Instances;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.list.ListKind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.list.ListTraverse;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.maybe.Maybe;
```
