## Steps: `from`, `let` and `yield` {#from-let-and-yield}

`For.from(monad, source)` starts the chain, and each later step adds values, filters the chain, or ends it:

| Step | What it does | Built on |
|---|---|---|
| `from(f)` | Adds the value inside the container `f` gives back | `flatMap` |
| `let(f)` | Adds a plain value worked out from the values so far | `map` |
| `when(test)` | Gives the instance's empty value when `test` fails | `MonadZero`'s `filter` |
| `par(f1, f2)` | Adds two to four values that do not need one another | `flatMap`, then `map2` to `map4` |
| `yield(f)` | Ends the chain with a result made from every value | `map` |

The first step's function receives the value bound so far. From the second value on, a step receives a tuple holding the values in the order they were bound, read as `t._1()` for the first, `t._2()` for the second, and so on. `yield` takes either the tuple or one parameter for each value. Written with `For`, the receipt reads top to bottom, and in it `t._1()` is the order and `t._2()` the total that `let` added:

```java
  // The same receipt, as a For comprehension: each step reads what the steps before it bound
  public static <F extends WitnessArity<TypeArity.Unary>> Kind<F, String> receipt(
      Monad<F> monad,
      Kind<F, Order> found,
      Function<Customer, Kind<F, EmailAddress>> verify,
      Function<BigDecimal, Kind<F, String>> charge) {
    return For.from(monad, found)
        .let(order -> total(order))
        .from(t -> verify.apply(t._1().customer()))
        .from(t -> charge.apply(t._2()))
        .yield(
            (order, total, email, confirmation) ->
                "receipt " + confirmation + " for " + total + " to " + email.value());
  }

```

Run it with `Either`'s `Monad`, once with an email that is verified and once with one that is not. `ORDER` is any order of two lines, totalling 50.00:

```java
    Monad<EitherKind.Witness<String>> eitherMonad = Instances.monad(either());
    Function<BigDecimal, Kind<EitherKind.Witness<String>, String>> charge =
        amount -> EITHER.widen(Either.right("PAY-1"));

    Either<String, String> sent =
        EITHER.narrow(
            receipt(
                eitherMonad,
                EITHER.widen(Either.right(ORDER)),
                customer -> EITHER.widen(Either.right(customer.email())),
                charge));
    // Right(receipt PAY-1 for 50.00 to ada@example.com)

    Either<String, String> unverified =
        EITHER.narrow(
            receipt(
                eitherMonad,
                EITHER.widen(Either.right(ORDER)),
                customer -> EITHER.widen(Either.left("email not verified")),
                charge));
    // Left(email not verified)
```

What happens when a step has no value is the instance's, as on the [Monad](monad.md#what-each-instance-does) page: `Either`'s stops at the `Left`, and the charge never runs.

## Filtering with `when` {#filtering-with-when}

`when` needs a [`MonadZero`](monad_zero.md): a `Monad` with an empty value, such as an empty list or `Nothing`, to give back when a test fails. The type you start the chain from decides. `Instances.monadZero(list())` gives a chain with `when`, and `Instances.monad(list())` gives one without:

<!-- verify:rejects "cannot find symbol" -->
```java
For.from(Instances.monad(list()), orders).when(order -> order.status() == OrderStatus.SHIPPED);
```

javac refuses it with "cannot find symbol", because a chain started from a plain `Monad` has no `when`. The instance decides what the empty value is: `List`'s drops the element, and `Maybe`'s gives `Nothing`:

```java
    MonadZero<ListKind.Witness> listMonad = Instances.monadZero(list());
    List<UUID> shippedIds =
        LIST.narrow(
            For.from(listMonad, LIST.widen(List.of(ORDER, SHIPPED)))
                .when(order -> order.status() == OrderStatus.SHIPPED)
                .yield(Order::id));
    // [00000000-0000-0000-0000-000000000002]

    MonadZero<MaybeKind.Witness> maybeMonad = Instances.monadZero(maybe());
    Maybe<UUID> notShipped =
        MAYBE.narrow(
            For.from(maybeMonad, MAYBE.just(ORDER))
                .when(order -> order.status() == OrderStatus.SHIPPED)
                .yield(Order::id));
    // Nothing
```

## Independent steps with `par` {#independent-steps-with-par}

`par` binds values that do not need one another. `For.par(monad, …)` starts a chain with two to five of them, and a step's `par(f1, …)` fans out two to four from the values bound before it. Its result is an ordinary step, so `from`, `let`, `when` or another `par` can follow.

`par` combines its values with the instance's `map2` to `map5`, where `from` uses `flatMap`. So it does what the instance's [Applicative](applicative.md#what-each-instance-does) does: with `Validated`'s instance, `par` reports both errors, and `from` stops at the first. The step's `par` fans out from the order:

```java
    Monad<ValidatedKind.Witness<List<String>>> validatedMonad =
        Instances.validated(Semigroups.list());
    Kind<ValidatedKind.Witness<List<String>>, String> blankSku =
        VALIDATED.widen(Validated.invalid(List.of("SKU is blank")));
    Kind<ValidatedKind.Witness<List<String>>, Integer> noQuantity =
        VALIDATED.widen(Validated.invalid(List.of("quantity must be positive")));

    Validated<List<String>, String> independent =
        VALIDATED.narrow(
            For.par(validatedMonad, blankSku, noQuantity)
                .yield((sku, quantity) -> quantity + " x " + sku));
    // Invalid([SKU is blank, quantity must be positive])

    Validated<List<String>, String> inSequence =
        VALIDATED.narrow(
            For.from(validatedMonad, blankSku)
                .from(sku -> noQuantity)
                .yield((sku, quantity) -> quantity + " x " + sku));
    // Invalid([SKU is blank])

    Either<String, String> fannedOut =
        EITHER.narrow(
            For.from(eitherMonad, EITHER.widen(Either.right(ORDER)))
                .par(
                    order -> EITHER.widen(Either.right(order.customer().email())),
                    order -> EITHER.widen(Either.right(total(order))))
                .yield((order, email, total) -> total + " to " + email.value()));
    // Right(50.00 to ada@example.com)
```

`par` says the values are independent, and it runs them at the same time only where the instance's `map2` does. No instance's `map2` starts its values together: `IO`'s and `VTask`'s run them one after the other, and `CompletableFuture`'s waits for futures that may already be running. To run two `VTask`s at the same time, use [`ForPath.par`](../effect/forpath_par.md) on two `VTaskPath`s, which calls `Par.map2`, or call [`Par.map2`](../monads/vtask_monad.md#parallel-composition-with-par) yourself.

## Traversing inside a chain {#traversing-inside-a-chain}

A step can run an effect on every element of a collection the chain holds, and bind the collected results:

| Step | What it binds | Built on |
|---|---|---|
| `traverse(traversable, extractor, f)` | `f` run on every element, the results collected | [`Traverse`](foldable_and_traverse.md)'s `traverse` |
| `sequence(traversable, extractor)` | A collection of containers, turned into one container of a collection | `Traverse`'s `sequenceA`, which is `traverse` with the identity function |
| `flatTraverse(traversable, innerMonad, extractor, f)` | `f` run on every element, the nested collections flattened | `traverse`, then the collection's own `flatMap` |

`traversable` is the collection's `Traverse`, such as `ListTraverse.INSTANCE`, and `innerMonad` the collection's own `Monad`. `extractor` picks the collection out of the values bound so far; here the chain starts from it, so it is `lines -> lines`. `LIST.widen` puts the list in, and `.map(LIST::narrow)` takes the result out. `priceInStock` prices a line, or gives `Nothing` for a line with no stock, so one missing line makes the whole result `Nothing`. With `maybeMonad` from the `when` example:

```java
    Function<LineItem, Kind<MaybeKind.Witness, BigDecimal>> priceInStock =
        line -> line.quantity() > 0 ? MAYBE.just(lineTotal(line)) : MAYBE.nothing();

    Maybe<List<BigDecimal>> priced =
        MAYBE
            .narrow(
                For.from(maybeMonad, MAYBE.just(LIST.widen(ORDER.lines())))
                    .traverse(ListTraverse.INSTANCE, lines -> lines, priceInStock)
                    .yield((lines, prices) -> prices))
            .map(LIST::narrow);
    // Just([40.00, 10.00])

    Maybe<List<BigDecimal>> backordered =
        MAYBE
            .narrow(
                For.from(maybeMonad, MAYBE.just(LIST.widen(BACKORDERED.lines())))
                    .traverse(ListTraverse.INSTANCE, lines -> lines, priceInStock)
                    .yield((lines, prices) -> prices))
            .map(LIST::narrow);
    // Nothing

    Maybe<List<Integer>> quantities =
        MAYBE
            .narrow(
                For.from(maybeMonad, MAYBE.just(LIST.widen(List.of(MAYBE.just(1), MAYBE.just(4)))))
                    .sequence(ListTraverse.INSTANCE, items -> items)
                    .yield((items, values) -> values))
            .map(LIST::narrow);
    // Just([1, 4])

    Maybe<List<Integer>> flattened =
        MAYBE
            .narrow(
                For.from(maybeMonad, MAYBE.just(LIST.widen(List.of(1, 2))))
                    .flatTraverse(
                        ListTraverse.INSTANCE,
                        Instances.monad(list()),
                        numbers -> numbers,
                        n -> MAYBE.just(LIST.widen(List.of(n, n * 10))))
                    .yield((numbers, flat) -> flat))
            .map(LIST::narrow);
    // Just([1, 10, 2, 20])
```

## Which comprehension should you use? {#which-comprehension}

| You are writing | Use |
|---|---|
| Application code over one Effect Path | [`ForPath`](../effect/forpath_comprehension.md), which takes and gives back Paths |
| A method for any container, over `Kind` | `For`, on this page |
| A chain whose values are easier to name than to number | [`ForState`](forstate_comprehension.md), with named fields in a record, or `toState` from `For`, as [ForState's two ways in](forstate_comprehension.md#two-ways-in) shows |
| Steps that read a field, convert a value or match a case | `focus`, `through` and `match`, as [For with Optics](for_optics.md) shows: `through` works on the first step only, and `match` needs a `MonadZero` |

A monad transformer, such as `StateT`, is a `Monad` over `Kind`, so `For` chains it as it does any other: the [StateT](../transformers/statet_transformer.md) page shows one.

## Things people get wrong {#things-people-get-wrong}

~~~admonish warning title="Common Misunderstandings"
- **"`par` runs the steps at the same time."** It marks them as independent, and the instance's `map2` decides how they run. `ForPath.par` on `VTaskPath` is the one that runs them at once.
- **"`List` has `when`, so my chain does."** Only a chain started from a `MonadZero`, such as `Instances.monadZero(list())`, has `when`. Started from `Instances.monad(list())`, it has none.
- **"`For` is Java's version of Scala's `for`."** It is a library builder, with no compiler support. Each step is a method call you can read in the source.
~~~

~~~admonish tip title="You can ship now"
You can now write a chain of dependent steps once for any container, as `receipt` is, with plain values between the steps. You can filter it with `when`, mark independent steps with `par` knowing what each instance does with them, and traverse a collection inside it. The rest of this page is for how many values a chain holds, and for readers who know Scala's `for`.
~~~

~~~admonish question title="Checkpoint: `when` on a `MonadZero` held as a `Monad`" id="check-for-when"
`Maybe`'s instance is a `MonadZero`. Why does this chain not compile, and what one change gives it `when`?

<!-- verify:rejects "cannot find symbol" -->
```java
Monad<MaybeKind.Witness> monad = Instances.monadZero(maybe());
For.from(monad, found).when(order -> order.status() == OrderStatus.SHIPPED);
```
~~~

~~~admonish success title="Answer and why" collapsible=true id="check-for-when-answer"
**The declared type decides, and `monad` is declared a `Monad`, so the chain has no `when` and javac reports "cannot find symbol".** Declare it `MonadZero<MaybeKind.Witness>`, and `For.from` starts the chain that has `when`.

Where this lives: [Filtering with `when`](#filtering-with-when).
~~~

~~~admonish question title="Checkpoint: `par` over two failures" id="check-for-par"
With `Either`'s instance, `For.par` starts a chain from `Left("SKU is blank")` and `Left("quantity must be positive")`. What does `yield` give?
~~~

~~~admonish success title="Answer and why" collapsible=true id="check-for-par-answer"
**`Left(SKU is blank)`.** `par` combines its values with the instance's `map2`, and `Either`'s keeps the first failure. Only an instance whose `map2` joins errors, such as `Validated`'s, reports both.

Where this lives: [Independent steps with `par`](#independent-steps-with-par).
~~~

---

## The fine print {#the-fine-print}

### Twelve values, and their tuples {#twelve-values-and-their-tuples}

A chain holds up to twelve values, from any mix of `from`, `let`, `par` and the optic steps. The first step is written by hand, and the steps that hold two to twelve values are generated when Higher-Kinded-J is built, so your build needs no processor for them. The twelfth has no room for another value, so it can only `yield`, call `toState`, or filter with `when` in a chain started from a `MonadZero`. Past four or five values, positions such as `t._4()` get hard to follow, and `toState` moves the values into a record with named fields.

### Coming from Scala {#coming-from-scala}

Scala builds the same chain into its syntax:

```scala
for {
  order        <- found
  total         = order.total
  email        <- verify(order.customer)
  confirmation <- charge(total)
} yield s"receipt $confirmation for $total to $email"
```

The compiler turns every `<-` but the last into `flatMap` and the last into `map`, an `if` line into `withFilter`, and `=` into a `map` that carries the value. `For` gives the same steps as methods: `from`, `when`, `let` and `yield`.

---

~~~admonish tip title="See Also"
- [Monad](monad.md): the `flatMap` that `from` is built on
- [Applicative](applicative.md): the `map2` that `par` is built on
- [ForPath Comprehension](../effect/forpath_comprehension.md): the same steps over Effect Paths, with [ForPath Traverse](../effect/forpath_traverse.md) for collections
- [ForState](forstate_comprehension.md): a chain whose values are named fields, entered with `toState`
- [For with Optics](for_optics.md): `focus`, `match`, `ForTraversal` and `ForIndexed`
- [For with MTL Capabilities](for_mtl.md): reader, state and writer capabilities as steps
- [Order Workflow](../hkts/order-walkthrough.md): a longer chain, end to end
~~~

~~~admonish tip title="Further Reading"
- **Scala documentation**, [For Comprehensions](https://docs.scala-lang.org/tour/for-comprehensions.html): the syntax `For` follows
~~~

~~~admonish info title="Hands-On Learning"
Practise `par` on Paths in the [ForPath Parallel journey](../tutorials/expression/forpath_parallel_journey.md) (9 exercises), and `traverse` in [Tutorial 03: For Traverse Comprehension](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/tutorial/expression/Tutorial03_ForTraverseComprehension.java) (14 exercises).
~~~

# Check Your Understanding

_Twelve questions on the Ship pages, each answer proved by the build._

These questions cover the pages from [One Line, Six Layers](one_line_six_layers.md) to [For Comprehensions](../functional/for_comprehension.md). They start with recall and end with writing code of your own, and they do not follow page order, on purpose. Answer each question before you open its answer, in your head or on paper. Each answer ends with a link to the section that teaches it, and [Where to go next](#where-to-go-next) turns your score into a plan.

~~~admonish question title="Checkpoint 1: changing the value inside a `Kind`" id="check-self-kind"
You hold a `Kind<MaybeKind.Witness, Order>`. What do you call `map` on to work out the order's total, and how do you get a plain `Maybe` back?
~~~

~~~admonish success title="Answer and why" collapsible=true id="check-self-kind-answer"
**On `Maybe`'s instance, such as `Instances.functor(maybe())`, and then `MAYBE.narrow`.** Any of the `functor`, `applicative` or `monad` lookups gives that instance. A `Kind` has no methods of its own, so calling `map` on it does not compile: javac cannot find a "method map" on it.

<!-- verify:rejects "method map" -->
```java
Kind<MaybeKind.Witness, Order> found = MAYBE.widen(Maybe.just(order));
found.map(placed -> placed.customer());
```

Where this lives: [The five steps](usage-guide.md#the-five-steps).
~~~

~~~admonish question title="Checkpoint 2: a step that can fail, on a Path" id="check-self-via"
On an `EitherPath`, which method runs a step that can itself fail? Does that method go through `Either`'s `Monad` instance?
~~~

~~~admonish success title="Answer and why" collapsible=true id="check-self-via-answer"
**`via`, and no.** An `EitherPath` holds an `Either` and calls it directly, with no `Kind` and no instance between. `Monad`'s `flatMap` is the same operation over `Kind`, and the two give the same result.

Where this lives: [Two encodings, one algebra](one_line_six_layers.md#two-encodings-one-algebra).
~~~

~~~admonish question title="Checkpoint 3: nothing to give back" id="check-self-unit"
A step runs only for its effect, and has no value to give back. What goes in the `A` of its `Kind<F, A>`, and why not `Void`?
~~~

~~~admonish success title="Answer and why" collapsible=true id="check-self-unit-answer"
**`Unit`, whose one value is `Unit.INSTANCE`.** `Void`'s only value is `null`, and what `of(null)` does depends on the container: `Either`'s throws, and `Maybe`'s gives `Nothing`. `Unit.INSTANCE` is a value that every container holds.

Where this lives: [`Unit`: a value for "nothing to return"](core-concepts.md#unit).
~~~

~~~admonish question title="Checkpoint 4: what a future's handler receives" id="check-self-future"
A `CompletableFuture` fails inside `supplyAsync`, and a later `thenApply` stage passes the failure on. What does `handleErrorWith`'s handler receive: a `CompletionException`, or the exception the supplier threw?
~~~

~~~admonish success title="Answer and why" collapsible=true id="check-self-future-answer"
**The exception the supplier threw.** `CompletableFuture`'s instance unwraps the cause from any `CompletionException` before it calls the handler.

Where this lives: [What each instance does](../functional/monad_error.md#what-each-instance-does).
~~~

~~~admonish question title="Checkpoint 5: three lookups on `Try`" id="check-self-one-object"
How many different objects do `Instances.monad(try_())`, `Instances.applicative(try_())` and `Instances.functor(try_())` give back?
~~~

~~~admonish success title="Answer and why" collapsible=true id="check-self-one-object-answer"
**One.** A token's instance implements the whole `Functor`, `Applicative`, `Monad` chain, so the three lookups return that same object, for `Try` as for `Maybe`.

Where this lives: [Zero-argument lookups](../functional/instances_facade.md#zero-argument-lookups).
~~~

~~~admonish question title="Checkpoint 6: a lookup inside `map`" id="check-self-nest"
`found` is a `Kind<EitherKind.Witness<String>, Order>`, `monad` is `Either`'s `Monad`, and `findCustomer` gives back a `Kind<EitherKind.Witness<String>, Customer>`. What does `monad.map(findCustomer, found)` give back, and which method gives a `Kind<EitherKind.Witness<String>, Customer>` instead?
~~~

~~~admonish success title="Answer and why" collapsible=true id="check-self-nest-answer"
**A `Kind` inside a `Kind`, and `flatMap`.** `map` puts whatever its function returns inside the container, a container included. `flatMap` runs a step that gives back a container, and flattens the result into one. The compiler agrees with both types:

<!-- verify -->
```java
Kind<EitherKind.Witness<String>, Kind<EitherKind.Witness<String>, Customer>> nested =
    monad.map(findCustomer, found);
Kind<EitherKind.Witness<String>, Customer> flat = monad.flatMap(findCustomer, found);
```

Where this lives: [What a `Monad` adds](../functional/monad.md#what-a-monad-adds).
~~~

~~~admonish question title="Checkpoint 7: every bad line, or the first" id="check-self-traverse"
You `traverse` three line items with a check that fails on two of them, once through `Instances.validated(Semigroups.list())` and once through `Either`'s instance. How many errors come back from each?
~~~

~~~admonish success title="Answer and why" collapsible=true id="check-self-traverse-answer"
**Two from `Validated`, and one from `Either`.** `traverse` combines the results with the instance it is given, so it keeps what that instance's `map2` keeps. `Validated`'s joins the errors with its semigroup, here one that keeps them all, and `Either`'s keeps the first.

Where this lives: [What each instance does](../functional/applicative.md#what-each-instance-does), and [What builds on `Applicative`](../functional/applicative.md#what-builds-on-applicative).
~~~

~~~admonish question title="Checkpoint 8: widening twice" id="check-self-widen"
You widen the same `Maybe` twice, and the same `Optional` twice. Is each pair the same object?
~~~

~~~admonish success title="Answer and why" collapsible=true id="check-self-widen-answer"
**The `Maybe` pair is, and the `Optional` pair is not.** A `Maybe` is its own `Kind`, so widening returns it. An `Optional` cannot implement `Kind`, so each widen wraps it in a new holder.

Where this lives: [The pieces each type provides](core-concepts.md#the-pieces-each-type-provides).
~~~

~~~admonish question title="Checkpoint 9: `par` or `from`, on a list" id="check-self-par"
With `List`'s instance, `For.par` starts a chain from the SKUs `["LAMP", "DESK"]` and the quantities `[1, 2]`. How many values does `yield` give? Does a chain that binds the same two lists with `from` give a different count?
~~~

~~~admonish success title="Answer and why" collapsible=true id="check-self-par-answer"
**Four, and no.** `par` combines with `List`'s `map2`, which gives every combination. `from` uses `List`'s `flatMap`, which runs the next step on every element, so it reaches the same four.

Where this lives: [Independent steps with `par`](../functional/for_comprehension.md#independent-steps-with-par), and [What each instance does](../functional/applicative.md#what-each-instance-does).
~~~

~~~admonish question title="Checkpoint 10: write `countLines`" id="check-self-count"
Write `countLines`, which gives the number of lines of the order in any container. Ask for the least capability it needs.
~~~

~~~admonish success title="Answer and why" collapsible=true id="check-self-count-answer"
**A `Functor` is enough**, because counting needs only `map`:

```java
  // Only map is needed, so the method asks for a Functor
  public static <F extends WitnessArity<TypeArity.Unary>> Kind<F, Integer> countLines(
      Functor<F> functor, Kind<F, Order> orders) {
    return functor.map(order -> order.lines().size(), orders);
  }

```

Where this lives: [One method for any container](../functional/functor.md#one-method-for-any-container).
~~~

~~~admonish question title="Checkpoint 11: write `receiptLine`" id="check-self-receipt"
Write `receiptLine`, which builds `"<order id> to <email>"` from an order and an email address looked up independently, in any container. Ask for the least capability it needs, and say what it gives with `Instances.validated(Semigroups.list())` when neither was found.
~~~

~~~admonish success title="Answer and why" collapsible=true id="check-self-receipt-answer"
**An `Applicative`**, because the two values do not depend on each other, so `map2` combines them:

```java
  // The order and the email are found apart, so the method asks for an Applicative
  public static <F extends WitnessArity<TypeArity.Unary>> Kind<F, String> receiptLine(
      Applicative<F> applicative, Kind<F, Order> order, Kind<F, EmailAddress> email) {
    return applicative.map2(order, email, (o, e) -> o.id() + " to " + e.value());
  }

```

With `Validated`'s instance and a semigroup that joins lists, it gives both errors.

Where this lives: [One method for any container](../functional/applicative.md#one-method-for-any-container).
~~~

~~~admonish question title="Checkpoint 12: write `fromCacheOr`" id="check-self-cache"
Write `fromCacheOr`, which gives the order from a cache lookup, or calls the order service when the cache missed, in any container with an error type. The service must not be called when the cache has the order.
~~~

~~~admonish success title="Answer and why" collapsible=true id="check-self-cache-answer"
**A `MonadError`, with the service call in a handler**, so it runs only when the cache missed. `recoverWith` would take the service's result as a value, so Java would call the service first, whatever the cache gave:

```java
  // The service is called only when the cache missed, so the call sits in a handler
  public static <F extends WitnessArity<TypeArity.Unary>, E> Kind<F, Order> fromCacheOr(
      MonadError<F, E> monadError, Kind<F, Order> cached, Supplier<Kind<F, Order>> service) {
    return monadError.handleErrorWith(cached, missed -> service.get());
  }

```

Where this lives: [Should you use `recoverWith` or `handleErrorWith`?](../functional/monad_error.md#constant-fallbacks-recover-and-recoverwith)
~~~

~~~admonish example title="See Example Code"
**The answers' code is [SelfCheckBook.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/selfcheck/SelfCheckBook.java), and [SelfCheckBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/selfcheck/SelfCheckBookTest.java) holds every result an answer predicts.** The build also checks the refusal in Checkpoint 1 against javac's own words, and compiles the two types in Checkpoint 6. Open them after the twelve, since they hold the answers.
~~~

<!-- description: Why Java needs an encoding for higher-kinded types, how a witness declares its arity, what one call costs, and how to add your own type. -->

# Higher-Kinded Types

<!-- source: Thomas Pynchon, Gravity's Rainbow (1973), "Proverbs for Paranoids, 3", p. 251 in the Vintage edition; wording as in the e-book text, where one hand transcription adds "the" before "answers" -->
> _"If they can get you asking the wrong questions, they don't have to worry about answers."_
>
> — Thomas Pynchon, *Gravity's Rainbow* (Proverbs for Paranoids, 3)

---

The right question is not "how do I map over this `List`?" or "how do I map over this `Optional`?" The right question is "how do I map over *any* container?" Java cannot ask it directly, because a type parameter cannot stand for a container that still waits for its element type. This group is about the encoding that lets Higher-Kinded-J ask it anyway.

Read this group when you want the reasons and the mechanics behind the encoding, or when you add a type of your own. The Ship pages in the [Chapter Contents](foundations_intro.md#chapter-contents) are enough to write code that works for any container, so you can skip the group until then.

---

## Pages in this group {#pages-in-this-group}

1. [Why Higher-Kinded Types?](hkt_introduction.md): What a higher-kinded type is, and why Java needs an encoding for one
2. [Type Arity](type-arity.md): How a witness declares how many type arguments its container still needs
3. [Lifting the Hood](lifting_the_hood.md): One call traced through the encoding, step by step
4. [Adding Your Own Type](extending-simulation.md): A witness, a helper and an instance for a type of your own, the laws that prove them, and a Path

The [Foundations FAQ](faq.md) answers the design questions this group raises, such as why `Kind` has two type parameters, and what `widen` and `narrow` cost.

---

**Previous:** [Check Your Understanding](self_check.md)
**Next:** [Why Higher-Kinded Types?](hkt_introduction.md)

<!-- Source: hkts/hkt_introduction.md -->

<!-- description: Why Java needs higher-kinded types to write one method for any container, why a shared interface is not enough, and where Higher-Kinded-J's encoding comes from. -->

# Why Higher-Kinded Types?

_One method for every container, and why Java needs an encoding to write it._

![Two hands turning a Rubik's cube](../images/rubiks1932.jpg)

> **You:** I want one `map` for every container, so I write `<F, A, B> F<B> map(F<A> box, Function<A, B> f)`.
>
> **javac:** `unexpected type`. `F` is a type parameter, and a type parameter takes no type arguments of its own.
>
> **You:** Then my containers share an interface, `Mappable<A>`, and I write `map` once over that.
>
> **javac:** Hand that method a `Box<Integer>` and it gives back a `Mappable<String>`, not a `Box`. And `List` and `Optional` belong to the JDK, so they will never implement your interface.
>
> **You:** So I need a name for the container alone, without what it holds, that you can pass as an ordinary type.
>
> **javac:** A witness class can be that name. Write `Kind<ListKind.Witness, Integer>`, and I carry `ListKind.Witness` through your method like any other type.
>
> **You:** But a `Kind` has no `map`.
>
> **javac:** Then take a `Functor<F>` that maps inside `F`. I will check that its `F` and the box's `F` are the same.
>
> **You:** And give back a `Kind<F, B>`: the same container, holding the new value.

javac's three refusals are real, and [What javac refuses](#what-javac-refuses) shows each one. The rest of the page states every step plainly.

~~~admonish info title="What You'll Learn"
- Tell a type, a type constructor and a higher-kinded type apart
- Say why plain Java needs a method for each container, and why a shared interface does not fix it
- Read the one method that serves every container, written with Higher-Kinded-J
- Name the paper the encoding comes from, and what the encoding costs in Java
~~~

~~~admonish example title="See Example Code"
**The code on this page is [WhyHktBook.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/why/WhyHktBook.java)**: the page includes it, so the build compiles and runs it. It reuses `totals` from the chapter introduction's [FoundationsIntroBook.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/intro/FoundationsIntroBook.java). [WhyHktBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/why/WhyHktBookTest.java) holds each claim the page makes.
~~~

---

## Types, type constructors and higher kinds {#types-type-constructors-and-higher-kinds}

`String` is a type: you can declare a variable of it. `List` is a *type constructor*, which waits for a type argument and then gives a type, such as `List<String>`. (Java still accepts a raw `List`, for code older than generics.) A type constructor does for types what a function does for values.

A *higher-kinded* type goes one level further: its parameter stands for a type constructor rather than a type. The same step separates a higher-order function from an ordinary one:

| Level | Takes | Example |
|---|---|---|
| Function | values | `int square(int n)` |
| Higher-order function | a function | `<A, B> List<B> map(List<A> list, Function<A, B> f)` |
| Type constructor | types | `List<_>`, waiting for its element type |
| Higher-kinded type | a type constructor | `Functor<F<_>>`, where `F` stands for `List`, `Maybe` or any other container |

So `List` is not higher-kinded, because it takes a type. `Functor` is, because its `F` stands for a container rather than for what the container holds. Java has type constructors, and methods generic over types, but cannot declare `F<_>`. Higher-Kinded-J's `Functor<F>` simulates it, with `F` a witness type that stands in for the container.

Type theory calls the type of a type its *kind*. `String` has kind `*`, `List` has kind `* -> *`, and `Functor` asks for an `F` of kind `* -> *`. Higher-Kinded-J's `Kind<F, A>` borrows the word.

## One method for every container {#one-method-for-every-container}

![Cardboard boxes, plastic crates and metal flight cases, stacked side by side](../images/containers.png)

In plain Java, one line of logic needs a method for each container, because Java has no type for "any container", and each container spells `map` its own way:

```java
  // One line of logic, written again for each container
  public static List<BigDecimal> totalsOfList(List<Order> orders) {
    return orders.stream().map(FoundationsIntroBook::total).toList();
  }

  public static Optional<BigDecimal> totalOfOptional(Optional<Order> order) {
    return order.map(FoundationsIntroBook::total);
  }

  public static CompletableFuture<BigDecimal> totalOfFuture(CompletableFuture<Order> order) {
    return order.thenApply(FoundationsIntroBook::total);
  }

```

| Container | Plain Java | With Higher-Kinded-J |
|---|---|---|
| `List` | `totalsOfList`, with `stream().map(…).toList()` | `totals(functor(list()), …)` |
| `Optional` | `totalOfOptional`, with `map` | `totals(functor(optional()), …)` |
| `CompletableFuture` | `totalOfFuture`, with `thenApply` | `totals(functor(completableFuture()), …)` |

Three methods are no great burden for one line. The cost grows with what you build on them, since anything that combines or checks totals is written again for each container. With higher kinds you write the method once, for any `F`, and pass the `Functor` that knows how to map inside it. It is the same `totals` that the [chapter introduction](foundations_intro.md) runs on `Maybe`, `Either` and `List`:

```java
  // One method for any container F: the Functor passed in knows how to map inside F
  public static <F extends WitnessArity<TypeArity.Unary>> Kind<F, BigDecimal> totals(
      Functor<F> functor, Kind<F, Order> orders) {
    return functor.map(FoundationsIntroBook::total, orders);
  }

```

Here it runs on the three containers the plain methods cover, and gives the same totals. `widen` takes each value into the encoding, `narrow` brings the result back, and `functor(list())` asks for the `Functor` that maps inside a `List`:

```java
    List<BigDecimal> listTotals =
        LIST.narrow(totals(functor(list()), LIST.widen(List.of(order, refill))));
    // [50.00, 7.50]
    Optional<BigDecimal> optionalTotal =
        OPTIONAL.narrow(totals(functor(optional()), OPTIONAL.widen(Optional.of(order))));
    // Optional[50.00]
    BigDecimal futureTotal =
        FUTURE
            .narrow(
                totals(
                    functor(completableFuture()),
                    FUTURE.widen(CompletableFuture.completedFuture(order))))
            .join();
    // 50.00
```

[How Higher-Kinded-J encodes it](#how-higher-kinded-j-encodes-it) says where each piece comes from, and [Type Arity](type-arity.md) explains the bound on `F`.

## What javac refuses {#what-javac-refuses}

The dialogue's three refusals, as javac reports them.

**A type parameter cannot take type arguments.** The first attempt names the container as `F` and gives it an element type:

<!-- verify:rejects "type parameter F" -->
```java
<F, A, B> F<B> map(F<A> box, Function<A, B> f);
```

javac answers `unexpected type`, with `found: type parameter F`. [How the Encoding Works](core-concepts.md#why-java-needs-an-encoding) meets the same refusal.

**A shared interface loses the container.** You can write `map` once over an interface, and a `Box` can even override it to return a `Box`. But a method written for any `Mappable` gives back a `Mappable`, and nothing in its type says it was a `Box`:

<!-- verify:rejects "no instance(s) of type variable(s)" -->
```java
interface Mappable<A> {
    <B> Mappable<B> map(Function<A, B> f);
}

record Box<A>(A value) implements Mappable<A> {
    public <B> Box<B> map(Function<A, B> f) {
        return new Box<>(f.apply(value));
    }
}

static <A, B> Mappable<B> mapAny(Mappable<A> box, Function<A, B> f) {
    return box.map(f);
}

Box<String> label = mapAny(new Box<>(42), n -> "No. " + n);
```

javac answers `incompatible types`: there are `no instance(s) of type variable(s)` that make the `Mappable<B>` from `mapAny` conform to `Box<String>`. The JDK's `List`, `Optional` and `CompletableFuture` could not implement `Mappable` anyway, since you cannot change them. The [FAQ](faq.md#why-cant-this-just-be-an-interface-mappablet) takes the same question further.

**The `Functor` must match the container.** Once the container has a name, `totals` takes that name as an ordinary type parameter. javac then checks that the `Functor` and the `Kind` name the same container, so the `Functor` for `Maybe`, given a `List`, is refused:

<!-- verify:rejects "incompatible equality constraints" -->
```java
totals(functor(maybe()), LIST.widen(List.of(order)));
```

javac answers that `totals` `cannot be applied to given types`, because the inference variable `F` has `incompatible equality constraints`: `ListKind.Witness` and `MaybeKind.Witness`.

## How Higher-Kinded-J encodes it {#how-higher-kinded-j-encodes-it}

Higher-Kinded-J follows Jeremy Yallop and Leo White's paper [Lightweight higher-kinded polymorphism](https://www.cl.cam.ac.uk/~jdy22/papers/lightweight-higher-kinded-polymorphism.pdf) (FLOPS 2014). It was written for OCaml, which can abstract over a type constructor only in its module language. The paper *defunctionalises* the type constructor instead. The constructor gets a name that is an ordinary type, and "the constructor applied to `A`" becomes one type that takes the name and `A`:

| In the paper | In Higher-Kinded-J | What it does |
|---|---|---|
| a brand, such as `List.t` | a witness, such as `ListKind.Witness` | Names a type constructor, and is never instantiated |
| `('a, 'f) app` | `Kind<F, A>` | Stands for the constructor that `F` names, applied to `A` |
| `inj` | `widen` | Moves a value into the encoding |
| `prj` | `narrow` | Moves it back out |

Type classes such as `Functor<F>` are then ordinary generic interfaces over `Kind<F, A>`, and each container supplies an instance of them. [How the Encoding Works](core-concepts.md) covers each piece, and [Lifting the Hood](lifting_the_hood.md) traces one call through them.

## What it costs {#what-it-costs}

The encoding asks more of you than a language with higher kinds built in:

- **Every value crosses twice.** `widen` takes it into the encoding, and `narrow` brings it back.
- **Every generic method takes its instance**, such as the `Functor` that `totals` asks for.
- **`narrow` casts, and checks the cast at run time.** Any class can implement `Kind<ListKind.Witness, A>`, so javac cannot prove that such a `Kind` holds a `List`. `narrow` throws `KindUnwrapException` when it does not, where a language with higher kinds has no cast to check. [When `narrow` throws](core-concepts.md#when-narrow-throws) says how that can happen.

Application code rarely needs to pay this. [Effect Paths](../effect/ch_intro.md) such as `EitherPath` give the same operations on concrete types, with no witness to write, and the [chapter introduction](foundations_intro.md) says which level fits your code. Higher kinds earn their place in code written once for any container, such as [`traverse`](../functional/foldable_and_traverse.md), [`For`](../functional/for_comprehension.md), or a method like `totals`.

---

~~~admonish info title="Hands-On Learning"
Practise `Kind<F, A>` from the first exercise in the [Core Types Tutorial Track](../tutorials/coretypes/ch_intro.md), which runs from widening a value to production workflows.
~~~

---

**Next:** [Type Arity](type-arity.md)

<!-- description: How a Higher-Kinded-J witness declares how many type arguments its container still needs, and why Functor takes a List but not a two-sided Either. -->

# Type Arity: Classifying Type Constructors

_How many type arguments a container still needs, and which type classes accept it._

<!-- source: Euclid, Elements, Book I, Definitions 1-2, trans. T. L. Heath (1908); checked against D. E. Joyce's online edition -->
> _"A point is that which has no part. A line is breadthless length."_
>
> — Euclid, *Elements* (Book I, Definitions 1 and 2, trans. T. L. Heath)

---

Euclid defines a point and a line by what each lacks. Arity defines a type constructor by what it still lacks: `String` nothing, `List` one type, `Either` two. Higher-Kinded-J writes that number into every witness, so javac can refuse a container whose shape a type class cannot use.

~~~admonish info title="What You'll Learn"
- Tell a witness, which names a container, from its arity, which counts the type arguments the container still needs
- Read the arity bounds on `Kind`, `Kind2` and the type classes
- Say why `Functor` takes `EitherKind.Witness<L>` but refuses `EitherKind2.Witness`
- Declare the arity of a witness for a type of your own
~~~

---

## Witness and `WitnessArity` {#witness-and-witnessarity}

Two ideas meet in every witness:

- **The witness answers "which container?"** `ListKind.Witness` stands for `List`, and `IOKind.Witness` for `IO`. It is an empty class that nothing instantiates.
- **`WitnessArity` answers "how many type arguments does it still need?"** Each witness implements `WitnessArity<TypeArity.Unary>` or `WitnessArity<TypeArity.Binary>`.

`TypeArity` is a sealed interface with exactly two cases, `Unary` and `Binary`. Here is a witness for a type of your own, declared unary:

<!-- verify -->
```java
public interface MyTypeKind<A> extends Kind<MyTypeKind.Witness, A> {
    final class Witness implements WitnessArity<TypeArity.Unary> {
        private Witness() {}
    }
}

public record MyType<A>(A value) implements MyTypeKind<A> {}
```

[Adding Your Own Type](extending-simulation.md) goes on from here to the helper and the instances.

## Unary and binary {#unary-and-binary}

The kind column uses the notation from [Why Higher-Kinded Types?](hkt_introduction.md#types-type-constructors-and-higher-kinds): `*` is a complete type, and each arrow takes one more type argument.

| Arity | What is missing | Example witnesses | Type classes that accept it |
|---|---|---|---|
| `Unary`, kind `* -> *` | One type argument, as in `List<_>` | `ListKind.Witness`, `MaybeKind.Witness`, `IOKind.Witness`, `EitherKind.Witness<L>` | `Functor`, `Applicative`, `Monad`, `MonadError`, `Traverse`, and every other type class over `Kind` but `Bifunctor` and `Profunctor` |
| `Binary`, kind `* -> * -> *` | Two type arguments, as in `Either<_, _>` | `EitherKind2.Witness`, `ValidatedKind2.Witness`, `Tuple2Kind2.Witness`, `FunctionKind.Witness` | `Bifunctor` and `Profunctor` |

`Either` has a witness of each arity. `EitherKind.Witness<L>` fixes the left type, which leaves one type argument missing, so `Functor`, `Monad` and `MonadError` take it. `EitherKind2.Witness` leaves both missing, for `Bifunctor`. [The Hierarchy](../functional/ch_intro.md#the-hierarchy) shows which type class extends which.

## How the bounds fit together {#how-the-bounds-fit-together}

```mermaid
flowchart BT
    accTitle: How witnesses meet the bounds on Kind, Kind2 and the type classes
    accDescr: Unary witnesses such as ListKind.Witness and EitherKind.Witness of L implement WitnessArity of Unary, which fits Kind and the unary type classes such as Functor and Monad. Binary witnesses such as EitherKind2.Witness and FunctionKind.Witness implement WitnessArity of Binary, which fits Kind, Kind2, Bifunctor and Profunctor.
    UW["ListKind.Witness<br/>EitherKind.Witness#lt;L#gt;"]
    BW["EitherKind2.Witness<br/>FunctionKind.Witness"]
    U["WitnessArity#lt;Unary#gt;"]
    B["WitnessArity#lt;Binary#gt;"]
    TC1["Functor, Monad<br/>and the rest"]
    K["Kind"]
    K2["Kind2"]
    TC2["Bifunctor<br/>Profunctor"]
    UW -->|implements| U
    BW -->|implements| B
    U -->|fits| TC1
    U -->|fits| K
    B -->|fits| K
    B -->|fits| K2
    B -->|fits| TC2
    classDef step fill:#8caaee,stroke:#1e66f5,color:#232634
    class UW,BW,U,B,TC1,K,K2,TC2 step
```

In words: `Kind<F, A>` accepts a witness of either arity, and `Kind2<F, A, B>` only a binary one. Each type class narrows the bound further, as `Functor<F extends WitnessArity<TypeArity.Unary>>` and `Bifunctor<F extends WitnessArity<TypeArity.Binary>>` do. A class that implements no `WitnessArity` meets none of these bounds, so it cannot stand in for `F` at all.

## What the compiler refuses {#what-the-compiler-refuses}

A binary witness where a unary one is needed does not compile, and nor does a class that is not a witness:

<!-- verify:rejects "is not within bounds of type-variable" -->
```java
Functor<EitherKind2.Witness> functor;
```

<!-- verify:rejects "is not within bounds of type-variable" -->
```java
Kind<String, Integer> notAWitness;
```

javac answers each with the same message: the type argument `is not within bounds of type-variable F`. The check happens at compile time, and nothing at run time looks at a witness's arity.

The cure for `Either` is its other witness. Fix the left type, and the unary witness fits every unary type class, while the binary one serves `Bifunctor`:

<!-- verify -->
```java
// As a Monad: unary, with the left type fixed to String
Monad<EitherKind.Witness<String>> eitherMonad = Instances.monad(either());
Kind<EitherKind.Witness<String>, Integer> result =
    eitherMonad.flatMap(x -> eitherMonad.of(x + 1), eitherMonad.of(42));

// As a Bifunctor: binary, mapping both sides
Either<String, Integer> parsed = Either.right(42);
Bifunctor<EitherKind2.Witness> bifunctor = EitherBifunctor.INSTANCE;
Kind2<EitherKind2.Witness, Integer, String> mapped =
    bifunctor.bimap(String::length, Object::toString, EITHER.widen2(parsed));
```

---

**Previous:** [Why Higher-Kinded Types?](hkt_introduction.md)
**Next:** [Lifting the Hood](lifting_the_hood.md)

<!-- Source: hkts/lifting_the_hood.md -->

<!-- description: Follow one widen, flatMap and narrow through Higher-Kinded-J: how the call reaches its instance, what runs inside it, and what each step allocates. -->

# Lifting the Hood

_One round trip through the encoding, call by call, with what each step costs._

<!-- source: Leo Tolstoy, Anna Karenina (1878), Part One, Chapter 1, trans. Richard Pevear and Larissa Volokhonsky (2000) -->
> _"All happy families are alike; each unhappy family is unhappy in its own way."_
>
> — Leo Tolstoy, *Anna Karenina* (Part One, Chapter 1, trans. Richard Pevear and Larissa Volokhonsky)

---

Most library types widen alike: each is already a `Kind`, so `widen` hands back the same object. Each JDK type is wrapped in a holder of its own. This page follows one round trip through the encoding, call by call, and shows where that difference costs something.

~~~admonish info title="What You'll Learn"
- Trace what runs when you call `flatMap` on a `Kind`
- Say how the call reaches `EitherMonad`: through a value you pass, not a lookup at compile time
- Count what each step allocates, for a library type and for a JDK type
- Choose a library type when a holder's allocation matters
~~~

~~~admonish example title="See Example Code"
**The round trip is the `roundtrip` region of [EncodingBook.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/encoding/EncodingBook.java)**, the code How the Encoding Works shows. [LiftingTheHoodBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/hood/LiftingTheHoodBookTest.java) holds the claims a test can hold, and reads the compiled class for the claim about the bytecode.
~~~

---

## The round trip {#the-expression-we-are-tracing}

The round trip from [How the Encoding Works](core-concepts.md#the-witness-and-kind) widens an `Either`, calls `flatMap` on it, and narrows the result:

```java
    Monad<EitherKind.Witness<String>> monad = Instances.monad(either());

    Either<String, Integer> start = Either.right(10);
    // widen: the same Either, seen as a Kind
    Kind<EitherKind.Witness<String>, Integer> kind = EITHER.widen(start);
    // flatMap takes the function first, then the Kind, and returns a Kind
    Kind<EitherKind.Witness<String>, String> result =
        monad.flatMap(i -> EITHER.widen(Either.right("v=" + i)), kind);
    // narrow: back to an Either
    Either<String, String> finalEither = EITHER.narrow(result);
    // Right(v=10)
```

Four calls matter: `Instances.monad`, `widen`, `flatMap` and `narrow`. The next three sections take them in turn.

## How the call finds `EitherMonad` {#how-the-call-finds-eithermonad}

The instance is a value. `either()` returns a token that holds the one `EitherMonad`, and `Instances.monad(either())` hands that object back, typed as `Monad<EitherKind.Witness<String>>`. The witness type does not choose the instance. It makes javac check that `monad` and `kind` name the same container. [Obtaining Instances](../functional/instances_facade.md) covers the lookup.

`monad.flatMap(...)` is then an ordinary interface call. javac emits `invokeinterface Monad.flatMap`, and the JVM reaches `EitherMonad.flatMap` at run time, as it would for any interface method. While only one instance class passes through a call site, the JIT usually inlines the call, so the interface costs little in a hot loop. The test reads the compiled class and finds that `invokeinterface`.

## `widen` and `narrow` {#widen-and-narrow}

```mermaid
%%{init: {"sequence": {"actorMargin": 20, "diagramMarginX": 10}}}%%
sequenceDiagram
    accTitle: widen and narrow on an Either
    accDescr: Your code passes Right(10) to the EITHER helper's widen, which checks it is not null and returns the same object, typed as a Kind. Later your code passes the result to narrow, which checks it is not null and is an Either, and returns the same object, typed as an Either.
    participant Y as Your code
    participant H as EITHER helper
    Y->>H: widen(Right(10))
    Note over H: not null?
    H-->>Y: the same Right(10), as a Kind
    Y->>H: narrow(result)
    Note over H: not null? an Either?
    H-->>Y: the same object, as an Either
```

In words: `widen` checks for `null` and returns its argument, because an `Either` already implements `Kind`. `narrow` checks for `null` and for an `Either`, then casts. Neither allocates. Given another container's `Kind`, `narrow` throws `KindUnwrapException`, as [When `narrow` throws](core-concepts.md#when-narrow-throws) explains.

Most of the library's own types work this way. The JDK's types widen into a holder instead, since the library cannot change them, and so does `Lazy`, which memoises its value in fields that change, where every `Kind` must be immutable. [The pieces each type provides](core-concepts.md#the-pieces-each-type-provides) lists both kinds. An `Either` is a `Kind`, so you could skip `widen`. Keep it, so that every type reads the same and every value is checked.

## Inside `flatMap` {#inside-flatmap}

`EitherMonad.flatMap` narrows the `Kind` it is given, and hands `Either.flatMap` a lambda that calls your function:

```mermaid
%%{init: {"sequence": {"actorMargin": 20, "diagramMarginX": 10}}}%%
sequenceDiagram
    accTitle: Inside EitherMonad.flatMap on a Right
    accDescr: Your code calls flatMap on EitherMonad. It checks its arguments, narrows the Kind to an Either, and calls Right(10)'s flatMap with a lambda. The lambda calls your function f with 10, gets back Right("v=10") as a Kind, and narrows it. EitherMonad widens the result, which is the same Right("v=10"), and returns it.
    participant Y as Your code
    participant M as EitherMonad
    participant E as Right(10)
    participant F as f
    Y->>M: flatMap(f, kind)
    Note over M: checks, then narrow(kind)
    M->>E: flatMap(lambda)
    E->>F: apply(10)
    F-->>E: Right("v=10")
    Note over E: lambda narrows it
    E-->>M: Right("v=10")
    M-->>Y: widen: the same object
```

The diagram shows two narrows: one for the `Kind` you pass in, and one for the `Kind` your function returns. On a `Left`, `Either.flatMap` returns the same `Left` without calling the lambda. So your function never runs, and the `Kind` that comes back is the very `Left` that went in.

## What each step allocates {#what-each-step-allocates}

The same round trip, on a library type and on a JDK type:

| Step | `Either`, a library type | `Optional`, a JDK type |
|---|---|---|
| `Instances.monad(either())`, or `optional()` | One small token, from `either()` | One small token, from `optional()` |
| `widen` | Nothing: the same object comes back | One holder |
| `flatMap`'s own work | One lambda that captures your function | One lambda, and one holder for the result |
| Inside your function | What it builds: here a `String` and a `Right` | What it builds, and one holder when it widens its result |
| `narrow` | Nothing: a type check and a cast | Nothing: a type check and a field read |

So for a type that is its own `Kind`, the machinery allocates one short-lived lambda per `flatMap`. A held type, such as `Optional` or `Lazy`, adds a holder at each `widen`. The token comes once, when you look the instance up. The JIT can often remove short-lived objects that never leave the method, by escape analysis, but it cannot always. [Benchmarks & Performance](../benchmarks.md) has measured numbers.

That asymmetry is the tax on a type that cannot be its own `Kind`. The fix is not technical; it is to use library types (`Maybe`, `Either`) when the cost matters.

## What this means in practice {#what-this-means-in-practice}

- **On a type that is its own `Kind`, `widen` and `narrow` cost a check, not an object.** Write them wherever they make the code clearer.
- **On a JDK type, each `widen` allocates a holder.** In a tight loop, prefer `Maybe`, `Either` or `Try`. In a method that runs once per request, the holder does not show.
- **The instance is a value you pass.** Look it up once, outside a loop. A call site that sees one instance class is usually inlined. One that sees many, as code generic over `F` can, pays for an ordinary interface call.

An `EitherPath` skips the `Kind`, the instance and the lambda: it holds an `Either` and calls it directly. Its `via` builds no path of its own: a failed path comes back as itself, and otherwise you get the path your function built. Its `map` builds a new path at each step. [Two encodings, one algebra](one_line_six_layers.md#two-encodings-one-algebra) sets the two side by side, and the [chapter introduction](foundations_intro.md) says which level fits your code.

---

~~~admonish tip title="See Also"
- [How the Encoding Works](core-concepts.md): the pieces this page traces
- [Foundations FAQ](faq.md): the design questions this trace raises
- [Benchmarks & Performance](../benchmarks.md): measured numbers
~~~

---

**Previous:** [Type Arity](type-arity.md)
**Next:** [Adding Your Own Type](extending-simulation.md)

<!-- Source: hkts/extending-simulation.md -->

<!-- description: Make a type of your own work with Higher-Kinded-J's type classes: a witness, a helper and an instance, the law tests that prove them, and a Path. -->

# Adding Your Own Type

_A witness, a helper and an instance, the laws they must keep, and a Path at the end._

<!-- source: Sophocles, Antigone, ll. 453-455, trans. F. Storr (Loeb Classical Library, 1912); checked against Project Gutenberg eBook #31 -->
> _"Nor did I deem that thou, a mortal man, / Could'st by a breath annul and override / The immutable unwritten laws of Heaven."_
>
> — Sophocles, *Antigone* (trans. F. Storr)

No compiler reads the functor and monad laws, yet every caller of your instance relies on them. `hkj-test` writes them down, so your build can check them.

![A red tower crane against a blue sky, its hook lowered](../images/extending-crane.png)

Read this page when your codebase has a container type of its own, and you want `Functor`, `Monad`, `For` and the rest to work with it. The worked example is `Result`: a value, or the reason there is none, of the kind many codebases write for themselves. If `Either` or an `EitherPath` would serve, use it instead, and skip this page.

~~~admonish info title="What You'll Learn"
- Give a type of your own a witness, a helper and a `Monad`
- Prove that the instance keeps its laws, with `hkj-test`
- Pass the instance to code written for any container, such as `For`
- Hold a type you cannot change, such as the JDK's `Set`, in a holder
- Turn your type into a Path with `GenericPath`
~~~

~~~admonish example title="See Example Code"
**The code on this page is in the [owntype](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/tree/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/owntype) package**: the page includes it, so the build compiles and runs it. [OwnTypeBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/owntype/OwnTypeBookTest.java) runs the law tests and holds each claim the page makes.
~~~

---

## What you write {#what-you-write}

| Piece | For `Result` | What it does |
|---|---|---|
| A witness, inside a `Kind` interface | `ResultKind.Witness` | Names the container, so code generic over `F` can mention it |
| The type itself | `Result`, which extends `ResultKind` | Is its own `Kind`, so `widen` allocates nothing |
| A helper | `ResultKindHelper.RESULT` | Moves a `Result` into the encoding with `widen`, and back with `narrow` |
| An instance | `ResultMonad.INSTANCE` | Gives `of`, `map`, `ap` and `flatMap` for `Result` |

[The pieces each type provides](core-concepts.md#the-pieces-each-type-provides) shows the same pieces for the library's own types. Most of those also have a token in `Witnesses`, for `Instances` to look up. Those tokens can only be built inside the library, so your code passes `ResultMonad.INSTANCE` wherever a `Monad` is asked for.

## Your own type: `Result` {#your-own-type}

`Result` extends its own `Kind` interface, which declares the witness:

```java
/** The Kind view of a {@link Result}: a Result whose container is named by {@link Witness}. */
public interface ResultKind<A> extends Kind<ResultKind.Witness, A> {

  /** Names the Result container. Nothing instantiates it; it appears only as a type argument. */
  final class Witness implements WitnessArity<TypeArity.Unary> {
    private Witness() {}
  }
}
```

`Result` itself is a sealed interface with two records:

```java
public sealed interface Result<A> extends ResultKind<A> {

  record Ok<A>(A value) implements Result<A> {
    public Ok {
      Objects.requireNonNull(value, "value");
    }
  }

  record Failed<A>(String reason) implements Result<A> {
    public Failed {
      Objects.requireNonNull(reason, "reason");
    }
  }

  static <A> Result<A> ok(A value) {
    return new Ok<>(value);
  }

  static <A> Result<A> failed(String reason) {
    return new Failed<>(reason);
  }
}
```

A `Result` is a `ResultKind`, so it is already a `Kind<ResultKind.Witness, A>`. The helper's `widen` therefore returns its argument, and `narrow` needs only a type pattern:

```java
public enum ResultKindHelper {
  RESULT;

  // A Result is already its own Kind, so widen returns the same object
  public <A> Kind<ResultKind.Witness, A> widen(Result<A> result) {
    return Objects.requireNonNull(result, "result");
  }

  public <A> Result<A> narrow(Kind<ResultKind.Witness, A> kind) {
    return switch (kind) {
      case null -> throw new KindUnwrapException("Cannot narrow a null Kind to a Result");
      case Result<A> result -> result;
      default -> throw new KindUnwrapException("Not a Result: " + kind.getClass().getName());
    };
  }
}
```

`narrow` throws `KindUnwrapException` for `null`, and for any other class that claims the witness, as the library's own helpers do. [Type Arity](type-arity.md) explains the witness's `WitnessArity<TypeArity.Unary>` bound.

If your type carries its error type as a parameter, as a `Result<E, A>` would, the witness takes it too, as `EitherKind.Witness<L>` does. The instance then becomes a class with a generic `instance()` method, as `EitherMonad` has, since an enum cannot take a type parameter.

## The instance {#the-instance}

A `Monad` asks for four methods: `of`, `map`, `ap` and `flatMap`. `map` and `flatMap` narrow their argument and switch over the two records, `of` widens an `Ok`, and `ap` is built from `flatMap` and `map`:

```java
public enum ResultMonad implements Monad<ResultKind.Witness> {
  INSTANCE;

  @Override
  public <A> Kind<ResultKind.Witness, A> of(A value) {
    return RESULT.widen(Result.ok(value));
  }

  @Override
  public <A, B> Kind<ResultKind.Witness, B> map(
      Function<? super A, ? extends B> f, Kind<ResultKind.Witness, A> fa) {
    return RESULT.widen(
        switch (RESULT.narrow(fa)) {
          case Result.Ok<A>(var value) -> Result.ok(f.apply(value));
          case Result.Failed<A>(var reason) -> Result.failed(reason);
        });
  }

  @Override
  public <A, B> Kind<ResultKind.Witness, B> flatMap(
      Function<? super A, ? extends Kind<ResultKind.Witness, B>> f,
      Kind<ResultKind.Witness, A> ma) {
    return switch (RESULT.narrow(ma)) {
      // narrow refuses a step that returned null, as the library's own instances do
      case Result.Ok<A>(var value) -> RESULT.widen(RESULT.narrow(f.apply(value)));
      case Result.Failed<A>(var reason) -> RESULT.widen(Result.failed(reason));
    };
  }

  @Override
  public <A, B> Kind<ResultKind.Witness, B> ap(
      Kind<ResultKind.Witness, ? extends Function<A, B>> ff, Kind<ResultKind.Witness, A> fa) {
    Objects.requireNonNull(fa, "fa");
    return flatMap(f -> map(f, fa), ff);
  }
}
```

A `Failed` passes through `map` and `flatMap` untouched, so a chain stops at its first failure, as an `Either` chain does. `ap` stops at the first failure too: when both arguments have failed, the function's reason wins. The instance holds no state, so one shared instance is enough, and a single-element `enum` gives you that.

The instance never builds or passes on `null`. `Ok` and `Failed` refuse it, so `of(null)` throws, and `flatMap` narrows what a step returns, so a step that returns `null` throws too. [Values, never null](core-concepts.md#values-never-null) gives the rule.

## Prove your instance {#prove-your-instance}

No compiler checks how `map` and `flatMap` behave. A `map` that turned an `Ok` into a `Failed` would compile, and so would a `flatMap` that lost a `Failed`'s reason. The laws say what callers rely on, and `hkj-test`'s law checkers turn them into assertions:

```java
  static Stream<Kind<ResultKind.Witness, Order>> results() {
    return Stream.of(
        RESULT.widen(Result.ok(ORDER)), RESULT.widen(Result.failed("order not found")));
  }

  @ParameterizedTest
  @MethodSource("results")
  @DisplayName("Result's helper and Monad keep their laws, on an Ok and on a Failed")
  void resultKeepsItsLaws(Kind<ResultKind.Witness, Order> fa) {
    Monad<ResultKind.Witness> monad = ResultMonad.INSTANCE;
    var same = KindEquivalence.<ResultKind.Witness>byEqualsAfter(RESULT::narrow);
    Function<Order, Kind<ResultKind.Witness, Customer>> findCustomer =
        order -> RESULT.widen(Result.ok(order.customer()));
    Function<Customer, Kind<ResultKind.Witness, EmailAddress>> verifyEmail =
        customer -> RESULT.widen(Result.failed("email not verified"));

    KindHelperLaws.assertRoundTrip(RESULT.narrow(fa), RESULT::widen, RESULT::narrow);
    FunctorLaws.assertIdentity(monad, fa, same);
    FunctorLaws.assertComposition(monad, fa, Order::customer, Customer::email, same);
    MonadLaws.assertLeftIdentity(monad, ORDER, findCustomer, same);
    MonadLaws.assertLeftIdentity(monad, ORDER.customer(), verifyEmail, same);
    MonadLaws.assertRightIdentity(monad, fa, same);
    MonadLaws.assertAssociativity(monad, fa, findCustomer, verifyEmail, same);
  }

```

- **`KindHelperLaws.assertRoundTrip`** checks that `narrow(widen(r))` is the very `r` you started with.
- **`FunctorLaws`** checks identity, that mapping with `x -> x` changes nothing, and composition, that two maps equal one map of the composed function. [Functor's laws](../functional/functor.md#two-laws) states them.
- **`MonadLaws`** checks left identity, right identity and associativity, as [Monad's laws](../functional/monad.md#three-laws) states them.
- **`KindEquivalence.byEqualsAfter(RESULT::narrow)`** says when two `Kind`s count as the same: narrow both, then compare with `equals`, which a record already has.

Every law that takes a `Result` runs on an `Ok` and on a `Failed`, since each record has its own branch. `verifyEmail` always fails, so left identity is checked with a failing step too. `ApplicativeLaws` checks `ap` the same way, in the same test file. A law checker for `MonadError` is not supported yet.

The law checkers ship in [`hkj-test`](../tooling/test_assertions.md#adding-the-dependency). `ORDER` is any `Order`, and the test uses these imports, with JUnit's:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import static org.higherkindedj.example.book.foundations.owntype.ResultKindHelper.RESULT;

import java.util.function.Function;
import java.util.stream.Stream;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.foundations.owntype.Result;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.foundations.owntype.ResultKind;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.foundations.owntype.ResultMonad;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.Customer;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.EmailAddress;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.Order;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Kind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Monad;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.assertions.KindEquivalence;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.laws.FunctorLaws;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.laws.KindHelperLaws;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.laws.MonadLaws;
```

## Use it with generic code {#use-it-with-generic-code}

`ResultMonad.INSTANCE` is a `Monad<ResultKind.Witness>`, so code written for any container takes it. The chapter introduction's `totals`, and a `For` comprehension, both run on a `Result`:

```java
    Result<BigDecimal> total =
        RESULT.narrow(totals(ResultMonad.INSTANCE, RESULT.widen(Result.ok(order))));
    // Ok[value=40.00]
    Result<String> receipt =
        RESULT.narrow(
            For.from(ResultMonad.INSTANCE, RESULT.widen(Result.ok(order)))
                .from(placed -> RESULT.widen(verified(placed.customer().email())))
                .yield((placed, email) -> "order " + placed.id() + " to " + email));
    // Ok[value=order 00000000-0000-0000-0000-000000000001 to ada@example.com]
```

Neither knows about `Result`; the instance does. A `Failed` order would give a `Failed` from both, with its reason.

## A type you cannot change {#a-type-you-cannot-change}

The JDK's `Set` cannot extend your `Kind` interface, so the helper wraps it in a holder record that does:

```java
public interface SetKind<A> extends Kind<SetKind.Witness, A> {

  final class Witness implements WitnessArity<TypeArity.Unary> {
    private Witness() {}
  }
}

public enum SetKindHelper {
  SET;

  // The holder is the Kind; the Set inside it is untouched
  private record SetHolder<A>(Set<A> set) implements SetKind<A> {
    SetHolder {
      Objects.requireNonNull(set, "set");
    }
  }

  public <A> Kind<SetKind.Witness, A> widen(Set<A> set) {
    return new SetHolder<>(set);
  }

  public <A> Set<A> narrow(Kind<SetKind.Witness, A> kind) {
    return switch (kind) {
      case null -> throw new KindUnwrapException("Cannot narrow a null Kind to a Set");
      case SetHolder<A>(var set) -> set;
      default -> throw new KindUnwrapException("Not a Set: " + kind.getClass().getName());
    };
  }
}
```

`widen` now allocates a holder, and `narrow` reads the `Set` back out with a record pattern. The round-trip law still holds, because `narrow` returns the very `Set` that went in. The holder shares that `Set` with the caller rather than copying it, so widen an unmodifiable one, such as `Set.of` or `Set.copyOf` gives. [Lifting the Hood](lifting_the_hood.md) counts what a holder costs. Write an instance for `Set` as `ResultMonad` is written, through `SET.narrow` and `SET.widen`.

## Your type gets a Path {#your-type-gets-a-path}

Once the instance exists, `GenericPath.of` turns a `Kind` of your type into a Path. Application code can then use `map` and `via`, with no `widen` or `narrow` between the steps:

```java
  // A Result as a Path, with Result's Monad behind it
  public static <A> GenericPath<ResultKind.Witness, A> path(Result<A> result) {
    return GenericPath.of(RESULT.widen(result), ResultMonad.INSTANCE);
  }

  // An email at example.com is verified; any other fails
  public static Result<String> verified(EmailAddress email) {
    return email.value().endsWith("@example.com")
        ? Result.ok(email.value())
        : Result.failed("email not verified");
  }

```

```java
    Result<String> email =
        RESULT.narrow(
            path(Result.ok(order))
                .map(Order::customer)
                .via(customer -> path(verified(customer.email())))
                .runKind());
    // Ok[value=ada@example.com]
    Result<String> missing =
        RESULT.narrow(
            path(Result.<Order>failed("order not found"))
                .map(Order::customer)
                .via(customer -> path(verified(customer.email())))
                .runKind());
    // Failed[reason=order not found]
```

`via` takes a step that returns another Path, here built from the `Result` that `verified` returns. `runKind` hands back the `Kind`, and `RESULT.narrow` the `Result`. The Path's `recover` and `recoverWith` need a `MonadError`: built from a `Monad` alone, as here, they throw `UnsupportedOperationException`. Give `GenericPath.of` a `MonadError` instance, and they work. [Generic Paths](../effect/path_generic.md) covers the rest. [Recovery with `errorType`](../effect/path_source.md#recovery-with-errortype) shows a `MonadError` for a type shaped like `Result`, and how `@PathSource` generates a named Path for it, with no `runKind` or `narrow`.

---

**Previous:** [Lifting the Hood](lifting_the_hood.md)
**Next:** [Type Classes](../functional/ch_intro.md)

<!-- Source: functional/ch_intro.md -->

<!-- description: The type classes beyond Functor, Applicative, Monad and MonadError, with one hierarchy read from the library itself. -->

# Type Classes

<!-- source: Alfred North Whitehead, An Introduction to Mathematics (1911), Chapter V, "The Symbolism of Mathematics", p. 61; checked against Project Gutenberg eBook #41568 -->
> _"Civilization advances by extending the number of important operations which we can perform without thinking about them."_
>
> — Alfred North Whitehead, *An Introduction to Mathematics* (Chapter V)

---

This group adds operations you can perform on a container without thinking about which one it is: `foldMap`, `traverse`, `orElse`, `select`. Each needs the container to have an instance of the type class that declares it, and [the hierarchy](#the-hierarchy) shows which class that is.

The Ship pages in the [Chapter Contents](../hkts/foundations_intro.md#chapter-contents) teach the four type classes most code needs: `Functor`, `Applicative`, `Monad` and `MonadError`. The rest are here, with the `For` comprehensions that build on them. Read one when a task calls for it, such as folding a list into one value, validating every element with `traverse`, falling back with `orElse`, or mapping both sides of an `Either`. Until then you can skip the group.

---

## The hierarchy {#the-hierarchy}

```mermaid
flowchart BT
    accTitle: Which type class extends which
    accDescr: Functor and Foldable extend no other type class. Applicative extends Functor. Traverse extends Functor and Foldable. Alternative extends Applicative. Selective extends Applicative. Monad extends Applicative. MonadError extends Monad. MonadZero extends Monad and Alternative. Each box also lists the abstract methods its type class declares.
    Functor["Functor<br/><small>map</small>"]
    Foldable["Foldable<br/><small>foldMap</small>"]
    Applicative["Applicative<br/><small>of, ap</small>"]
    Traverse["Traverse<br/><small>traverse</small>"]
    Alternative["Alternative<br/><small>empty, orElse</small>"]
    Selective["Selective<br/><small>select</small>"]
    Monad["Monad<br/><small>flatMap</small>"]
    MonadError["MonadError<br/><small>raiseError, handleErrorWith</small>"]
    MonadZero["MonadZero<br/><small>zero</small>"]
    Applicative --> Functor
    Traverse --> Functor
    Traverse --> Foldable
    Alternative --> Applicative
    Selective --> Applicative
    Monad --> Applicative
    MonadError --> Monad
    MonadZero --> Monad
    MonadZero --> Alternative
    classDef step fill:#8caaee,stroke:#1e66f5,color:#232634
    class Functor,Foldable,Applicative,Traverse,Alternative,Selective,Monad,MonadError,MonadZero step
```

Each arrow means *extends*, and each box lists the abstract methods its type class declares. A test in the examples module reads the boxes and arrows from `hkj-api` itself, so the diagram cannot show an `extends` the library does not have. An instance of a type class is an instance of every class it extends, so a `Monad<F>` can go wherever code asks for a `Functor<F>`.

`MonadReader`, `MonadState` and `MonadWriter` extend `Monad` too, and [MTL Capabilities](../transformers/mtl_capabilities.md) covers them. `Bifunctor` and `Profunctor` take a binary witness, which leaves two type arguments open, as [Type Arity](../hkts/type-arity.md) explains, and extend no other type class. `Semigroup` and `Monoid` take a plain type rather than a witness, and `Monoid` extends `Semigroup`.

---

## Pages in this group {#pages-in-this-group}

1. [Semigroup and Monoid](semigroup_and_monoid.md): Combining two values of one type, and the empty value a combination starts from
2. [Foldable and Traverse](foldable_and_traverse.md): Reducing a container to one value, and running a step on every element to get one result holding them all
3. [Natural Transformation](natural_transformation.md): Moving values from one container type to another, such as a `Maybe` to a `List`
4. [Selective](selective.md): Branching on an earlier result, with both branches given up front
5. [Alternative](alternative.md): `empty` and `orElse`, which `Maybe` reads as a fallback and `List` as both lists joined
6. [MonadZero](monad_zero.md): A `Monad` with an empty value, which lets a `For` comprehension filter
7. [Applicative, Selective or Monad?](abstraction_levels.md): Deciding by whether a step needs an earlier result
8. [Bifunctor](bifunctor.md): Mapping either or both type arguments of a type such as `Either` or `Tuple2`
9. [Profunctor](profunctor.md): Adapting what a function takes and what it returns
10. [For with Optics](for_optics.md): Lenses, prisms and isos as steps in a `For` comprehension, and edits over a traversal
11. [For with MTL Capabilities](for_mtl.md): `MonadReader`, `MonadState` and `MonadWriter` operations as steps in a `For` comprehension
12. [ForState Comprehension](forstate_comprehension.md): A comprehension that keeps its values in a named record rather than a tuple
