<!-- description: Combine values with Semigroup, start from a Monoid's empty value, total money exactly with bigDecimalAddition, and build your own monoid with Monoid.of. -->

# Semigroup and Monoid: Foundational Type Classes

_Combine two values of one type, and start from an empty value to total any number of them._

~~~admonish info title="What You'll Learn"
- Combine two values of one type with a `Semigroup`, and say what associativity promises
- Start a combination from a `Monoid`'s empty value, so an empty list totals to zero
- Total money exactly with `Monoids.bigDecimalAddition()`, and build a monoid the library lacks with `Monoid.of`
- Pick a ready-made monoid for numbers, strings, collections, optional values and updates
- Check a monoid against its three laws
~~~

~~~admonish example title="See Example Code"
**The code on this page is [MonoidBook.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/monoid/MonoidBook.java)**: the page includes it, so the build compiles and runs it. [MonoidBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/monoid/MonoidBookTest.java) holds what the page says, and [FoundationsLawsBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/FoundationsLawsBookTest.java) checks the laws.
~~~

---

## Combining two values {#combining-two-values}

A `Semigroup<A>` has one method, `combine`, which takes two values of type `A` and returns one. Unlike the type classes on the pages around this one, it works on plain values, not on a container `Kind<F, A>`: two lists of errors become one list, and two strings become one string.

The code on this page uses these imports, with `LineItem` from the order records the [Optics](../optics/ch_intro.md) chapter introduces:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import java.math.BigDecimal;
import java.util.Comparator;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.Map;
import java.util.Optional;
import java.util.TreeMap;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.LineItem;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Monoid;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Monoids;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Semigroup;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Semigroups;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Update;
```

```java
    Semigroup<List<String>> errors = Semigroups.list();
    List<String> both =
        errors.combine(List.of("SKU is blank"), List.of("quantity must be positive"));
    // [SKU is blank, quantity must be positive]

    Semigroup<String> joined = Semigroups.string("; ");
    String message = joined.combine("SKU is blank", "quantity must be positive");
    // the message reads "SKU is blank; quantity must be positive"
```

`combine` must be associative: `combine(combine(a, b), c)` equals `combine(a, combine(b, c))`. So a list of values combines to the same result however you group the calls, and a library can split a long list and combine the halves.

The `Semigroups` interface holds the common ones:

| Factory | Combines two values by |
|---|---|
| `Semigroups.list()` | Joining two lists, the first's elements first |
| `Semigroups.set()` | Taking the union of two sets |
| `Semigroups.string()` | Joining two strings |
| `Semigroups.string(delimiter)` | Joining two strings with `delimiter` between them |
| `Semigroups.first()` | Keeping the first value |
| `Semigroups.last()` | Keeping the last value |

The library's main use for one is [`Validated`](../monads/validated_monad.md)'s error accumulation. `Instances.validated(Semigroups.list())` gives an `Applicative` whose `map2` joins the errors of every invalid input with the semigroup, as [Applicative](applicative.md#what-each-instance-does) shows.

~~~admonish tip title="The non-empty alternative: `NonEmptyList.semigroup()`"
For errors, a [`NonEmptyList`](../monads/nonemptylist_monad.md) says in its type what an invalid result promises: at least one error, so its `head` and `reduce` need no empty case. `NonEmptyList.semigroup()` joins two of them, left to right. It lives on the type rather than in `Semigroups`, because `NonEmptyList` is in `hkj-core` and `Semigroups` in `hkj-api`. `Path.validNel` and `Path.invalidNel` build it in, so the common validation needs no `Semigroup` argument.
~~~

---

## Starting from an empty value {#starting-from-an-empty-value}

A `Monoid<A>` is a `Semigroup<A>` with one more method, `empty`, which returns a value that changes nothing when combined with another. Zero is addition's empty value, and an empty list is joining's. With it, `combineAll`, a default method on every `Monoid`, can total any number of values, none included, since a total of nothing is the empty value.

If you have called `stream.reduce(identity, accumulator)`, you have used a monoid: `reduce` asks for an identity and an associative accumulator, and `Monoid` names that pair.

The examples total an order's lines:

```java
  /** An order's lines: one lamp at 40.00, and four bulbs at 2.50 each. */
  public static final List<LineItem> LINES =
      List.of(
          new LineItem("LAMP", 1, new BigDecimal("40.00")),
          new LineItem("BULB", 4, new BigDecimal("2.50")));

  /** A later order's lines: three more bulbs. */
  public static final List<LineItem> REFILL =
      List.of(new LineItem("BULB", 3, new BigDecimal("2.50")));

  /** What a line costs: its price times its quantity. */
  public static BigDecimal lineTotal(LineItem line) {
    return line.price().multiply(BigDecimal.valueOf(line.quantity()));
  }

```

An order's total is the monoid for `BigDecimal` addition, run over each line's total:

```java
    Monoid<BigDecimal> money = Monoids.bigDecimalAddition();
    BigDecimal total = money.combineAll(LINES.stream().map(MonoidBook::lineTotal).toList());
    // 50.00

    BigDecimal noLines = money.combineAll(List.of());
    // 0
```

On an [Effect Path](../effect/ch_intro.md), `ListPath.foldMap` takes the monoid and the function together, and [Foldable and Traverse](foldable_and_traverse.md#foldable-one-structure-many-summaries) does the same for any container.

Money is `BigDecimal`, as `LineItem.price` is, since `Monoids.doubleAddition()` rounds, as [Things people get wrong](#things-people-get-wrong) shows. A `BigDecimal` sum keeps the larger scale of its two values, so prices with two decimal places total to a value with two.

### Ready-made monoids {#ready-made-monoids}

| Factory | Combines by | Empty value |
|---|---|---|
| `integerAddition()`, `longAddition()` | `+` | `0` |
| `integerMultiplication()`, `longMultiplication()` | `*` | `1` |
| `doubleAddition()`, `doubleMultiplication()` | `+`, `*`, which round, so regrouping can change the result, as [Things people get wrong](#things-people-get-wrong) shows | `0.0`, `1.0` |
| `bigDecimalAddition()` | `BigDecimal.add` | `BigDecimal.ZERO` |
| `booleanAnd()`, `booleanOr()` | `&&`, `\|\|` | `true`, `false` |
| `string()` | Joining two strings | `""` |
| `list()`, `set()` | Joining two lists, or the union of two sets | An empty list, or an empty set |
| `firstOptional()`, `lastOptional()` | Keeping the first, or the last, present value | `Optional.empty()` |
| `maximum()`, `minimum()`, with or without a `Comparator` | Keeping the greater, or the lesser, present value | `Optional.empty()` |
| `update()` | Applying one `Update` and then the other | `Update.identity()` |

Each is a static method on `Monoids`, such as `Monoids.list()`. [Quick Reference](../hkts/quick_reference.md#ready-made-semigroups-and-monoids) lists every one, and every `Semigroups` factory too.

### A monoid of your own {#a-monoid-of-your-own}

`Monoid.of(empty, combine)` builds one from its empty value and a `Semigroup`, which can be a lambda. Counting the units of each SKU across orders needs a monoid `Monoids` does not have: an empty map, and a merge that adds the counts.

```java
  // Units per SKU, which no Monoids factory gives: an empty map, and a merge adding the counts
  public static final Monoid<Map<String, Integer>> UNITS =
      Monoid.of(
          Map.of(),
          (left, right) -> {
            Map<String, Integer> sum = new TreeMap<>(left);
            right.forEach((sku, units) -> sum.merge(sku, units, Integer::sum));
            return Collections.unmodifiableMap(sum);
          });

  static Map<String, Integer> units(List<LineItem> lines) {
    return UNITS.combineAll(
        lines.stream().map(line -> Map.of(line.sku(), line.quantity())).toList());
  }

```

```java
    Map<String, Integer> stock = UNITS.combine(units(LINES), units(REFILL));
    // a map of BULB to 7 and LAMP to 1
```

`Monoid.of` does not check the laws, so check them as [Three laws](#three-laws) does.

---

## What every monoid can do {#what-every-monoid-can-do}

Besides `combineAll`, every `Monoid` has two more default methods. `combineN(value, n)` combines a value with itself `n` times: `n` of `0` gives the empty value, and a negative `n` throws `IllegalArgumentException`. `isEmpty` tests whether a value is the empty value.

```java
    BigDecimal sixLamps = money.combineN(new BigDecimal("40.00"), 6);
    // 240.00

    boolean nothingOwed = money.isEmpty(new BigDecimal("0.00"));
    // true
```

---

## Choosing among optional values {#choosing-among-optional-values}

The optional monoids pick one present value from many. `firstOptional` and `lastOptional` pick by position and compare nothing, so `firstOptional` suits a fallback chain: a setting from the environment, then one from a file, then a default. `maximum` and `minimum` compare the values, by the `Comparator` you pass or by the type's natural order.

```java
    Monoid<Optional<LineItem>> priciest = Monoids.maximum(Comparator.comparing(LineItem::price));
    Optional<LineItem> dearest = priciest.combineAll(LINES.stream().map(Optional::of).toList());
    // Optional[LineItem[sku=LAMP, quantity=1, price=40.00]]

    Optional<LineItem> none = priciest.combineAll(List.of());
    // an empty Optional, the monoid's empty value
```

Each skips an empty `Optional`, and a list of only empty ones, or of none, gives `Optional.empty()`.

---

## Composing updates {#composing-updates}

Every monoid so far combines data. `Monoids.update()` combines behaviour. An `Update<S>` is a transformation from an `S` to a new `S`. It extends `UnaryOperator<S>`, so it goes wherever Java takes a `Function<S, S>`, such as `Stream.map`, and its `andThen` returns an `Update`, so a chain of them stays one. The monoid's `combine(f, g)` applies `f` and then `g`, and its empty value is `Update.identity()`, which returns its input unchanged.

```java
    Update<LineItem> restock = line -> new LineItem(line.sku(), line.quantity() + 10, line.price());
    Update<LineItem> markDown =
        line ->
            new LineItem(
                line.sku(), line.quantity(), line.price().subtract(new BigDecimal("5.00")));

    Monoid<Update<LineItem>> steps = Monoids.update();
    Update<LineItem> restockThenMarkDown = steps.combineAll(List.of(restock, markDown));
    LineItem lamp = restockThenMarkDown.apply(LINES.getFirst());
    // LineItem[sku=LAMP, quantity=11, price=35.00]
```

A step with nothing to change can be `Update.identity()`. That is what makes sparse updates work: the [`Edits` builder](../optics/multi_edit.md) folds optic edits through `Monoids.update()`, and a field the request left out contributes `Update.identity()`. Fold updates with `combineAll`, which applies them in a loop. A fold through `combine`, such as `foldMap`, nests one call per update, and a long enough list overflows the stack.

~~~admonish note title="For the functionally curious"
In functional programming this is the `Endo` monoid, for *endomorphism*, a function from a type to itself. Higher-Kinded-J names it `Update` for clarity.
~~~

---

## Three laws {#three-laws}

A `Semigroup` keeps one law and a `Monoid` two more. Together they say that grouping does not matter, and that the empty value adds nothing.

~~~admonish note title="Laws"
- **Associativity: grouping the combines differently changes nothing.** `combine(combine(a, b), c)` equals `combine(a, combine(b, c))`.
- **Left identity: combining the empty value first changes nothing.** `combine(empty(), a)` equals `a`.
- **Right identity: combining the empty value last changes nothing.** `combine(a, empty())` equals `a`.

Values are equal by `equals`. For `bigDecimalAddition`, the identity laws hold by `equals` for a value of scale zero or more, which includes any price. Added to `ZERO`, `new BigDecimal("1E+3")` comes back as `1000`: equal by `compareTo`, but not by `equals`.

Checking the `Semigroup` and `Monoid` laws with `hkj-test` is not supported yet, so the test checks them by hand, for `bigDecimalAddition` and for the units monoid. It uses these imports, with JUnit's:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import static org.assertj.core.api.Assertions.assertThat;

import java.math.BigDecimal;
import java.util.Map;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.foundations.monoid.MonoidBook;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Monoid;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Monoids;
```

```java
  <A> void assertMonoidLaws(Monoid<A> monoid, A a, A b, A c) {
    // combining with empty, on either side, changes nothing
    assertThat(monoid.combine(monoid.empty(), a)).isEqualTo(a);
    assertThat(monoid.combine(a, monoid.empty())).isEqualTo(a);
    // grouping the combines differently changes nothing
    assertThat(monoid.combine(monoid.combine(a, b), c))
        .isEqualTo(monoid.combine(a, monoid.combine(b, c)));
  }

  @Test
  @DisplayName("BigDecimal addition and the page's units monoid keep the monoid laws")
  void monoidLaws() {
    assertMonoidLaws(
        Monoids.bigDecimalAddition(),
        new BigDecimal("40.00"),
        new BigDecimal("10.00"),
        new BigDecimal("7.50"));
    assertMonoidLaws(
        MonoidBook.UNITS, Map.of("LAMP", 1), Map.of("BULB", 4), Map.of("BULB", 3, "DESK", 1));
  }

```
~~~

---

## Things people get wrong {#things-people-get-wrong}

~~~admonish warning title="Common Misunderstandings"
- **"`doubleAddition` will do for money."** It rounds: `0.1` and `0.2` give `0.30000000000000004`, where `bigDecimalAddition` sums `0.10` and `0.20` to exactly `0.30`.
- **"Every monoid in `Monoids` keeps the laws."** The `double` ones do not, since each `+` or `*` rounds. Combining `1e16`, `-1e16` and `1` gives `1.0` grouped as `(1e16 + -1e16) + 1`, and `0.0` grouped as `1e16 + (-1e16 + 1)`. [Laws and Where HKJ Bends Them](../hkts/laws.md) lists every instance that bends a law.
- **"`combine` must be commutative."** It must be associative, and that is all. `Monoids.string()` combines `"LAMP"` and `"BULB"` into `"LAMPBULB"`, and the other way round into `"BULBLAMP"`.
- **"Any semigroup becomes a monoid once you name an empty value."** Only if that value changes nothing. The `combineAll` of `Monoid.of("", Semigroups.string(", "))` turns `"LAMP"` and `"BULB"` into `", LAMP, BULB"`: it starts from `""`, and joining `""` adds a delimiter.
- **"`isEmpty` always compares with `equals`."** `bigDecimalAddition`'s is true for zero at any scale, so `0.00` is empty, although `BigDecimal.ZERO.equals(new BigDecimal("0.00"))` is false.
~~~

---

## The fine print {#the-fine-print}

### What takes a Semigroup or a Monoid {#what-takes-a-semigroup-or-a-monoid}

- **`Instances.eitherOrBoth(semigroup)` joins the warnings each step carries.** [EitherOrBoth](../monads/either_or_both_monad.md) shows how.
- **`Instances.writer(monoid)` combines each step's log, starting from the empty log.** [Writer](../monads/writer_monad.md) shows how.
- **`Foldable.foldMap(monoid, f, fa)` turns each element into a monoid value and combines them.** [Foldable and Traverse](foldable_and_traverse.md) shows how.
- **`Const`'s `Applicative` takes a monoid to combine what it collects.** [Const](../monads/const_type.md) shows how.

---

~~~admonish tip title="See Also"
- [Foldable and Traverse](foldable_and_traverse.md): folding any container with a monoid
- [Applicative](applicative.md): how `Validated` combines errors with a semigroup
- [Multi-Edit and Sparse Updates](../optics/multi_edit.md): the `Edits` builder that `Monoids.update()` powers
~~~

<!-- description: Fold any container to one value with foldMap and a monoid, and check every element with traverse, where the instance decides what a failure does. -->

# Foldable & Traverse: Reducing and Reshaping Structures

_Fold a container into one value, or run a check on each element and get one result._

<!-- source: Alfred, Lord Tennyson, "Ulysses", in Poems (1842), line 18; text checked against Project Gutenberg eBook #8601, The Early Poems of Alfred Lord Tennyson, ed. John Churton Collins -->
> _"I am a part of all that I have met;"_
>
> — Alfred, Lord Tennyson, *Ulysses*

A fold's result is a part of every element it met: `foldMap` turns an order's line items into one total.

~~~admonish info title="What You'll Learn"
- Fold any container to one value with `foldMap` and a [monoid](semigroup_and_monoid.md)
- Get a total, a check and a list from the same fold, by choosing the monoid and the function that feeds it
- Run a check on every element with `traverse`, and get one result for the whole container
- Predict whether a traversal keeps the first failure or reports them all, from the instance you pass
- Say when `sequenceA` fits, and check a `Traverse` against its identity law
~~~

~~~admonish example title="See Example Code"
**The code on this page is [FoldableBook.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/foldable/FoldableBook.java)**: the page includes it, so the build compiles and runs it. [FoldableBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/foldable/FoldableBookTest.java) holds what the page says each instance does, and [FoundationsLawsBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/FoundationsLawsBookTest.java) checks the law.
~~~

---

## Foldable: one structure, many summaries {#foldable-one-structure-many-summaries}

Summing a list, joining strings and checking that every element passes a test are one walk with three answers. Each answer is a [monoid](semigroup_and_monoid.md): a way to combine two values, and an empty value to start from. `Foldable` names the walk, and its one method, `foldMap(monoid, f, fa)`, turns each element of `fa` into a monoid value with `f` and combines them. For a `List`, it is `stream().map(f).reduce(monoid.empty(), monoid::combine)`.

The code on this page uses these imports, with `LineItem` from the order records the [Optics](../optics/ch_intro.md) chapter introduces. `LINES` and `lineTotal` come from [Semigroup and Monoid](semigroup_and_monoid.md#starting-from-an-empty-value): `LINES` holds a lamp at 40.00 and four bulbs at 2.50, and `lineTotal` multiplies a line's price by its quantity.

<!-- verify -->
```java
import static org.higherkindedj.example.book.foundations.monoid.MonoidBook.LINES;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.either.EitherKindHelper.EITHER;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.either;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.maybe;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.list.ListKindHelper.LIST;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.maybe.MaybeKindHelper.MAYBE;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.validated.ValidatedKindHelper.VALIDATED;

import java.math.BigDecimal;
import java.util.List;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.foundations.monoid.MonoidBook;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.LineItem;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Applicative;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Foldable;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Kind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Monoids;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Semigroups;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.TypeArity;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.WitnessArity;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.effect.ListPath;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.either.Either;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.either.EitherKind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Instances;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.list.ListKind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.list.ListTraverse;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.maybe.Maybe;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.maybe.MaybeKind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.maybe.MaybeTraverse;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.validated.Validated;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.validated.ValidatedKind;
```

On an [Effect Path](../effect/ch_intro.md), `ListPath`, `NonDetPath` and `StreamPath` have `foldMap` themselves, and application code stays there:

```java
    BigDecimal pathTotal =
        ListPath.of(LINES).foldMap(Monoids.bigDecimalAddition(), MonoidBook::lineTotal);
    // 50.00
```

This page is for a method written once for any container.

### Same data, three summaries {#same-data-three-summaries}

```java
    Foldable<ListKind.Witness> foldable = ListTraverse.INSTANCE;
    Kind<ListKind.Witness, LineItem> lines = LIST.widen(LINES);

    BigDecimal sum = foldable.foldMap(Monoids.bigDecimalAddition(), MonoidBook::lineTotal, lines);
    // 50.00

    Boolean everyQuantityPositive =
        foldable.foldMap(Monoids.booleanAnd(), line -> line.quantity() > 0, lines);
    // true

    List<String> skus = foldable.foldMap(Monoids.list(), line -> List.of(line.sku()), lines);
    // [LAMP, BULB]
```

The three calls differ in the monoid and in the function that feeds it: a line's total for the sum, a test for the check, and a one-element list for the SKUs. `ListTraverse.INSTANCE` is `List`'s `Foldable`, and `Traverse` extends `Foldable`, so every type with a `Traverse` can be folded.

### One method for any container {#one-method-for-any-container}

`total` is written over `Foldable`: it takes the `Foldable` for `F`, and calls `foldMap` with the monoid for `BigDecimal` addition.

```java
  // One method for any container F: the Foldable passed in walks F, and the monoid adds up
  public static <F extends WitnessArity<TypeArity.Unary>> BigDecimal total(
      Foldable<F> foldable, Kind<F, LineItem> lines) {
    return foldable.foldMap(Monoids.bigDecimalAddition(), MonoidBook::lineTotal, lines);
  }

```

Run on a `List` and on a `Maybe`, it gives:

```java
    BigDecimal orderTotal = total(ListTraverse.INSTANCE, LIST.widen(LINES));
    // 50.00

    BigDecimal oneLine = total(MaybeTraverse.INSTANCE, MAYBE.just(LINES.getFirst()));
    // 40.00

    BigDecimal noLine = total(MaybeTraverse.INSTANCE, MAYBE.nothing());
    // 0
```

A `Maybe` holds at most one element, so its total is that line's, and an empty `Maybe` totals to the monoid's empty value.

---

## Traverse: effectful iteration {#traverse-effectful-iteration}

`Traverse` extends `Foldable` and `Functor` with `traverse(applicative, f, ta)`. It runs `f` on every element of `ta`, where each call returns a value in a second container, such as a `Validated`, which this page calls an effect. It combines the results with the `Applicative` you pass, into one effect holding a container of every value: a `Kind<T, A>` and a function `A -> Kind<G, B>` give a `Kind<G, Kind<T, B>>`. Once `f` has run on each element, the list holds `Validated`s, and `traverse` turns it inside out:

<pre class="hkj-ascii-diagram" role="img" aria-label="traverse turns a list of Validated values into one Validated holding a list. A list of Valid(1), Valid(2) and Valid(3) becomes Valid of the list 1, 2, 3. A list of Valid(1), Invalid(e1) and Valid(3) becomes Invalid(e1).">
   Before: List&lt; Validated&lt;E, A&gt; &gt;
           ┌──────────┬──────────┬──────────┐
           │ Valid(1) │ Valid(2) │ Valid(3) │
           └──────────┴──────────┴──────────┘

   After:  Validated&lt; E, List&lt;A&gt; &gt;
           Valid( [1, 2, 3] )

   With an error:
           ┌──────────┬─────────────┬──────────┐
           │ Valid(1) │ Invalid(e1) │ Valid(3) │
           └──────────┴─────────────┴──────────┘

   becomes:
           Invalid( e1 )
</pre>

Validating every entry in a form, fetching a record for every id or parsing every line of a file all want this. Written by hand, each needs a loop, a list to collect errors and a flag for whether anything failed. `traverse` is that loop.

### Checking every line of an order {#checking-every-line-of-an-order}

An order can ship only when every line asks for at least one unit. The check runs on one line:

```java
  // A check on one line, which fails a line that asks for nothing
  static Validated<List<String>, LineItem> checkLine(LineItem line) {
    return line.quantity() > 0
        ? Validated.valid(line)
        : Validated.invalid(List.of(line.sku() + ": quantity must be positive"));
  }

```

This order's chair and desk lines ask for nothing:

```java
  /** An order whose chair and desk lines ask for nothing. */
  static final List<LineItem> ZERO_QUANTITIES =
      List.of(
          new LineItem("LAMP", 1, new BigDecimal("40.00")),
          new LineItem("CHAIR", 0, new BigDecimal("60.00")),
          new LineItem("DESK", 0, new BigDecimal("120.00")));

```

Traversing its lines runs the check on each. The instance decides what a failure does:

```java
    Applicative<ValidatedKind.Witness<List<String>>> validatedApplicative =
        Instances.validated(Semigroups.list());
    Validated<List<String>, List<LineItem>> everyError =
        VALIDATED
            .narrow(
                ListTraverse.INSTANCE.traverse(
                    validatedApplicative,
                    line -> VALIDATED.widen(checkLine(line)),
                    LIST.widen(ZERO_QUANTITIES)))
            .map(LIST::narrow);
    // Invalid([CHAIR: quantity must be positive, DESK: quantity must be positive])

    Applicative<EitherKind.Witness<List<String>>> eitherApplicative =
        Instances.applicative(either());
    Either<List<String>, List<LineItem>> firstError =
        EITHER
            .narrow(
                ListTraverse.INSTANCE.traverse(
                    eitherApplicative,
                    line -> EITHER.widen(checkLine(line).toEither()),
                    LIST.widen(ZERO_QUANTITIES)))
            .map(LIST::narrow);
    // Left([CHAIR: quantity must be positive])
```

`Validated`'s `Applicative` joins the errors of every failing line. `Either`'s keeps the first error and drops the rest, though the check still runs on every line. When every line passes, both give the whole list of lines. The [Applicative](applicative.md#what-each-instance-does) page says what each instance's combining does.

### `sequenceA` {#sequencea}

`sequenceA` is `traverse` with the identity function, for a container whose elements already are effects. A `List` of `Maybe`s becomes a `Maybe` of a `List`: a `Just` of every value when each is present, or `Nothing` when any is missing.

```java
    // Each SKU's price, looked up: the second SKU has none
    List<Kind<MaybeKind.Witness, BigDecimal>> prices =
        List.of(MAYBE.just(new BigDecimal("40.00")), MAYBE.nothing());
    Maybe<List<BigDecimal>> everyPrice =
        MAYBE
            .narrow(
                ListTraverse.INSTANCE.sequenceA(Instances.applicative(maybe()), LIST.widen(prices)))
            .map(LIST::narrow);
    // Nothing
```

---

## Traverse inside a `For` comprehension {#traverse-inside-for-comprehensions}

`For` has `traverse`, `sequence` and `flatTraverse` steps, so a chain can check every element of a collection without leaving the comprehension. [Traversing inside a chain](for_comprehension.md#traversing-inside-a-chain) shows them, and [ForPath Traverse](../effect/forpath_traverse.md) the same steps on Effect Paths.

---

## The traverse laws {#the-traverse-laws}

`Foldable`'s one law ties it to `Traverse`: for a type with both, `foldMap` gives what `traverse` gives with [`Const`](../monads/const_type.md)'s applicative. `hkj-test` does not check it. `Traverse` keeps three laws.

~~~admonish note title="Laws"
- **Identity: traversing with an effect that does nothing changes nothing.** `traverse` with the [`Id`](../monads/identity.md) applicative and `Id::of` gives back `ta`, wrapped in an `Id`.
- **Naturality: converting the effect afterwards equals converting each step.** For an applicative transformation `t`, a [natural transformation](natural_transformation.md) that keeps `of` and `map2`, `t` applied to `traverse(f)` equals `traverse` of `f` followed by `t`.
- **Composition: two traversals equal one traversal through both effects.** Traversing with `f` and then with `g` equals one traversal with the two applicatives composed.

Two containers are equal when they hold equal values in the same shape. The identity law pins down the result's shape: it holds every element once, in its place.

`TraverseLaws`, in [`hkj-test`](../tooling/test_assertions.md#adding-the-dependency)'s `org.higherkindedj.hkt.laws` package, checks the identity law on any instance. Checking naturality and composition with `hkj-test` is not supported yet. `KindEquivalence.byEqualsAfter`, in `org.higherkindedj.hkt.assertions`, builds the equality from a function it applies to both sides before comparing them with `equals`. `ORDER` is any `Order`, and the test uses these imports, with JUnit's:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.list.ListKindHelper.LIST;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.maybe.MaybeKindHelper.MAYBE;

import org.higherkindedj.example.book.foundations.monoid.MonoidBook;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.Order;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.assertions.KindEquivalence;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.laws.TraverseLaws;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.list.ListKind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.list.ListTraverse;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.maybe.MaybeKind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.maybe.MaybeTraverse;
```

```java
  @Test
  @DisplayName("List's and Maybe's Traverse keep the identity law")
  void traverseLaws() {
    TraverseLaws.assertIdentity(
        ListTraverse.INSTANCE,
        LIST.widen(MonoidBook.LINES),
        KindEquivalence.<ListKind.Witness>byEqualsAfter(LIST::narrow));
    TraverseLaws.assertIdentity(
        MaybeTraverse.INSTANCE,
        MAYBE.just(ORDER),
        KindEquivalence.<MaybeKind.Witness>byEqualsAfter(MAYBE::narrow));
    TraverseLaws.assertIdentity(
        MaybeTraverse.INSTANCE,
        MAYBE.<Order>nothing(),
        KindEquivalence.<MaybeKind.Witness>byEqualsAfter(MAYBE::narrow));
  }

```
~~~

---

## Things people get wrong {#things-people-get-wrong}

~~~admonish warning title="Common Misunderstandings"
- **"`Foldable` is `Stream.reduce` with extra steps."** It is the same idea, for any container with a `Foldable` instance: `List`, `Maybe`, `Either` or your own. `Stream.reduce` works on a `Stream`, and `foldMap` on whatever you pass it.
- **"`traverse` runs its steps at the same time."** It runs them in the container's order, and the `Applicative` decides how their results combine. `IO`'s runs each step after the one before. `Validated`'s joins every error, and `Either`'s keeps the first. For `VTask`s that run at once, use [`Par.traverse`](../monads/vtask_monad.md#parallel-composition-with-par).
- **"`sequenceA` and `traverse` do different things."** `sequenceA(applicative, ta)` is `traverse(applicative, x -> x, ta)`. They differ only in whether there is a function to run on the way.
- **"A `Monoid` and a `Foldable` are one type class."** They are two. `foldMap` pairs them: the `Foldable` walks the container, and the monoid combines what it finds.
~~~

---

~~~admonish tip title="See Also"
- [Semigroup and Monoid](semigroup_and_monoid.md): the monoids that `foldMap` combines with
- [Applicative](applicative.md): what each instance does when `traverse` combines its steps
- [Validated](../monads/validated_monad.md): the type that reports every error
- [Effect Path Cheatsheet](../cheatsheet.md): the fold methods on `ListPath`, `NonDetPath`, `StreamPath` and `VStreamPath`
~~~

~~~admonish tip title="Further Reading"
- **Baeldung**, [Java Stream reduce()](https://www.baeldung.com/java-stream-reduce): Java's own fold, the idea `Foldable` generalises
- **Mojang**, [DataFixerUpper](https://github.com/Mojang/DataFixerUpper): Minecraft's Java library, which uses traversals to migrate data between game versions
~~~

<!-- Source: functional/natural_transformation.md -->

<!-- description: Convert one container into another with Natural, without looking at the value inside, check the naturality law, and see where interpreters use it. -->

# Natural Transformation

_Turn one container into another, such as a `Maybe` into a `List`, whatever value it holds._

<!-- source: Franz Kafka, Metamorphosis (1915), opening sentence, translated by David Wyllie; text checked against Project Gutenberg eBook #5200 -->
> _"One morning, when Gregor Samsa woke from troubled dreams, he found himself transformed in his bed into a horrible vermin."_
>
> — Franz Kafka, *Metamorphosis* (trans. David Wyllie)

Gregor wakes in a new shell with the same thoughts: `maybeToList` gives a line item a new container, and leaves the item as it was.

~~~admonish info title="What You'll Learn"
- Say what a natural transformation is, and how it differs from `map`
- Write one as a generic method and use it as a `Natural` through a method reference
- Chain transformations with `andThen`, and start a chain from `Natural.identity()`
- Check a transformation against the naturality law
- Find the interpreters and conversions in the library that take a `Natural`
~~~

~~~admonish example title="See Example Code"
**The code on this page is [NaturalBook.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/natural/NaturalBook.java)**: the page includes it, so the build compiles and runs it. [NaturalBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/natural/NaturalBookTest.java) holds what the page says each transformation does, and [FoundationsLawsBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/FoundationsLawsBookTest.java) checks the laws.
~~~

---

## The container changes, never the cargo {#the-container-changes-never-the-cargo}

A natural transformation turns every `F<A>` into a `G<A>`, for any `A`, without looking at the value inside. It changes the container, never the cargo. [Functor](functor.md)'s `map` does the opposite: it changes the value and keeps the container.

You have called some already. `optional.stream()` turns an `Optional` into a `Stream`, and `list.stream().findFirst()` turns a `List` into an `Optional`. The library adds `maybe.toOptional()`, `Try.toEither(mapper)` and, on an [Effect Path](../effect/ch_intro.md), `MaybePath.toEitherPath(error)`. Application code stays with methods like these.

This page writes three over the `Kind` encoding, as an interpreter needs:

| From | To | The rule |
|---|---|---|
| `Maybe<A>` | `List<A>` | `Nothing` becomes `[]`, and `Just(x)` becomes `[x]` |
| `List<A>` | `Maybe<A>` | Keep the first element, if there is one |
| `Either<E, A>` | `Maybe<A>` | `Left` becomes `Nothing`, and `Right(x)` becomes `Just(x)` |

Each rule is about the shape of its input, and none needs to know what the input holds.

## Write one as a generic method {#writing-one}

The code on this page uses these imports, with `LineItem` from the order records the [Optics](../optics/ch_intro.md) chapter introduces:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.either.EitherKindHelper.EITHER;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.list.ListKindHelper.LIST;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.maybe.MaybeKindHelper.MAYBE;

import java.util.List;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.LineItem;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Kind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Natural;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.either.Either;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.either.EitherKind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.list.ListKind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.maybe.Maybe;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.maybe.MaybeKind;
```

`Natural<F, G>` has one abstract method, `<A> Kind<G, A> apply(Kind<F, A> fa)`. Its `<A>` is on the method, not the interface, so one `Natural` works for every element type. That also means a lambda cannot implement it, since a Java lambda cannot be generic:

<!-- verify:rejects "invalid functional descriptor" -->
```java
Natural<MaybeKind.Witness, ListKind.Witness> toList = fa -> NaturalBook.maybeToList(fa);
```

javac refuses it with "invalid functional descriptor for lambda expression". Write each conversion as a generic method:

```java
  // Each works for every A, and none looks at the value it carries
  public static <A> Kind<ListKind.Witness, A> maybeToList(Kind<MaybeKind.Witness, A> fa) {
    Maybe<A> maybe = MAYBE.narrow(fa);
    return LIST.widen(maybe.isJust() ? List.of(maybe.get()) : List.of());
  }

  public static <A> Kind<MaybeKind.Witness, A> eitherToMaybe(
      Kind<EitherKind.Witness<String>, A> fa) {
    return MAYBE.widen(EITHER.<String, A>narrow(fa).fold(error -> Maybe.nothing(), Maybe::just));
  }

  public static <A> Kind<MaybeKind.Witness, A> firstOf(Kind<ListKind.Witness, A> fa) {
    List<A> list = LIST.narrow(fa);
    return list.isEmpty() ? MAYBE.nothing() : MAYBE.just(list.getFirst());
  }

```

A method reference makes one a `Natural`. `LAMP` is a lamp line item: `Just(LAMP)` becomes a one-element list, and `Nothing` an empty one.

```java
    Natural<MaybeKind.Witness, ListKind.Witness> toList = NaturalBook::maybeToList;

    List<LineItem> found = LIST.narrow(toList.apply(MAYBE.just(LAMP)));
    // [LineItem[sku=LAMP, quantity=1, price=40.00]]

    List<LineItem> notFound = LIST.narrow(toList.apply(MAYBE.nothing()));
    // []
```

## Chaining {#chaining}

`andThen` runs one transformation and then another, and `compose` runs them the other way round. `Natural.identity()` returns its input unchanged, so it can start a chain built in a loop. Here a `Right` becomes a one-element list, and a `Left` an empty one, its error dropped:

```java
    Natural<EitherKind.Witness<String>, MaybeKind.Witness> toMaybe = NaturalBook::eitherToMaybe;
    Natural<EitherKind.Witness<String>, ListKind.Witness> eitherToList = toMaybe.andThen(toList);

    List<LineItem> priced = LIST.narrow(eitherToList.apply(EITHER.widen(Either.right(LAMP))));
    // [LineItem[sku=LAMP, quantity=1, price=40.00]]

    List<LineItem> unpriced =
        LIST.narrow(eitherToList.apply(EITHER.widen(Either.left("no price for LAMP"))));
    // []
```

## The naturality law {#the-naturality-law}

One rule makes a transformation natural: for any function `f`, mapping `f` and then converting gives what converting and then mapping gives.

<pre class="hkj-ascii-diagram" role="img" aria-label="The naturality square. From F of A, mapping f then applying nat reaches G of B, and so does applying nat then mapping f. Both routes give the same result.">
            map(f)
   F&lt;A&gt;  ──────────▶  F&lt;B&gt;
    │                   │
   nat                 nat
    ▼                   ▼
   G&lt;A&gt;  ──────────▶  G&lt;B&gt;
            map(f)
</pre>

Both routes to the bottom-right corner give the same answer. That is what lets you move a conversion before or after a `map` without changing the result.

~~~admonish note title="Laws"
- **Naturality: converting and mapping can swap places.** `nat.apply(map(f, fa))` equals `map(f, nat.apply(fa))`, for every function `f` and value `fa`.
- **Identity: the identity changes nothing, on either side.** `Natural.identity().andThen(nat).apply(fa)` equals `nat.apply(fa)`, and so does `nat.andThen(Natural.identity()).apply(fa)`.
- **Associativity: grouping a chain differently changes nothing.** `f.andThen(g).andThen(h).apply(fa)` equals `f.andThen(g.andThen(h)).apply(fa)`.

The laws compare what each side gives for the same input. Two `Natural`s are different objects, so `equals` between them says nothing. The laws assume functions that never return `null`, and [Values, never null](../hkts/core-concepts.md#values-never-null) says what each type does with one. A transformation that looks at the value inside, such as one that treats an `Integer` differently from a `String`, breaks the naturality law, and is a function of another kind.

Checking these laws with `hkj-test` is not supported yet, so the test checks them by hand, for `maybeToList` and `firstOf`. It uses these imports, with JUnit's, and `ORDER` is any `Order`:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import static org.assertj.core.api.Assertions.assertThat;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.list;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.maybe;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.list.ListKindHelper.LIST;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.maybe.MaybeKindHelper.MAYBE;

import java.util.List;
import java.util.function.BiPredicate;
import java.util.function.Function;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.foundations.natural.NaturalBook;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.Order;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Functor;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Kind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Natural;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.TypeArity;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.WitnessArity;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.assertions.KindEquivalence;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Instances;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.list.ListKind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.maybe.MaybeKind;
```

```java
  <F extends WitnessArity<TypeArity.Unary>, G extends WitnessArity<TypeArity.Unary>, A, B>
      void assertNaturality(
          Natural<F, G> nat,
          Functor<F> from,
          Functor<G> to,
          Kind<F, A> fa,
          Function<A, B> f,
          BiPredicate<Kind<G, ?>, Kind<G, ?>> same) {
    // mapping and then converting equals converting and then mapping
    assertThat(same.test(nat.apply(from.map(f, fa)), to.map(f, nat.apply(fa)))).isTrue();
  }

  @Test
  @DisplayName("maybeToList and firstOf are natural, and andThen keeps identity and grouping")
  void naturalLaws() {
    Natural<MaybeKind.Witness, ListKind.Witness> toList = NaturalBook::maybeToList;
    Natural<ListKind.Witness, MaybeKind.Witness> first = NaturalBook::firstOf;
    BiPredicate<Kind<ListKind.Witness, ?>, Kind<ListKind.Witness, ?>> sameList =
        KindEquivalence.byEqualsAfter(LIST::narrow);
    BiPredicate<Kind<MaybeKind.Witness, ?>, Kind<MaybeKind.Witness, ?>> sameMaybe =
        KindEquivalence.byEqualsAfter(MAYBE::narrow);

    for (Kind<MaybeKind.Witness, Order> fa : List.of(MAYBE.just(ORDER), MAYBE.<Order>nothing())) {
      assertNaturality(
          toList,
          Instances.functor(maybe()),
          Instances.functor(list()),
          fa,
          Order::customer,
          sameList);
      // the identity, on either side, changes nothing
      assertThat(
              sameList.test(
                  Natural.<MaybeKind.Witness>identity().andThen(toList).apply(fa),
                  toList.apply(fa)))
          .isTrue();
      assertThat(sameList.test(toList.andThen(Natural.identity()).apply(fa), toList.apply(fa)))
          .isTrue();
      // grouping the conversions differently changes nothing
      assertThat(
              sameList.test(
                  toList.andThen(first).andThen(toList).apply(fa),
                  toList.andThen(first.andThen(toList)).apply(fa)))
          .isTrue();
    }
    Order second =
        new Order(
            ORDER.id(),
            ORDER.customer(),
            List.of(),
            ORDER.placedAt(),
            ORDER.currency(),
            ORDER.status());
    for (List<Order> orders : List.of(List.<Order>of(), List.of(ORDER), List.of(ORDER, second))) {
      assertNaturality(
          first,
          Instances.functor(list()),
          Instances.functor(maybe()),
          LIST.widen(orders),
          Order::lines,
          sameMaybe);
    }
  }

```
~~~

## Where the library takes one {#where-the-library-takes-one}

A [Free](../monads/free_monad.md) program holds its steps, its instructions, as data. An interpreter runs it by turning each instruction into a real effect, and that turning is a `Natural`.

- **A Free program's interpreter is a `Natural`.** `program.foldMap(interpreter, monad)` runs a program by converting each instruction with a `Natural<F, M>`, and a `FreeAp` program's `foldMap` does the same with an applicative. A test interpreter is another `Natural` for the same program, and `FreePath.foldMap` takes one too.
- **`Free.translate(program, nat, functor)` moves a program from one instruction set to another.** [EitherF](../monads/eitherf.md#moving-a-program-into-the-pair) shows it.
- **`Interpreters.combine` joins two interpreters into one.** A program over two instruction sets needs it, as [EitherF](../monads/eitherf.md) shows.
- **`EffectBoundary.of` takes the interpreter to `IO` that runs a program at the edge.**
- **A transformer's `mapT` takes `nat::apply`.** `EitherT`, `MaybeT`, `OptionalT`, `ReaderT`, `StateT` and `WriterT` each have one. It changes the outer container, for example from `IO` to `VTask`, and leaves the inner value alone.
- **`GenericPath.mapK(nat, monad)` moves a path to another container.** `VStreamTransformations` gives the `Natural`s between `VStream`, `List` and `Stream`, as the VStream [Advanced Features](../monads/vstream_advanced.md#natural-transformations) page shows.

## Things people get wrong {#things-people-get-wrong}

~~~admonish warning title="Common Misunderstandings"
- **"A `Natural` is a lambda."** Its `apply` is generic, and a Java lambda cannot be. Write a generic method, and pass it as a method reference.
- **"I can branch on `A`."** Not in a natural transformation. A body that inspects the value, with `instanceof` for example, compiles, but it breaks the naturality law.
- **"A natural transformation keeps every value."** It keeps the values it keeps unchanged, but it may drop some: `firstOf` keeps only the first element, and `eitherToMaybe` drops the error. Both are natural.
- **"Naturality is theory I can ignore."** It is what lets you move a conversion past a `map`. An interpreter that breaks it gives a program a meaning that depends on where its steps were mapped.
~~~

---

~~~admonish tip title="See Also"
- [Functor](functor.md): the `map` that the naturality law is stated in
- [Free Monad](../monads/free_monad.md): programs that a `Natural` interprets
- [Free Applicative](../monads/free_applicative.md): independent programs that a `Natural` interprets
~~~

~~~admonish tip title="Further Reading"
- **Bartosz Milewski**, [Natural Transformations](https://bartoszmilewski.com/2015/04/07/natural-transformations/): an introduction with diagrams
~~~

~~~admonish info title="Hands-On Learning"
Practise natural transformations in [Tutorial 08: Natural Transformation](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/tutorial/coretypes/Tutorial08_NaturalTransformation.java) (5 exercises).
~~~

<!-- description: Run an effect only when an earlier result calls for it with select, whenS and ifS, every branch given up front, and see what that buys. -->

# Selective: Conditional Effects

_Run an effect only when an earlier result calls for it, with every branch named up front._

~~~admonish info title="What You'll Learn"
- Apply a function only to a value that still needs it, with `select`
- Run an effect only when a condition holds with `whenS`, and pick one of two with `ifS`
- Say what giving every branch up front buys, and where a program can be inspected before it runs
- Get the `Selective` instance for each type that has one
- Check an instance against the two laws `hkj-test` checks
~~~

~~~admonish example title="See Example Code"
**The code on this page is [SelectiveBook.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/selective/SelectiveBook.java)**: the page includes it, so the build compiles and runs it. [SelectiveBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/selective/SelectiveBookTest.java) holds what the page says each operation runs, and [FoundationsLawsBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/FoundationsLawsBookTest.java) checks the laws.
~~~

---

## Between Applicative and Monad {#between-applicative-and-monad}

[Applicative](applicative.md)'s `map2` combines effects that do not depend on one another: no value one effect gives can decide whether another runs. [Monad](monad.md)'s `flatMap` lets a step choose the next effect from the last result, but the next effect does not exist until the step runs. `Selective` sits between them: an earlier result decides whether an effect runs, and every effect that might run is named up front.

`Selective` extends `Applicative`, and every `Selective` in the library is also a `Monad`. [Applicative, Selective or Monad?](abstraction_levels.md) walks through choosing among the three for a task. On an [Effect Path](../effect/ch_intro.md), a `via` step with an `if` does this job for one container.

The code on this page uses these imports:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.io.IOKindHelper.IO_OP;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.maybe.MaybeKindHelper.MAYBE;

import java.math.BigDecimal;
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.function.Function;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Kind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Selective;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Unit;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.io.IO;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.io.IOKind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.io.IOSelective;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.maybe.Maybe;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.maybe.MaybeKind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.maybe.MaybeSelective;
```

Import the library's `IO` by name: Java 25 has a `java.lang.IO` too.

## `select`: apply a function only where it is needed {#select}

A `Choice<A, B>` is a `Left` holding an `A` or a `Right` holding a `B`, and `Selective.left` and `Selective.right` build one. Here `Left` means not done yet, not failure.

`select(fab, ff)` takes a container of `Choice<A, B>` and a container of a function from `A` to `B`. A `Left(a)` still needs the function, so `select` applies it. A `Right(b)` is already the answer, so `select` returns it untouched. With `ap`, a `Nothing` function would make any result `Nothing`:

```java
    Selective<MaybeKind.Witness> selective = MaybeSelective.INSTANCE;
    Kind<MaybeKind.Witness, Function<String, BigDecimal>> parse = MAYBE.just(BigDecimal::new);

    // A Left still needs parsing, so select applies the function to it
    Maybe<BigDecimal> parsed =
        MAYBE.narrow(selective.select(MAYBE.just(Selective.left("40.00")), parse));
    // Just(40.00)

    // A Right is ready, so select never needs the function, even a missing one
    Maybe<BigDecimal> ready =
        MAYBE.narrow(
            selective.select(MAYBE.just(Selective.right(new BigDecimal("2.50"))), MAYBE.nothing()));
    // Just(2.50)
```

## `whenS`: run an effect only when a condition holds {#whens}

The condition is itself an effect: an `IO` that, when it runs, says whether an order's total is over 100.00.

```java
  // An order above this total is large: it is reviewed, and it goes by freight
  static final BigDecimal LARGE = new BigDecimal("100.00");

  static Kind<IOKind.Witness, Boolean> isLarge(BigDecimal total) {
    return IO_OP.widen(IO.delay(() -> total.compareTo(LARGE) > 0));
  }

```

`whenS(condition, effect)` runs the effect only when the condition gives `true`, and gives `Unit` either way. Flagging an order for review runs only for a large one:

```java
  // Flags an order for review, but only when it is large
  static Kind<IOKind.Witness, Unit> reviewIfLarge(BigDecimal total, List<String> flagged) {
    Kind<IOKind.Witness, Unit> flag =
        IO_OP.widen(IO.fromRunnable(() -> flagged.add("review " + total)));
    return IOSelective.INSTANCE.whenS(isLarge(total), flag);
  }

  // Runs the review for each total, and gives what it flagged
  static List<String> reviewAll(List<BigDecimal> totals) {
    List<String> flagged = new ArrayList<>();
    totals.forEach(total -> IO_OP.narrow(reviewIfLarge(total, flagged)).unsafeRunSync());
    return flagged;
  }

```

Run for an order of 50.00 and one of 120.00, it flags only the second:

```java
    List<String> flagged = reviewAll(List.of(new BigDecimal("50.00"), new BigDecimal("120.00")));
    // [review 120.00]
```

`whenS_` does the same for an effect that gives a value, and drops the value.

## `ifS`: pick one of two effects {#ifs}

`ifS(condition, then, else)` gives `then` when the condition gives `true` and `else` when it gives `false`. Both are given up front, and `IO`'s `ifS` runs the condition, then only the branch it picks. A large order goes by freight, and any other by post:

```java
  // Books freight for a large order and post for any other: only the chosen booking runs
  static Kind<IOKind.Witness, String> bookCourier(BigDecimal total, List<String> booked) {
    Kind<IOKind.Witness, String> freight = book("freight", booked);
    Kind<IOKind.Witness, String> post = book("post", booked);
    return IOSelective.INSTANCE.ifS(isLarge(total), freight, post);
  }

  // Books a courier for the total, and gives every booking made
  static List<String> bookingsFor(BigDecimal total) {
    List<String> booked = new ArrayList<>();
    IO_OP.narrow(bookCourier(total, booked)).unsafeRunSync();
    return booked;
  }

  static Kind<IOKind.Witness, String> book(String courier, List<String> booked) {
    return IO_OP.widen(
        IO.delay(
            () -> {
              booked.add(courier);
              return courier;
            }));
  }

```

```java
    List<String> booked = bookingsFor(new BigDecimal("50.00"));
    // [post]
```

A condition that holds more than one answer gives more than one branch. `List`'s `ifS` gives both branches' results for `[true, false]`. `Validated`'s gathers both branches' errors when the condition is itself invalid.

## Other operations {#other-operations}

| Operation | What it does |
|---|---|
| `branch(fab, onLeft, onRight)` | Applies `onLeft` to a `Left` and `onRight` to a `Right`, running only the handler it applies |
| `orElse(alternatives)` | Gives the first `Right` in a list of `Choice` containers, or the last `Left` when none is a `Right` |
| `apS(initial, steps)` | Runs each step on the value while it is a `Right`, and stops at the first `Left` |

This `orElse` takes `Choice` containers. The [Alternative](alternative.md) on the next page has an `orElse` of its own, a different method.

## What "given up front" buys {#what-given-up-front-buys}

With `flatMap`, the next effect is built by a function from the last result, so nothing can see it before the program runs. With `Selective`, every effect that might run is a value you passed in, so a program held as data could be inspected before it runs. Holding a `Selective` program as data is not supported yet. A [Free Applicative](../monads/free_applicative.md) program has no conditions, and `FreeApAnalyzer` lists every effect it will run.

An instance such as `IOSelective` or `MaybeSelective` runs a program, and inspects nothing. None of them runs two branches at the same time.

## Laws {#laws}

The [Selective Applicative Functors](https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3341694) paper gives `select` three laws: identity, distributivity and associativity. They do not require a `select` to skip the function for a `Right`. Checking them with `hkj-test` is not supported yet. `SelectiveLaws` checks two stricter properties, which the library's instances keep.

~~~admonish note title="Laws"
- **Left pure: a known `Left` applies the function.** `select(of(Left(a)), ff)` equals `ap(ff, of(a))`.
- **Right pure: a known `Right` skips the function.** `select(of(Right(b)), ff)` equals `of(b)`, even when `ff` has failed.

Two containers are equal when they hold equal values, or equal errors, in the same shape. The checks assume functions that never return `null`, and [Values, never null](../hkts/core-concepts.md#values-never-null) says what each type does with one.

`SelectiveLaws`, in [`hkj-test`](../tooling/test_assertions.md#adding-the-dependency)'s `org.higherkindedj.hkt.laws` package, checks both on any instance. `KindEquivalence.byEqualsAfter`, in `org.higherkindedj.hkt.assertions`, builds the equality from a function it applies to both sides before comparing them with `equals`. The test uses these imports, with JUnit's:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.either.EitherKindHelper.EITHER;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.maybe.MaybeKindHelper.MAYBE;

import java.math.BigDecimal;
import java.util.function.Function;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Kind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Selective;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.assertions.KindEquivalence;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.either.Either;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.either.EitherKind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.either.EitherSelective;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.laws.SelectiveLaws;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.maybe.MaybeKind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.maybe.MaybeSelective;
```

```java
  @Test
  @DisplayName("Maybe's and Either's Selective keep the two pure laws")
  void selectiveLaws() {
    Kind<MaybeKind.Witness, Function<String, BigDecimal>> parseMaybe = MAYBE.just(BigDecimal::new);
    Kind<EitherKind.Witness<String>, Function<String, BigDecimal>> parseEither =
        EITHER.widen(Either.right(BigDecimal::new));
    Selective<EitherKind.Witness<String>> eitherSelective = EitherSelective.instance();

    SelectiveLaws.assertLeftPure(
        MaybeSelective.INSTANCE,
        "40.00",
        parseMaybe,
        KindEquivalence.<MaybeKind.Witness>byEqualsAfter(MAYBE::narrow));
    // a Right must skip the function, so give it a function that has failed
    SelectiveLaws.assertRightPure(
        MaybeSelective.INSTANCE,
        new BigDecimal("2.50"),
        MAYBE.<Function<String, BigDecimal>>nothing(),
        KindEquivalence.<MaybeKind.Witness>byEqualsAfter(MAYBE::narrow));
    SelectiveLaws.assertLeftPure(
        eitherSelective,
        "40.00",
        parseEither,
        KindEquivalence.<EitherKind.Witness<String>>byEqualsAfter(EITHER::narrow));
    SelectiveLaws.assertRightPure(
        eitherSelective,
        new BigDecimal("2.50"),
        EITHER.<String, Function<String, BigDecimal>>widen(Either.left("no parser")),
        KindEquivalence.<EitherKind.Witness<String>>byEqualsAfter(EITHER::narrow));
  }

```
~~~

## Things people get wrong {#things-people-get-wrong}

~~~admonish warning title="Common Misunderstandings"
- **"A `Selective` program is analysed before it runs."** An instance such as `IOSelective` runs it, and holding a `Selective` program as data is not supported yet.
- **"`ifS` runs both branches."** `IO`'s runs the condition and then the chosen branch. Only a condition that holds more than one answer, as a `List` can, gives both.
- **"Both branches run at the same time."** No instance runs them together. For `VTask`s that run at once, use [`Par`](../monads/vtask_monad.md#parallel-composition-with-par).
- **"`whenS` needs a `Monad`."** It needs only `select`, which a `Selective` provides.
~~~

---

## The fine print {#the-fine-print}

### Which types have a `Selective` {#which-types-have-a-selective}

| Type | Instance |
|---|---|
| `Maybe` | `MaybeSelective.INSTANCE` |
| `Optional` | `OptionalSelective.INSTANCE` |
| `List` | `ListSelective.INSTANCE` |
| `IO` | `IOSelective.INSTANCE` |
| `Either<E, ?>` | `EitherSelective.instance()` |
| `Reader<R, ?>` | `ReaderSelective.instance()` |
| `Id` | `IdSelective.instance()` |
| `Validated<E, ?>` | `ValidatedSelective.instance(semigroup)`, whose semigroup joins the errors `select` meets |

`Instances` has no `selective` factory, so ask the class for its instance.

### A `Selective` of your own {#a-selective-of-your-own}

An instance needs `select`, with `of` and `ap` from `Applicative` and `map` from `Functor`. The defaults for `whenS`, `ifS` and `branch` run a guarded effect exactly when your `select` runs the function's effect. Skip that effect for a `Right`, as the library's instances do, and the defaults run only the chosen effect. A `select` built from `ap` runs every effect.

---

~~~admonish tip title="See Also"
- [Applicative, Selective or Monad?](abstraction_levels.md): choosing by whether a step needs an earlier result
- [Free Applicative](../monads/free_applicative.md): programs that can be inspected before they run
- [Applicative](applicative.md): combining effects that do not depend on one another
~~~

~~~admonish tip title="Further Reading"
- **Andrey Mokhov, Georgy Lukyanov, Simon Marlow and Jeremie Dimino**, [Selective Applicative Functors](https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3341694): the paper that introduced `Selective`
~~~

<!-- Source: functional/alternative.md -->

<!-- description: Fall back from one container to another with Alternative's orElse, which Maybe reads as a fallback and List as joining, and check the Alternative laws. -->

# Alternative: Choosing Between Containers

_Try one value and fall back to another, with `empty` for a value that is not there._

~~~admonish info title="What You'll Learn"
- Fall back from one `Maybe` to another with `orElse`, building the fallback only when it is needed
- Predict what `orElse` does for each instance: fall back for `Maybe` and `Optional`, join for `List`
- Try any number of sources with `orElseAll`
- Turn a test into a value with `guard`
- Check an instance against the Alternative laws
~~~

~~~admonish example title="See Example Code"
**The code on this page is [AlternativeBook.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/alternative/AlternativeBook.java)**: the page includes it, so the build compiles and runs it. [AlternativeBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/alternative/AlternativeBookTest.java) holds what the page says each instance does, and [FoundationsLawsBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/FoundationsLawsBookTest.java) checks the laws.
~~~

---

## Trying a fallback {#trying-a-fallback}

`Alternative` extends [Applicative](applicative.md) with two methods. `empty()` gives a container with nothing in it, and `orElse(fa, fb)` picks between two containers. `fb` is a `Supplier`, so an instance can build the fallback only when it needs it. If you have written `optional.or(() -> other)`, you have used one.

On an [Effect Path](../effect/ch_intro.md), `MaybePath.orElse` falls back in the same way, and application code stays there. This page uses the `Kind` encoding, which lets one method serve any container.

The code on this page uses these imports:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.list;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.maybe;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.list.ListKindHelper.LIST;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.maybe.MaybeKindHelper.MAYBE;

import java.math.BigDecimal;
import java.util.List;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Alternative;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Kind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Unit;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Instances;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.list.ListKind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.maybe.Maybe;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.maybe.MaybeKind;
```

`Instances.alternative(maybe())` gives `Maybe`'s instance. A SKU's price comes from today's offers if there is one, and otherwise from the catalogue:

```java
  static final Alternative<MaybeKind.Witness> MAYBE_ALT = Instances.alternative(maybe());

  // Where a SKU's price can come from: today's offers first, then the catalogue
  static Kind<MaybeKind.Witness, BigDecimal> offerFor(String sku) {
    return sku.equals("BULB") ? MAYBE.just(new BigDecimal("2.00")) : MAYBE.nothing();
  }

  static Kind<MaybeKind.Witness, BigDecimal> catalogueFor(String sku) {
    return sku.equals("CHAIR") ? MAYBE.nothing() : MAYBE.just(new BigDecimal("40.00"));
  }

```

`Maybe`'s `orElse` gives the first `Just`, and asks for the fallback only when the first is `Nothing`:

```java
    Alternative<MaybeKind.Witness> maybeAlt = MAYBE_ALT;

    Maybe<BigDecimal> lampPrice =
        MAYBE.narrow(maybeAlt.orElse(offerFor("LAMP"), () -> catalogueFor("LAMP")));
    // Just(40.00)

    Maybe<BigDecimal> bulbPrice =
        MAYBE.narrow(maybeAlt.orElse(offerFor("BULB"), () -> catalogueFor("BULB")));
    // Just(2.00)

    Maybe<BigDecimal> chairPrice =
        MAYBE.narrow(maybeAlt.orElse(offerFor("CHAIR"), () -> catalogueFor("CHAIR")));
    // Nothing
```

## One method, two meanings {#one-method-two-meanings}

`List`'s `orElse` does not fall back: it joins the two lists, and so it always builds the second.

```java
    Alternative<ListKind.Witness> listAlt = Instances.alternative(list());

    List<String> skus =
        LIST.narrow(
            listAlt.orElse(LIST.widen(List.of("LAMP", "BULB")), () -> LIST.widen(List.of("DESK"))));
    // [LAMP, BULB, DESK]
```

| Type | `empty()` | `orElse(fa, fb)` | Instance |
|---|---|---|---|
| `Maybe` | `Nothing` | `fa` if it is a `Just`, otherwise `fb` | `Instances.alternative(maybe())` |
| `Optional` | `Optional.empty()` | `fa` if it is present, otherwise `fb` | `Instances.alternative(optional())` |
| `List` | `[]` | `fa`'s elements, then `fb`'s | `Instances.alternative(list())` |
| `Stream` | An empty stream | `fa`'s elements, then `fb`'s, lazily | `Instances.alternative(stream())` |
| `VStream` | An empty stream | `fa`'s elements, then `fb`'s, lazily | `Instances.alternative(vstream())` |

`MaybeMonad`, `OptionalMonad`, `ListMonad` and `StreamMonad` are each a [MonadZero](monad_zero.md), which extends both `Monad` and `Alternative`.

## Many sources {#many-sources}

`orElseAll(fa, fb, more...)` tries any number of sources in order, and stops at the first present value for `Maybe`. Each source after the first is a `Supplier`, so a source after that value is never built.

```java
  // The offer, then the catalogue, then a default: the first present price wins
  @SuppressWarnings(
      "unchecked") // varargs orElseAll: Java allows no @SafeVarargs on a default method
  static Kind<MaybeKind.Witness, BigDecimal> priceOrDefault(String sku) {
    return MAYBE_ALT.orElseAll(
        offerFor(sku), () -> catalogueFor(sku), () -> MAYBE.just(new BigDecimal("60.00")));
  }

```

```java
    Maybe<BigDecimal> chairOrDefault = MAYBE.narrow(priceOrDefault("CHAIR"));
    // Just(60.00)
```

A call to this form draws javac's unchecked warning for a generic varargs array, since Java allows no `@SafeVarargs` on an interface's default method. Suppress it on the method that makes the call, as the example does. `orElseAll(Iterable)` takes a list built at run time, with no warning. Its sources are values, not `Supplier`s. For `Maybe` it gives the first present source, and `List`, `Stream` and `VStream` join them all. Every instance but `Stream`'s walks the whole list, so it never ends on an infinite one. `Stream`'s walks only as far as you read the result, so `limit(3)` ends even on an endless `Iterable`.

## Turning a test into a value {#turning-a-test-into-a-value}

`guard(test)` gives `of(Unit)` when the test holds and `empty()` when it fails. `()` is how `Unit` prints:

```java
    // guard turns a test into a value: of(Unit) when it holds, empty when it fails
    BigDecimal total = new BigDecimal("50.00");

    Maybe<Unit> overForty = MAYBE.narrow(maybeAlt.guard(total.compareTo(new BigDecimal("40")) > 0));
    // Just(())

    Maybe<Unit> overHundred =
        MAYBE.narrow(maybeAlt.guard(total.compareTo(new BigDecimal("100")) > 0));
    // Nothing
```

A monad with a zero can continue from it, so a failed test ends the chain. [MonadZero](monad_zero.md)'s `filter` and a `For` comprehension's `when` work this way.

## Laws {#laws}

An `Alternative` keeps five laws. The first three make `orElse` and `empty` behave like joining strings and the empty string, and the last two make `empty` absorb `ap`.

~~~admonish note title="Laws"
- **Left identity: an empty first choice changes nothing.** `orElse(empty(), () -> fa)` equals `fa`.
- **Right identity: an empty fallback changes nothing.** `orElse(fa, () -> empty())` equals `fa`.
- **Associativity: grouping three choices differently changes nothing.** `orElse(orElse(fa, () -> fb), () -> fc)` equals `orElse(fa, () -> orElse(fb, () -> fc))`. The order of the choices still matters: for `List`, `orElse(fa, () -> fb)` and `orElse(fb, () -> fa)` give the elements in different orders.
- **Left absorption: an empty function gives empty.** `ap(empty(), fa)` equals `empty()`.
- **Right absorption: an empty value gives empty.** `ap(ff, empty())` equals `empty()`.

Two containers are equal when they hold equal values in the same shape. The laws assume functions that never return `null`, and [Values, never null](../hkts/core-concepts.md#values-never-null) says what each type does with one.

Checking these laws with `hkj-test` is not supported yet, so the test checks them by hand, for `Maybe` and `List`. It uses these imports, with JUnit's:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import static org.assertj.core.api.Assertions.assertThat;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.list;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.maybe;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.list.ListKindHelper.LIST;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.maybe.MaybeKindHelper.MAYBE;

import java.util.List;
import java.util.function.BiPredicate;
import java.util.function.Function;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Alternative;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Kind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.TypeArity;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.WitnessArity;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.assertions.KindEquivalence;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Instances;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.list.ListKind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.maybe.MaybeKind;
```

```java
  <F extends WitnessArity<TypeArity.Unary>, A> void assertAlternativeLaws(
      Alternative<F> alt,
      Kind<F, A> fa,
      Kind<F, A> fb,
      Kind<F, A> fc,
      Kind<F, Function<A, A>> ff,
      BiPredicate<Kind<F, ?>, Kind<F, ?>> same) {
    // empty changes nothing, on either side
    assertThat(same.test(alt.orElse(alt.empty(), () -> fa), fa)).isTrue();
    assertThat(same.test(alt.orElse(fa, alt::empty), fa)).isTrue();
    // grouping the choices differently changes nothing
    assertThat(
            same.test(
                alt.orElse(alt.orElse(fa, () -> fb), () -> fc),
                alt.orElse(fa, () -> alt.orElse(fb, () -> fc))))
        .isTrue();
    // an empty function, or an empty value, makes ap empty
    assertThat(same.test(alt.ap(alt.<Function<A, A>>empty(), fa), alt.empty())).isTrue();
    assertThat(same.test(alt.ap(ff, alt.<A>empty()), alt.empty())).isTrue();
  }

  @Test
  @DisplayName("Maybe's and List's Alternative keep identity, associativity and absorption")
  void alternativeLaws() {
    Alternative<MaybeKind.Witness> maybeAlt = Instances.alternative(maybe());
    Alternative<ListKind.Witness> listAlt = Instances.alternative(list());
    Function<String, String> lower = String::toLowerCase;

    for (Kind<MaybeKind.Witness, String> fa :
        List.of(MAYBE.just("LAMP"), MAYBE.<String>nothing())) {
      assertAlternativeLaws(
          maybeAlt,
          fa,
          MAYBE.just("BULB"),
          MAYBE.just("DESK"),
          MAYBE.just(lower),
          KindEquivalence.<MaybeKind.Witness>byEqualsAfter(MAYBE::narrow));
    }
    assertAlternativeLaws(
        listAlt,
        LIST.widen(List.of("LAMP", "BULB")),
        LIST.widen(List.of("DESK")),
        LIST.widen(List.<String>of()),
        LIST.widen(List.of(lower)),
        KindEquivalence.<ListKind.Witness>byEqualsAfter(LIST::narrow));
  }

```
~~~

## Things people get wrong {#things-people-get-wrong}

~~~admonish warning title="Common Misunderstandings"
- **"`orElse` always falls back."** `Maybe`'s and `Optional`'s do. `List`'s, `Stream`'s and `VStream`'s join both containers.
- **"`orElse` picks whichever value is present."** It prefers the first. Two present prices give the offer, not the catalogue's.
- **"Every type with a `Monad` has an `Alternative`."** Only `Maybe`, `Optional`, `List`, `Stream` and `VStream` have one. `Instances.alternative(either())` compiles, and throws `ClassCastException` when it runs, as [what the compiler checks](instances_facade.md#what-the-compiler-checks) explains.
- **"This `orElse` is `Selective`'s."** [Selective](selective.md)'s `orElse` takes a list of `Choice` containers, and is a different method.
~~~

---

~~~admonish tip title="See Also"
- [MonadZero](monad_zero.md): the `Alternative` that is also a `Monad`, and filtering with its zero
- [Applicative](applicative.md): the type class `Alternative` extends
- [AlternativeConfigExample.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/basic/alternative/AlternativeConfigExample.java): fallback chains for configuration, with both `orElseAll` forms
~~~

~~~admonish tip title="Further Reading"
- **Baeldung**, [Java Optional orElse Patterns](https://www.baeldung.com/java-optional-or-else-optional): Java's own fallbacks, the idea `Alternative` generalises
~~~

---

**Next:** [MonadZero](monad_zero.md)
