<!-- description: Filter inside a monadic chain with MonadZero: drop a value with filter, guard a For comprehension with when, and check the zero laws. -->

# MonadZero: Filtering in Monadic Chains

_Give a monad an empty value, and a chain can drop whatever fails a test._

~~~admonish info title="What You'll Learn"
- Say what a `zero` adds to a `Monad`, and why a chain ends at one
- Drop a value that fails a test with `filter`, in a method written once for any container
- Guard a step of a `For` comprehension with `when`
- Name the types that have a zero, and what each one's is
- Check an instance against the two zero laws
~~~

~~~admonish example title="See Example Code"
**The code on this page is [MonadZeroBook.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/monadzero/MonadZeroBook.java)**: the page includes it, so the build compiles and runs it. [MonadZeroBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/monadzero/MonadZeroBookTest.java) holds what the page says each instance does, and [FoundationsLawsBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/FoundationsLawsBookTest.java) checks the laws.
~~~

---

## A monad with an empty value {#a-monad-with-an-empty-value}

`MonadZero` extends [Monad](monad.md) and [Alternative](alternative.md), and adds `zero()`: a container with nothing in it, which is also its `Alternative`'s `empty()`. A `flatMap` over a zero has no value to run its step on, so the chain ends there. For a `List`, each element runs the rest of the chain on its own, so a zero ends only that element's run. That is enough to filter: replace a value that fails a test with a zero. If you have called `stream.filter` or `optional.filter`, you have done this for one container.

On an [Effect Path](../effect/ch_intro.md), `MaybePath.filter` does it for a `Maybe`, and application code stays there. This page is for a method written once for any container. Its code uses these imports, with `LineItem` from the order records the [Optics](../optics/ch_intro.md) chapter introduces:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.list;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.maybe;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.list.ListKindHelper.LIST;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.maybe.MaybeKindHelper.MAYBE;

import java.math.BigDecimal;
import java.util.List;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.LineItem;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Kind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.MonadZero;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.TypeArity;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.WitnessArity;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.expression.For;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Instances;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.list.ListKind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.maybe.Maybe;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.maybe.MaybeKind;
```

The examples use two baskets:

```java
  // Two baskets: a lamp and four bulbs, then three more bulbs
  static final LineItem LAMP = new LineItem("LAMP", 1, new BigDecimal("40.00"));

  static final List<List<LineItem>> BASKETS =
      List.of(
          List.of(LAMP, new LineItem("BULB", 4, new BigDecimal("2.50"))),
          List.of(new LineItem("BULB", 3, new BigDecimal("2.50"))));

```

## Filtering with `filter` {#filtering-with-filter}

`filter(predicate, ma)` keeps a value that passes and gives a zero for one that fails: it is `flatMap(a -> predicate.test(a) ? of(a) : zero(), ma)`, with the arguments in `flatMap`'s order, the test first. Written once over `MonadZero`, one method filters any container that has a zero:

```java
  // One method for any container with a zero: keeps a line only when it asks for more than one
  public static <F extends WitnessArity<TypeArity.Unary>> Kind<F, LineItem> bulk(
      MonadZero<F> monadZero, Kind<F, LineItem> lines) {
    return monadZero.filter(line -> line.quantity() > 1, lines);
  }

```

```java
    MonadZero<ListKind.Witness> listZero = Instances.monadZero(list());
    MonadZero<MaybeKind.Witness> maybeZero = Instances.monadZero(maybe());

    List<LineItem> bulkLines = LIST.narrow(bulk(listZero, LIST.widen(BASKETS.getFirst())));
    // [LineItem[sku=BULB, quantity=4, price=2.50]]

    Maybe<LineItem> bulkLamp = MAYBE.narrow(bulk(maybeZero, MAYBE.just(LAMP)));
    // Nothing
```

Each type's zero is what a failed test leaves:

| Type | `zero()` | A failed test |
|---|---|---|
| `List` | `[]` | Drops that element from the list |
| `Maybe` | `Nothing` | Ends the chain at `Nothing` |
| `Optional` | `Optional.empty()` | Ends the chain at an empty `Optional` |
| `Stream` | An empty stream | Drops that element, lazily |

`Instances.monadZero(token)` gives the instance for `list()`, `maybe()`, `optional()` and `stream()`, and for any other token fails when it runs, as [what the compiler checks](instances_facade.md#what-the-compiler-checks) explains.

## Guarding a `For` comprehension with `when` {#guarding-a-for-comprehension-with-when}

A `For` comprehension given a `MonadZero`, as in `For.from(monadZero, ...)`, has a `when(test)` step, and so do `For.par` and `ForState.withState` given one. When the test fails for a combination, that combination gives a zero, and the comprehension drops it. The test sees what the comprehension holds so far: one value as itself, two or more as a tuple, and in `ForState` the state. Here it holds a basket and a line, so `t._2()` is the line, and `when` keeps only the lines that ask for more than one unit:

```java
    List<String> bulkSkus =
        LIST.narrow(
            For.from(listZero, LIST.widen(BASKETS))
                .from(basket -> LIST.widen(basket))
                .when(t -> t._2().quantity() > 1)
                .yield((basket, line) -> line.sku() + " x" + line.quantity()));
    // [BULB x4, BULB x3]
```

A comprehension started with a plain `Monad` has no `when`, and javac says so:

<!-- verify:rejects "cannot find symbol" -->
```java
For.from(Instances.monad(list()), LIST.widen(List.of(1, 2, 3)))
    .when(n -> n > 1)
    .yield(n -> n);
```

javac refuses it with "cannot find symbol", naming `when`. [For Comprehension](for_comprehension.md#filtering-with-when) shows `when` in a longer chain.

## Two laws {#two-laws}

A `MonadZero` keeps two laws beyond the `Monad` and `Alternative` ones. Together they say a zero ends a chain whichever end it is at.

~~~admonish note title="Laws"
- **Left zero: no step runs after a zero.** `flatMap(f, zero())` equals `zero()`.
- **Right zero: a step that gives a zero makes the chain a zero.** `flatMap(a -> zero(), fa)` equals `zero()`.

Two containers are equal when they hold equal values in the same shape. The laws assume functions that never return `null`, and [Values, never null](../hkts/core-concepts.md#values-never-null) says what each type does with one.

Checking these laws with `hkj-test` is not supported yet, so the test checks them by hand, for `Maybe` and `List`. `ORDER` is any `Order`, and the test uses these imports, with JUnit's:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import static org.assertj.core.api.Assertions.assertThat;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.list;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.maybe;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.list.ListKindHelper.LIST;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.maybe.MaybeKindHelper.MAYBE;

import java.util.List;
import java.util.function.BiPredicate;
import java.util.function.Function;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.Order;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Kind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.MonadZero;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.TypeArity;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.WitnessArity;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.assertions.KindEquivalence;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Instances;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.list.ListKind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.maybe.MaybeKind;
```

```java
  <F extends WitnessArity<TypeArity.Unary>, A, B> void assertMonadZeroLaws(
      MonadZero<F> monadZero,
      Kind<F, A> fa,
      Function<A, Kind<F, B>> f,
      BiPredicate<Kind<F, ?>, Kind<F, ?>> same) {
    // a zero ends the chain: no step after it runs
    assertThat(same.test(monadZero.flatMap(f, monadZero.<A>zero()), monadZero.zero())).isTrue();
    // a step that gives zero makes the chain zero
    assertThat(same.test(monadZero.flatMap(a -> monadZero.<B>zero(), fa), monadZero.zero()))
        .isTrue();
  }

  @Test
  @DisplayName("Maybe's and List's MonadZero keep the zero laws")
  void monadZeroLaws() {
    MonadZero<MaybeKind.Witness> maybeZero = Instances.monadZero(maybe());
    MonadZero<ListKind.Witness> listZero = Instances.monadZero(list());

    assertMonadZeroLaws(
        maybeZero,
        MAYBE.just(ORDER),
        order -> MAYBE.just(order.customer()),
        KindEquivalence.<MaybeKind.Witness>byEqualsAfter(MAYBE::narrow));
    assertMonadZeroLaws(
        listZero,
        LIST.widen(List.of(ORDER, ORDER)),
        order -> LIST.widen(order.lines()),
        KindEquivalence.<ListKind.Witness>byEqualsAfter(LIST::narrow));
  }

```
~~~

## Things people get wrong {#things-people-get-wrong}

~~~admonish warning title="Common Misunderstandings"
- **"Filtering needs a `For` comprehension."** `filter(predicate, ma)` filters directly, as `bulk` does.
- **"`when` works with any `Monad`."** Only a comprehension given a `MonadZero` has it.
- **"A filtered `Maybe` keeps the value it failed on."** It gives `Nothing`, and the value is gone, as it is from `optional.filter`.
- **"`Either` has a zero."** `Either` has no empty value to give, since a `Left` must hold an error. `Instances.monadZero(either())` compiles, and throws `ClassCastException` when it runs.
~~~

---

~~~admonish tip title="See Also"
- [Alternative](alternative.md): `empty` and `orElse`, which every `MonadZero` has
- [For Comprehension](for_comprehension.md): `when` among the other comprehension steps
~~~

---

**Previous:** [Alternative](alternative.md)
**Next:** [Applicative, Selective or Monad?](abstraction_levels.md)

<!-- Source: functional/abstraction_levels.md -->

<!-- description: Choose between Applicative, Selective and Monad by one question, and see why an applicative program can be read and checked before it runs. -->

# Applicative, Selective or Monad?

_Pick the least powerful of the three that does the job, and keep what that buys._

<!-- source: Arthur Conan Doyle, "A Scandal in Bohemia" (1891), in The Adventures of Sherlock Holmes; text checked against Project Gutenberg eBook #1661 -->
> _"It is a capital mistake to theorise before one has data."_
>
> — Arthur Conan Doyle, *A Scandal in Bohemia*

Theorising about a `flatMap` chain before it runs is Holmes's mistake: its next step is built from data that arrives only when the program runs. An applicative program names every step before any runs, so once it is held as data, you can read it first.

~~~admonish info title="What You'll Learn"
- Choose between `Applicative`, `Selective` and `Monad` by whether a step needs an earlier result
- Say what each level can express that the one before cannot
- Hold an applicative program as data, and count or check its instructions before it runs
- Say why a monadic program cannot be read in advance
~~~

~~~admonish example title="See Example Code"
**The code on this page is [AbstractionBook.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/abstraction/AbstractionBook.java)**, with examples from the [Applicative](applicative.md) and [Monad](monad.md) pages: the page includes them, so the build compiles and runs them. [AbstractionBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/abstraction/AbstractionBookTest.java) holds what the page says. [StaticAnalysisExample.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/basic/free_ap/StaticAnalysisExample.java) and [PermissionCheckingExample.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/basic/free_ap/PermissionCheckingExample.java) take the analysis further.
~~~

---

## Two questions decide {#two-questions-decide}

Ask whether an earlier result decides which effect runs next, and then whether you can name every effect it might choose before the program runs.

```mermaid
flowchart TD
    accTitle: Choosing between Applicative, Selective and Monad
    accDescr: If no earlier result decides which effect runs, use Applicative. If one does, and every effect it might choose can be named up front, use Selective. If the next effect is built from the result, use Monad.
    q1{"Does an earlier result decide<br/>which effect runs next?"}
    q2{"Can you name every effect<br/>it might choose, up front?"}
    app["Applicative<br/><small>map2 to map5</small>"]
    sel["Selective<br/><small>select, whenS, ifS</small>"]
    mon["Monad<br/><small>flatMap</small>"]
    q1 -->|No| app
    q1 -->|Yes| q2
    q2 -->|Yes| sel
    q2 -->|No| mon
    classDef decision fill:#e5c890,stroke:#df8e1d,color:#232634
    classDef domain fill:#a6d189,stroke:#40a02b,color:#232634
    class q1,q2 decision
    class app,sel,mon domain
```

In words: if results are only combined, as `map2` adds two prices, use `Applicative`. If a result only picks among effects you can name, use `Selective`. If the next step is built from the result, use `Monad`. Each level can express what the one before it can, and each step up gives something away. An applicative program held as data can be read before it runs. A selective one names every effect it might run, and a monadic one names only its first.

## Applicative: steps that do not depend on one another {#applicative}

The code on this page uses these imports, with `LineItem`, `Order`, `OrderLenses` and `OrderStatus` from the order records the [Optics](../optics/ch_intro.md) chapter introduces:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import static org.higherkindedj.example.book.foundations.OrderOpKindHelper.ORDER_OP;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.id.IdKindHelper.ID;

import java.math.BigDecimal;
import java.util.Map;
import java.util.function.Function;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.foundations.OrderOp;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.foundations.OrderOpInterpreter;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.foundations.OrderOpKind;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.LineItem;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.Order;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.OrderLenses;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.OrderStatus;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Applicative;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Kind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Monad;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.TypeArity;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.WitnessArity;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.free_ap.FreeAp;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.free_ap.FreeApAnalyzer;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.id.Id;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.id.IdKind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.id.IdMonad;
```

A line item's SKU, quantity and price are checked on their own, so `map3` combines them, and no check can change which others run. With `Validated`'s instance, every failing check is reported, as [Applicative](applicative.md) shows:

```java
  // One method for any container F: the Applicative passed in decides what a failure does
  public static <F extends WitnessArity<TypeArity.Unary>> Kind<F, LineItem> lineItem(
      Applicative<F> applicative,
      Kind<F, String> sku,
      Kind<F, Integer> quantity,
      Kind<F, BigDecimal> price) {
    return applicative.map3(sku, quantity, price, LineItem::new);
  }

```

## Selective: one of a few effects, named up front {#selective}

A large order is flagged for review, and a small one is not. The flag's effect is passed in before the condition runs, and the condition only decides whether it runs. `whenS` and `ifS` work this way, as [Selective](selective.md) shows. Every effect that might run is a value you passed in.

## Monad: the next step built from the last result {#monad}

Saving an order needs the order the lookup found, so the save is built from that result, and does not exist until the lookup runs. That is `flatMap`, as [Monad](monad.md) shows:

```java
  // One chain for any container F: save needs the order that was found
  public static <F extends WitnessArity<TypeArity.Unary>> Kind<F, Order> markPaid(
      Monad<F> monad, Kind<F, Order> found, Function<Order, Kind<F, Order>> save) {
    return monad.flatMap(
        order -> save.apply(OrderLenses.status().set(OrderStatus.PAID, order)), found);
  }

```

## Reading a program before it runs {#reading-a-program-before-it-runs}

An applicative program can be held as data, so you can check it before anything runs. Count its instructions to estimate the work, look for one that needs a permission, or log what it will do. A [Free Applicative](../monads/free_applicative.md) holds a program this way. Reading a `Selective` program before it runs is not supported yet, and a monadic one cannot be read in advance.

The instructions here are the order service's. Each is a record: `PriceOf` asks for a SKU's price, and `Cancel` cancels an order. Its `k` turns the instruction's result into the program's value, so `price -> price` keeps the price. [`@EffectAlgebra`](../effect/effect_handlers.md#defining-effects) generates `OrderOpKind`, `ORDER_OP` and `OrderOpInterpreter` from it.

```java
/**
 * The order service's instructions, held as data so that a program built from them can be inspected
 * before an interpreter runs it. {@code @EffectAlgebra} generates the {@code Kind} encoding, the
 * {@code Functor}, the interpreter base class and the {@code Free} constructors.
 */
@NullMarked
@EffectAlgebra
public sealed interface OrderOp<A> permits OrderOp.PriceOf, OrderOp.Cancel {

  /** Maps a function over the result type. */
  <B> OrderOp<B> mapK(Function<? super A, ? extends B> f);

  /**
   * Looks up the price of a SKU.
   *
   * @param sku the SKU to price
   * @param k continuation from the price to {@code A}
   * @param <A> the result type
   */
  record PriceOf<A>(String sku, Function<BigDecimal, A> k) implements OrderOp<A> {
    @Override
    public <B> OrderOp<B> mapK(Function<? super A, ? extends B> f) {
      return new PriceOf<>(sku, k.andThen(f));
    }
  }

  /**
   * Cancels an order.
   *
   * @param orderId the order to cancel
   * @param k continuation from {@link Unit} to {@code A}
   * @param <A> the result type
   */
  record Cancel<A>(UUID orderId, Function<Unit, A> k) implements OrderOp<A> {
    @Override
    public <B> OrderOp<B> mapK(Function<? super A, ? extends B> f) {
      return new Cancel<>(orderId, k.andThen(f));
    }
  }
}
```

A program prices two SKUs, neither depending on the other, and adds the prices:

```java
  // One instruction, held as data: nothing is priced until an interpreter runs it
  static FreeAp<OrderOpKind.Witness, BigDecimal> priceOf(String sku) {
    return FreeAp.lift(ORDER_OP.widen(new OrderOp.PriceOf<>(sku, price -> price)));
  }

  // Two prices that do not depend on one another, added together
  static final FreeAp<OrderOpKind.Witness, BigDecimal> LAMP_AND_BULB =
      priceOf("LAMP").map2(priceOf("BULB"), BigDecimal::add);

```

`FreeApAnalyzer` reads the program without running it:

```java
    int instructions = FreeApAnalyzer.countOperations(LAMP_AND_BULB);
    // 2

    boolean cancels =
        FreeApAnalyzer.containsOperation(
            LAMP_AND_BULB, op -> ORDER_OP.narrow(op) instanceof OrderOp.Cancel<?>);
    // false
```

An interpreter then decides what each instruction does, and `foldMap` runs the program with it. [`Id`](../monads/identity.md) adds nothing to a value, so the run gives the plain total:

```java
  // Prices from a catalogue: the interpreter decides what each instruction does
  static class CataloguePrices extends OrderOpInterpreter<IdKind.Witness> {
    private final Map<String, BigDecimal> catalogue =
        Map.of("LAMP", new BigDecimal("40.00"), "BULB", new BigDecimal("2.50"));

    @Override
    protected <A> Kind<IdKind.Witness, A> handlePriceOf(OrderOp.PriceOf<A> op) {
      BigDecimal price = catalogue.get(op.sku());
      if (price == null) {
        throw new IllegalArgumentException("no price for " + op.sku());
      }
      return ID.widen(Id.of(op.k().apply(price)));
    }

    @Override
    protected <A> Kind<IdKind.Witness, A> handleCancel(OrderOp.Cancel<A> op) {
      throw new UnsupportedOperationException("a price list cancels nothing");
    }
  }

```

```java
    BigDecimal total =
        ID.narrow(LAMP_AND_BULB.foldMap(new CataloguePrices(), IdMonad.instance())).value();
    // 42.50
```

A `flatMap` chain cannot be read this way: its second step is a function, and what that function does is known only when it runs on the first step's result.

## Things people get wrong {#things-people-get-wrong}

~~~admonish warning title="Common Misunderstandings"
- **"`Monad` is the safe default."** It can do everything, and costs what the others keep. `Validated`'s `map3` reports every failing check where its `flatMap` stops at the first, and a `flatMap` chain cannot be read before it runs.
- **"An applicative program runs its steps at the same time."** No instance on these pages does, as [Applicative](applicative.md#things-people-get-wrong) says.
- **"A `Selective` program can be analysed before it runs."** Only a Free Applicative program can be read first.
- **"Only built-in operations can be read first."** Any sealed set of instructions will do, such as `OrderOp`.
~~~

---

~~~admonish tip title="See Also"
- [Applicative](applicative.md): combining steps that do not depend on one another
- [Selective](selective.md): one of a few effects, chosen by an earlier result
- [Monad](monad.md): chaining steps that depend on the last result
- [Free Applicative](../monads/free_applicative.md): applicative programs held as data
~~~

~~~admonish tip title="Further Reading"
- **Chris Penner**, [Monads Are Too Powerful](https://chrispenner.ca/posts/expressiveness-spectrum): the article this page's question comes from
- **Andrey Mokhov, Georgy Lukyanov, Simon Marlow and Jeremie Dimino**, [Selective Applicative Functors](https://dl.acm.org/doi/10.1145/3341694): the paper that introduced `Selective`
~~~

---

**Next:** [Bifunctor](bifunctor.md)

<!-- description: Map an Either's error, its value or both with Bifunctor's bimap, write one method for any two-sided type, and check its laws with hkj-test. -->

# Bifunctor: Mapping Over Both Sides

_Map an error, a value or both, with one method written for any type with two sides._

~~~admonish info title="What You'll Learn"
- Map either side of a type with two type arguments, or both, with `bimap`, `first` and `second`
- Write one method for any two-sided type, through its `Bifunctor`
- Predict which of `bimap`'s two functions runs, for each instance
- Tell a type's `Bifunctor` from its `Functor`, and know which to use
- Check an instance against the four Bifunctor laws with `hkj-test`'s `BifunctorLaws`
~~~

~~~admonish example title="See Example Code"
**The code on this page is [BifunctorBook.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/bifunctor/BifunctorBook.java)**, with `OrderError` from [OrderError.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/OrderError.java): the page includes both, so the build compiles and runs them. [BifunctorBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/bifunctor/BifunctorBookTest.java) holds what the page says each instance does, and [FoundationsLawsBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/FoundationsLawsBookTest.java) checks the laws. [BifunctorExample.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/basic/bifunctor/BifunctorExample.java) maps an `Either`, a `Tuple2`, a `Validated` and a `Writer` in turn.
~~~

---

## Two sides, two functions {#two-sides-two-functions}

If you have caught a service's exception and thrown your own, while converting what it returns, you have written a `bimap` by hand. With the error held as a value, `bimap` converts both sides without throwing, and one method can serve any type with two sides.

An `Either<L, R>` holds an error or a value, and a `Tuple2<A, B>` holds two values. A [Functor](functor.md) maps one type argument and leaves the other alone. `Bifunctor` maps either of the two, or both.

For one value, each type has its own `bimap`, such as `Either.bimap` or an [Effect Path](../effect/ch_intro.md)'s `EitherPath.bimap`, and application code stays there. This page is for a method written once for any type with two sides. `Bifunctor<F>` declares `bimap`, and builds `first` and `second` from it:

<!-- verify -->
```java
public interface Bifunctor<F extends WitnessArity<TypeArity.Binary>> {
  <A, B, C, D> Kind2<F, C, D> bimap(
      Function<? super A, ? extends C> f, Function<? super B, ? extends D> g, Kind2<F, A, B> fab);

  default <A, B, C> Kind2<F, C, B> first(Function<? super A, ? extends C> f, Kind2<F, A, B> fab) {
    return bimap(f, Function.identity(), fab);
  }

  default <A, B, D> Kind2<F, A, D> second(Function<? super B, ? extends D> g, Kind2<F, A, B> fab) {
    return bimap(Function.identity(), g, fab);
  }
}
```

`bimap` takes a function for each side. `first` maps only the first side and `second` only the second, each passing `Function.identity()` for the side it leaves. `F` has [binary arity](../hkts/type-arity.md#unary-and-binary), so `Kind2<F, A, B>` keeps both type arguments open.

The code on this page uses these imports, with `Order` and `OrderStatus` from the order records the [Optics](../optics/ch_intro.md) chapter introduces:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.either.EitherKindHelper.EITHER;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.validated.ValidatedKindHelper.VALIDATED;

import org.higherkindedj.example.book.foundations.OrderError;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.Order;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.OrderStatus;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Bifunctor;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Kind2;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.TypeArity;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.WitnessArity;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.either.Either;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.either.EitherBifunctor;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.validated.Validated;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.validated.ValidatedBifunctor;
```

## One method for any two-sided type {#one-method-for-any-two-sided-type}

The order service refuses a request with an `OrderError`:

```java
/** Why the order service refused a request. */
public sealed interface OrderError {

  /** No order has this id. */
  record NotFound(UUID orderId) implements OrderError {}

  /** The order has shipped, so it can no longer change. */
  record AlreadyShipped(UUID orderId) implements OrderError {}

  /** Typed as {@code OrderError}, so a chain that starts with it can end in any other variant. */
  static OrderError notFound(UUID orderId) {
    return new NotFound(orderId);
  }

  static OrderError alreadyShipped(UUID orderId) {
    return new AlreadyShipped(orderId);
  }
}
```

A caller sees a message rather than the `OrderError`, and the order's status rather than the whole `Order`. `forCaller` makes that change on both sides, for any type with a `Bifunctor`:

```java
  // One method for any type with two sides: the error becomes the message a caller sees, and the
  // order becomes its status
  public static <F extends WitnessArity<TypeArity.Binary>> Kind2<F, String, OrderStatus> forCaller(
      Bifunctor<F> bifunctor, Kind2<F, OrderError, Order> result) {
    return bifunctor.bimap(BifunctorBook::message, Order::status, result);
  }

  static String message(OrderError error) {
    return switch (error) {
      case OrderError.NotFound _ -> "no such order";
      case OrderError.AlreadyShipped _ -> "the order has shipped";
    };
  }

```

The same method runs on an `Either` and on a `Validated`, where `ORDER` is any `Order` with status `NEW`. `widen2` and `narrow2` are `widen` and `narrow` for a `Kind2`, as [Bifunctor and Functor](#bifunctor-and-functor) explains:

```java
    Either<String, OrderStatus> found =
        EITHER.narrow2(forCaller(EitherBifunctor.INSTANCE, EITHER.widen2(Either.right(ORDER))));
    // Right(NEW)

    Validated<String, OrderStatus> missing =
        VALIDATED.narrow2(
            forCaller(
                ValidatedBifunctor.INSTANCE,
                VALIDATED.widen2(Validated.invalid(OrderError.notFound(ORDER.id())))));
    // Invalid(no such order)
```

`forCaller` never asks which side it was given. Each instance applies the function for the side its value holds.

## What `bimap` runs {#what-bimap-runs}

Whether one function runs or both depends on the instance:

| Type | `bimap` runs | Instance |
|---|---|---|
| `Either`: a `Left` or a `Right` | The first function on a `Left`, the second on a `Right` | `EitherBifunctor.INSTANCE` |
| `Validated`: an `Invalid` or a `Valid` | The first on an `Invalid`, the second on a `Valid` | `ValidatedBifunctor.INSTANCE` |
| `EitherOrBoth`: a `Left`, a `Right` or a `Both` | One on a `Left` or a `Right`, and both on a `Both` | `EitherOrBothBifunctor.INSTANCE` |
| `Tuple2`: two values | Both | `Tuple2Bifunctor.INSTANCE` |
| `Writer`: a log and a value | Both: the first on the log, the second on the value | `WriterBifunctor.INSTANCE` |
| `Const`: a value, and a type it holds no value of | Only the first: the second changes a type, and never runs | `ConstBifunctor.INSTANCE` |

These are all the instances Higher-Kinded-J ships. `Instances` has no entry for a type class with a binary witness, so take each from its `INSTANCE` field. [Const](../monads/const_type.md) explains why its second type is a [phantom type](../glossary/type-system.md#phantom-type).

## Bifunctor and Functor {#bifunctor-and-functor}

`Either` has two witnesses. `EitherKind.Witness<L>` fixes the error type, and is the one `Functor`, `Monad` and `MonadError` take. `EitherKind2.Witness` leaves both types open, and is the one `Bifunctor` takes. From the same `Either`, `EITHER.widen` gives the `Kind` a `Functor` takes, and `EITHER.widen2` the `Kind2` a `Bifunctor` takes.

`Instances.functor(either())` maps only the `Right`, as `second` does. Use the `Functor` when only the value changes, and the `Bifunctor` when the error changes too.

## Four laws {#four-laws}

A `Bifunctor` keeps four laws. The first two are [Functor's](functor.md#two-laws) laws for both sides at once. The last two tie `first` and `second` to `bimap`, and matter because most instances write their own, as `Either`'s `first` calls `mapLeft`.

~~~admonish note title="Laws"
- **Identity: mapping both sides with `a -> a` changes nothing.** `bimap(a -> a, b -> b, fab)` equals `fab`.
- **Composition: two bimaps equal one bimap of both pairs.** `bimap(f2, g2, bimap(f1, g1, fab))` equals `bimap(f1.andThen(f2), g1.andThen(g2), fab)`.
- **First: `first` is a `bimap` that leaves the second side alone.** `first(f, fab)` equals `bimap(f, b -> b, fab)`.
- **Second: `second` is a `bimap` that leaves the first side alone.** `second(g, fab)` equals `bimap(a -> a, g, fab)`.

Two values are equal when they hold equal values on the same sides. The laws assume functions that never return `null`, and [Values, never null](../hkts/core-concepts.md#values-never-null) says what each type does with one.

`BifunctorLaws`, in [`hkj-test`](../tooling/test_assertions.md#adding-the-dependency)'s `org.higherkindedj.hkt.laws` package, checks all four on any instance, your own included. You pass it the equality to use. `KindEquivalence` builds equalities for a `Kind`, not a `Kind2`, so the test writes this one as a lambda that narrows both sides. It uses these imports, with JUnit's:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.either.EitherKindHelper.EITHER;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.tuple.Tuple2KindHelper.TUPLE2;

import java.util.function.BiPredicate;
import java.util.function.Function;
import java.util.stream.Stream;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.foundations.OrderError;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.Customer;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.Order;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Bifunctor;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Kind2;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.TypeArity;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.WitnessArity;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.either.Either;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.either.EitherBifunctor;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.laws.BifunctorLaws;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.tuple.Tuple2;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.tuple.Tuple2Bifunctor;
```

The test checks the laws on an `Either`, both a `Right` and a `Left`, and on a `Tuple2`, where `ORDER` is any `Order`:

```java
  <F extends WitnessArity<TypeArity.Binary>, A, B> void assertBifunctorLaws(
      Bifunctor<F> bifunctor,
      Kind2<F, A, B> fab,
      Function<A, String> describe,
      Function<B, Customer> customerOf,
      BiPredicate<Kind2<F, ?, ?>, Kind2<F, ?, ?>> same) {
    BifunctorLaws.assertIdentity(bifunctor, fab, same);
    BifunctorLaws.assertComposition(
        bifunctor, fab, describe, String::toUpperCase, customerOf, Customer::name, same);
    BifunctorLaws.assertFirstConsistency(bifunctor, fab, describe, same);
    BifunctorLaws.assertSecondConsistency(bifunctor, fab, customerOf, same);
  }

  static Stream<Either<OrderError, Order>> results() {
    return Stream.of(Either.right(ORDER), Either.left(OrderError.notFound(ORDER.id())));
  }

  @ParameterizedTest
  @MethodSource("results")
  @DisplayName("Either's Bifunctor keeps all four laws, on a Right and on a Left")
  void eitherBifunctorLaws(Either<OrderError, Order> result) {
    assertBifunctorLaws(
        EitherBifunctor.INSTANCE,
        EITHER.widen2(result),
        error -> error.getClass().getSimpleName(),
        Order::customer,
        (a, b) -> EITHER.narrow2(a).equals(EITHER.narrow2(b)));
  }

  @Test
  @DisplayName("Tuple2's Bifunctor keeps all four laws, mapping both of its values")
  void tuple2BifunctorLaws() {
    assertBifunctorLaws(
        Tuple2Bifunctor.INSTANCE,
        TUPLE2.widen2(new Tuple2<>("checked", ORDER)),
        String::strip,
        Order::customer,
        (a, b) -> TUPLE2.narrow2(a).equals(TUPLE2.narrow2(b)));
  }

```
~~~

## Things people get wrong {#things-people-get-wrong}

~~~admonish warning title="Common Misunderstandings"
- **"`bimap` runs both functions."** Only where both sides hold a value: a `Tuple2`, a `Writer` and an `EitherOrBoth`'s `Both`. An `Either` or a `Validated` runs one.
- **"`bimap` can turn a `Left` into a `Right`."** It maps each side in place, so a `Left` stays a `Left`. To recover from an error, use `EitherPath.recover` or [MonadError](monad_error.md#recovering).
- **"`Const`'s `second` changes its value."** It changes only a type. A `Const` holds no value of its second type, so the function never runs.
- **"A type's `Functor` is its `Bifunctor`."** They are separate instances, over separate witnesses. `Either`'s `Functor` maps only the `Right`, as its `Bifunctor`'s `second` does.
~~~

---

~~~admonish tip title="See Also"
- [Profunctor](profunctor.md): two type arguments, where the first is what a function takes
- [Functor](functor.md): mapping a container with one type argument
- [Either](../monads/either_monad.md), [Validated](../monads/validated_monad.md) and [EitherOrBoth](../monads/either_or_both_monad.md): two-sided types whose first side is an error or a warning
- [Type Arity](../hkts/type-arity.md): binary witnesses, and how they differ from unary ones
~~~

~~~admonish tip title="Further Reading"
- **Mark Seemann**, [Bifunctors](https://blog.ploeh.dk/2018/12/24/bifunctors/): bifunctors explained with `Either` and tuples
~~~

---

**Previous:** [Applicative, Selective or Monad?](abstraction_levels.md)
**Next:** [Profunctor](profunctor.md)

<!-- Source: functional/profunctor.md -->

<!-- description: Adapt what a function takes and gives with Profunctor's lmap, rmap and dimap, and see why lmap's function goes from the new input to the old one. -->

# Profunctor: Building Adaptable Data Pipelines

_Adapt what a function takes with `lmap`, what it gives with `rmap`, and both at once with `dimap`._

<!-- source: Lewis Carroll, Through the Looking-Glass, and What Alice Found There (1871), chapter V, "Wool and Water"; text checked against Project Gutenberg eBook #12 -->
> _"It's a poor sort of memory that only works backwards," the Queen remarked._
>
> — Lewis Carroll, *Through the Looking-Glass*

A profunctor's adapters point both ways. `rmap`'s goes forwards, from the old output to the new one, and `lmap`'s goes backwards, from the new input to the old one.

~~~admonish info title="What You'll Learn"
- Adapt a function's input with `lmap`, its output with `rmap`, and both with `dimap`
- Say why `lmap`'s function goes from the new input to the old one, which is what contravariant means
- Write the same adaptation with `Function`'s `compose` and `andThen`, and know which to reach for
- Say how Higher-Kinded-J's optics relate to `Profunctor`
- Check the two Profunctor laws by hand
~~~

~~~admonish example title="See Example Code"
**The code on this page is [ProfunctorBook.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/profunctor/ProfunctorBook.java)**, which uses `lineTotal` from [MonoidBook.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/monoid/MonoidBook.java): the page includes it, so the build compiles and runs it. [ProfunctorBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/profunctor/ProfunctorBookTest.java) holds what the page says, and [FoundationsLawsBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/FoundationsLawsBookTest.java) checks the laws. [ProfunctorExample.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/basic/profunctor/ProfunctorExample.java) adapts functions for an API, a validation pipeline and a data transformation.
~~~

---

## A function has two ends {#a-function-has-two-ends}

You adapt a `Function<A, B>` at both ends already. `andThen` runs a function after it, from `B` to the new output. `compose` runs one before it, from the new input to `A`. The second points backwards: the input type changes from `A` to the new one, but the function you supply goes from the new input to `A`. So a function's input side is contravariant and its output side covariant, as [variance in the glossary](../glossary/type-system.md#contravariant) explains. A [Bifunctor](bifunctor.md)'s two sides are both outputs.

For one function, `compose` and `andThen` are all you need, as [Plain Java does the same](#plain-java-does-the-same) shows. On an [Effect Path](../effect/ch_intro.md), a `ReaderPath` is a function from an environment: its `local` adapts the input, and its `map` the output. `Profunctor` names the two adaptations for any type with an input and an output:

<!-- verify -->
```java
public interface Profunctor<P extends WitnessArity<TypeArity.Binary>> {
  <A, B, C, D> Kind2<P, C, D> dimap(
      Function<? super C, ? extends A> f, Function<? super B, ? extends D> g, Kind2<P, A, B> pab);

  default <A, B, C> Kind2<P, C, B> lmap(Function<? super C, ? extends A> f, Kind2<P, A, B> pab) {
    return dimap(f, Function.identity(), pab);
  }

  default <A, B, C> Kind2<P, A, C> rmap(Function<? super B, ? extends C> g, Kind2<P, A, B> pab) {
    return dimap(Function.identity(), g, pab);
  }
}
```

`lmap` and `rmap` are `dimap` with `Function.identity()` on the end they leave alone. For a function, `dimap(f, g, p)` runs `f`, then `p`, then `g`. Higher-Kinded-J ships one instance, `FunctionProfunctor.INSTANCE`, for `Function`, and `Instances` has no entry for it.

## Adapting a function {#adapting-a-function}

The code on this page uses these imports, with `LineItem` from the order records the [Optics](../optics/ch_intro.md) chapter introduces:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.func.FunctionKindHelper.FUNCTION;

import java.math.BigDecimal;
import java.util.function.Function;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.foundations.monoid.MonoidBook;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.LineItem;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Kind2;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.func.FunctionKind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.func.FunctionProfunctor;
```

[Semigroup and Monoid](semigroup_and_monoid.md)'s `lineTotal` takes a `LineItem` and gives its price times its quantity. The warehouse's feed sends each line as text, and a card payment is taken in pence:

```java
  // A line as the warehouse's feed sends it, such as "BULB,4,2.50"
  public static LineItem parse(String feedLine) {
    String[] fields = feedLine.split(",");
    return new LineItem(fields[0], Integer.parseInt(fields[1]), new BigDecimal(fields[2]));
  }

  // A total in pence, as a card payment is taken
  public static int pence(BigDecimal total) {
    return total.movePointRight(2).intValueExact();
  }

```

`lmap` lets `lineTotal` take the feed's text, `rmap` gives its result in pence, and `dimap` does both:

```java
    FunctionProfunctor profunctor = FunctionProfunctor.INSTANCE;
    Kind2<FunctionKind.Witness, LineItem, BigDecimal> total = FUNCTION.widen(MonoidBook::lineTotal);

    // lmap: the function now takes the feed's text, and parses it before it totals
    Function<String, BigDecimal> fromFeed =
        FUNCTION.getFunction(profunctor.lmap(ProfunctorBook::parse, total));
    BigDecimal bulbs = fromFeed.apply("BULB,4,2.50");
    // 10.00

    // rmap: the function now gives its total in pence
    Function<LineItem, Integer> inPence =
        FUNCTION.getFunction(profunctor.rmap(ProfunctorBook::pence, total));
    int lamp = inPence.apply(new LineItem("LAMP", 1, new BigDecimal("40.00")));
    // 4000

    // dimap: both at once, from the feed's text to pence
    Function<String, Integer> feedToPence =
        FUNCTION.getFunction(profunctor.dimap(ProfunctorBook::parse, ProfunctorBook::pence, total));
    int bulbsInPence = feedToPence.apply("BULB,4,2.50");
    // 1000
```

`FunctionProfunctor`'s `lmap`, `rmap` and `dimap` run nothing when you call them. Each builds a new function, and the adapters run every time that function is applied.

## Plain Java does the same {#plain-java-does-the-same}

For one function you hold, `Function`'s own `compose` and `andThen` give the same result, with no `Kind2` to widen or unwrap:

```java
    Function<LineItem, BigDecimal> lineTotal = MonoidBook::lineTotal;
    Function<String, Integer> plainJava =
        lineTotal.compose(ProfunctorBook::parse).andThen(ProfunctorBook::pence);
    int samePence = plainJava.apply("BULB,4,2.50");
    // 1000
```

~~~admonish tip title="Plain Java wins here"
To adapt one `Function`, use `compose` and `andThen`. `Profunctor` earns its place only in code written for any type with an input and an output, such as a parser or an encoder of your own that you give an instance.
~~~

## Optics adapt the same way, but are not built on it {#optics-adapt-the-same-way}

Higher-Kinded-J's optics are van Laarhoven optics. Each is built on one method, `modifyF`, which runs a function on the part the optic focuses on inside a `Functor` or `Applicative`, and rebuilds the whole. An `Optic`'s `modifyF` takes an `Applicative`, and a `Lens` also has one that needs only a `Functor`.

`Optic` has its own `contramap`, `map` and `dimap`, which adapt an optic's source and result as `lmap`, `rmap` and `dimap` adapt a function. [Profunctor Optics](../optics/profunctor_optics.md) shows them: that page is named for these adapters, not for the way the optics are built. `Optic` is not a `Profunctor` instance.

## Two laws {#two-laws}

A `Profunctor` keeps two laws: adapting with identities changes nothing, and two adaptations in turn equal one.

~~~admonish note title="Laws"
- **Identity: adapting both ends with `a -> a` changes nothing.** `dimap(a -> a, b -> b, p)` equals `p`.
- **Composition: two dimaps equal one dimap of both pairs.** `dimap(f2, g2, dimap(f1, g1, p))` equals `dimap(f2.andThen(f1), g1.andThen(g2), p)`. The input adapters join in the opposite order to the output ones, since the outer `dimap`'s `f2` runs first.

Two functions are equal when they give equal results for every input. A test cannot try every input, so it tries a few. The laws assume functions that never return `null`.

Checking the `Profunctor` laws with `hkj-test` is not supported yet, so the test checks them by hand, on a few inputs. It uses these imports, with JUnit's:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import static org.assertj.core.api.Assertions.assertThat;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.func.FunctionKindHelper.FUNCTION;

import java.math.BigDecimal;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.function.Function;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.foundations.monoid.MonoidBook;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.foundations.profunctor.ProfunctorBook;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.LineItem;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Kind2;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.func.FunctionKind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.func.FunctionProfunctor;
```

```java
  // Two functions are equal when they give equal results for every input; the test tries several
  <A, B> void assertSameResults(List<A> inputs, Function<A, B> expected, Function<A, B> actual) {
    for (A input : inputs) {
      assertThat(actual.apply(input)).isEqualTo(expected.apply(input));
    }
  }

  @Test
  @DisplayName("Function's Profunctor keeps both laws, on every input tried")
  void functionProfunctorLaws() {
    FunctionProfunctor profunctor = FunctionProfunctor.INSTANCE;
    Function<LineItem, BigDecimal> lineTotal = MonoidBook::lineTotal;
    Kind2<FunctionKind.Witness, LineItem, BigDecimal> p = FUNCTION.widen(lineTotal);

    assertSameResults(
        MonoidBook.LINES,
        lineTotal,
        FUNCTION.getFunction(
            profunctor.dimap(Function.<LineItem>identity(), Function.<BigDecimal>identity(), p)));

    Function<String, LineItem> f1 = ProfunctorBook::parse;
    Function<String, String> f2 = String::strip;
    Function<BigDecimal, Integer> g1 = ProfunctorBook::pence;
    Function<Integer, String> g2 = pence -> pence + "p";
    assertSameResults(
        List.of(" BULB,4,2.50", "LAMP,1,40.00 "),
        FUNCTION.getFunction(profunctor.dimap(f2.andThen(f1), g1.andThen(g2), p)),
        FUNCTION.getFunction(profunctor.dimap(f2, g2, profunctor.dimap(f1, g1, p))));
  }

```
~~~

## Things people get wrong {#things-people-get-wrong}

~~~admonish warning title="Common Misunderstandings"
- **"`lmap`'s function runs after the original."** It runs before, turning the input you have into the one the original takes.
- **"`Either` is a profunctor."** Both of its sides are results, so it is a [Bifunctor](bifunctor.md). A profunctor's first side is an input.
- **"Higher-Kinded-J's optics are profunctor optics."** They are van Laarhoven optics. `Optic.dimap` has the shape of `Profunctor`'s `dimap`, as a method on the optic.
- **"`dimap` runs its adapters when you call it."** `FunctionProfunctor`'s `dimap` builds a new function, and the adapters run each time that function is applied.
~~~

---

~~~admonish tip title="See Also"
- [Bifunctor](bifunctor.md): two type arguments, both of them results
- [Profunctor Optics](../optics/profunctor_optics.md): adapting an optic with `contramap`, `map` and `dimap`
- [Semigroup and Monoid](semigroup_and_monoid.md): where `lineTotal` comes from
- [Glossary](../glossary/type-system.md#variance-summary-table): covariant, contravariant and invariant, with Java examples
~~~

~~~admonish tip title="Further Reading"
- **Mojang**, [DataFixerUpper](https://github.com/Mojang/DataFixerUpper): Minecraft's Java library for migrating saved data, built on profunctor optics
- **Hablapps**, [Don't Fear the Profunctor Optics](https://github.com/hablapps/DontFearTheProfunctorOptics/blob/master/ProfunctorOptics.md): an introduction to profunctor optics, the encoding Higher-Kinded-J's optics do not use
~~~

# For with Optics

_Use lenses, Isos and prisms as `For` steps, and edit every element a traversal reaches._

<!-- source: William Blake, "Auguries of Innocence" (c. 1803), in Poems of William Blake, edited by Alice Meynell (1911); text checked against Project Gutenberg eBook #79363 -->
> _"To see the world in a grain of sand, / And a heaven in a wild flower; / Hold infinity in the palm of your hand, / And eternity in an hour."_
>
> — William Blake, *Auguries of Innocence*

Blake finds the whole world in one grain. An optic step goes the other way: from a whole record, it brings one part into the chain, as `focus` brings out a consignment's state.

~~~admonish info title="What You'll Learn"
- Add a field to a `For` chain with `focus`, a converted value with `through`, and a matched case with `match`
- Say what each optic step is short for, and which of them take an optic
- Edit every element an optic reaches with `ForTraversal`, and by position with `ForIndexed`
- Say what `ForTraversal`'s `filter` tests, and why its `Applicative` adds no effect
~~~

~~~admonish example title="See Example Code"
**The code on this page is [ForOpticsBook.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/foroptics/ForOpticsBook.java)**: the page includes it, so the build compiles and runs it. [ForOpticsBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/foroptics/ForOpticsBookTest.java) holds what the page says each step does.
~~~

---

## Optic steps in a `For` chain {#optic-steps-in-a-for-chain}

In plain Java you read a field with its accessor, convert a value with a method, and test for one case of a sealed type with `instanceof`. An optic does each of these as a value you can pass around. A `Lens` reads one field, an `Iso` converts a value to another type and back, and a `Prism` picks one case. `@GenerateLenses`, `@GeneratePrisms` and `@GenerateTraversals` write them for your records, as the [Optics](../optics/ch_intro.md) chapter explains.

On an [Effect Path](../effect/ch_intro.md), [`ForPath`](../effect/forpath_comprehension.md)'s `focus` takes a `FocusPath`, such as `ConsignmentFocus.state()`. A `ForPath` over a `MaybePath` or an `OptionalPath` has `match`, and so does one over a `NonDetPath` after its first step. This page is for `For`, over `Kind`.

| Step | In plain Java | Short for | Takes |
|---|---|---|---|
| `focus(lens)` | `consignment.state()` | `let(lens::get)` | A `Lens` straight after `For.from`, and a function of the tuple after that |
| `through(iso)` | `email.value()` | `let(iso::get)` | An `Iso`, straight after `For.from` only |
| `match(prism)` | `state instanceof ConsignmentState.Dispatched sent` | A `from` that gives the instance's `zero()` when the prism misses | A `Prism` straight after `For.from`, and a function of the tuple giving an `Optional` after that |

Each step adds the value it reads as the chain's next value. The three steps only read. `through` never calls the Iso's way back, so it adds a second value beside the first, as `let` would. `match` needs an empty value to give on a miss, so, like `when`, it is there only on a chain started from a [`MonadZero`](monad_zero.md), as [Filtering with `when`](for_comprehension.md#filtering-with-when) explains.

The code on this page uses these imports, with `EmailAddress`, `Order` and `LineItem` from the order records the [Optics](../optics/ch_intro.md) chapter introduces:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.id.IdKindHelper.ID;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.id;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.list;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.maybe;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.list.ListKindHelper.LIST;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.maybe.MaybeKindHelper.MAYBE;

import java.math.BigDecimal;
import java.math.RoundingMode;
import java.util.List;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.ConsignmentLenses;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.ConsignmentStatePrisms;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.EmailAddress;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.LineItem;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.LineItemLenses;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.Order;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.OrderTraversals;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Monad;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.MonadZero;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.expression.For;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.expression.ForIndexed;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.expression.ForTraversal;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.id.IdKind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Instances;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.list.ListKind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.maybe.Maybe;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.maybe.MaybeKind;
import org.higherkindedj.optics.Iso;
import org.higherkindedj.optics.indexed.Pair;
import org.higherkindedj.optics.util.IndexedTraversals;
import org.higherkindedj.optics.util.Traversals;
```

A consignment is the parcel an order ships in, with the address it goes to and the state it has reached. Its state is one case of a sealed interface:

```java
@GenerateLenses
@GenerateFocus
public record Consignment(UUID orderId, Address to, ConsignmentState state) {}
```

```java
/** Where a consignment has got to. */
@GeneratePrisms
public sealed interface ConsignmentState
    permits ConsignmentState.Pending, ConsignmentState.Dispatched, ConsignmentState.Returned {

  record Pending() implements ConsignmentState {}

  @GenerateFocus
  record Dispatched(Instant at) implements ConsignmentState {}

  @GenerateFocus
  record Returned(String reason) implements ConsignmentState {}
}
```

`CONSIGNMENTS` holds three: one dispatched to Leeds, one waiting to go to York, and one returned from Hull. The chain reads each one's state and keeps the dispatched one, and `List`'s instance drops the others:

```java
    MonadZero<ListKind.Witness> listMonad = Instances.monadZero(list());

    List<String> dispatched =
        LIST.narrow(
            For.from(listMonad, LIST.widen(CONSIGNMENTS))
                .focus(ConsignmentLenses.state())
                .match(t -> ConsignmentStatePrisms.dispatched().getOptional(t._2()))
                .yield((consignment, state, sent) -> consignment.to().city() + " " + sent.at()));
    // [Leeds 2026-10-02T08:00:00Z]
```

`focus` comes straight after `For.from`, so it takes the lens. By `match`, the chain holds two values, so `match` takes a function of the tuple that says which value the prism reads: `t._2()` is the state `focus` added.

An `EmailAddress` holds one `String`, so an Iso converts between the two without loss, and `through` adds the text beside the address. `ORDER` is an order of one lamp at 40.00 and four bulbs at 2.50:

```java
  // An EmailAddress holds one String, so the two convert both ways without loss
  public static final Iso<EmailAddress, String> EMAIL_TEXT =
      Iso.of(EmailAddress::value, EmailAddress::new);

```

```java
    MonadZero<MaybeKind.Witness> maybeMonad = Instances.monadZero(maybe());

    Maybe<String> domain =
        MAYBE.narrow(
            For.from(maybeMonad, MAYBE.just(ORDER.customer().email()))
                .through(EMAIL_TEXT)
                .yield((email, text) -> text.substring(text.indexOf('@') + 1)));
    // Just(example.com)
```

## Editing every element with `ForTraversal` {#bulk-operations-with-fortraversal}

In plain Java, editing some of an order's lines means streaming the lines, changing the ones you want, and building a new order around the new list. `ForTraversal.over(traversal, source, applicative)` does it as a list of steps, over every element a [Traversal](../optics/traversals.md) reaches, such as `OrderTraversals.lines()`:

| Step | What it does |
|---|---|
| `filter(test)` | Makes the `modify` and `set` steps after it change only the elements that pass |
| `modify(lens, f)` | Runs `f` on the field the lens reads, in each element |
| `set(lens, value)` | Sets that field in each element |
| `run()` | Gives the edited whole, inside the instance |
| `toList()` | Gives the elements that pass every filter |

The examples price lines with these helpers:

```java
  // Prices are rounded to pence, and prices lists each line's SKU and price for printing
  static BigDecimal tenPercentOff(BigDecimal price) {
    return price.multiply(new BigDecimal("0.90")).setScale(2, RoundingMode.HALF_UP);
  }

  static BigDecimal halfPrice(BigDecimal price) {
    return price.divide(BigDecimal.TWO, 2, RoundingMode.HALF_UP);
  }

  static List<String> prices(List<LineItem> lines) {
    return lines.stream().map(line -> line.sku() + " " + line.price()).toList();
  }

```

Here the lines with a quantity of two or more get ten per cent off, and `toList` names which lines those are:

```java
    Monad<IdKind.Witness> idMonad = Instances.monad(id());

    Order discounted =
        ID.unwrap(
            ForTraversal.over(OrderTraversals.lines(), ORDER, idMonad)
                .filter(line -> line.quantity() >= 2)
                .modify(LineItemLenses.price(), ForOpticsBook::tenPercentOff)
                .run());
    List<String> discountedPrices = prices(discounted.lines());
    // [LAMP 40.00, BULB 2.25]

    List<LineItem> bulk =
        ID.unwrap(
            ForTraversal.over(OrderTraversals.lines(), ORDER, idMonad)
                .filter(line -> line.quantity() >= 2)
                .toList());
    // [LineItem[sku=BULB, quantity=4, price=2.50]]
```

The chain runs over `Id`, the container that adds nothing, as [Identity](../monads/identity.md) explains, so `ID.unwrap` takes the order out. Every step takes a plain function, so no step has an effect to run, and the `Applicative` only wraps the result with its `of`. Over `List`'s instance, `run` gives a list of one order.

### What `filter` and `toList` read {#collecting-results}

`filter` tests each element as it was in the source, not as an earlier `modify` left it. A `modify` before the `filter` still changes every element, and a `filter` that follows it sees the values from before that change. `toList` reads the source too, so it lists the elements that pass the filters with none of the edits.

~~~admonish tip title="Plain optics win for one edit"
`Traversals.modify` runs one plain function through an optic, with no `Applicative` to pass and nothing to unwrap. `filtered` narrows the traversal to the lines that pass, and `andThen` goes on from each line to its price:

```java
    // filtered keeps the lines with a quantity of two or more, and andThen reaches each price
    Order viaOptics =
        Traversals.modify(
            OrderTraversals.lines()
                .filtered(line -> line.quantity() >= 2)
                .andThen(LineItemLenses.price()),
            ForOpticsBook::tenPercentOff,
            ORDER);
    List<String> viaOpticsPrices = prices(viaOptics.lines());
    // [LAMP 40.00, BULB 2.25]
```

`ForTraversal` reads better when several edits share a filter.
~~~

## Editing by position with `ForIndexed` {#position-aware-traversals-with-forindexed}

`ForIndexed.overIndexed(indexedTraversal, source, applicative)` has the same steps, with each element's index beside it. `filterIndex` tests the index, and `filter` the index and the element together. `modify` passes the index to its function, `set` takes a function of the index, and `toIndexedList` gives each element that passes the filters with its index. `IndexedTraversals.forList()` counts from 0, so a packing slip adds 1:

```java
  // A packing slip numbers its lines from 1; the traversal's index counts from 0
  static String slipLine(Pair<Integer, LineItem> indexed) {
    LineItem line = indexed.second();
    return (indexed.first() + 1) + ". " + line.quantity() + " x " + line.sku();
  }
```

The slip numbers every line. Then every second line goes half price: `filterIndex` keeps the odd indexes, and `modify`'s function takes the index beside the price:

```java
    List<String> slip =
        ID
            .unwrap(
                ForIndexed.overIndexed(IndexedTraversals.forList(), ORDER.lines(), idMonad)
                    .toIndexedList())
            .stream()
            .map(ForOpticsBook::slipLine)
            .toList();
    // [1. 1 x LAMP, 2. 4 x BULB]

    List<LineItem> secondHalfPrice =
        ID.unwrap(
            ForIndexed.overIndexed(IndexedTraversals.forList(), ORDER.lines(), idMonad)
                .filterIndex(index -> index % 2 == 1)
                .modify(LineItemLenses.price(), (index, price) -> halfPrice(price))
                .run());
    List<String> halfPrices = prices(secondHalfPrice);
    // [LAMP 40.00, BULB 1.25]
```

`ForIndexed`'s steps are plain functions too, and its `filter` tests the source element, as `ForTraversal`'s does. [Indexed Optics](../optics/indexed_optics.md) explains the indexed traversals.

## Things people get wrong {#things-people-get-wrong}

~~~admonish warning title="Common Misunderstandings"
- **"`through` keeps the two values in step."** It calls the Iso's `get` once, as `let(iso::get)` would. The way back never runs.
- **"Every chain has `match`."** Only a chain started from a `MonadZero` has it, since a miss gives the instance's empty value.
- **"`filter` tests what an earlier `modify` left."** It tests each element as it was in the source.
- **"`ForTraversal` runs an effect on each element."** Its steps are plain functions, and the `Applicative` only wraps the result.
~~~

---

~~~admonish tip title="See Also"
- [For Comprehensions](for_comprehension.md): the steps the optic steps join
- [ForState](forstate_comprehension.md): a chain whose values live in a record, changed through lenses, Isos and traversals
- [ForPath Comprehension](../effect/forpath_comprehension.md): `focus` and `match` on Effect Paths
- [Lenses](../optics/lenses.md), [Prisms](../optics/prisms.md), [Isomorphisms](../optics/iso.md) and [Traversals](../optics/traversals.md): the optics the steps take
~~~

---

**Previous:** [Profunctor](profunctor.md)
**Next:** [For with MTL Capabilities](for_mtl.md)

<!-- Source: functional/for_mtl.md -->

<!-- description: Chain a capability's operations in a For comprehension: read settings with MonadReader, change a basket with MonadState and keep a log with MonadWriter. -->

# For with MTL Capabilities

_Write a chain once for any container that can read settings, carry state or keep a log._

~~~admonish info title="What You'll Learn"
- Pass a `MonadReader`, `MonadState` or `MonadWriter` to `For.from`, and use its operations as steps
- Write a method for any container with a capability, and run it over `ReaderT`, `StateT` or `WriterT`
- Tell `MonadState`'s state from the record `ForState` carries
~~~

~~~admonish example title="See Example Code"
**The code on this page is [ForMtlBook.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/formtl/ForMtlBook.java)**, with `PricingConfig`, `Basket` and `AuditEntry` from the [Foundations examples](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/tree/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations): the page includes them, so the build compiles and runs them. [ForMtlBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/formtl/ForMtlBookTest.java) holds what the page says.
~~~

---

## A capability is a `Monad` {#a-capability-is-a-monad}

In plain Java, a method that needs the VAT rate takes it as a parameter, one that changes a basket gives back a new basket, and one that logs is handed a logger. A capability puts the same need in the type. `MonadReader<F, R>` reads an `R`, `MonadState<F, S>` reads and replaces an `S`, and `MonadWriter<F, W>` adds to a `W`. The interfaces follow Haskell's mtl, the monad transformer library, and each names one thing a container can do. [MTL Capabilities](../transformers/mtl_capabilities.md) explains why a method asks for a capability rather than a transformer.

On an [Effect Path](../effect/ch_intro.md), `ReaderPath`, `WithStatePath` and `WriterPath` give their own versions of these operations for one effect, such as `ReaderPath.asks(PricingConfig::vatRate)`, and application code stays there. This page is for a method written once for any container with the capability.

Each capability extends `Monad`, so `For.from` takes it, and its operations give the values the steps bind:

| Capability | Main operations | Provided by |
|---|---|---|
| [`MonadReader<F, R>`](../transformers/mtl_reader.md) | `ask()`, `asks(f)` and `local(f, ma)` | `ReaderTMonadReader`, for [`ReaderT`](../transformers/readert_transformer.md) |
| [`MonadState<F, S>`](../transformers/mtl_state.md) | `get()`, `put(s)`, `modify(f)` and `gets(f)` | `StateTMonadState`, for [`StateT`](../transformers/statet_transformer.md) |
| [`MonadWriter<F, W>`](../transformers/mtl_writer.md) | `tell(w)`, `listen(ma)` and `censor(f, ma)` | `WriterTMonad`, for [`WriterT`](../transformers/writert_transformer.md) |

`ask`, `asks`, `get` and `gets` give a value to bind, and `put`, `modify` and `tell` give `Unit`. `local`, `listen` and `censor` each take a whole computation, `ma`, and run it with a changed environment, beside its log, or with its log changed. Each capability's page lists the rest.

`StateTMonadState` takes the state type first and the container second, `StateTMonadState<Basket, IdKind.Witness>`, the other way round from `ReaderTMonadReader<IdKind.Witness, PricingConfig>` and `WriterTMonad`.

The code on this page uses these imports, with `Order` and `LineItem` from the order records the [Optics](../optics/ch_intro.md) chapter introduces:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.id.IdKindHelper.ID;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.id;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.reader_t.ReaderTKindHelper.READER_T;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.state_t.StateTKindHelper.STATE_T;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.writer_t.WriterTKindHelper.WRITER_T;

import java.math.BigDecimal;
import java.math.RoundingMode;
import java.util.List;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.foundations.AuditEntry;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.foundations.Basket;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.foundations.PricingConfig;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.LineItem;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.Order;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.OrderStatus;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Kind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Monad;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.MonadReader;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.MonadState;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.MonadWriter;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Monoids;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Pair;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.TypeArity;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.WitnessArity;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.expression.For;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.id.IdKind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Instances;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.reader_t.ReaderTMonadReader;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.state.StateTuple;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.state_t.StateTMonadState;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.writer_t.WriterTMonad;
```

## Reading settings with `MonadReader` {#reading-configuration-with-monadreader}

Pricing reads its settings from the environment:

```java
/**
 * What pricing reads from its environment: the VAT rate, and the delivery charge for an order below
 * the free-delivery threshold.
 */
public record PricingConfig(
    BigDecimal vatRate, BigDecimal deliveryCharge, BigDecimal freeDeliveryFrom) {

  public PricingConfig {
    Objects.requireNonNull(vatRate, "vatRate");
    Objects.requireNonNull(deliveryCharge, "deliveryCharge");
    Objects.requireNonNull(freeDeliveryFrom, "freeDeliveryFrom");
  }
}
```

`toPay` asks for the settings, works out the delivery charge from them, and adds VAT to the whole:

```java
  // What the customer pays: delivery is added below the threshold, then VAT on the whole
  public static <F extends WitnessArity<TypeArity.Unary>> Kind<F, BigDecimal> toPay(
      MonadReader<F, PricingConfig> pricing, BigDecimal net) {
    return For.from(pricing, pricing.ask())
        .let(
            config ->
                net.compareTo(config.freeDeliveryFrom()) >= 0
                    ? BigDecimal.ZERO
                    : config.deliveryCharge())
        .yield(
            (config, delivery) ->
                net.add(delivery)
                    .multiply(BigDecimal.ONE.add(config.vatRate()))
                    .setScale(2, RoundingMode.HALF_UP));
  }

```

The caller picks the container. Here it is `ReaderT` over `Id`, the container that adds nothing, and the settings arrive when the result is run:

```java
    Monad<IdKind.Witness> idMonad = Instances.monad(id());
    PricingConfig config =
        new PricingConfig(new BigDecimal("0.20"), new BigDecimal("4.95"), new BigDecimal("50.00"));
    ReaderTMonadReader<IdKind.Witness, PricingConfig> pricing = new ReaderTMonadReader<>(idMonad);

    BigDecimal small =
        ID.unwrap(READER_T.narrow(toPay(pricing, new BigDecimal("10.00"))).run().apply(config));
    // 17.94

    BigDecimal large =
        ID.unwrap(READER_T.narrow(toPay(pricing, new BigDecimal("60.00"))).run().apply(config));
    // 72.00
```

The same `toPay` runs unchanged over `ReaderT` on `CompletableFuture` in production, or on `Maybe`, since only the caller names the container.

## Changing a basket with `MonadState` {#threading-state-with-monadstate}

A basket gives back a new basket for each line added. `LAMP` is one lamp at 40.00, and `BULBS` four bulbs at 2.50:

```java
/** The lines a customer has chosen so far. Adding a line gives a new basket. */
public record Basket(List<LineItem> lines) {

  public static final Basket EMPTY = new Basket(List.of());

  public Basket {
    lines = List.copyOf(lines);
  }

  public Basket add(LineItem line) {
    return new Basket(Stream.concat(lines.stream(), Stream.of(line)).toList());
  }

  /** Each line's price times its quantity, summed. */
  public BigDecimal total() {
    return lines.stream()
        .map(line -> line.price().multiply(BigDecimal.valueOf(line.quantity())))
        .reduce(BigDecimal.ZERO, BigDecimal::add);
  }
}
```

Each step sees the basket the step before it left. `modify` gives `Unit`, so the next step's parameter is `_`:

```java
  // Each step sees the basket the step before it left
  public static <F extends WitnessArity<TypeArity.Unary>> Kind<F, BigDecimal> addBoth(
      MonadState<F, Basket> basket, LineItem first, LineItem second) {
    return For.from(basket, basket.modify(chosen -> chosen.add(first)))
        .from(_ -> basket.modify(chosen -> chosen.add(second)))
        .from(_ -> basket.gets(Basket::total))
        .yield((_, _, total) -> total);
  }

```

`runStateT` starts from an empty basket, and gives the value with the basket the steps left. This state is the State effect itself: it lives in the container, and only `get`, `put`, `modify` and `gets` reach it.

```java
    StateTMonadState<Basket, IdKind.Witness> basket = new StateTMonadState<>(idMonad);

    StateTuple<Basket, BigDecimal> after =
        ID.unwrap(STATE_T.narrow(addBoth(basket, LAMP, BULBS)).runStateT(Basket.EMPTY));
    BigDecimal total = after.value();
    // 50.00
    int lines = after.state().lines().size();
    // 2
```

## Keeping a log with `MonadWriter` {#accumulating-output-with-monadwriter}

The audit log is a list of entries:

```java
/** One thing that happened to an order, kept for the audit log. */
public record AuditEntry(UUID orderId, String event) {

  public AuditEntry {
    Objects.requireNonNull(orderId, "orderId");
    Objects.requireNonNull(event, "event");
  }
}
```

`ship` tells the log one entry for packing and one for shipping, and gives the order's new status:

```java
  // Each tell adds to the log, and the log's Monoid joins the entries
  public static <F extends WitnessArity<TypeArity.Unary>> Kind<F, OrderStatus> ship(
      MonadWriter<F, List<AuditEntry>> audit, Order order) {
    return For.from(audit, audit.tell(List.of(new AuditEntry(order.id(), "packed"))))
        .from(_ -> audit.tell(List.of(new AuditEntry(order.id(), "shipped"))))
        .yield((_, _) -> OrderStatus.SHIPPED);
  }

```

`WriterTMonad` takes the log's `Monoid`, here `Monoids.list()`, which joins the entries in the order they were told. Running gives the value and the log as a `Pair`, for `ORDER`, a paid order:

```java
    WriterTMonad<IdKind.Witness, List<AuditEntry>> audit =
        new WriterTMonad<>(idMonad, Monoids.list());

    Pair<OrderStatus, List<AuditEntry>> shipped =
        ID.unwrap(WRITER_T.narrow(ship(audit, ORDER)).run());
    List<String> events = shipped.second().stream().map(AuditEntry::event).toList();
    // [packed, shipped]
```

## Things people get wrong {#things-people-get-wrong}

~~~admonish warning title="Common Misunderstandings"
- **"`MonadState` and `ForState` are the same thing."** `MonadState`'s state lives in the container. [`ForState`](forstate_comprehension.md) carries a record of your own through any `Monad`, `Either`'s included, and changes it through lenses.
- **"A chain can take only one capability."** `For.from` takes one, but a step can call another's operations over the same `F`. [Combining Capabilities](../transformers/mtl_combining.md) shows a method that takes two.
- **"`modify` gives the new state."** It gives `Unit`. Read the state with `get` or `gets` in a later step, as `addBoth` does.
~~~

---

~~~admonish tip title="See Also"
- [For Comprehensions](for_comprehension.md): the steps the capabilities' operations fill
- [MTL Capabilities](../transformers/mtl_capabilities.md), with [MonadReader](../transformers/mtl_reader.md), [MonadState](../transformers/mtl_state.md) and [MonadWriter](../transformers/mtl_writer.md): each capability in full
- [ForState](forstate_comprehension.md): a chain whose values live in a record with named fields
~~~

~~~admonish info title="Hands-On Learning"
Practise writing methods against the capabilities in [Tutorial 04: Polymorphic Capabilities (MTL)](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/tutorial/transformers/Tutorial04_PolymorphicCapabilities.java) (14 exercises).
~~~
