<!-- description: Keep a chain's values in a record with named fields, and change them through lenses with ForState: guards, effects, traversals, Isos and zoom. -->

# ForState: Named State Comprehensions

_Keep a chain's values in a record's named fields, and change them through lenses._

<!-- source: Ludwig Wittgenstein, Tractatus Logico-Philosophicus (1922), proposition 5.6, translated by C. K. Ogden; text checked against Project Gutenberg eBook #5740 -->
> _"The limits of my language mean the limits of my world."_
>
> — Ludwig Wittgenstein, *Tractatus Logico-Philosophicus* (5.6, trans. C. K. Ogden)

A tuple gives a chain a language of `_1` to `_12`, and what you can say about each value shrinks to match. `ForState` gives each value a name.

~~~admonish info title="What You'll Learn"
- Start a `ForState` from a value you have with `withState`, or from a `For` chain with `toState`
- Change a record's fields through lenses with `update`, `modify` and `modifyVia`
- Guard the chain with `when`, given a `MonadZero`
- Edit a collection field with `traverse`, a field in another form with `modifyVia`, and part of the record with `zoom`
- Say when each step runs, and how `ForState` differs from the State monad
~~~

~~~admonish example title="See Example Code"
**The code on this page is [ForStateBook.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/forstate/ForStateBook.java)**, with [Checkout.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/forstate/Checkout.java): the page includes both, so the build compiles and runs them. [ForStateBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/forstate/ForStateBookTest.java) holds what the page says each step does.
~~~

---

## When tuple positions stop reading well {#when-tuple-positions-stop-reading-well}

In plain Java, each step's result goes in a local variable with a name. A [`For`](for_comprehension.md) chain keeps its values in a tuple instead, and by the fourth or fifth value `t._4()` says nothing about what it holds. Insert a step, and every later position moves. `ForState` keeps the values in a record you declare, and each step reads and writes them by name, through [lenses](../optics/lenses.md). A `For` chain stops at twelve values, and a record has as many fields as you give it.

An [Effect Path](../effect/ch_intro.md) has no `ForState`. On a Path, `map` with a lens changes a record, as in `path.map(order -> OrderLenses.status().set(OrderStatus.PAID, order))`, and `via` runs a step with an effect. This page is for a chain over `Kind`, written once for any container with a `Monad`.

The code on this page uses these imports, with `Order`, `Customer` and `EmailAddress` from the order records the [Optics](../optics/ch_intro.md) chapter introduces, and their generated lenses:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.either.EitherKindHelper.EITHER;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.either;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.maybe;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.maybe.MaybeKindHelper.MAYBE;

import java.math.BigDecimal;
import java.util.function.Function;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.foundations.foroptics.ForOpticsBook;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.Customer;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.CustomerLenses;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.EmailAddress;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.Order;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.OrderLenses;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.OrderStatus;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Kind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Monad;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.MonadZero;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.TypeArity;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.WitnessArity;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.either.Either;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.either.EitherKind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.expression.For;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.expression.ForState;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Instances;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.maybe.Maybe;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.maybe.MaybeKind;
import org.higherkindedj.optics.util.Traversals;
```

## Two ways in {#two-ways-in}

`ForState` starts from a whole value, so every field of the record has a value before the first step runs. There are two ways in:

- **`ForState.withState(monad, value)`** starts from a value you already have, such as an order to update.
- **`toState(build)`**, as a step of a `For` chain, builds the record from the values the chain has bound, taking one parameter per value, or the tuple. It is there at every arity, from one value to twelve.

Never fill a field with `null` for a later step to set. Gather the values with `For` first, and build the record with `toState` once you have them all, so that every field holds a value from the start, as [Values, never null](../hkts/core-concepts.md#values-never-null) asks.

Given a [`MonadZero`](monad_zero.md), `withState` and `toState` give a chain with `when` and `matchThen`, as `For.from` gives one with `when`.

## Updating an order {#updating-an-order}

`inStock` checks a line against what the warehouse holds:

```java
  // What the warehouse holds of each SKU; it holds no chairs
  static final Map<String, Integer> STOCK = Map.of("LAMP", 3, "BULB", 20, "DESK", 1);

  static Kind<MaybeKind.Witness, LineItem> inStock(LineItem line) {
    return STOCK.getOrDefault(line.sku(), 0) >= line.quantity()
        ? MAYBE.just(line)
        : MAYBE.nothing();
  }

```

`pay` starts from the order, with `MAYBE_MONAD` as `Instances.monadZero(maybe())`, and its steps run in order:

1. `when` lets only a new order through.
2. `traverse` checks every line with `inStock`, and one `Nothing` ends the chain.
3. `modifyVia` lower-cases the customer's email as text. `andThen` joins two lenses, from the order to its customer and on to the email.
4. `update` marks the order paid.

```java
  // Pay for a new order: every line must be in stock, and the email is kept in lower case
  public static Maybe<Order> pay(Order order) {
    return MAYBE.narrow(
        ForState.withState(MAYBE_MONAD, MAYBE.just(order))
            .when(current -> current.status() == OrderStatus.NEW)
            .traverse(OrderLenses.lines(), Traversals.forList(), ForStateBook::inStock)
            .modifyVia(
                OrderLenses.customer().andThen(CustomerLenses.email()),
                ForOpticsBook.EMAIL_TEXT,
                String::toLowerCase)
            .update(OrderLenses.status(), OrderStatus.PAID)
            .yield());
  }

```

`EMAIL_TEXT` is the Iso from [For with Optics](for_optics.md#optic-steps-in-a-for-chain):

```java
  // An EmailAddress holds one String, so the two convert both ways without loss
  public static final Iso<EmailAddress, String> EMAIL_TEXT =
      Iso.of(EmailAddress::value, EmailAddress::new);

```

`ORDER` is a new order of a lamp and four bulbs, its email typed with capitals. `SHIPPED` has shipped, and `BACKORDERED` asks for a chair:

```java
    Maybe<String> paid =
        pay(ORDER).map(order -> order.status() + " " + order.customer().email().value());
    // Just(PAID ada@example.com)

    Maybe<Order> alreadyShipped = pay(SHIPPED);
    // Nothing

    Maybe<Order> backordered = pay(BACKORDERED);
    // Nothing
```

## Gathering, then naming {#gathering-then-naming}

A receipt needs an order, a verified email and a total, and the first two come from steps with an effect. `For` gathers them, and `toState` names them in a `Checkout`, with `total` summing each line's price times its quantity. `@GenerateLenses` generates the checkout's lenses as `CheckoutLenses`:

```java
/** A checkout in progress: the order, the address its receipt goes to, and what it costs. */
@GenerateLenses
public record Checkout(Order order, EmailAddress sendTo, BigDecimal total) {

  public Checkout {
    Objects.requireNonNull(order, "order");
    Objects.requireNonNull(sendTo, "sendTo");
    Objects.requireNonNull(total, "total");
  }
}
```

```java
  // For gathers the order and a verified email; toState names them, with the total, in a Checkout
  public static <F extends WitnessArity<TypeArity.Unary>> Kind<F, String> receipt(
      Monad<F> monad,
      Kind<F, Order> found,
      Function<Customer, Kind<F, EmailAddress>> verify,
      BigDecimal discount) {
    return For.from(monad, found)
        .from(order -> verify.apply(order.customer()))
        .toState((order, email) -> new Checkout(order, email, total(order)))
        .modify(CheckoutLenses.total(), total -> total.subtract(discount))
        .zoom(CheckoutLenses.order())
        .update(OrderLenses.status(), OrderStatus.PAID)
        .endZoom()
        .yield(
            checkout ->
                checkout.order().status()
                    + ": "
                    + checkout.total()
                    + " to "
                    + checkout.sendTo().value());
  }

```

`zoom` narrows the chain to the order inside the checkout, so the `update` after it takes a lens on `Order`, and `endZoom` widens it back to the whole `Checkout`. With `Either`'s instance, a `Left` from `verify` stops the chain before `toState` builds anything:

```java
    Monad<EitherKind.Witness<String>> eitherMonad = Instances.monad(either());
    BigDecimal discount = new BigDecimal("5.00");

    Either<String, String> sent =
        EITHER.narrow(
            receipt(
                eitherMonad,
                EITHER.widen(Either.right(ORDER)),
                customer -> EITHER.widen(Either.right(new EmailAddress("ada@example.com"))),
                discount));
    // Right(PAID: 45.00 to ada@example.com)

    Either<String, String> unverified =
        EITHER.narrow(
            receipt(
                eitherMonad,
                EITHER.widen(Either.right(ORDER)),
                customer -> EITHER.widen(Either.left("email not verified")),
                discount));
    // Left(email not verified)
```

## Every step {#every-step}

| Step | What it does | Built on |
|---|---|---|
| `update(lens, value)` | Sets a field | `map` |
| `modify(lens, f)` | Runs `f` on a field | `map` |
| `from(f)` | Runs an effect, and keeps the record as it was | `flatMap` |
| `fromThen(f, lens)` | Runs an effect, and stores its value through the lens | `flatMap` |
| `when(test)` | Gives the instance's empty value when `test` fails | `MonadZero`'s `filter` |
| `matchThen(source, prism, target)` | Reads the `source` field, matches it with the prism, and stores the case through `target`; gives the empty value on a miss | `flatMap` |
| `matchThen(extract, target)` | The same, with a function that gives an `Optional` | `flatMap` |
| `traverse(lens, traversal, f)` | Runs `f`, which has an effect, on every element of a collection field, and stores the results | the traversal's `modifyF`, which runs `f` on each element inside the instance |
| `traverseOver(traversal, f)` | The same, where the record is itself the collection | the traversal's `modifyF` |
| `modifyThrough(traversal, f)` | Runs a plain `f` on every element the traversal reaches; a second form takes a lens into each element | `map` |
| `modifyVia(lens, iso, f)` | Runs `f` on the field in the Iso's other form, as `modify(lens.andThen(iso), f)` would | `map` |
| `updateVia(lens, iso, value)` | Sets the field to the Iso's `reverseGet` of `value` | `map` |
| `zoom(optic)` … `endZoom()` | Between the two, `update`, `modify` and `fromThen` take lenses on the part the optic reaches | the optic's `get` and `set` |
| `yield()` or `yield(f)` | Ends the chain with the record, or with `f` of it | Nothing, or `map` |

`when` and both `matchThen` steps are there only on a chain that started from a `MonadZero`. `zoom` takes a `Lens`, a `FocusPath` (the lens path `@GenerateFocus` writes) or an `Iso`. On a chain started from a `MonadZero` it also takes an `AffinePath`, a path to a part that may be absent, and gives the empty value when it is.

`For` has a `traverse` step too, which takes a `Traverse` instance and binds the collected results as a new value, as [Traversing inside a chain](for_comprehension.md#traversing-inside-a-chain) shows. `ForState`'s takes a lens and a traversal optic, and writes the results back into the record.

## Things people get wrong {#things-people-get-wrong}

~~~admonish warning title="Common Misunderstandings"
- **"`ForState` is the State monad."** It carries a record through any `Monad`'s `map` and `flatMap`, `Either`'s included. For the State effect, see `MonadState` in [For with MTL Capabilities](for_mtl.md#threading-state-with-monadstate).
- **"The steps run at `yield`."** Each step calls the instance's `map` or `flatMap` as you add it. Over `Maybe` or `Either`, a step's function runs then. Over `IO`, nothing runs until you run the result.
- **"`ForState` is better than `For` past three steps."** It needs a record and a lens for each field. For a short chain, `For`'s tuple is less to write, and [Which comprehension should you use?](for_comprehension.md#which-comprehension) helps you choose.
~~~

---

~~~admonish tip title="See Also"
- [For Comprehensions](for_comprehension.md): the chain `toState` starts from
- [For with Optics](for_optics.md): `focus`, `through` and `match` in a `For` chain, and `ForTraversal`
- [Lenses](../optics/lenses.md) and [Isomorphisms](../optics/iso.md): the optics the steps take
~~~

~~~admonish info title="Hands-On Learning"
Practise `ForState` in the [ForState journey](../tutorials/expression/forstate_journey.md) (13 exercises), and its traversal and Iso steps, with `For`'s `through`, in [Tutorial 04: Enhanced Optics Integration](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/tutorial/expression/Tutorial04_EnhancedOpticsIntegration.java) (10 exercises).
~~~

**Next:** [Core Types](../monads/ch_intro.md)

<!-- Source: monads/ch_intro.md -->

<!-- description: Find the Higher-Kinded-J core type for a task, such as Either for a typed error, Maybe for a missing value or IO for a side effect. -->

# Core Types

<!-- source: Rudyard Kipling, "The Elephant's Child", in Just So Stories (1902), the closing verse; text checked against Project Gutenberg eBook #2781 -->
> _"I keep six honest serving-men: / (They taught me all I knew) / Their names are What and Where and When / And How and Why and Who."_
>
> — Rudyard Kipling, *Just So Stories* (The Elephant's Child)

---

Each family asks one of them of a value: what, if anything, it holds; why it failed; when it runs; where its settings come from; how many there are; or who runs it.

A core type holds a value, as Java's `Optional` does, and the library adds its own. Application code reaches most of them through an [Effect Path](../effect/ch_intro.md), and generic code through type class instances; the [Foundations introduction](../hkts/foundations_intro.md) says which you are writing.

Read a page when a task needs its type, and skip the group until then. [Choosing a Type](supported-types.md#choosing-a-type) and [What each type supports](supported-types.md#what-each-type-supports) help you choose.

---

## Pages in this group {#pages-in-this-group}

- **Failure**
  - [Either](either_monad.md): A value, or the first typed error
  - [Validated](validated_monad.md): A value, or every error found, and [Accumulating Assembly](validated_assembly.md) for records of validated fields
  - [Try](try_monad.md): A value, or a caught exception
  - [EitherOrBoth](either_or_both_monad.md): A value, an error, or a value with warnings
  - [NonEmptyList](nonemptylist_monad.md): A list with at least one element, such as errors
- **Absence**
  - [Maybe](maybe_monad.md): A value, or nothing, and never `null`
  - [Optional](optional_monad.md): Java's `Optional`, with the same instances as `Maybe`
- **Deferred**
  - [IO](io_monad.md): A side effect described now and run later
  - [Lazy](lazy_monad.md): A value worked out when first asked for, then kept
  - [CompletableFuture](cf_monad.md): Java's asynchronous result, already running
- **Virtual threads**
  - [VTask](vtask_monad.md): A deferred task, which `runAsync`, `Par` and `Scope` run on virtual threads, with [Structured Concurrency](vtask_scope.md) and [Resource Management](vtask_resource.md)
  - [VStream](vstream.md): A stream that produces each element when pulled, with [HKT and Type Classes](vstream_hkt.md), [Parallel Operations](vstream_parallel.md), [Performance](vstream_performance.md), [Resource-Safe Streaming](vstream_resources.md) and [Advanced Features](vstream_advanced.md)
- **Environment and state**
  - [Reader](reader_monad.md): A value worked out from settings
  - [Context](context_scoped.md): Request values bound to a `ScopedValue`
  - [State](state_monad.md): A step that reads a state and gives a new one
  - [Writer](writer_monad.md): A value with a log beside it
- **Collections**
  - [List](list_monad.md): Java's `List`, read as many possible results
  - [Stream](stream_monad.md): Java's `Stream`, each element worked out when needed
- **Programs as data**
  - [Identity](identity.md): A value and nothing else, for generic code with no effect
  - [Trampoline](trampoline_monad.md): A recursion that runs without growing the stack
  - [Free](free_monad.md): A program written as data, for an interpreter to run
  - [Free Applicative](free_applicative.md): Independent steps an interpreter can inspect before running them
  - [EitherF](eitherf.md): Two instruction sets joined, so one program can use both
  - [Coyoneda](coyoneda.md): A `Functor` for any type, which joins its `map` calls into one
  - [Const](const_type.md): A value of its first type and none of its second, for folds and getters

---

~~~admonish info title="Hands-On Learning"
Practise the core types together in [Tutorial 07: Real World](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/tutorial/coretypes/Tutorial07_RealWorld.java) (6 exercises).
~~~

**Previous:** [ForState Comprehension](../functional/forstate_comprehension.md)
**Next:** [Either](either_monad.md)

<!-- Source: monads/either_monad.md -->

<!-- description: Return a typed error from a Java method as an Either instead of throwing an exception, and chain the steps that can fail. -->

# Either: Modelling Success or Failure

_Return a typed error as a value, and chain steps that stop at the first error._

~~~admonish abstract title="Either at a glance"
| | |
|---|---|
| **Models** | A success (`Right`) or a typed error (`Left`), never both |
| **Replaces** | A method that throws a checked exception |
| **Effect Path** | [`EitherPath`](../effect/path_either.md) |
| **Instances** | `Functor`, `Applicative`, `Selective`, `Monad`, `MonadError<L>`, `Foldable`, `Traverse`, `Bifunctor` |
| **Choose it when** | The first error, and why it happened, is enough; otherwise [Validated](validated_monad.md), or compare every type in [Choosing a Type](supported-types.md#choosing-a-type) |
~~~

~~~admonish info title="What You'll Learn"
- Return a typed error from a method as an `Either`, instead of throwing it
- Chain steps that can fail, and say which step's error reaches the end
- Read an `Either` with a `switch` or `fold`, without a getter that throws
- Choose between `Either`, `EitherPath`, `Try` and `Validated` for a failure
- Pass an `Either` to generic code through its `MonadError` instance
~~~

~~~admonish example title="See Example Code"
**The code on this page is [EitherBook.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/either/EitherBook.java)**: the page includes it, so the build compiles and runs it. [EitherBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/either/EitherBookTest.java) holds what the page says each method does.
~~~

---

## Failure as a value {#failure-as-a-value}

A method that can fail usually says so with a checked exception, as `Order find(UUID id) throws OrderNotFoundException` does. The failure is in the signature but not in the value: a lambda passed to `map` cannot throw it, and every caller must catch it or declare it again. `Either<L, R>` puts the failure in the return type, as an ordinary value. A `Right` holds the success, and a `Left` holds the error, often a sealed type that says which failure it was. Where `Optional` and `Maybe` only say that a value is missing, `Either` says why. You can think of it as `Maybe` with a story to tell about its `Nothing`.

The code on this page uses these imports. `Order` is one of the order records the [Optics](../optics/ch_intro.md) chapter introduces, and `OrderFocus` holds its generated optics:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import java.util.Map;
import java.util.UUID;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.foundations.OrderError;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.Order;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.OrderFocus;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.OrderStatus;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Unit;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.either.Either;
```

The error is a sealed interface, with one record for each way the order service can refuse:

```java
/** Why the order service refused a request. */
public sealed interface OrderError {

  /** No order has this id. */
  record NotFound(UUID orderId) implements OrderError {}

  /** The order has shipped, so it can no longer change. */
  record AlreadyShipped(UUID orderId) implements OrderError {}

  /** Typed as {@code OrderError}, so a chain that starts with it can end in any other variant. */
  static OrderError notFound(UUID orderId) {
    return new NotFound(orderId);
  }

  static OrderError alreadyShipped(UUID orderId) {
    return new AlreadyShipped(orderId);
  }
}
```

Each step returns its error rather than throwing it:

```java
  // Each step returns its error as a value, rather than throwing it
  static Either<OrderError, Order> find(Map<UUID, Order> orders, UUID id) {
    Order order = orders.get(id);
    return order == null ? Either.left(OrderError.notFound(id)) : Either.right(order);
  }

  static Either<OrderError, Order> requireOpen(Order order) {
    return order.status() == OrderStatus.SHIPPED
        ? Either.left(OrderError.alreadyShipped(order.id()))
        : Either.right(order);
  }

```

## Chaining steps that can fail {#chaining-steps-that-can-fail}

`flatMap` runs the next step on a `Right`'s value, and `map` changes the value with a function that cannot fail, here `OrderFocus.status().set`, which returns a copy of the order with a new status. On a `Left`, both pass the error on untouched:

```java
  static Either<OrderError, Order> markPaid(Map<UUID, Order> orders, UUID id) {
    return find(orders, id)
        .flatMap(EitherBook::requireOpen)
        .map(order -> OrderFocus.status().set(OrderStatus.PAID, order));
  }

```

`ORDERS` holds two orders, `PLACED`, which is open, and `SHIPPED`. No order has the id `UNKNOWN`:

```java
    Either<OrderError, Order> paid = markPaid(ORDERS, PLACED.id());
    // Right(Order[id=00000000-0000-0000-0000-000000000001, ..., status=PAID])
    Either<OrderError, Order> missing = markPaid(ORDERS, UNKNOWN);
    // Left(NotFound[orderId=00000000-0000-0000-0000-000000000009])
    Either<OrderError, Order> refused = markPaid(ORDERS, SHIPPED.id());
    // Left(AlreadyShipped[orderId=00000000-0000-0000-0000-000000000002])
```

<pre class="hkj-railway-diagram" role="img" aria-label="markPaid's two tracks. On the Right track, the order is found, passes requireOpen and is marked paid. A Left from find stays on the Left track, and nothing after it runs. A Left from requireOpen, for an order that has shipped, also ends on the Left track.">
         find    requireOpen    mark paid
Right  ════●══════════●═════════════●═════▶  Right(paid order)
                       ╲  the order has shipped
Left   ────●────────────●─────────────────▶  Left(error)
         no order:
         nothing after it runs
</pre>

The diagram shows the two ways onto the `Left` track: `find` finds no order, so `requireOpen` never runs, or `requireOpen` refuses an order that has shipped. Either way, the error reaches the end of the chain as the step returned it, and no `if` in `markPaid` checks for it.

## Reading an `Either` {#reading-an-either}

A `switch` over the two cases reads the result, and the compiler checks that every case is handled. A record pattern can look inside the error as well:

```java
  static String describe(Either<OrderError, Order> result) {
    return switch (result) {
      case Either.Right(Order order) -> "order is " + order.status();
      case Either.Left(OrderError.NotFound(UUID id)) -> "no order " + id;
      case Either.Left(OrderError.AlreadyShipped(UUID id)) -> "order " + id + " has shipped";
    };
  }

```

`fold(onLeft, onRight)` does the same with two functions, when both sides give one type. `orElse(other)` returns a `Right`'s value or `other`, and `orElseGet(recovery)` builds that fallback from the `Left`'s error. `getLeft` and `getRight` throw `NoSuchElementException` on the wrong side, so keep them for tests.

## Creating instances {#creating-instances}

```java
    Either<OrderError, Order> found = Either.right(PLACED);
    Either<OrderError, Order> notFound = Either.left(OrderError.notFound(UNKNOWN));

    // A step that succeeds with nothing to return
    Either<OrderError, Unit> saved = Either.right(Unit.INSTANCE);
```

A `Right` and a `Left` always hold a value. `Either.right(null)`, `Either.left(null)`, `orElse(null)` and a `map` or `orElseGet` whose function returns `null` throw `NullPointerException`, as [Values, never null](../hkts/core-concepts.md#values-never-null) says. A step with nothing to return gives `Unit.INSTANCE`.

## Methods {#methods}

| Method | What it does |
|---|---|
| `Either.right(value)`, `Either.left(error)` | Build a success or an error |
| `isRight()`, `isLeft()` | Say which side it holds |
| `map(f)`, `mapRight(f)` | Change a `Right`'s value; a `Left` passes through |
| `flatMap(f)` | Runs a step that can fail on a `Right`'s value; a `Left` passes through |
| `mapLeft(f)` | Changes a `Left`'s error; a `Right` passes through |
| `bimap(onLeft, onRight)` | Changes whichever side it holds |
| `swap()` | Turns a `Left` into a `Right`, and a `Right` into a `Left` |
| `fold(onLeft, onRight)` | Turns either side into one result |
| `orElse(other)`, `orElseGet(recovery)` | Return a `Right`'s value, or a fallback |
| `toOptional()` | Gives a `Right`'s value as an `Optional`, or an empty one for a `Left` |
| `ifLeft(action)`, `ifRight(action)` | Run an action for the side it holds |
| `getLeft()`, `getRight()` | Return that side's value, or throw `NoSuchElementException` |

A `Maybe` becomes an `Either` with `toEither(error)`, a `Validated` with `toEither()`, and an `Optional` with `Either.fromOptional(optional, error)`.

## Working with Kind {#working-with-kind}

Generic code, written once for any container, takes `Either` through its `MonadError` instance, as [MonadError](../functional/monad_error.md) shows. The instance works over `Kind<EitherKind.Witness<L>, R>`, so you widen an `Either` to pass it in, and narrow the result to read it. These are the extra imports:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.either.EitherKindHelper.EITHER;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.either;

import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Kind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.MonadError;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.either.EitherKind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Instances;
```

```java
    MonadError<EitherKind.Witness<OrderError>, OrderError> orderErrors =
        Instances.monadError(either());

    Kind<EitherKind.Witness<OrderError>, Order> shipped = EITHER.widen(find(ORDERS, SHIPPED.id()));
    Kind<EitherKind.Witness<OrderError>, Order> checked =
        orderErrors.flatMap(order -> EITHER.widen(requireOpen(order)), shipped);
    Kind<EitherKind.Witness<OrderError>, String> answer =
        orderErrors.handleError(orderErrors.map(order -> "open", checked), error -> "refused");

    Either<OrderError, Order> stillShipped = EITHER.narrow(checked);
    // Left(AlreadyShipped[orderId=00000000-0000-0000-0000-000000000002])
    Either<OrderError, String> reply = EITHER.narrow(answer);
    // Right(refused)
```

The instance's `flatMap` and `map` do what `Either`'s own methods do, and `handleError` turns an error into a value. Widening allocates nothing: `Either` implements `EitherKind` itself, so `widen` checks for `null` and returns the same object. `Either` has instances of other type classes too, such as `Traverse` and `Bifunctor`, each an object of its own, and *Either at a glance*, at the head of the page, lists them.

## Back to the one-liner {#back-to-the-one-liner}

```java
  static EitherPath<OrderError, Order> changeEmail(
      OrderRepository orders, UUID id, String newEmail) {
    return orders
        .find(id)
        .toEitherPath(OrderError.notFound(id))
        .map(order -> OrderFocus.customer().email().value().set(newEmail, order))
        .via(orders::save);
  }

```

In [the one-liner](../hkts/one_line_six_layers.md), `toEitherPath(OrderError.notFound(id))` turns the repository's `MaybePath` into an `EitherPath`, so a missing order becomes a typed `Left`. `via(orders::save)` runs `save` only on a `Right`, as `flatMap` does on this page, and a refused save is a `Left` too. An `EitherPath` holds an `Either` and calls its methods directly, and `run()` hands that `Either` back.

## When to use `Either` {#when-to-use-either}

| You need | Use |
|---|---|
| The first error from a run of steps, typed by your domain | `Either<MyError, A>` |
| The same steps as a fluent chain in application code | [EitherPath](../effect/path_either.md) |
| A failure that is a caught exception | [Try](try_monad.md) |
| Every error from checks that do not depend on one another | [Validated](validated_monad.md) |
| A success that can carry warnings beside it | [EitherOrBoth](either_or_both_monad.md) |
| Typed errors inside another effect, such as `CompletableFuture` | An [Effect Path](../effect/ch_intro.md), or the [EitherT](../transformers/eithert_transformer.md) transformer |

~~~admonish info title="Hands-On Learning"
Practise choosing between `Either`, `Maybe` and `Validated` in [Tutorial 06: Concrete Types](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/tutorial/coretypes/Tutorial06_ConcreteTypes.java) (7 exercises).
~~~

**Benchmarks:** `EitherBenchmark` measures how cheaply a `Left` passes along a chain. [Benchmarks & Performance](../benchmarks.md) says how to run it.

---

**Next:** [Validated](validated_monad.md)

<!-- Source: monads/validated_monad.md -->

<!-- description: Validate every field of a Java request with Validated and accumulate all the errors in one reply, not only the first. -->

# Validated: Accumulating Errors

_Check every field of a request, and report all the errors in one reply._

<!-- source: William Shakespeare, Hamlet, Act IV, Scene V (Claudius); text checked against Project Gutenberg eBook #1524 -->
> _"When sorrows come, they come not single spies, / But in battalions."_
>
> — William Shakespeare, *Hamlet* (IV.v)

A form's mistakes come the same way, and a fail-fast check reports them one spy at a time. `Validated` reports the battalion.

~~~admonish abstract title="Validated at a glance"
| | |
|---|---|
| **Models** | A valid value, or every error the checks found |
| **Replaces** | Error messages collected in a `List`, or Bean Validation's `Set<ConstraintViolation>` |
| **Effect Path** | [`ValidationPath`](../effect/path_validation.md) |
| **Instances** | `Functor`, `Applicative`, `Selective`, `Monad`, `MonadError<E>`, `Foldable`, `Traverse`, `Bifunctor` |
| **Choose it when** | The caller wants every error from checks that do not depend on one another; otherwise [Either](either_monad.md), or compare every type in [Choosing a Type](supported-types.md#choosing-a-type) |
~~~

~~~admonish info title="What You'll Learn"
- Report every failed check of a request in one result, instead of one per submit
- Join independent checks with `Validated.accumulate()`, and follow them with a check that needs their result
- Say why generic code gets every error from `Validated`'s `map2` but only the first from its `flatMap`
- Keep the same checks on a `ValidationPath`, and know which of its methods stop at the first error
- Choose between `Validated`, `ValidationPath` and Accumulating Assembly
~~~

~~~admonish example title="See Example Code"
**The code on this page is [ValidatedBook.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/validated/ValidatedBook.java)**: the page includes it, so the build compiles and runs it. [ValidatedBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/validated/ValidatedBookTest.java) holds what the page says each method does.
~~~

---

## The problem: death by a thousand submits {#the-problem-death-by-a-thousand-submits}

A customer types a delivery address at checkout and makes three mistakes: no street, no city, and a postcode of `XYZ`. They press Submit, and the page says **"street is required"**. They fill in the street and submit again, to see **"city is required"**. A third submit finally shows **"postcode XYZ is not a UK postcode"**.

Three round-trips. Three frustrations. Three errors that were all knowable from the very first submission.

The code on this page uses these imports, with `Address` from the order records the [Optics](../optics/ch_intro.md) chapter introduces:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.Address;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.nonemptylist.NonEmptyList;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.validated.Validated;
```

Each check returns a `Validated` rather than throwing. A `Valid` holds the checked value, and an `Invalid` holds the errors in a [`NonEmptyList`](nonemptylist_monad.md), since an invalid result always has at least one:

```java
  // Each check returns a valid value, or its error in a NonEmptyList
  static Validated<NonEmptyList<String>, String> street(String street) {
    return street.isBlank()
        ? Validated.invalidNel("street is required")
        : Validated.validNel(street.strip());
  }

  static Validated<NonEmptyList<String>, String> city(String city) {
    return city.isBlank()
        ? Validated.invalidNel("city is required")
        : Validated.validNel(city.strip());
  }

  static Validated<NonEmptyList<String>, String> postcode(String postcode) {
    return POSTCODE.matcher(postcode).matches()
        ? Validated.validNel(postcode)
        : Validated.invalidNel("postcode " + postcode + " is not a UK postcode");
  }

```

`POSTCODE` is a `Pattern` for the shape of a UK postcode.

Joined with `flatMap`, each check runs only when the one before it has passed, so the address takes three submits:

```java
  // flatMap runs a check only when the one before it passed
  static Validated<NonEmptyList<String>, Address> oneAtATime(
      String street, String city, String postcode) {
    return street(street)
        .flatMap(s -> city(city).flatMap(c -> postcode(postcode).map(p -> new Address(s, c, p))));
  }

```

```java
    Validated<NonEmptyList<String>, Address> first = oneAtATime("", "", "XYZ");
    // Invalid(NonEmptyList[street is required])
    Validated<NonEmptyList<String>, Address> second = oneAtATime("1 High Street", "", "XYZ");
    // Invalid(NonEmptyList[city is required])
    Validated<NonEmptyList<String>, Address> third = oneAtATime("1 High Street", "Leeds", "XYZ");
    // Invalid(NonEmptyList[postcode XYZ is not a UK postcode])
```

## Every error in one reply {#every-error-in-one-reply}

The three checks do not need one another's results, so none has to wait for another. Passed to `Validated.accumulate()` as arguments, every check runs, where `flatMap` put the later checks inside functions it might never call. `accumulate()` builds the address only when every check passed, and otherwise keeps every error, in the order of the checks:

```java
  static Validated<NonEmptyList<String>, Address> address(
      String street, String city, String postcode) {
    return Validated.accumulate()
        .and(street(street))
        .and(city(city))
        .and(postcode(postcode))
        .apply(Address::new);
  }

```

```java
    Validated<NonEmptyList<String>, Address> everyError = address("", "", "XYZ");
    // Invalid(NonEmptyList[street is required, city is required,
    //   postcode XYZ is not a UK postcode])
    Validated<NonEmptyList<String>, Address> valid = address("1 High Street", "Leeds", "LS1 4AP");
    // Valid(Address[street=1 High Street, city=Leeds, postcode=LS1 4AP])
```

```mermaid
flowchart TB
    accTitle: flatMap stops, accumulate runs every check
    accDescr: With flatMap, the street check fails, so the city and postcode checks never run and the result holds one error. With accumulate, the street, city and postcode checks all run, and the result holds all three errors.
    subgraph fast ["flatMap"]
        direction LR
        S1["street"] --> R1["Invalid: street"]
        C1["city, postcode: never run"]
    end
    subgraph all ["accumulate()"]
        direction LR
        S2["street"] --> R2["Invalid: street, city, postcode"]
        C2["city"] --> R2
        P2["postcode"] --> R2
    end
    fast ~~~ all
    classDef step fill:#8caaee,stroke:#1e66f5,color:#232634
    classDef error fill:#e78284,stroke:#d20f39,color:#232634
    class S1,C1,S2,C2,P2 step
    class R1,R2 error
```

The diagram shows the same three checks joined two ways. With `flatMap`, the first failure ends the run; with `accumulate()`, every check runs and every error is kept. Bean Validation also reports every violation, but of an object that already exists. A `Validated` builds the address only when every check passes, and its list of errors can never be empty. For a record with many fields, or errors that name the field they belong to, [Accumulating Assembly](validated_assembly.md) builds the record with `Validated.fields()`.

## When one check needs another {#when-one-check-needs-another}

Some checks need the value the others built. Whether the courier delivers to an address can only be asked of a whole address, so that check follows `accumulate()` with `flatMap`:

```java
  // This check needs a whole address, so it can only follow the others
  static Validated<NonEmptyList<String>, Address> deliverable(Address address) {
    String area = address.postcode().substring(0, address.postcode().indexOf(' '));
    return COURIER_AREAS.contains(area)
        ? Validated.validNel(address)
        : Validated.invalidNel("the courier does not deliver to " + area);
  }

  static Validated<NonEmptyList<String>, Address> deliveryAddress(
      String street, String city, String postcode) {
    return address(street, city, postcode).flatMap(ValidatedBook::deliverable);
  }

```

`COURIER_AREAS` holds the postcode areas the courier serves, such as `LS1`.

```java
    Validated<NonEmptyList<String>, Address> faraway =
        deliveryAddress("1 High Street", "York", "YO1 7HH");
    // Invalid(NonEmptyList[the courier does not deliver to YO1])
    Validated<NonEmptyList<String>, Address> blank = deliveryAddress("", "", "YO1 7HH");
    // Invalid(NonEmptyList[street is required, city is required])
```

An address with blank fields never reaches the courier check, so the reply holds only the fields' errors. Independent checks accumulate, and a check that needs their result follows them. [Applicative, Selective or Monad?](../functional/abstraction_levels.md) makes the same choice for any container.

## On a `ValidationPath` {#on-a-validationpath}

Application code can hold the same checks on a [`ValidationPath`](../effect/path_validation.md). `Path.accumulate()` keeps every error, as `Validated.accumulate()` does, and `via` chains a check that needs the value. These are the extra imports:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.effect.Path;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.effect.ValidationPath;
```

```java
  static ValidationPath<NonEmptyList<String>, Address> deliveryAddressPath(
      String street, String city, String postcode) {
    return Path.accumulate()
        .and(Path.validatedNel(street(street)))
        .and(Path.validatedNel(city(city)))
        .and(Path.validatedNel(postcode(postcode)))
        .apply(Address::new)
        .via(address -> Path.validatedNel(deliverable(address)));
  }

```

```java
    ValidationPath<NonEmptyList<String>, Address> onThePath = deliveryAddressPath("", "", "XYZ");
    // ValidationPath(Invalid(NonEmptyList[street is required, city is required,
    //   postcode XYZ is not a UK postcode]))
```

Two of a `ValidationPath`'s methods for joining Paths stop at the first error: `zipWith` and `zipWith3`. `zipWithAccum`, `zipWith3Accum`, `andAlso` and `andThen` keep every error.

## Methods {#methods}

| Method | What it does |
|---|---|
| `Validated.valid(value)`, `Validated.invalid(error)` | Build a valid value or an error |
| `Validated.validNel(value)`, `Validated.invalidNel(error)` | The same, with the error in a `NonEmptyList` |
| `Validated.validateThat(condition, error)` | Gives `Valid(Unit)` when the condition holds, and the error otherwise; the error may also come from a `Supplier` |
| `Validated.accumulate()` | Joins independent checks over `NonEmptyList` errors, keeping every error |
| `Validated.fields()` | The same, naming the field each error belongs to, as [Accumulating Assembly](validated_assembly.md) shows |
| `isValid()`, `isInvalid()` | Say which case it holds |
| `map(f)`, `mapError(f)`, `bimap(onError, onValid)` | Change the value, the error, or whichever it holds |
| `flatMap(f)` | Runs a check that needs the value; an `Invalid` passes through, so its errors are the only ones kept |
| `ap(function, semigroup)` | Applies a validated function, joining both errors with the semigroup when both are invalid |
| `fold(onInvalid, onValid)` | Turns either case into one result |
| `orElse(other)`, `orElseGet(supplier)` | Return the value, or a fallback |
| `orElseThrow(supplier)` | Returns the value, or throws the supplied exception |
| `get()`, `getError()` | Return the value or the error, or throw `NoSuchElementException` |
| `ifValid(action)`, `ifInvalid(action)` | Run an action for the case it holds |
| `asUnit()` | Replaces a valid value with `Unit`, and keeps an error |
| `toEither()` | Gives an `Either` holding the same value or error |

## Working with Kind {#working-with-kind}

Generic code takes `Validated` through its instance, from `Instances.validated(semigroup)`. The semigroup is how the instance joins two errors; `NonEmptyList.semigroup()` appends one list to the other. The instance's `map2` to `map5` keep every error, and its `handleErrorWith` can replace the errors with a fallback. These are the extra imports:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.validated.ValidatedKindHelper.VALIDATED;

import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Kind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.MonadError;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Instances;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.validated.ValidatedKind;
```

```java
    MonadError<ValidatedKind.Witness<NonEmptyList<String>>, NonEmptyList<String>> checks =
        Instances.validated(NonEmptyList.semigroup());

    Kind<ValidatedKind.Witness<NonEmptyList<String>>, Address> checked =
        checks.map3(
            VALIDATED.widen(street("")),
            VALIDATED.widen(city("")),
            VALIDATED.widen(postcode("LS1 4AP")),
            Address::new);
    Kind<ValidatedKind.Witness<NonEmptyList<String>>, Address> orPickup =
        checks.handleErrorWith(checked, errors -> checks.of(PICKUP_POINT));

    Validated<NonEmptyList<String>, Address> bothErrors = VALIDATED.narrow(checked);
    // Invalid(NonEmptyList[street is required, city is required])
    Validated<NonEmptyList<String>, Address> pickup = VALIDATED.narrow(orPickup);
    // Valid(Address[street=2 Station Road, city=Leeds, postcode=LS1 4DY])
```

`PICKUP_POINT` is an `Address` where an order can be collected instead. The postcode passed, so the reply holds the street's and the city's errors. `Validated` has instances of other type classes too, such as `Traverse` and `Bifunctor`, each an object of its own, and *Validated at a glance*, at the head of the page, lists them.

The instance's `map2` to `map5` keep every error, while its `flatMap`, and the `flatMap2` built on it, stop at the first, so generic code gets a different answer from each. [Where `Validated` bends a rule](../functional/applicative.md#where-validated-bends-a-rule) says why the two differ.

## When to use `Validated` {#when-to-use-validated}

| You need | Use |
|---|---|
| Every error from checks that do not depend on one another | `Validated`, with `accumulate()` |
| A record built from many checked fields, each error naming its field | [Accumulating Assembly](validated_assembly.md) |
| The same checks as a fluent chain in application code | [ValidationPath](../effect/path_validation.md) |
| The first error only, or a run of steps that each need the last one's value | [Either](either_monad.md) |
| A success that can carry warnings beside it | [EitherOrBoth](either_or_both_monad.md) |

~~~admonish info title="Hands-On Learning"
Practise accumulating errors with `map3`, beside `Either` and `Maybe`, in [Tutorial 06: Concrete Types](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/tutorial/coretypes/Tutorial06_ConcreteTypes.java) (7 exercises).
~~~

---

**Previous:** [Either](either_monad.md)
**Next:** [Accumulating Assembly](validated_assembly.md)

<!-- Source: monads/validated_assembly.md -->

<!-- description: Build a Java record from validated fields with Validated.fields(), and report every bad field at once, each error naming its field. -->

# Accumulating Assembly

_Building a record from N validated fields: every error reported at once._

~~~admonish info title="What You'll Learn"
- Build a record from independently validated fields with `Validated.fields()`, and report every bad field by name
- Read where each error belongs from its path, nested records included (`address.zip`)
- Guard a record whose constructor can refuse its fields, with `construct`
- Run the same assembly on `Validated`, `ValidationPath` or `EitherOrBoth`, and say what each does with a failed field
- Choose between the builder, `@GenerateAssembly`, `zipWithAccum` and the `mapN` family
~~~

~~~admonish example title="See Example Code"
**The code on this page is [ValidatedAssemblyBook.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/monads/assembly/ValidatedAssemblyBook.java)**: the page includes it, so the build compiles and runs it. [ValidatedAssemblyBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/monads/assembly/ValidatedAssemblyBookTest.java) holds the errors it reports.

[ValidatedAssemblyExample.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/basic/validated/ValidatedAssemblyExample.java) is a longer, standalone example.
~~~

## Which field was wrong? {#which-field-was-wrong}

[Validated](validated_monad.md#every-error-in-one-reply) checks fields independently and keeps every error. Unless a message spells out its field, as `street is required` does, a list such as `[not an email address, not a number]` names no field, and no code can read one back out. Whoever sent the request usually needs to know: a form shows each error beside its field, and an HTTP 422 body names the field it rejects. A request DTO becoming a domain record, or raw configuration becoming a settings object, needs every error *and* its field.

`Validated.fields()` gives each field a label as it joins the assembly, and every error that field reports carries the label.

---

## The front door: `fields()`

`Validated.fields()` opens a labelled assembly over `NonEmptyList<FieldError>`. A `FieldError` is a small record, `path` segments plus a `message`, with a `pathString()` such as `"address.zip"`. Each `field(label, value)` adds one validated field; `apply(...)` completes the assembly with a constructor reference or lambda of exactly the accumulated arity:

```java
    Validated<NonEmptyList<FieldError>, User> assembled =
        Validated.fields()
            .field("name", parseName(dto.name())) // Validated<NonEmptyList<FieldError>, Name>
            .field("email", parseEmail(dto.email()))
            .field("age", parseAge(dto.age()))
            .apply(User::new); // (Name, Email, Age) -> User

    // Invalid(NonEmptyList[email: not an email address, age: not a number]) - name was fine.
```

Three guarantees, all by construction:

- **Every bad field is reported at once.** Nothing short-circuits; accumulation is `NonEmptyList` concatenation.
- **Errors are located.** `field("email", ...)` prepends `"email"` onto each error's path via `FieldError.at`, so consumers can render `"email: not an email address"` or map errors onto form fields.
- **Errors emerge in field-declaration order.** The order of `field(...)` calls is the order of the errors, which makes downstream output (an HTTP 422 body, a CLI report) deterministic.

A leaf validator creates unlabelled errors with `FieldError.of("message")`; the assembly attaches the location.

### When the record refuses: `construct` {#construct}

A record often guards itself: a compact constructor that throws when its fields disagree. `apply` runs whatever function you hand it, so that exception escapes the assembly. It takes with it whatever an enclosing assembly had collected around it, and, on `EitherOrBoth`, the [tolerant carrier](#one-shape-three-carriers), the warnings this one had. `construct` completes the assembly the same way but guards the call. Once every field is valid, a `RuntimeException` the function throws becomes an unlabelled `FieldError` carrying the exception's message, or the fallback you pass when the message is missing or blank:

```java
// The record guards itself: a window must close after it opens.
record Window(LocalDate opens, LocalDate closes) {
  Window {
    if (!closes.isAfter(opens)) {
      throw new IllegalArgumentException("closes must be after opens");
    }
  }
}

    Validated<NonEmptyList<FieldError>, Window> window =
        Validated.fields()
            .field("opens", parseDate("2026-03-09"))
            .field("closes", parseDate("2026-03-07"))
            .construct(Window::new, "not a valid Window"); // apply(Window::new) would throw

    // Invalid(NonEmptyList[closes must be after opens]) - unlabelled: the whole window is refused
```

The rules are the generated mappings' own ([a record's own invariants](../mapping/absence.md#constructor-invariants)):

- **The function runs last.** It needs every field, so it runs only once all of them are valid, and a record reports either its fields' errors or its refusal, never both.
- **The refusal is unlabelled**, so the `field(label, ...)` that nests the assembly locates it: `window: closes must be after opens`.
- **Any `RuntimeException` counts**, so a bug in the function reaches the caller as its message, with its stack trace dropped. Keep the function to checks on its arguments, with messages written for whoever reads the errors.
- **Only the function is guarded.** A `null` it returns still throws, as it does from `apply`; only what the function itself throws is a refusal.
- **Every labelled builder has it**: `Validated.fields()`, `Path.fields()`, and `EitherOrBoth.fields()`. On the tolerant carrier a *failed* field still short-circuits the function, while a `Both` field's warning does not, so a refusal there comes back as a `Left` carrying those warnings and the refusal. The generic `accumulate()` builders have no `FieldError` to build, so they end in `apply` only.

### Nesting composes

Because `at()` *prepends*, assembling a sub-record under an outer label prefixes all its inner paths:

```java
    Validated<NonEmptyList<FieldError>, Address> address =
        Validated.fields()
            .field("street", parseStreet(raw.street()))
            .field("zip", parseZip(raw.zip())) // fails: "zip: not a postcode"
            .apply(Address::new);

    Validated<NonEmptyList<FieldError>, Customer> customer =
        Validated.fields()
            .field("name", parseName(raw.name()))
            .field("address", address) // prefixes: "address.zip: not a postcode"
            .apply(Customer::new);
```

Nesting is also the way past the builder's limit. `apply` takes a function of at most 16 fields, matching the shipped `Function3` to `Function16`, so a record with more fields nests a sub-record per group. That usually improves the domain model anyway, and [`@GenerateAssembly`](#generating-the-companion-generateassembly) has no limit of its own.

---

## The generic flavour: `accumulate()`

When your error type is not `FieldError`, `Validated.accumulate()` assembles the same way over `NonEmptyList<X>` for any payload `X`, joining fields with `and(value)`. [Validated](validated_monad.md#every-error-in-one-reply) shows it. It takes no `Semigroup`, the object that says how to join two errors, because the errors are always a `NonEmptyList`, joined by appending. The first of these trips up both builders, and the second only `accumulate()`:

~~~admonish note title="Two inference notes"
- **Inline literals need explicit type arguments.** A chained stage receives no target typing, so an inline factory call such as `.field("age", Validated.invalidNel(FieldError.of("bad")))` infers `Object` and the compile error surfaces later, at `apply`. Write `Validated.<FieldError, Integer>invalidNel(...)` for inline literals; values with declared types (the results of leaf validators) need none.
- **Error payloads are invariant.** Suppose an assembly collects a sealed `ConfigError`, and a leaf returns `Validated<NonEmptyList<PortError>, A>` for one of its subtypes, `PortError`. The leaf does not widen to the assembly's payload. Widen at the leaf: `parsePort(raw).mapError(nel -> nel.map(e -> (ConfigError) e))`, or have leaf validators return the hierarchy's root type directly.
~~~

---

## One shape, three carriers

The same two entry points exist on each carrier, so the assembly reads identically whichever error strategy the surrounding code uses:

| Carrier | Entries | Result | Behaviour |
|---|---|---|---|
| `Validated` | `Validated.fields()` / `Validated.accumulate()` | `Validated<NonEmptyList<E>, R>` | strict: any invalid field fails the assembly, all errors kept |
| `ValidationPath` | `Path.fields()` / `Path.accumulate()` | `ValidationPath<NonEmptyList<E>, R>` | the railway flavour; composes onward with `via`, `zipWithAccum`, `recover` |
| `EitherOrBoth` | `EitherOrBoth.fields()` / `EitherOrBoth.accumulate()` | `EitherOrBoth<NonEmptyList<E>, R>` | tolerant: warnings accumulate (`Both`) while the value keeps flowing; any `Left` dominates, still keeping every warning |

The tolerant flavour is the natural fit for lenient config parsing:

```java
    EitherOrBoth<NonEmptyList<String>, Config> cfg =
        EitherOrBoth.accumulate()
            .and(parsePortLenient(rawConfig.port())) // Both(NonEmptyList[port defaulted], 8080)
            .and(parseTimeoutLenient(rawConfig.timeout())) // Right(30)
            .apply(Config::new);
    // Both(NonEmptyList[port defaulted], Config[port=8080, timeout=30])
```

Under the hood every stage transition delegates to the existing accumulation primitives: `Validated.ap` with `NonEmptyList.semigroup()`, `ValidationPath.zipWithAccum`, and `EitherOrBoth.zipWithAccum`. The builder introduces no second accumulation mechanism.

---

## Generating the companion: `@GenerateAssembly` {#generating-the-companion-generateassembly}

For a record you own, the annotation processor generates a companion that removes three ways the hand-written chain can go wrong:

- **Labels come from the component names**, so a typo or a rename cannot break them.
- **Field order cannot be wrong**: the stage methods are named, and must be called in order.
- **The generator sets no limit on the number of fields** of its own: it emits the exact arity, even past 16.

```java
@GenerateAssembly
record User(Name name, Email email, Age age) {}


    Validated<NonEmptyList<FieldError>, User> generated =
        UserAssembly.fields()
            .name(parseName(dto.name())) // label "name" attached automatically
            .email(parseEmail(dto.email()))
            .age(parseAge(dto.age()))
            .assemble(); // canonical constructor baked in
```

A component whose type is itself annotated accepts its sub-companion's result directly, and the outer component name prefixes the inner paths (`address.zip`). The companion lives in the record's package, named `<Record>Assembly`; for a nested record the enclosing simple names are joined (`Outer.Inner` gives `OuterInnerAssembly`). Under the hood the companion merges through the same `Validated.ap` / `NonEmptyList.semigroup()` primitives as the builder, so the two agree on every input the record's constructor accepts. Where the constructor throws, they part. The builder's `apply` lets the exception propagate, while `assemble()` reports it as an unlabelled `FieldError` carrying the exception's message. That is the same [invariant guard](../mapping/absence.md#constructor-invariants) the generated mappings use. End the builder with [`construct`](#construct) instead of `apply` and the two agree again. Generic records are not supported yet; use the hand-written `fields()` builder for records you cannot annotate. The companion names the record and each component's type from the record's package, so the processor refuses one that is `private`, or nested in a `private` class, naming the class to change ([Compiler Messages](../optics/compiler_errors.md#companion-cannot-be-reached)).

---

## Choosing the right tool

| You are combining... | Reach for |
|---|---|
| N independent fields into a record | `fields()` / `accumulate()` |
| N validated fields into an **existing** value (sparse update / `PATCH`) | [`Edits.accumulate`](../optics/multi_edit.md) |
| A record you own, assembled in many places | [`@GenerateAssembly`](#generating-the-companion-generateassembly) |
| Two or three values, inline, no labels needed | `zipWithAccum` / `zipWith3Accum` on the Path types, or `EitherOrBoth.zipWithAccum` |
| Values inside generic `Kind`-polymorphic code | the `Applicative` `mapN` family |
| Steps where later ones depend on earlier results | `flatMap` / `via` (short-circuiting, by design) |

~~~admonish tip title="Testing located errors"
`hkj-test` asserts a whole accumulation as rendered `"path: message"` lines with `assertThatValidated(result).hasFieldErrors(...)`, and takes a single error apart with `assertThatFieldError`:

```java
    assertThatFieldError(FieldError.of("not a postcode").at("zip").at("address"))
        .hasPath("address.zip")
        .hasSegments("address", "zip")
        .hasMessageContaining("postcode");
```
~~~

~~~admonish info title="Key Takeaways"
- `fields()` assembles a record from N validated fields with located errors; `accumulate()` is the generic-payload twin.
- No `Semigroup` argument and no `Kind`. `apply` takes up to 16 fields; a wider record nests sub-records, or uses `@GenerateAssembly`.
- Errors always emerge in field-declaration order.
- Nesting prepends the outer label: `"address.zip"`.
- `construct(Record::new, "not a valid Record")` guards a constructor that may refuse the fields; `apply` does not.
- The same shape exists on `Validated`, `ValidationPath` (via `Path`), and `EitherOrBoth` (tolerant).
~~~

~~~admonish tip title="See Also"
- [Validated](validated_monad.md): the underlying strict accumulating type
- [NonEmptyList](nonemptylist_monad.md): the error carrier the builder is fixed to
- [EitherOrBoth](either_or_both_monad.md): the tolerant carrier
- [ValidationPath](../effect/path_validation.md): the railway validation API
- [Validated Prisms](../optics/validated_prism.md): a leaf parser with a faithful render-back *is* a `ValidatedPrism`: pass `vp.parse(raw)` to `field(label, ...)`. (`fields()` accumulates *siblings*; a prism's `andThen` short-circuits *nesting*.)
- [Applicative](../functional/applicative.md): the `mapN` family for `Kind`-generic code
- [Many Edits at Once](../optics/multi_edit.md): the same all-errors-at-once model for *updating* existing values
~~~

---

**Previous:** [Validated](validated_monad.md)
**Next:** [Try](try_monad.md)

<!-- Source: monads/try_monad.md -->

<!-- description: Run Java code that throws with Try, keep the exception as a value instead of a try/catch block, and chain and recover from it. -->

# Try: Capturing Exceptions as Values

_Run code that throws, and keep the exception as a value you can map, chain and recover from._

~~~admonish abstract title="Try at a glance"
| | |
|---|---|
| **Models** | A value, or the exception that stopped the computation |
| **Replaces** | A `try`/`catch` block around a call that throws |
| **Effect Path** | [`TryPath`](../effect/path_try.md) |
| **Instances** | `Functor`, `Applicative`, `Monad`, `MonadError<Throwable>`, `Foldable`, `Traverse` |
| **Choose it when** | The failure is an exception from code you call; otherwise [Either](either_monad.md), or compare every type in [Choosing a Type](supported-types.md#choosing-a-type) |
~~~

~~~admonish info title="What You'll Learn"
- Turn a call that throws into a `Try`, and choose between `Try.of` and `Try.attempt`
- Chain steps that can each throw, and say which exception reaches the end
- Recover from a failure with a value, or with another attempt
- Read a `Try` with a `switch` or `fold`
- Hand a `Try`'s failure to an `Either` chain through `TryPath`
~~~

~~~admonish example title="See Example Code"
**The code on this page is [TryBook.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/trymonad/TryBook.java)**: the page includes it, so the build compiles and runs it. [TryBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/trymonad/TryBookTest.java) holds what the page says each method does.
~~~

---

## Exceptions as values {#exceptions-as-values}

Much of the JDK reports failure by throwing: `Integer.parseInt`, `new BigDecimal` and `Files.readString` among them. A `try`/`catch` block splits the result in two, the value on one path and the exception in the `catch` block, so the outcome cannot be stored, passed to `map` or returned as one value. `Try<T>` runs the call and keeps what happened. A `Success` holds the value, and a `Failure` holds the exception the call threw. It is close to an `Either` whose left side is always an exception, with the `try`/`catch` written for you.

The code on this page uses these imports, with `LineItem` from the order records the [Optics](../optics/ch_intro.md) chapter introduces:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import java.math.BigDecimal;
import java.nio.file.Files;
import java.nio.file.Paths;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.LineItem;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.trymonad.Try;
```

A wholesaler's feed sends order lines as text, such as `LAMP,2,40.00`. Reading one calls two methods that throw on text they cannot read, so `Try.of` runs them:

```java
  // Integer.parseInt and new BigDecimal throw on text they cannot read
  static Try<LineItem> parseLine(String line) {
    return Try.of(
        () -> {
          String[] parts = line.split(",");
          return new LineItem(parts[0], Integer.parseInt(parts[1]), new BigDecimal(parts[2]));
        });
  }

```

```java
    Try<LineItem> lamp = parseLine("LAMP,2,40.00");
    // Success(LineItem[sku=LAMP, quantity=2, price=40.00])
    Try<LineItem> misspelt = parseLine("LAMP,two,40.00");
    // Failure(java.lang.NumberFormatException: For input string: "two")
```

`Try.of` takes a `Supplier`, and a `Supplier` cannot throw a checked exception, so javac refuses a call such as `Files.readString` there, with "unreported exception IOException; must be caught or declared to be thrown":

<!-- verify:rejects "must be caught or declared to be thrown" -->
```java
Try<String> feed = Try.of(() -> Files.readString(Paths.get("feed.csv")));
```

`Try.attempt` takes a function that may throw one. `MISSING_FEED` names a file that does not exist:

```java
  // Files.readString declares IOException, a checked exception
  static Try<String> readFeed(String file) {
    return Try.attempt(() -> Files.readString(Paths.get(file)));
  }

```

```java
    Try<String> feed = readFeed(MISSING_FEED);
    // Failure(java.nio.file.NoSuchFileException: missing-feed.csv)
```

Both catch any `Exception`, checked or not, and so do `Try`'s `map`, `flatMap`, `recover` and `recoverWith` when their function throws. A caught `InterruptedException` sets the thread's interrupt flag again. An `Error`, such as `StackOverflowError`, propagates out of them, as it passes a `catch (Exception e)` clause.

## Chaining and recovering {#chaining-and-recovering}

On a `Success`, `flatMap` passes the value to a step that returns a `Try` of its own. On a `Failure`, the step never runs, and the first exception reaches the end:

```java
  static Try<LineItem> requirePositivePrice(LineItem item) {
    return item.price().signum() > 0
        ? Try.success(item)
        : Try.failure(new IllegalArgumentException("price must be positive: " + item.price()));
  }

  static Try<LineItem> checkedLine(String line) {
    return parseLine(line).flatMap(TryBook::requirePositivePrice);
  }

```

```java
    Try<LineItem> free = checkedLine("LAMP,2,0.00");
    // Failure(java.lang.IllegalArgumentException: price must be positive: 0.00)
    Try<LineItem> unread = checkedLine("LAMP,two,0.00");
    // Failure(java.lang.NumberFormatException: For input string: "two")
```

`map` changes a `Success`'s value, and an exception its function throws becomes a `Failure` too. To leave the failure behind, `recoverWith` makes another attempt, and `recover` gives a value in its place:

```java
  // Some feeds put a pound sign before the price, so try again without it
  static Try<BigDecimal> price(String text) {
    return Try.of(() -> new BigDecimal(text))
        .recoverWith(
            error ->
                text.startsWith("£")
                    ? Try.of(() -> new BigDecimal(text.substring(1)))
                    : Try.failure(error));
  }

```

```java
    Try<BigDecimal> withSign = price("£40.00");
    // Success(40.00)
    Try<BigDecimal> unreadable = price("forty");
    // Failure(java.lang.NumberFormatException: ...)
    Try<String> noFeed = readFeed(MISSING_FEED).recover(error -> "");
    // Success()
```

A feed that cannot be read counts as empty here, so `recover` gives an empty `String`. Neither method runs its function on a `Success`, and an exception that function throws becomes the new `Failure`.

## Reading a `Try` {#handling-failures-foldfailurefirst-recover-recoverwith}

A `switch` over the two cases reads the result, and the compiler checks that both are handled:

```java
  static String describe(Try<LineItem> result) {
    return switch (result) {
      case Try.Success(LineItem item) -> item.quantity() + " x " + item.sku();
      case Try.Failure(Throwable cause) -> "unreadable line: " + cause.getMessage();
    };
  }

```

`fold(onFailure, onSuccess)` does the same with two functions, and takes the failure function first, as `Either.fold` takes the `Left` function first. `get()` returns the value or throws the exception, so it is declared `throws Throwable` and a caller must catch `Throwable`; keep it for tests.

## Creating instances {#creating-instances}

`Try.success(value)` and `Try.failure(throwable)` build either case directly. A `Success` always holds a value, so `Try.success(null)` throws `NullPointerException`. A function given to `Try.of`, `map` or `recover` that returns `null` gives a `Failure` holding a `NullPointerException`. NullAway flags one given to `Try.of` only with the options in [What a nullness checker sees](../hkts/core-concepts.md#what-a-nullness-checker-sees). A `flatMap` or `recoverWith` function that returns `null` in place of a `Try` throws `KindUnwrapException`. [Values, never null](../hkts/core-concepts.md#values-never-null) has the rule for every type.

## Methods {#methods}

| Method | What it does |
|---|---|
| `Try.of(supplier)` | Runs the supplier now, giving a `Success` of its value, or a `Failure` of the `Exception` it throws |
| `Try.attempt(supplier)` | The same, for a supplier that throws a checked exception |
| `Try.success(value)`, `Try.failure(throwable)` | Build either case directly |
| `isSuccess()`, `isFailure()` | Say which case it holds |
| `map(f)` | Changes a `Success`'s value; an exception `f` throws becomes a `Failure` |
| `flatMap(f)` | Runs a step that gives a `Try`; a `Failure` passes through |
| `recover(f)` | Turns a `Failure` into a `Success` of the value `f` gives |
| `recoverWith(f)` | Turns a `Failure` into the `Try` that `f` gives |
| `fold(onFailure, onSuccess)` | Turns either case into one result |
| `match(onSuccess, onFailure)` | Runs an action for the case it holds, and takes the success action first |
| `orElse(other)`, `orElseGet(supplier)` | Return the value, or a fallback |
| `get()` | Returns the value, or throws the exception it holds |
| `toEither(f)` | Gives an `Either` whose `Left` is `f` applied to the exception |

Releases before 0.5.0 had a `fold` that took the success function first. A call written for it compiles against this one wherever both functions accept either argument, and runs them the wrong way round. Before you move such code here, run `MigrateDeprecationsTo0_5_0` on it while it still compiles against 0.4.x, as [To 0.6.0](../release-history/upgrading.md#to-060) explains. `foldFailureFirst`, the same method under its 0.5.0 name, is deprecated for removal in 0.7.0.

## On a `TryPath` {#on-a-trypath}

Application code can hold the same steps on a [`TryPath`](../effect/path_try.md). `Path.tryPath` takes a `Try`, `via` chains a step, and `toEitherPath` turns the exception into an error of your own. From there, `via` chains on to `EitherPath` steps with the same error type, here `String`. These are the extra imports:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.effect.EitherPath;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.effect.Path;
```

```java
  static EitherPath<String, LineItem> lineOrReason(String line) {
    return Path.tryPath(parseLine(line))
        .via(item -> Path.tryPath(requirePositivePrice(item)))
        .toEitherPath(error -> "skipped " + line + ": " + error.getMessage());
  }

```

```java
    EitherPath<String, LineItem> skipped = lineOrReason("LAMP,2,0.00");
    // EitherPath(Left(skipped LAMP,2,0.00: price must be positive: 0.00))
```

## Working with Kind {#working-with-kind}

Generic code, written once for any container, takes `Try` through its `MonadError` instance, whose error type is `Throwable`, as [MonadError](../functional/monad_error.md) shows. The instance works over `Kind<TryKind.Witness, A>`, so you widen a `Try` to pass it in, and narrow the result to read it. These are the extra imports:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.try_;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.trymonad.TryKindHelper.TRY;

import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Kind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.MonadError;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Instances;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.trymonad.TryKind;
```

```java
    MonadError<TryKind.Witness, Throwable> tries = Instances.monadError(try_());

    Kind<TryKind.Witness, LineItem> parsed = TRY.widen(parseLine("LAMP,two,40.00"));
    Kind<TryKind.Witness, LineItem> checked =
        tries.flatMap(item -> TRY.widen(requirePositivePrice(item)), parsed);
    Kind<TryKind.Witness, String> answer =
        tries.handleError(
            tries.map(item -> "read " + item.sku(), checked),
            error -> "skipped: " + error.getClass().getSimpleName());

    Try<String> reply = TRY.narrow(answer);
    // Success(skipped: NumberFormatException)
```

The line could not be read, so `flatMap` never ran the price check, and `handleError` turned the exception into a reply. Widening allocates nothing: `Try` implements `TryKind` itself, so `widen` checks for `null` and returns the same object.

## When to use `Try` {#when-to-use-try}

| You need | Use |
|---|---|
| The exception from a JDK or library call, kept as a value | `Try` |
| The same steps as a fluent chain in application code | [TryPath](../effect/path_try.md) |
| An error type of your own, rather than `Throwable` | [Either](either_monad.md), converting with `toEither` |
| Every error from checks that do not depend on one another | [Validated](validated_monad.md) |
| Work described now and run later, rather than at once | [IO](io_monad.md) or [VTask](vtask_monad.md) |

~~~admonish info title="Hands-On Learning"
Practise recovering from errors with `MonadError`, and capturing an exception with `Try.of`, in [Tutorial 05: MonadError Handling](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/tutorial/coretypes/Tutorial05_MonadErrorHandling.java) (7 exercises).
~~~
