<!-- Source: monads/either_or_both_monad.md -->

<!-- description: Return a Java result that succeeds with warnings using EitherOrBoth, keep each non-fatal problem beside the value, and decide at the boundary what to do. -->

# EitherOrBoth: Success with Warnings

_Keep a value's warnings beside it, and decide at the boundary what they mean._

~~~admonish abstract title="EitherOrBoth at a glance"
| | |
|---|---|
| **Models** | A value, an error, or a value with warnings beside it |
| **Replaces** | A result record holding a value and a list of warnings |
| **Effect Path** | [`EitherOrBothPath`](../effect/path_either_or_both.md) |
| **Instances** | `Functor`, `Applicative`, `Monad`, `Foldable`, `Traverse`, `Bifunctor` |
| **Choose it when** | A success can carry problems that are not fatal; otherwise [Validated](validated_monad.md), or compare every type in [Choosing a Type](supported-types.md#choosing-a-type) |
~~~

~~~admonish info title="What You'll Learn"
- Return a value with warnings as a `Both`, keeping a fatal problem as a `Left`
- Join independent checks and keep every warning, even when one check is fatal
- Predict what `flatMap` does with the warnings of each case
- Decide at the boundary what happens to a `Both`'s warnings
- Keep the same steps on an `EitherOrBothPath`
~~~

~~~admonish example title="See Example Code"
**The code on this page is [EitherOrBothBook.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/eitherorboth/EitherOrBothBook.java)**: the page includes it, so the build compiles and runs it. [EitherOrBothBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/eitherorboth/EitherOrBothBookTest.java) holds what the page says each method does.
~~~

---

## A success with warnings {#a-success-with-warnings}

[Either](either_monad.md) and [Validated](validated_monad.md) are exclusive: a result is a success or a failure, never both. Some outcomes are both. An order line imported from a wholesaler's feed may need correcting before it can be used: a lower-case SKU upper-cased, or a price with a fraction of a penny rounded. The line is usable, and the importer should still report what it changed. Java code usually returns a record of the value and a `List` of warnings, which no `map` or `flatMap` knows how to carry.

`EitherOrBoth<L, R>` is an inclusive-or: a `Left`, a `Right`, or `Both` at once. Success goes on the right and problems on the left, so a `Both` is a value with the warnings found while producing it. Cats calls the type `Ior`, and Haskell `These`.

The code on this page uses these imports, with `LineItem` from the order records the [Optics](../optics/ch_intro.md) chapter introduces:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import java.math.BigDecimal;
import java.math.RoundingMode;
import java.util.Locale;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.LineItem;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.either.Either;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.eitherorboth.EitherOrBoth;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.maybe.Maybe;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.nonemptylist.NonEmptyList;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.validated.Validated;
```

Each of these checks passes, fails, or passes with something to say. A warning goes in a [`NonEmptyList`](nonemptylist_monad.md), since a `Both` always has at least one:

```java
  // A check can pass, fail, or pass with something to say
  static EitherOrBoth<NonEmptyList<String>, String> sku(String sku) {
    if (sku.isBlank()) {
      return EitherOrBoth.left(NonEmptyList.single("sku is missing"));
    }
    String upper = sku.toUpperCase(Locale.ROOT);
    return upper.equals(sku)
        ? EitherOrBoth.right(sku)
        : EitherOrBoth.both(NonEmptyList.single("sku " + sku + " upper-cased"), upper);
  }

  static EitherOrBoth<NonEmptyList<String>, Integer> quantity(int quantity) {
    return quantity > 0
        ? EitherOrBoth.right(quantity)
        : EitherOrBoth.left(NonEmptyList.single("quantity " + quantity + " is not positive"));
  }

  static EitherOrBoth<NonEmptyList<String>, BigDecimal> price(BigDecimal price) {
    if (price.scale() <= 2) {
      return EitherOrBoth.right(price);
    }
    BigDecimal rounded = price.setScale(2, RoundingMode.HALF_UP);
    return EitherOrBoth.both(
        NonEmptyList.single("price " + price + " rounded to " + rounded), rounded);
  }

```

## Every warning in one result {#every-warning-in-one-result}

`EitherOrBoth.accumulate()` joins independent checks, as `Validated.accumulate()` does, and keeps the value flowing past each warning. A single fatal check makes the whole result a `Left`, which holds the fatal problem beside every warning, in the order of the checks:

```java
  static EitherOrBoth<NonEmptyList<String>, LineItem> line(
      String sku, int quantity, BigDecimal price) {
    return EitherOrBoth.accumulate()
        .and(sku(sku))
        .and(quantity(quantity))
        .and(price(price))
        .apply(LineItem::new);
  }

```

```java
    EitherOrBoth<NonEmptyList<String>, LineItem> clean = line("LAMP", 2, new BigDecimal("40.00"));
    // Right(LineItem[sku=LAMP, quantity=2, price=40.00])
    EitherOrBoth<NonEmptyList<String>, LineItem> corrected =
        line("lamp", 2, new BigDecimal("40.005"));
    // Both(NonEmptyList[sku lamp upper-cased, price 40.005 rounded to 40.01],
    //   LineItem[sku=LAMP, quantity=2, price=40.01])
    EitherOrBoth<NonEmptyList<String>, LineItem> refused =
        line("lamp", 0, new BigDecimal("40.005"));
    // Left(NonEmptyList[sku lamp upper-cased, quantity 0 is not positive,
    //   price 40.005 rounded to 40.01])
```

A `switch` over the three cases reads the result, and the compiler checks that each is handled:

```java
  static String describe(EitherOrBoth<NonEmptyList<String>, LineItem> result) {
    return switch (result) {
      case EitherOrBoth.Right(LineItem line) -> "imported " + line.sku();
      case EitherOrBoth.Both(NonEmptyList<String> warnings, LineItem line) ->
          "imported " + line.sku() + " with " + warnings.size() + " warning(s)";
      case EitherOrBoth.Left(NonEmptyList<String> problems) ->
          "refused: " + String.join("; ", problems.toJavaList());
    };
  }

```

[Accumulating Assembly](validated_assembly.md) covers the builder in full, including `EitherOrBoth.fields()`, which names the field each warning belongs to.

## The `flatMap` contract {#the-flatmap-contract}

A step that needs the whole line can only follow the checks. `flatMap(semigroup, step)` takes a [`Semigroup`](../functional/semigroup_and_monoid.md), which joins two values, as well as the step, because a `Both`'s warnings must be joined with any the step adds. `accumulate()` needs none, because it takes `NonEmptyList` warnings and joins them itself. `REVIEW_LIMIT` is 100.00, and a line whose total exceeds it should be checked by a person:

```java
  // This step needs the whole line, so it can only follow the checks
  static EitherOrBoth<NonEmptyList<String>, LineItem> review(LineItem line) {
    BigDecimal total = line.price().multiply(BigDecimal.valueOf(line.quantity()));
    return total.compareTo(REVIEW_LIMIT) > 0
        ? EitherOrBoth.both(NonEmptyList.single("line total " + total + " flagged"), line)
        : EitherOrBoth.right(line);
  }

  static EitherOrBoth<NonEmptyList<String>, LineItem> reviewedLine(
      String sku, int quantity, BigDecimal price) {
    return line(sku, quantity, price).flatMap(NonEmptyList.semigroup(), EitherOrBothBook::review);
  }

```

```java
    EitherOrBoth<NonEmptyList<String>, LineItem> flagged =
        reviewedLine("lamp", 3, new BigDecimal("40.005"));
    // Both(NonEmptyList[sku lamp upper-cased, price 40.005 rounded to 40.01,
    //   line total 120.03 flagged], LineItem[sku=LAMP, quantity=3, price=40.01])
```

<pre class="hkj-railway-diagram" role="img" aria-label="EitherOrBoth's three tracks through an import, a review and a later step. A Right stays a Right, or crosses to the Both track when the review adds a warning. A Both carries its warnings forward and adds any later ones. A fatal check, or a later step that gives a Left, moves to the Left track, which keeps every warning; after a fatal check the review never runs.">
        import             review     later step
Right  ════●══════════════════●══════════════▶  Right(line)
                               ╲ review adds a warning
Both   ════●════════════════════●═══════●═════▶  Both(every warning, line)
                                         ╲ it gives a Left
Left   ────●──────────────────────────────●───▶  Left(every warning)
        a fatal check:
        the review never runs
</pre>

The diagram shows a `Left` as the only track that stops the chain, and a review that moves a `Right` onto the `Both` track. A `Left` is fatal, so `flatMap` never runs the step, and a `Right` gives whatever the step gives. For `Both(w, r).flatMap(semigroup, f)`, the warnings `w` join whatever `f(r)` gives:

| `f(r)` gives | `flatMap` gives |
|---|---|
| `Left(w2)` | `Left(w ⊕ w2)` |
| `Right(r2)` | `Both(w, r2)` |
| `Both(w2, r2)` | `Both(w ⊕ w2, r2)` |

`w ⊕ w2` is `semigroup.combine(w, w2)`: with `NonEmptyList.semigroup()`, the warnings carried in come first, then the step's.

## Converting at the boundary {#converting-at-the-boundary}

Code outside the importer may take an `Either`, a `Validated` or a `Maybe`. The two `Either` conversions are named for what they do with a `Both`'s warnings, so the choice is visible at the call. `toValidated()` and `toMaybe()` drop the warnings, so read them first with `getLeft()` if you need them:

```java
    Either<NonEmptyList<String>, LineItem> dropping = corrected.toEitherDroppingWarnings();
    // Right(LineItem[sku=LAMP, quantity=2, price=40.01])
    Either<NonEmptyList<String>, LineItem> failing = corrected.toEitherFailingOnWarnings();
    // Left(NonEmptyList[sku lamp upper-cased, price 40.005 rounded to 40.01])
    Validated<NonEmptyList<String>, LineItem> validated = corrected.toValidated();
    // Valid(LineItem[sku=LAMP, quantity=2, price=40.01])
    Maybe<LineItem> maybe = corrected.toMaybe();
    // Just(LineItem[sku=LAMP, quantity=2, price=40.01])
    Maybe<NonEmptyList<String>> warnings = corrected.getLeft();
    // Just(NonEmptyList[sku lamp upper-cased, price 40.005 rounded to 40.01])
```

| Conversion | `Left(l)` | `Right(r)` | `Both(l, r)` |
|---|---|---|---|
| `toEitherDroppingWarnings()` | `Left(l)` | `Right(r)` | `Right(r)`, warnings dropped |
| `toEitherFailingOnWarnings()` | `Left(l)` | `Right(r)` | `Left(l)`, warnings fatal |
| `toValidated()` | `Invalid(l)` | `Valid(r)` | `Valid(r)`, warnings dropped |
| `toMaybe()` | `Nothing` | `Just(r)` | `Just(r)`, warnings dropped |

`EitherOrBoth.fromEither` and `fromValidated` turn an `Either` or a `Validated` into an `EitherOrBoth`, and never give a `Both`.

## On an `EitherOrBothPath` {#on-an-eitherorbothpath}

Application code can hold the same steps on an [`EitherOrBothPath`](../effect/path_either_or_both.md). `Path.eitherOrBoth` takes a result and the semigroup that joins its warnings, `via` chains a step with the `flatMap` contract, and `warnings()` reads them. These are the extra imports:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.effect.EitherOrBothPath;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.effect.Path;
```

```java
  static EitherOrBothPath<NonEmptyList<String>, LineItem> reviewedLinePath(
      String sku, int quantity, BigDecimal price) {
    return Path.eitherOrBoth(line(sku, quantity, price), NonEmptyList.<String>semigroup())
        .via(line -> Path.eitherOrBoth(review(line), NonEmptyList.<String>semigroup()));
  }

```

```java
    EitherOrBothPath<NonEmptyList<String>, LineItem> onThePath =
        reviewedLinePath("LAMP", 3, new BigDecimal("40.00"));
    // EitherOrBothPath(Both(NonEmptyList[line total 120.00 flagged],
    //   LineItem[sku=LAMP, quantity=3, price=40.00]))
    Maybe<NonEmptyList<String>> flaggedOnPath = onThePath.warnings();
    // Just(NonEmptyList[line total 120.00 flagged])
```

## Methods {#methods}

| Method | What it does |
|---|---|
| `EitherOrBoth.left(l)`, `right(r)`, `both(l, r)` | Build each case; none accepts `null` |
| `isLeft()`, `isRight()`, `isBoth()` | Say which case it holds |
| `getLeft()`, `getRight()` | Return a `Maybe` of that side, never throwing; a `Both` has both |
| `map(f)` | Changes the value of a `Right` or a `Both` |
| `mapLeft(f)` | Changes the warnings or error of a `Left` or a `Both` |
| `bimap(onLeft, onRight)` | Changes whichever sides it holds |
| `flatMap(semigroup, f)` | Runs a step on the value, joining warnings as the contract table shows |
| `zipWithAccum(other, semigroup, f)` | Joins two results, keeping every warning from both, even across a `Left` |
| `fold(onLeft, onRight, onBoth)` | Turns any case into one result |
| `EitherOrBoth.accumulate()`, `fields()` | Join independent checks, keeping every warning |

A `Left`, a `Right` and a `Both` never hold `null`, as [Values, never null](../hkts/core-concepts.md#values-never-null) says for every type.

## Working with Kind {#working-with-kind}

Generic code, written once for any container, takes `EitherOrBoth` through its `Monad` instance, from `Instances.eitherOrBoth(semigroup)`. The semigroup is how the instance's `flatMap` joins warnings. The instance works over `Kind<EitherOrBothKind.Witness<L>, R>`, so you widen a result to pass it in, and narrow the result to read it. These are the extra imports:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.eitherorboth.EitherOrBothKindHelper.EITHER_OR_BOTH;

import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Kind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Monad;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.eitherorboth.EitherOrBothKind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Instances;
```

```java
    Monad<EitherOrBothKind.Witness<NonEmptyList<String>>> importing =
        Instances.eitherOrBoth(NonEmptyList.semigroup());

    Kind<EitherOrBothKind.Witness<NonEmptyList<String>>, LineItem> imported =
        EITHER_OR_BOTH.widen(line("lamp", 3, new BigDecimal("40.00")));
    Kind<EitherOrBothKind.Witness<NonEmptyList<String>>, LineItem> checked =
        importing.flatMap(item -> EITHER_OR_BOTH.widen(review(item)), imported);

    EitherOrBoth<NonEmptyList<String>, LineItem> bothWarnings = EITHER_OR_BOTH.narrow(checked);
    // Both(NonEmptyList[sku lamp upper-cased, line total 120.00 flagged],
    //   LineItem[sku=LAMP, quantity=3, price=40.00])
```

The instance is a `Monad`, not a `MonadError`, so it has no `raiseError` or `handleErrorWith`. `EitherOrBoth` has instances of other type classes too, such as `Traverse` and `Bifunctor`, each an object of its own, and *EitherOrBoth at a glance*, at the head of the page, lists them.

~~~admonish warning title="The instance's ap stops at a Left function"
`EitherOrBoth`'s `Monad` instance obeys the monad laws, so its `ap` agrees with its `flatMap`. When the function side is a `Left`, `ap` gives that `Left` and drops the argument's warnings, where `Validated`'s `ap` keeps both sides' errors. To keep every warning even across a `Left`, join with `zipWithAccum`, on the value or on an [`EitherOrBothPath`](../effect/path_either_or_both.md).
~~~

## When to use `EitherOrBoth` {#when-to-use-eitherorboth}

| You need | Use |
|---|---|
| A success that can carry warnings beside it | `EitherOrBoth` |
| The same steps as a fluent chain in application code | [EitherOrBothPath](../effect/path_either_or_both.md) |
| Every error, and no value at all when any check fails | [Validated](validated_monad.md) |
| The first error only, with no partial result | [Either](either_monad.md) |

~~~admonish info title="Hands-On Learning"
Practise keeping a value flowing past its warnings, in Exercise 5 of [Tutorial 12: Accumulating Assembly](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/tutorial/coretypes/Tutorial12_AccumulatingAssembly.java) (6 exercises).
~~~

**Previous:** [Try](try_monad.md)
**Next:** [NonEmptyList](nonemptylist_monad.md)

<!-- Source: monads/nonemptylist_monad.md -->

<!-- description: Hold a Java list that can never be empty with NonEmptyList, so head, reduce and max never throw, and use it for the errors of an invalid result. -->

# NonEmptyList: A List That Is Never Empty

_Hold a list that always has a first element, so reading it never throws._

~~~admonish abstract title="NonEmptyList at a glance"
| | |
|---|---|
| **Models** | A list with at least one element |
| **Replaces** | A `List` checked with `isEmpty()` before `getFirst()` |
| **Effect Path** | None: a Path holds it, as [`ValidationPath`](../effect/path_validation.md) holds its errors |
| **Instances** | `Functor`, `Applicative`, `Monad`, `Foldable`, `Traverse` |
| **Choose it when** | The list can never be empty, such as an invalid result's errors; otherwise [List](list_monad.md), or compare every type in [Choosing a Type](supported-types.md#choosing-a-type) |
~~~

~~~admonish info title="What You'll Learn"
- Turn a list that may be empty into a `NonEmptyList` once, at the boundary
- Total a list with `reduce`, and read its first or largest element, with nothing to guard
- Say why `List<String>` is the wrong type for an invalid result's errors
- Choose between `NonEmptyList`'s `ap` and its `semigroup()` for combining two lists
- Say why `NonEmptyList` has no empty value and no `Monoid`
~~~

~~~admonish example title="See Example Code"
**The code on this page is [NonEmptyListBook.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/nonemptylist/NonEmptyListBook.java)**: the page includes it, so the build compiles and runs it. [NonEmptyListBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/nonemptylist/NonEmptyListBookTest.java) holds what the page says each method does.
~~~

---

## An order's lines {#an-orders-lines}

Code that reads the first element of a `List`, or totals it, must first check `isEmpty()`: `getFirst()` throws on an empty list, and a total needs a starting value. `NonEmptyList<A>` holds at least one element by its type, so the check happens once, where the list is made, and nowhere after.

The code on this page uses these imports, with `Order` and `LineItem` from the order records the [Optics](../optics/ch_intro.md) chapter introduces:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import java.math.BigDecimal;
import java.util.Comparator;
import java.util.List;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.LineItem;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.Order;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Monoids;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.maybe.Maybe;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.nonemptylist.NonEmptyList;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.validated.Validated;
```

An order has a `List` of lines, and an order with none should not reach the code that totals it. `NonEmptyList.fromList` makes the check: it gives a `Maybe`, empty for an empty list. `PLACED` has two lines, and `EMPTIED` none:

```java
  // An order with no lines gives Nothing, so the empty case is handled once, here
  static Maybe<NonEmptyList<LineItem>> lines(Order order) {
    return NonEmptyList.fromList(order.lines());
  }

```

```java
    Maybe<NonEmptyList<LineItem>> placed = lines(PLACED);
    // Just(NonEmptyList[LineItem[sku=LAMP, quantity=2, price=40.00],
    //   LineItem[sku=SHADE, quantity=1, price=12.50]])
    Maybe<NonEmptyList<LineItem>> emptied = lines(EMPTIED);
    // Nothing
```

Past that check, the code needs no guard. `reduce` totals the lines with `Monoids.bigDecimalAddition()`, the book's way to add money, and uses only its join, never its empty value, because there is always a first line. `max` and `head` return a line, never an `Optional`:

```java
  static BigDecimal lineTotal(LineItem line) {
    return line.price().multiply(BigDecimal.valueOf(line.quantity()));
  }

  // reduce uses only the monoid's join: there is always a first line to start from
  static BigDecimal orderTotal(NonEmptyList<LineItem> lines) {
    return lines.map(NonEmptyListBook::lineTotal).reduce(Monoids.bigDecimalAddition());
  }

  static LineItem priciest(NonEmptyList<LineItem> lines) {
    return lines.max(Comparator.comparing(NonEmptyListBook::lineTotal));
  }

```

`Maybe`'s `map` runs them only on an order that has lines:

```java
    Maybe<BigDecimal> total = placed.map(NonEmptyListBook::orderTotal);
    // Just(92.50)
    Maybe<LineItem> first = placed.map(NonEmptyList::head);
    // Just(LineItem[sku=LAMP, quantity=2, price=40.00])
    Maybe<LineItem> priciest = placed.map(NonEmptyListBook::priciest);
    // Just(LineItem[sku=LAMP, quantity=2, price=40.00])
```

The same code over a `List` would need a starting value for the total, such as `BigDecimal.ZERO`, and an `Optional` for the largest line.

## Creating instances {#creating-instances}

Java has no literal for a list that is never empty, so each factory names where the first element comes from:

```java
    NonEmptyList<String> skus = NonEmptyList.of("LAMP", "SHADE", "BULB");
    NonEmptyList<String> one = NonEmptyList.single("LAMP");
    NonEmptyList<String> joined = one.concat(NonEmptyList.of("SHADE")).append("BULB");
    // NonEmptyList[LAMP, SHADE, BULB]
```

`fromList` and `fromIterable` take data that may be empty, and give a `Maybe` rather than throwing. Every element is checked when the list is built, so a `null` is refused, as [Values, never null](../hkts/core-concepts.md#values-never-null) says for every type.

## As the error list {#as-the-error-list}

An invalid result always has at least one error, yet its errors are often held in a `List`, which can be empty. Nothing stops the impossible case being built:

```java
    // An invalid result should hold at least one error, but a List can be empty
    Validated<List<String>, Order> noReason = Validated.invalid(List.of());
    // Invalid([])
```

`noReason.getError().getFirst()` throws `NoSuchElementException`. With a `NonEmptyList` as the error list, the impossible case cannot be built, and reading the first error needs no guard:

```java
    Validated<NonEmptyList<String>, Order> refused = Validated.invalidNel("order has no lines");
    // Invalid(NonEmptyList[order has no lines])
    String firstReason = refused.getError().head();
```

[Validated](validated_monad.md)'s `validNel`, `invalidNel`, `accumulate()` and `fields()` all hold the errors in a `NonEmptyList`, and Cats pairs its `Validated` with a `NonEmptyList` in the same way. `accumulate()` and `fields()` need no `Semigroup` argument. Neither does a `ValidationPath` built with `Path.validNel` or `Path.invalidNel`, which carries `NonEmptyList.semigroup()` with it. `Validated`'s own `ap`, and `Instances.validated`, still take one: pass `NonEmptyList.semigroup()`, which joins two error lists end to end, so the errors keep the order the checks ran.

## Methods {#methods}

| Method | What it does |
|---|---|
| `NonEmptyList.of(first, rest...)`, `single(a)` | Build a list from its first element and any others |
| `NonEmptyList.fromList(list)`, `fromIterable(items)` | Give a `Maybe`, empty for empty input |
| `head()`, `last()` | Return the first or last element |
| `tail()` | Returns the elements after the first, as a `List` that cannot be changed, possibly empty |
| `reduce(semigroup)` | Joins every element, with no starting value |
| `min(comparator)`, `max(comparator)` | Return the smallest or largest element |
| `map(f)`, `flatMap(f)` | Change each element, or replace each with a `NonEmptyList` |
| `concat(other)`, `append(a)`, `prepend(a)` | Join two lists, or add one element |
| `foldLeft(start, f)`, `reverse()`, `size()` | Fold from the left, reverse, or count |
| `stream()`, `toJavaList()` | Give a `Stream`, or a `List` that cannot be changed |
| `NonEmptyList.semigroup()` | Joins two lists end to end, keeping their order |

`NonEmptyList` is also `Iterable`, so a for-each loop reads it directly.

## Working with Kind {#working-with-kind}

Generic code, written once for any container, takes `NonEmptyList` through its `Monad` instance, from `Instances.monad(nonEmptyList())`. The instance works over `Kind<NonEmptyListKind.Witness, A>`, so you widen a list to pass it in, and narrow the result to read it. These are the extra imports:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.nonEmptyList;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.nonemptylist.NonEmptyListKindHelper.NON_EMPTY_LIST;

import java.util.function.Function;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Kind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Monad;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Instances;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.nonemptylist.NonEmptyListKind;
```

```java
    Monad<NonEmptyListKind.Witness> nonEmpty = Instances.monad(nonEmptyList());

    Kind<NonEmptyListKind.Witness, Function<BigDecimal, BigDecimal>> adjustments =
        NON_EMPTY_LIST.widen(
            NonEmptyList.of(
                price -> price.add(new BigDecimal("4.99")),
                price -> price.subtract(new BigDecimal("5.00"))));
    Kind<NonEmptyListKind.Witness, BigDecimal> prices =
        NON_EMPTY_LIST.widen(NonEmptyList.of(new BigDecimal("40.00"), new BigDecimal("12.50")));

    // ap pairs every adjustment with every price, as List's does
    NonEmptyList<BigDecimal> everyPairing = NON_EMPTY_LIST.narrow(nonEmpty.ap(adjustments, prices));
    // NonEmptyList[44.99, 17.49, 35.00, 7.50]
```

~~~admonish tip title="Two ways to combine"
`NonEmptyList.semigroup()` joins two lists end to end: `[a]` and `[b]` give `[a, b]`. The instance's `ap` pairs every element of one with every element of the other, as `List`'s does. Error lists are joined with the semigroup.
~~~

Widening allocates nothing: `NonEmptyList` implements `NonEmptyListKind` itself, so `widen` returns the same object. `NonEmptyList` has instances of other type classes too, such as `Traverse`, each an object of its own, and *NonEmptyList at a glance*, at the head of the page, lists them.

~~~admonish note title="No empty, by design"
No factory builds an empty `NonEmptyList`, so it has no `Monoid` instance and no `Alternative` or `MonadZero`. Each of those needs an empty value, which would bring back the case the type exists to rule out. `NonEmptyList.semigroup()` joins two lists, but nothing gives a `Monoid`'s empty one.
~~~

## When to use `NonEmptyList` {#when-to-use-nonemptylist}

| You need | Use |
|---|---|
| A list that can never be empty, such as an invalid result's errors | `NonEmptyList` |
| A list that may be empty | [List](list_monad.md) |
| The errors of independent checks, kept together | [Validated](validated_monad.md), whose `invalidNel` holds them in a `NonEmptyList` |
| A record built from many checked fields | [Accumulating Assembly](validated_assembly.md) |

---

**Previous:** [EitherOrBoth](either_or_both_monad.md)
**Next:** [Maybe](maybe_monad.md)

<!-- description: Return a value that may be missing from a Java method with Maybe instead of null, chain lookups that may find nothing, and give the absence a reason. -->

# Maybe: Representing Optional Values

_Return a value that may be missing, without ever handing back `null`._

~~~admonish abstract title="Maybe at a glance"
| | |
|---|---|
| **Models** | A value, or nothing, and never `null` |
| **Replaces** | A method that may return `null` |
| **Effect Path** | [`MaybePath`](../effect/path_maybe.md) |
| **Instances** | `Functor`, `Applicative`, `Selective`, `Monad`, `MonadError<Unit>`, `Alternative`, `MonadZero`, `Foldable`, `Traverse` |
| **Choose it when** | Absence needs no reason; otherwise [Either](either_monad.md), or compare every type in [Choosing a Type](supported-types.md#choosing-a-type) |
~~~

~~~admonish info title="What You'll Learn"
- Turn a lookup that may return `null` into a `Maybe`, once, where the value comes in
- Chain lookups that may each find nothing, and say when a later one runs
- Read a `Maybe` with a fallback, or give its absence a reason with `toEither`
- Hand a `Maybe` to code that takes an `Optional` or a `Stream`
- Use `Maybe` in generic code through its `MonadError` instance
~~~

~~~admonish example title="See Example Code"
**The code on this page is [MaybeBook.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/maybe/MaybeBook.java)**: the page includes it, so the build compiles and runs it. [MaybeBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/maybe/MaybeBookTest.java) holds what the page says each method does.
~~~

---

## Absence without `null` {#absence-without-null}

`Map.get` returns `null` for a key it does not hold, and so do many older APIs. Nothing in the signature says so, and code that forgets to check fails later with a `NullPointerException`. A `Maybe<T>` is a value that may be missing, with the missing case in the type: a `Just` holds a value, which is never `null`, and `Nothing` holds none. It is close to Java's `Optional`, which [Optional](optional_monad.md) covers, and treats `null` the same way: `Maybe.just`, like `Optional.of`, refuses it.

The code on this page uses these imports, with `Order`, `Consignment` and `Address` from the order records the [Optics](../optics/ch_intro.md) chapter introduces:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import java.util.List;
import java.util.Map;
import java.util.Optional;
import java.util.UUID;
import java.util.stream.Stream;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.foundations.OrderError;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.Address;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.Consignment;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.Order;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.either.Either;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.maybe.Maybe;
```

`ORDERS` and `CONSIGNMENTS` are `Map`s keyed by order id, and only an order that has shipped has a consignment. Each lookup turns `Map.get`'s `null` into `Nothing`, once, where the value comes in:

```java
  // Map.get gives null for a missing key, and fromNullable turns that null into Nothing
  static Maybe<Order> findOrder(UUID id) {
    return Maybe.fromNullable(ORDERS.get(id));
  }

  static Maybe<Consignment> findConsignment(Order order) {
    return Maybe.fromNullable(CONSIGNMENTS.get(order.id()));
  }

  static Maybe<Order> findArchived(UUID id) {
    return Maybe.fromNullable(ARCHIVE.get(id));
  }

```

## Chaining lookups {#chaining-lookups}

`flatMap` runs the next lookup on a `Just`'s value, and `map` changes the value. On `Nothing`, neither runs, and `Nothing` reaches the end:

```java
  static Maybe<Address> deliveryAddress(UUID id) {
    return findOrder(id).flatMap(MaybeBook::findConsignment).map(Consignment::to);
  }

```

`PLACED` has not shipped, `SHIPPED` has, and no order has the id `UNKNOWN`:

```java
    Maybe<Address> dispatched = deliveryAddress(SHIPPED.id());
    // Just(Address[street=1 High Street, city=Leeds, postcode=LS1 4AP])
    Maybe<Address> notDispatched = deliveryAddress(PLACED.id());
    // Nothing
    Maybe<Address> noSuchOrder = deliveryAddress(UNKNOWN);
    // Nothing
```

The two `Nothing` results look the same: one order has not shipped, and the other does not exist. `Maybe` says only that something is missing, not why.

## Reading a `Maybe` {#reading-a-maybe}

`orElse` gives a fallback for `Nothing`, but its text cannot say why the value is missing: an unknown order reads "not dispatched yet" too. When the caller needs the reason, `toEither` gives the absence one, and the chain continues as an [Either](either_monad.md):

```java
    String city = deliveryAddress(PLACED.id()).map(Address::city).orElse("not dispatched yet");

    Either<OrderError, Order> withReason =
        findOrder(UNKNOWN).toEither(OrderError.notFound(UNKNOWN));
    // Left(NotFound[orderId=00000000-0000-0000-0000-000000000009])
```

`city` is `"not dispatched yet"`. `get()` returns a `Just`'s value and throws `NoSuchElementException` on `Nothing`, so keep it for tests. `Just` and `Nothing` are not public types, so a `switch` cannot name them: read a `Maybe` through its methods.

## Bridging to the JDK {#interacting-with-maybe-values}

`toOptional()` hands a `Maybe` to code that takes an `Optional`, and `Maybe.fromOptional` brings one back. Neither conversion can fail, because neither type holds `null`. `stream()` gives a stream of zero or one element, so `flatMap(Maybe::stream)` keeps only the lookups that found something:

```java
    Optional<Address> forJdkCode = deliveryAddress(SHIPPED.id()).toOptional();
    // Optional[Address[street=1 High Street, city=Leeds, postcode=LS1 4AP]]

    // Keep only the ids that find an order
    List<UUID> found =
        Stream.of(PLACED.id(), UNKNOWN, SHIPPED.id())
            .map(MaybeBook::findOrder)
            .flatMap(Maybe::stream)
            .map(Order::id)
            .toList();
    // [00000000-0000-0000-0000-000000000001, 00000000-0000-0000-0000-000000000002]
```

Among the library's types that can fail, only `Maybe` has `stream()`, as `Optional` does. An empty stream says that a value is missing, while the same conversion on `Either` or `Try` would drop the error it holds.

## Creating instances {#creating-instances}

`Maybe.just(value)` builds a `Just`, and throws `NullPointerException` for `null`. `Maybe.fromNullable(value)` gives `Nothing` for `null`, and `Maybe.nothing()` gives `Nothing` directly. `Maybe`'s `map` gives `Nothing`, rather than failing, when its function returns `null`, and its instance's `of(null)` gives `Nothing` too, as [Values, never null](../hkts/core-concepts.md#values-never-null) sets out.

## On a `MaybePath` {#on-a-maybepath}

Application code can hold the same chain on a [`MaybePath`](../effect/path_maybe.md). `Path.maybe` takes a `Maybe`, or a value that may be `null`, `via` chains the next lookup, and `getOrElse` reads it. These are the extra imports:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.effect.MaybePath;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.effect.Path;
```

```java
  static MaybePath<String> deliveryCity(UUID id) {
    return Path.maybe(findOrder(id))
        .via(order -> Path.maybe(findConsignment(order)))
        .map(consignment -> consignment.to().city());
  }

```

```java
    MaybePath<String> leeds = deliveryCity(SHIPPED.id());
    // MaybePath(Just(Leeds))
    String unknownCity = deliveryCity(UNKNOWN).getOrElse("unknown");
```

`unknownCity` is `"unknown"`.

## Methods {#methods}

| Method | What it does |
|---|---|
| `Maybe.just(value)`, `Maybe.nothing()` | Build a `Just`, throwing `NullPointerException` for `null`, or `Nothing` |
| `Maybe.fromNullable(value)` | Gives `Nothing` for `null`, and a `Just` otherwise |
| `Maybe.fromOptional(optional)`, `toOptional()` | Convert from and to Java's `Optional` |
| `Maybe.fromEither(either)` | Keeps a `Right`'s value as a `Just`, and gives `Nothing` for a `Left`, dropping its error |
| `isJust()`, `isNothing()` | Say which case it holds |
| `map(f)` | Changes a `Just`'s value; a function that returns `null` gives `Nothing` |
| `flatMap(f)` | Runs a step that gives a `Maybe` on a `Just`'s value; `Nothing` passes through |
| `orElse(other)`, `orElseGet(supplier)` | Return the value, or a fallback |
| `toEither(error)`, `toEither(supplier)` | Give an `Either` whose `Left` says why the value is missing |
| `stream()` | Gives a stream of zero or one element |
| `get()` | Returns the value, or throws `NoSuchElementException` on `Nothing` |

## Working with Kind {#working-with-kind}

Generic code, written once for any container, takes `Maybe` through its `MonadError` instance, as [MonadError](../functional/monad_error.md) shows. Its error type is `Unit`: `Nothing` is the error, and it carries no detail. The instance works over `Kind<MaybeKind.Witness, A>`, so you widen a `Maybe` to pass it in, and narrow the result to read it. These are the extra imports:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.maybe;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.maybe.MaybeKindHelper.MAYBE;

import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Kind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.MonadError;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Unit;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Instances;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.maybe.MaybeKind;
```

`ARCHIVED` is an order that has moved to the archive, so `findOrder` misses it and `findArchived` finds it there:

```java
    MonadError<MaybeKind.Witness, Unit> absence = Instances.monadError(maybe());

    Kind<MaybeKind.Witness, Order> lookedUp = MAYBE.widen(findOrder(ARCHIVED.id()));
    Kind<MaybeKind.Witness, Order> orArchived =
        absence.handleErrorWith(lookedUp, unit -> MAYBE.widen(findArchived(ARCHIVED.id())));

    Maybe<Order> fromArchive = MAYBE.narrow(orArchived);
    // Just(Order[id=00000000-0000-0000-0000-000000000003, ...])
    Maybe<Order> raised = MAYBE.narrow(absence.raiseError(Unit.INSTANCE));
    // Nothing
```

Widening allocates nothing: `Maybe` implements `MaybeKind` itself, so `widen` checks for `null` and returns the same object, and `Nothing` is one shared instance. `Maybe` has instances of other type classes too, such as `Selective` and `Traverse`, each an object of its own, and *Maybe at a glance*, at the head of the page, lists them.

## Back to the one-liner {#back-to-the-one-liner}

```java
  static EitherPath<OrderError, Order> changeEmail(
      OrderRepository orders, UUID id, String newEmail) {
    return orders
        .find(id)
        .toEitherPath(OrderError.notFound(id))
        .map(order -> OrderFocus.customer().email().value().set(newEmail, order))
        .via(orders::save);
  }

```

[The one-liner](../hkts/one_line_six_layers.md) starts with `orders.find(id)`, which gives a `MaybePath`: the order, or nothing. `toEitherPath(OrderError.notFound(id))` gives that absence a reason, so the rest of the line can fail with an `OrderError`. A `MaybePath` holds a `Maybe` and calls its methods directly.

## When to use `Maybe` {#when-to-use-maybe}

| You need | Use |
|---|---|
| A value that may be missing, in code you control | `Maybe` |
| The same lookups as a fluent chain in application code | [MaybePath](../effect/path_maybe.md) |
| To work with an API that returns `Optional` | [Optional](optional_monad.md), or bridge with `fromOptional` and `toOptional` |
| A missing value that must say why | [Either](either_monad.md), converting with `toEither` |

~~~admonish info title="Hands-On Learning"
Practise `Maybe.just`, `Maybe.nothing` and converting to `Either`, beside `Either` and `Validated`, in [Tutorial 06: Concrete Types](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/tutorial/coretypes/Tutorial06_ConcreteTypes.java) (7 exercises).
~~~

**Benchmarks:** `MaybeBenchmark` measures how cheaply `Nothing` passes along a chain, and the cost of `fromNullable`. [Benchmarks & Performance](../benchmarks.md) says how to run it.

<!-- description: Use Java's Optional with Higher-Kinded-J: read Optional fields through OptionalPath, pass Optional to generic code, and choose between Optional and Maybe. -->

# Optional: Java's Optional in Generic Code

_Keep Java's own `Optional`, with an Effect Path and the same instances as `Maybe`._

~~~admonish abstract title="Optional at a glance"
| | |
|---|---|
| **Models** | A value, or empty, in Java's own type |
| **Replaces** | Nothing: it is Java's own type |
| **Effect Path** | [`OptionalPath`](../effect/path_optional.md) |
| **Instances** | `Functor`, `Applicative`, `Selective`, `Monad`, `MonadError<Unit>`, `Alternative`, `MonadZero`, `Foldable`, `Traverse` |
| **Choose it when** | A method you call already returns an `Optional`; otherwise [Maybe](maybe_monad.md), or compare every type in [Choosing a Type](supported-types.md#choosing-a-type) |
~~~

~~~admonish info title="What You'll Learn"
- Read a record's `Optional` fields with a fallback, through an `OptionalPath`
- Pass an `Optional` to code written once for any container, through its `MonadError` instance
- Say what widening an `Optional` costs, and why `Maybe` pays nothing
- Choose between `Optional` and `Maybe`, and convert between them
~~~

~~~admonish example title="See Example Code"
**The code on this page is [OptionalBook.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/optional/OptionalBook.java)**: the page includes it, so the build compiles and runs it. [OptionalBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/optional/OptionalBookTest.java) holds what the page says each method does.
~~~

---

## Java's own type {#javas-own-type}

`java.util.Optional` is everywhere: repositories return it, configuration lookups return it, and records hold it in fields that may be empty. Higher-Kinded-J does not replace it. It gives `Optional` an Effect Path, `OptionalPath`, for application code, and a `MonadError` instance, so that a method written once for any container accepts an `Optional` too. [Maybe](maybe_monad.md) is the library's own type for the same job.

The code on this page uses these imports, with `CustomerProfile` and `EmailAddress` from the order records the [Optics](../optics/ch_intro.md) chapter introduces:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import java.util.Optional;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.CustomerProfile;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.EmailAddress;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.effect.OptionalPath;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.effect.Path;
```

## Optional fields on an `OptionalPath` {#optional-fields-on-an-optionalpath}

A `CustomerProfile` holds a `nickname` and a second email address, `altEmail`, each as an `Optional`. `Path.optional` takes one, `map` changes the value when there is one, and `getOrElse` reads it with a fallback:

```java
  // nickname is an Optional field, as many records' optional fields are
  static String greeting(CustomerProfile profile) {
    return Path.optional(profile.nickname())
        .map(nickname -> "Hello, " + nickname)
        .getOrElse("Hello, " + profile.name());
  }

  static OptionalPath<String> secondAddress(CustomerProfile profile) {
    return Path.optional(profile.altEmail()).map(EmailAddress::value);
  }

```

`ADA` has the nickname `"Countess"` and no second address, and `CHARLES` has a second address and no nickname:

```java
    String forAda = greeting(ADA);
    String forCharles = greeting(CHARLES);

    OptionalPath<String> charlesAddress = secondAddress(CHARLES);
    // OptionalPath(charles@example.org)
    Optional<String> adaAddress = secondAddress(ADA).run();
    // Optional.empty
```

`forAda` is `"Hello, Countess"`, and `forCharles` is `"Hello, Charles"`. `Optional`'s own `map` and `orElse` do as much for one field. The Path pays off when `via` chains it into another Path, or `toEitherPath` gives the absence a reason. `run()` gives the `Optional` back, and `toMaybePath()` turns the Path into a `MaybePath`.

## Methods {#methods}

`Optional`'s own methods are the JDK's. The library adds these:

| Method | What it does |
|---|---|
| `Path.optional(optional)`, `Path.present(a)`, `Path.absent()` | Build an `OptionalPath` |
| `map(f)`, `via(f)`, `filter(p)` | Change the value, chain a step, or keep the value only if it passes; a `map` function that returns `null` gives an empty Path |
| `getOrElse(a)`, `orElsePath(a)` | Read the value with a fallback, or keep the Path, filling an empty one with the fallback |
| `run()`, `isPresent()`, `isEmpty()` | Give the `Optional` back, or say which case it holds |
| `toMaybePath()`, `toEitherPath(error)` | Convert to a `MaybePath`, or to an `EitherPath` whose error says why |
| `Maybe.fromOptional(optional)`, `maybe.toOptional()` | Convert between `Optional` and `Maybe` |
| `OPTIONAL.widen(optional)`, `OPTIONAL.narrow(kind)` | Move an `Optional` in and out of `Kind` |

## Working with Kind {#working-with-kind}

Generic code, written once for any container, takes `Optional` through its `MonadError` instance, as [MonadError](../functional/monad_error.md) shows. Its error type is `Unit`: an empty `Optional` is the error, and it carries no detail. `Maybe`'s instance has the same shape, so one method serves both. These are the extra imports for writing the method and calling it:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.maybe;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.optional;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.maybe.MaybeKindHelper.MAYBE;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.optional.OptionalKindHelper.OPTIONAL;

import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Kind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.MonadError;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.TypeArity;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Unit;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.WitnessArity;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Instances;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.maybe.Maybe;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.maybe.MaybeKind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.optional.OptionalKind;
```

`F` stands for any container, and its [`WitnessArity` bound](../hkts/core-concepts.md#the-witnessarity-bound) says the container holds one type:

```java
  // Written once, for any container whose error is absence
  static <F extends WitnessArity<TypeArity.Unary>> Kind<F, String> contact(
      MonadError<F, Unit> absence, Kind<F, EmailAddress> email, String fallback) {
    return absence.handleError(absence.map(EmailAddress::value, email), unit -> fallback);
  }

```

```java
    MonadError<OptionalKind.Witness, Unit> optionals = Instances.monadError(optional());
    MonadError<MaybeKind.Witness, Unit> maybes = Instances.monadError(maybe());

    Optional<String> fromOptional =
        OPTIONAL.narrow(contact(optionals, OPTIONAL.widen(CHARLES.altEmail()), "no address"));
    // Optional[charles@example.org]
    Maybe<String> fromMaybe =
        MAYBE.narrow(
            contact(maybes, MAYBE.widen(Maybe.fromOptional(ADA.altEmail())), "no address"));
    // Just(no address)
```

`Optional` is a JDK class, so it cannot implement `OptionalKind`. `OPTIONAL.widen` wraps it in a small holder object each time, and `narrow` unwraps it, where widening a `Maybe` allocates nothing. The instance's `of(null)` gives an empty `Optional`, and so does a `map` whose function returns `null`, as `Optional.map` does. `Optional` has instances of other type classes too, such as `Selective` and `Traverse`, each an object of its own, and *Optional at a glance*, at the head of the page, lists them.

## Should you use `Optional` or `Maybe`? {#when-to-use-optional-vs-maybe}

Both model a value that may be missing, treat `null` alike, and have the same instances. They differ in who owns the type, and so in what widening costs:

| You need | Use |
|---|---|
| To work with APIs and records that already use `Optional` | `Optional`, with `OptionalPath` or its instance |
| A value that may be missing, in code you control | [Maybe](maybe_monad.md): it is its own `Kind`, so widening allocates nothing, and `toEither` gives its absence a reason |
| Generic code that must accept an `Optional` from callers | `Optional`'s instance, widening with `OPTIONAL.widen` |
| To move between the two | `Maybe.fromOptional(optional)` and `maybe.toOptional()` |
| A missing value that must say why | [Either](either_monad.md), through `toEitherPath(error)` |

---

**Previous:** [Maybe](maybe_monad.md)
**Next:** [IO](io_monad.md)

<!-- Source: monads/io_monad.md -->

<!-- description: Describe a side effect in Java with IO and run it once, at the edge: read the time through TimeSource, compose steps, and catch failures on an IOPath. -->

# IO: Describing Side Effects

_Describe a side effect now, and run it once, at the edge._

<!-- source: John Keats, "Ode on a Grecian Urn", stanza II, in Lamia, Isabella, The Eve of St. Agnes, and Other Poems (1820); text checked against Project Gutenberg eBook #23684 -->
> _"Heard melodies are sweet, but those unheard / Are sweeter"_
>
> — John Keats, *Ode on a Grecian Urn*

An `IO` is Keats's unheard melody: until you run it, it is a description you can still arrange and combine, and nothing has played.

~~~admonish abstract title="IO at a glance"
| | |
|---|---|
| **Models** | A side effect described now and run later |
| **Replaces** | A method that performs its side effect as soon as it is called |
| **Effect Path** | [`IOPath`](../effect/path_io.md) |
| **Instances** | `Functor`, `Applicative`, `Selective`, `Monad` |
| **Choose it when** | The effect waits until you run it, throws no checked exception, and need not run at the same time as other work; otherwise [VTask](vtask_monad.md), or compare every type in [Choosing a Type](supported-types.md#choosing-a-type) |
~~~

~~~admonish info title="What You'll Learn"
- Read the time through `TimeSource`, so code that uses it can be tested with a fixed clock
- Build a program from steps that each describe an effect, and say when each one runs
- Say why running an `IO` twice runs its effects twice
- Catch a failing step on an `IOPath`, since `IO` itself lets the exception through
- Use `IO` in generic code through its `Monad` instance
~~~

~~~admonish example title="See Example Code"
**The code on this page is [IOBook.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/io/IOBook.java)**: the page includes it, so the build compiles and runs it. [IOBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/io/IOBookTest.java) holds what the page says each method does.
~~~

---

## Reading the clock {#reading-the-clock}

A method that calls `Instant.now()` reads the clock the moment it is called, so no test can predict its result. Injecting a `java.time.Clock` fixes the test, but the read still happens at the call. An `IO<A>` is close to a `Supplier<A>` that nothing has called yet. What it adds is `map` and `flatMap`, which join steps into one description without running any of them, so you build the program in one place and run it in another. `TimeSource` wraps a `Clock`: its `now()` is `IO.delay(clock::instant)`, and `nowAsync()` gives the same read as a `VTask`.

The code on this page uses these imports, with `Order`, `Consignment`, `ConsignmentState` and `Address` from the order records the [Optics](../optics/ch_intro.md) chapter introduces. Import the library's `IO` by name: Java 25 has a `java.lang.IO` too.

<!-- verify -->
```java
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.List;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.Address;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.Consignment;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.ConsignmentState;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.Order;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.io.IO;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.time.TimeSource;
```

Dispatching an order stamps its consignment with the time:

```java
  // The time is read when the IO runs, not when dispatch is called
  static IO<Consignment> dispatch(TimeSource time, Order order, Address to) {
    return time.now()
        .map(now -> new Consignment(order.id(), to, new ConsignmentState.Dispatched(now)));
  }

```

`NINE_AM` is the instant a fixed time source reads, `ORDER` is a paid order ready to ship, and `LEEDS` is the address it goes to:

```java
    TimeSource time = TimeSource.fixed(NINE_AM);
    IO<Consignment> described = dispatch(time, ORDER, LEEDS);

    Consignment dispatched = described.unsafeRunSync();
    // Consignment[orderId=00000000-0000-0000-0000-000000000001,
    //   to=Address[street=1 High Street, city=Leeds, postcode=LS1 4AP],
    //   state=Dispatched[at=2026-10-31T09:00:00Z]]
```

Nothing is read until the effect runs, and each run reads afresh. In tests, inject `TimeSource.fixed(...)`, or `TimeSource.of(steppableClock)` with `hkj-test`'s [`SteppableClock`](../tooling/test_assertions.md#steppableclock), and the same code becomes deterministic. It is named `TimeSource`, not `Clock`, so that it never clashes with `java.time.Clock`.

## Describing, then running {#describing-then-running}

`flatMap` joins a step that needs the result of the one before, and `map` changes the result. Both build a new `IO`, and neither runs anything. `IO.delay` describes any effect, here adding the consignment to a log:

```java
  static IO<Consignment> record(List<Consignment> log, Consignment consignment) {
    return IO.delay(
        () -> {
          log.add(consignment);
          return consignment;
        });
  }

  static IO<Consignment> dispatchAndRecord(
      TimeSource time, List<Consignment> log, Order order, Address to) {
    return dispatch(time, order, to).flatMap(consignment -> record(log, consignment));
  }

```

`unsafeRunSync` runs the whole description, in the order it was built. Its name warns that this is where the effects happen: call it once, at the edge of the application, never deep inside its logic:

```java
    List<Consignment> log = new ArrayList<>();
    IO<Consignment> program = dispatchAndRecord(time, log, ORDER, LEEDS);

    int beforeRunning = log.size();
    // 0
    program.unsafeRunSync();
    program.unsafeRunSync();
    int afterTwoRuns = log.size();
    // 2
```

The log is empty until the program runs, and holds two consignments after two runs. An `IO` is a description, not a stored result: each run does the work again, and reads the clock again. To reuse a result, run the `IO` once and keep its value. [Lazy](lazy_monad.md) keeps the first result of a computation with no side effect.

## On an `IOPath` {#on-an-iopath}

`IO` has no `MonadError` instance: an exception a step throws leaves `unsafeRunSync`, as it would leave a direct call. Application code usually holds its effects on an [`IOPath`](../effect/path_io.md), which adds the failure handling: `catching` turns an exception into a `Left`, `handleError` into a value, and `asTry` into a `Try`. These are the extra imports:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.effect.IOPath;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.effect.Path;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.either.Either;
```

`bookCourier` stands in for a courier call that throws. `Path.ioPath` puts an `IO` on a Path, `via` chains the next step, and `catching` turns the exception into a `Left`. An `IOPath` runs with `unsafeRun`, as an `IO` runs with `unsafeRunSync`:

```java
  static IO<Consignment> bookCourier(Consignment consignment) {
    return IO.delay(
        () -> {
          throw new IllegalStateException("the courier is closed");
        });
  }

  static IOPath<Either<String, Consignment>> dispatchSafely(
      TimeSource time, Order order, Address to) {
    return Path.ioPath(dispatch(time, order, to))
        .via(consignment -> Path.ioPath(bookCourier(consignment)))
        .catching(error -> "booking failed: " + error.getMessage());
  }

```

```java
    Either<String, Consignment> refused = dispatchSafely(time, ORDER, LEEDS).unsafeRun();
    // Left(booking failed: the courier is closed)
```

`IOPath` also releases resources with `bracket` and `withResource`, and retries or times out a step, as [IOPath](../effect/path_io.md) shows.

## Creating instances {#creating-instances}

`IO.delay(supplier)` describes an effect that gives a value, and `IO.fromRunnable(action)` one that gives [`Unit`](../hkts/core-concepts.md#unit). A plain value goes in as `IO.delay(() -> value)`, or as `Path.ioPure(value)` on a Path. `IO.delay` takes a `Supplier`, which cannot throw a checked exception, so catch one inside the lambda, as [IOPath](../effect/path_io.md#creation) shows, or use [VTask](vtask_monad.md), whose `Callable` can throw one. An `IO` always gives a value: a supplier or `map` function that returns `null` fails the `IO` when it runs, and the instance's `of(null)` throws at the call, as [Values, never null](../hkts/core-concepts.md#values-never-null) sets out. NullAway flags a lambda or method reference given to `IO.delay` that returns `null` only with the options in [What a nullness checker sees](../hkts/core-concepts.md#what-a-nullness-checker-sees). It flags a `Supplier<@Nullable T>` variable given to `IO.delay` in JSpecify mode alone.

## Methods {#methods}

| Method | What it does |
|---|---|
| `IO.delay(supplier)` | Describes an effect; the supplier runs only when the `IO` does |
| `IO.fromRunnable(action)` | Describes an effect that gives `Unit` |
| `map(f)` | Builds an `IO` whose result is changed by `f`, running nothing |
| `flatMap(f)` | Builds an `IO` that runs the next step on this one's result, running nothing |
| `asUnit()` | Keeps the effect and replaces the result with `Unit` |
| `unsafeRunSync()` | Runs the description, blocking the calling thread until it gives its result or throws its exception |
| `TimeSource.system()`, `fixed(instant)`, `of(clock)` | Give a `TimeSource` over the system clock in UTC, a fixed instant, or any `Clock` |
| `timeSource.now()`, `nowAsync()` | Give an `IO<Instant>`, or a `VTask<Instant>`, that reads the time when it runs |

## Working with Kind {#working-with-kind}

Generic code, written once for any container, takes `IO` through its `Monad` instance, from `Instances.monad(io())`. The instance works over `Kind<IOKind.Witness, A>`, so you widen an `IO` to pass it in, and run the result with `IO_OP.unsafeRunSync`. These are the extra imports:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.io;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.io.IOKindHelper.IO_OP;

import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Kind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Monad;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Instances;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.io.IOKind;
```

```java
    Monad<IOKind.Witness> effects = Instances.monad(io());

    Kind<IOKind.Witness, Consignment> stamped = IO_OP.widen(dispatch(time, ORDER, LEEDS));
    Kind<IOKind.Witness, String> label =
        effects.map(consignment -> "to " + consignment.to().city(), stamped);

    String ran = IO_OP.unsafeRunSync(label);
```

`ran` is `"to Leeds"`. The instance's `map` builds a new `IO`, and nothing reads the clock until `IO_OP.unsafeRunSync` runs it. The `Monad` helpers, such as `peek` and `flatMapIfOrElse`, work on it too, as [Helpers built on `flatMap` and `map`](../functional/monad.md#helpers) shows. Widening allocates nothing: `IO` implements `IOKind` itself, so `widen` checks for `null` and returns the same object. `IO` has a `Selective` instance too, an object of its own, and *IO at a glance*, at the head of the page, lists it.

## When to use `IO` {#when-to-use-io}

| You need | Use |
|---|---|
| A side effect described now and run once, at the edge | `IO` |
| The same, with failure handling, resources and retries | [IOPath](../effect/path_io.md) |
| A value worked out once and kept, with no side effect | [Lazy](lazy_monad.md) |
| An effect that throws a checked exception, or runs at the same time as other work | [VTask](vtask_monad.md) |
| Work that has already started, on another thread | [CompletableFuture](cf_monad.md) |
| A program as data, which several interpreters can run | [Free](free_monad.md) |

~~~admonish info title="Hands-On Learning"
Practise deferring a side effect with `IOPath`, in Exercise 3 of [Tutorial 01: Effect Path Basics](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/tutorial/effect/Tutorial01_EffectPathBasics.java) (9 exercises).
~~~

**Benchmarks:** `IOBenchmark` measures building and running `IO` chains, and `VTaskVsIOBenchmark` compares them with `VTask`. [Benchmarks & Performance](../benchmarks.md) says how to run them.

---

**Previous:** [Optional](optional_monad.md)
**Next:** [Lazy](lazy_monad.md)

<!-- Source: monads/lazy_monad.md -->

<!-- description: Lazy evaluation in Java: defer an expensive computation with Lazy, memoise the result on first use, even across threads, and choose between Lazy and IO. -->

# Lazy: Computing a Value Once, When Needed

_Work a value out the first time it is asked for, then keep it._

~~~admonish abstract title="Lazy at a glance"
| | |
|---|---|
| **Models** | A value worked out when first asked for, then kept |
| **Replaces** | A field filled in on first use, behind a `null` check |
| **Effect Path** | `LazyPath`, shown in [On a `LazyPath`](#on-a-lazypath) |
| **Instances** | `Functor`, `Applicative`, `Monad` |
| **Choose it when** | The work has no side effect and its result can be kept; otherwise [IO](io_monad.md), or compare every type in [Choosing a Type](supported-types.md#choosing-a-type) |
~~~

~~~admonish info title="What You'll Learn"
- Defer expensive work until a reader asks for it, and do it only once
- Say what happens when that work fails, and when it is read from many threads
- Compose deferred values without working any of them out
- Choose between `Lazy` and `IO` for deferred work
~~~

~~~admonish example title="See Example Code"
**The code on this page is [LazyBook.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/lazy/LazyBook.java)**: the page includes it, so the build compiles and runs it. [LazyBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/lazy/LazyBookTest.java) holds what the page says each method does.
~~~

---

## Working it out once {#working-it-out-once}

A field that is costly to fill and rarely read is often filled on first use, the lazy initialisation idiom: the getter checks for `null`, works the value out, and stores it. Written by hand, that check must also be made safe for several threads, and a `Supplier` would work the value out again on every `get()`. A `Lazy<A>` holds the work and does it the first time it is forced, once, then memoises the result.

The code on this page uses these imports, with `Order` and `LineItem` from the order records the [Optics](../optics/ch_intro.md) chapter introduces:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import java.math.BigDecimal;
import java.util.concurrent.atomic.AtomicInteger;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.LineItem;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.Order;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.lazy.Lazy;
```

An order view shows a shipping quote only when the order is opened, and working one out is slow. `quotesWorkedOut` counts how often it happens:

```java
  // Working out a shipping quote is slow, and most views never show it
  static BigDecimal quoteShipping(Order order, AtomicInteger quotesWorkedOut) {
    quotesWorkedOut.incrementAndGet();
    int items = order.lines().stream().mapToInt(LineItem::quantity).sum();
    return new BigDecimal("4.99")
        .add(new BigDecimal("1.50").multiply(BigDecimal.valueOf(items - 1)));
  }

  record OrderView(Order order, Lazy<BigDecimal> shipping) {}

  static OrderView view(Order order, AtomicInteger quotesWorkedOut) {
    return new OrderView(order, Lazy.defer(() -> quoteShipping(order, quotesWorkedOut)));
  }

```

`ORDER` has three items across two lines:

```java
    AtomicInteger quotes = new AtomicInteger();
    OrderView opened = view(ORDER, quotes);

    int beforeReading = quotes.get();
    // 0
    BigDecimal shipping = opened.shipping().force();
    // 7.99
    BigDecimal readAgain = opened.shipping().force();
    // 7.99
    int afterTwoReads = quotes.get();
    // 1
```

Building the view does no work. The first `force()` works the quote out, and the second returns the same value without working it out again. `force()` is declared `throws Throwable`, because the work may throw anything, so the method that calls it must catch `Throwable` or declare it.

`map` and `flatMap` build a new `Lazy` from ones that have not been forced, and work nothing out themselves. The order's total needs both its subtotal and the quote:

```java
  // The total needs the quote; flatMap joins them without working either out
  static Lazy<BigDecimal> totalWithShipping(Order order, Lazy<BigDecimal> shipping) {
    Lazy<BigDecimal> subtotal =
        Lazy.defer(
            () ->
                order.lines().stream()
                    .map(line -> line.price().multiply(BigDecimal.valueOf(line.quantity())))
                    .reduce(BigDecimal.ZERO, BigDecimal::add));
    return subtotal.flatMap(sum -> shipping.map(sum::add));
  }

```

```java
    AtomicInteger counted = new AtomicInteger();
    Lazy<BigDecimal> total = totalWithShipping(ORDER, view(ORDER, counted).shipping());
    int beforeForcing = counted.get();
    // 0
    BigDecimal forced = total.force();
    // 100.49
```

## When the work fails {#when-the-work-fails}

`Lazy` keeps a failure as it keeps a value: if the work throws on the first `force()`, every later `force()` throws the same exception, without trying the work again. Forced from many threads at once, a `Lazy` still does its work once, and every thread gets the same result.

## On a `LazyPath` {#on-a-lazypath}

Application code can hold the same deferred work on a `LazyPath`. `Path.lazyDefer` takes a `Supplier`, `map` changes the value without working it out, and `get()` works it out once and returns it, rethrowing an exception the work threw. These are the extra imports:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.effect.LazyPath;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.effect.Path;
```

```java
  static LazyPath<String> shippingLabel(Order order, AtomicInteger quotesWorkedOut) {
    return Path.lazyDefer(() -> quoteShipping(order, quotesWorkedOut))
        .map(cost -> "shipping " + cost);
  }

```

```java
    LazyPath<String> label = shippingLabel(ORDER, quotes);
    boolean workedOutYet = label.isEvaluated();
    // false
    String shown = label.get();
```

`shown` is `"shipping 7.99"`. `toIOPath()` turns a `LazyPath` into an `IOPath` and keeps the work deferred, while `toMaybePath()` and `toTryPath()` work the value out at once.

## Creating instances {#creating-instances}

`Lazy.defer(work)` holds work to do later. Its `ThrowableSupplier` may throw any exception, a checked one included. `Lazy.now(value)` holds a value already worked out. A `Lazy` always gives a value: `Lazy.now(null)` throws at the call, and work or a `map` function that gives `null` fails when forced, a failure the `Lazy` keeps like any other. [Values, never null](../hkts/core-concepts.md#values-never-null) sets out the rule.

## Methods {#methods}

| Method | What it does |
|---|---|
| `Lazy.defer(work)` | Holds work to do on the first `force()`; the work may throw any exception |
| `Lazy.now(value)` | Holds a value already worked out |
| `force()` | Works the value out the first time, then returns it or rethrows its failure; declared `throws Throwable` |
| `map(f)`, `flatMap(f)` | Build a new `Lazy`, working nothing out |
| `isEvaluated()`, `hasFailed()` | Say whether a value is held, as it is from `Lazy.now`, and whether the work failed |
| `Path.lazyDefer(supplier)`, `Path.lazyNow(value)` | Build a `LazyPath` |
| `get()` on a `LazyPath` | Works the value out once and returns it; rethrows an unchecked failure as it is, and wraps a checked one in a `RuntimeException` |
| `force()` on a `LazyPath` | The same, rethrowing any failure unchanged |
| `LAZY.widen(lazy)`, `LAZY.narrow(kind)`, `LAZY.force(kind)` | Move a `Lazy` in and out of `Kind`, or force one |

## Working with Kind {#working-with-kind}

Generic code, written once for any container, takes `Lazy` through its `Monad` instance, from `Instances.monad(lazy())`. The instance works over `Kind<LazyKind.Witness, A>`, so you widen a `Lazy` to pass it in, and force the result with `LAZY.force`, which is also declared `throws Throwable`. These are the extra imports:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.lazy;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.lazy.LazyKindHelper.LAZY;

import java.math.RoundingMode;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Kind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Monad;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Instances;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.lazy.LazyKind;
```

```java
    Monad<LazyKind.Witness> deferred = Instances.monad(lazy());

    Kind<LazyKind.Witness, BigDecimal> quoted = LAZY.widen(view(ORDER, quotes).shipping());
    Kind<LazyKind.Witness, BigDecimal> withVat =
        deferred.map(
            cost -> cost.multiply(new BigDecimal("1.20")).setScale(2, RoundingMode.HALF_UP),
            quoted);

    BigDecimal grossShipping = LAZY.force(withVat);
    // 9.59
```

`Lazy` does not implement `LazyKind`, so `LAZY.widen` wraps it in a small holder object each time, and `narrow` unwraps it. The instance has no `MonadError`, so catch a failure around `LAZY.force`.

## When to use `Lazy` {#when-to-use-lazy}

`Lazy` and [IO](io_monad.md) both defer work, and differ in what they do with the result. A kept result is safe only when the work has no side effect: a `Lazy` that read the clock would show the same time for ever.

| You need | Use |
|---|---|
| A costly value, worked out only if read, and then kept | `Lazy` |
| The same as a fluent chain in application code | `LazyPath` |
| Work that reads a file, calls a service or writes to a database, done again on every run | [IO](io_monad.md) |
| Work on another thread | [CompletableFuture](cf_monad.md), already running, or [VTask](vtask_monad.md), which runs when you run it |
| A recursion too deep for the stack | [Trampoline](trampoline_monad.md) |

---

**Previous:** [IO](io_monad.md)
**Next:** [CompletableFuture](cf_monad.md)

<!-- Source: monads/cf_monad.md -->

<!-- description: Use Java's CompletableFuture as a monad: chain and recover futures on a CompletableFuturePath, pass them to generic code through MonadError, or choose VTask. -->

# CompletableFuture: Asynchronous Results

_Keep Java's own `CompletableFuture`, with an Effect Path and a `MonadError` instance._

~~~admonish abstract title="CompletableFuture at a glance"
| | |
|---|---|
| **Models** | Java's asynchronous result, already running |
| **Replaces** | Nothing: it is Java's own type |
| **Effect Path** | `CompletableFuturePath`, shown in [On a `CompletableFuturePath`](#on-a-completablefuturepath) |
| **Instances** | `Functor`, `Applicative`, `Monad`, `MonadError<Throwable>` |
| **Choose it when** | An API gives you a future, or the work is already running on another thread; otherwise [VTask](vtask_monad.md), or compare every type in [Choosing a Type](supported-types.md#choosing-a-type) |
~~~

~~~admonish info title="What You'll Learn"
- Say when a `CompletableFuture`'s work starts, and how that differs from `IO` and `VTask`
- Recover from a failed lookup on a `CompletableFuturePath`, and say why retrying needs the work, not the future
- Combine futures that are already running through `CompletableFuture`'s `MonadError` instance
- Say what the instance's error handler receives, and what widening a future costs
~~~

~~~admonish example title="See Example Code"
**The code on this page is [CompletableFutureBook.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/future/CompletableFutureBook.java)**: the page includes it, so the build compiles and runs it. [CompletableFutureBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/future/CompletableFutureBookTest.java) holds what the page says each method does.
~~~

---

## Java's own type {#javas-own-type}

`java.util.concurrent.CompletableFuture` is how much of Java hands back a result that is not ready yet. The work behind a future has usually started by the time you hold it, often on another thread, where an [IO](io_monad.md) or a [VTask](vtask_monad.md) waits until you run it. Its own methods, such as `thenApply`, `thenCompose` and `exceptionallyCompose`, already chain and recover. Higher-Kinded-J adds an Effect Path, `CompletableFuturePath`, with recovery, timeouts and conversions for application code. It also adds a `MonadError` instance, so that a method written once for any container accepts a future.

The code on this page uses these imports, with `Order` from the order records the [Optics](../optics/ch_intro.md) chapter introduces:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import java.math.BigDecimal;
import java.math.RoundingMode;
import java.util.concurrent.CompletableFuture;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.Order;
```

Pricing an order in euros needs two lookups, each from a slow service, and `supplyAsync` starts each on another thread at once. `rateServiceDown` stands in for the rate service failing:

```java
  // Each lookup starts on another thread as soon as it is called
  static CompletableFuture<BigDecimal> rateToEuros() {
    return CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> new BigDecimal("1.17"));
  }

  static CompletableFuture<BigDecimal> shippingQuote(Order order) {
    return CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(() -> new BigDecimal("7.99"));
  }

  // A lookup whose service fails
  static CompletableFuture<BigDecimal> rateServiceDown() {
    return CompletableFuture.supplyAsync(
        () -> {
          throw new IllegalStateException("the rate service is down");
        });
  }

  static BigDecimal subtotal(Order order) {
    return order.lines().stream()
        .map(line -> line.price().multiply(BigDecimal.valueOf(line.quantity())))
        .reduce(BigDecimal.ZERO, BigDecimal::add);
  }

  static BigDecimal inEuros(Order order, BigDecimal rate, BigDecimal shipping) {
    return subtotal(order).add(shipping).multiply(rate).setScale(2, RoundingMode.HALF_UP);
  }

```

## On a `CompletableFuturePath` {#on-a-completablefuturepath}

`Path.future` puts a future on a `CompletableFuturePath`. `recover` gives a value in place of a failure, here `CACHED_RATE`, 1.15, the rate to use when the rate service cannot answer. `parZipWith` joins the result with another Path's, and `join()` waits for the answer. `via` chains a step that needs the previous result, as `thenCompose` does, and [Migration Cookbook: Recipe 4](../effect/migration_cookbook.md#recipe-4-completablefuture-nesting-to-completablefuturepath) turns a nested chain into one. These are the extra imports:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.effect.CompletableFuturePath;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.effect.Path;
```

```java
  static CompletableFuturePath<BigDecimal> priceInEuros(
      Order order, CompletableFuture<BigDecimal> rate) {
    return Path.future(rate)
        .recover(error -> CACHED_RATE)
        .parZipWith(
            Path.future(shippingQuote(order)), (r, shipping) -> inEuros(order, r, shipping));
  }

```

`ORDER` is worth 92.50 in pounds and shipping costs 7.99, so the live rate gives (92.50 + 7.99) × 1.17 = 117.57:

```java
    BigDecimal priced = priceInEuros(ORDER, rateToEuros()).join();
    // 117.57
    BigDecimal withCachedRate = priceInEuros(ORDER, rateServiceDown()).join();
    // 115.56
```

A `CompletableFuturePath` also times out with `withTimeout`, which fails the Path but leaves the work behind the future running, since a future cannot interrupt it. `toEitherPath()` waits for the future, then holds a failure as a `Left`. A future that has failed stays failed, so to try failed work again, start it with `CompletableFuturePath.supplyAsyncWithRetry(supplier, attempts, delay)`, which runs the supplier again. A future that completes with `null` fails the Path, which catches every `CompletableFuture<Void>` from `runAsync` or `allOf`: map one to `Unit` first, with `thenApply(_ -> Unit.INSTANCE)`.

## Methods {#methods}

`CompletableFuture`'s own methods are the JDK's. The library adds these:

| Method | What it does |
|---|---|
| `Path.future(future)`, `Path.futureAsync(supplier)` | Put a future on a `CompletableFuturePath`, or start one |
| `Path.futureCompleted(value)`, `Path.futureFailed(exception)` | Build a Path that has already succeeded, or failed with an `Exception` |
| `CompletableFuturePath.supplyAsyncWithRetry(supplier, attempts, delay)` | Start work, and run the supplier again after a failure |
| `map(f)`, `via(f)`, `recover(f)` | Change the value, chain a step, or give a value in place of a failure |
| `parZipWith(other, f)`, `race(other)` | Join two Paths' results, or keep whichever succeeds first, failing only if both fail |
| `withTimeout(duration)` | Fails the Path if the future has not completed in time; the work behind it runs on |
| `join()`, `toEitherPath()` | Wait for the result, or wait and then hold a failure as a `Left` |
| `FUTURE.widen(future)`, `FUTURE.narrow(kind)`, `FUTURE.join(kind)` | Move a future in and out of `Kind`, or wait for one |

## Working with Kind {#working-with-kind}

Generic code, written once for any container, takes a `CompletableFuture` through its `MonadError` instance, whose error type is `Throwable`, as [MonadError](../functional/monad_error.md) shows. Each operation is one of the future's own methods:

| Instance operation | `CompletableFuture` method |
|---|---|
| `map(f, fa)` | `thenApply(f)` |
| `flatMap(f, fa)` | `thenCompose(f)` |
| `ap(ff, fa)`, and `map2` to `map5` | `thenCombine`, completing once both have |
| `of(value)` | `completedFuture(value)` |
| `raiseError(error)` | `failedFuture(error)` |
| `handleErrorWith(fa, handler)` | `exceptionallyCompose(handler)` |

These are the extra imports:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.future.CompletableFutureKindHelper.FUTURE;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.completableFuture;

import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Kind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.MonadError;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.future.CompletableFutureKind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Instances;
```

```java
    MonadError<CompletableFutureKind.Witness, Throwable> futures =
        Instances.monadError(completableFuture());

    // Both lookups are already running when map2 is called
    Kind<CompletableFutureKind.Witness, BigDecimal> price =
        futures.map2(
            FUTURE.widen(rateToEuros()),
            FUTURE.widen(shippingQuote(ORDER)),
            (rate, shipping) -> inEuros(ORDER, rate, shipping));
    BigDecimal euros = FUTURE.join(price);
    // 117.57

    Kind<CompletableFutureKind.Witness, BigDecimal> rate =
        futures.handleErrorWith(
            FUTURE.widen(rateServiceDown()),
            error -> FUTURE.widen(CompletableFuture.completedFuture(CACHED_RATE)));
    BigDecimal fallback = FUTURE.join(rate);
    // 1.15
```

`map2` neither starts nor waits for its futures: both lookups were already running, and the future it gives completes once both have. `FUTURE.join` is what waits.

The handler given to `handleErrorWith` receives the exception that failed the future, not the `CompletionException` the JDK wraps it in. `FUTURE.join` rethrows that exception too when it is unchecked; a checked one stays wrapped. A constant fallback is shorter with [`recover` or `recoverWith`](../functional/monad_error.md#constant-fallbacks-recover-and-recoverwith), which build their fallback before the call, so a fallback future runs even when the first one succeeds.

`CompletableFuture` is a JDK class, so it cannot implement `CompletableFutureKind`: `FUTURE.widen` wraps it in a small holder object each time, and `narrow` unwraps it. The instance's `of(null)` keeps the JDK's `null`, where a `CompletableFuturePath` fails.

## When to use `CompletableFuture` {#when-to-use-completablefuture}

| You need | Use |
|---|---|
