<!-- description: Read configuration in Java with Reader: price an order from settings no method takes, run it against two configurations, and adapt it with ReaderPath.local. -->

# Reader: Reading a Shared Environment

_Write each step against the configuration, and supply it once, when the program runs._

~~~admonish abstract title="Reader at a glance"
| | |
|---|---|
| **Models** | A result worked out from an environment, such as settings, given when it runs |
| **Replaces** | Settings injected into a service, or passed down through every method that needs them |
| **Effect Path** | [`ReaderPath`](../effect/advanced_effects.md#readerpath-the-environment-you-inherit) |
| **Instances** | `Functor`, `Applicative`, `Selective`, `Monad` |
| **Choose it when** | You pass the environment to `run` yourself; otherwise [Context](context_scoped.md), which reads what the caller bound to a `ScopedValue`, or compare every type in [Choosing a Type](supported-types.md#choosing-a-type) |
~~~

~~~admonish info title="What You'll Learn"
- Price an order from a `PricingConfig` that no method takes as a parameter
- Say what each step of a `Reader` chain receives, and when
- Run one reader against two configurations, so a test needs no mock
- Change the configuration for one part of a computation with `ReaderPath.local`
- Use `Reader` in generic code through its `Monad` instance
~~~

~~~admonish example title="See Example Code"
**The code on this page is [ReaderBook.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/reader/ReaderBook.java)**: the page includes it, so the build compiles and runs it. [ReaderBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/reader/ReaderBookTest.java) holds what the page says each method does.
~~~

---

## Pricing from one configuration {#pricing-from-one-configuration}

What an order costs depends on settings: the VAT rate, the delivery charge, and the order value from which delivery is free. Pass those settings to every method that prices something and they spread through each signature on the way down. Inject them into a service's constructor instead, and the service prices everything with the settings it was built with. A `Reader<R, A>` is a function from an environment `R` to a result `A`. `Function.andThen` already gives you its `map`. What a `Reader` adds is `flatMap`, which hands the next step the previous result and the same environment. So the settings go in once, when you run the reader, and each run can be given different ones.

The code on this page uses these imports, with `Order` from the order records the [Optics](../optics/ch_intro.md) chapter introduces, and `PricingConfig` from this chapter's examples:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import java.math.BigDecimal;
import java.math.RoundingMode;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.foundations.PricingConfig;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.Order;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.effect.Path;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.effect.ReaderPath;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.reader.Reader;
```

`PricingConfig` is a record of the examples' own:

```java
/**
 * What pricing reads from its environment: the VAT rate, and the delivery charge for an order below
 * the free-delivery threshold.
 */
public record PricingConfig(
    BigDecimal vatRate, BigDecimal deliveryCharge, BigDecimal freeDeliveryFrom) {

  public PricingConfig {
    Objects.requireNonNull(vatRate, "vatRate");
    Objects.requireNonNull(deliveryCharge, "deliveryCharge");
    Objects.requireNonNull(freeDeliveryFrom, "freeDeliveryFrom");
  }
}
```

Delivery is charged on an order under the threshold, and VAT is added to the whole:

```java
  // What the goods cost, before delivery and VAT
  static BigDecimal net(Order order) {
    return order.lines().stream()
        .map(line -> line.price().multiply(BigDecimal.valueOf(line.quantity())))
        .reduce(BigDecimal.ZERO, BigDecimal::add);
  }

  // Delivery is charged below the threshold
  static Reader<PricingConfig, BigDecimal> delivery(BigDecimal net) {
    return Reader.of(
        config ->
            net.compareTo(config.freeDeliveryFrom()) >= 0
                ? BigDecimal.ZERO
                : config.deliveryCharge());
  }

  // VAT on the whole
  static Reader<PricingConfig, BigDecimal> withVat(BigDecimal amount) {
    return Reader.of(
        config ->
            amount
                .multiply(BigDecimal.ONE.add(config.vatRate()))
                .setScale(2, RoundingMode.HALF_UP));
  }

  // No method here takes the configuration: each step is given it when the reader runs
  static Reader<PricingConfig, BigDecimal> toPay(Order order) {
    BigDecimal net = net(order);
    return delivery(net).flatMap(charge -> withVat(net.add(charge)));
  }

```

`toPay` describes the price without a configuration to hand. `STANDARD` charges VAT at 20%, and 4.95 for delivery on an order under 50.00. `SALE` is the same, with free delivery from 30.00. `LAMP_ORDER` is one lamp at 40.00. Run the reader with one configuration, then with the other:

```java
    Reader<PricingConfig, BigDecimal> lampToPay = toPay(LAMP_ORDER);

    BigDecimal usually = lampToPay.run(STANDARD);
    // 53.94
    BigDecimal inTheSale = lampToPay.run(SALE);
    // 48.00
```

The lamp costs 40.00, under the usual threshold of 50.00, so the usual price adds the 4.95 delivery charge before VAT: (40.00 + 4.95) × 1.20 = 53.94. The sale's threshold is 30.00, so the same reader charges no delivery: 40.00 × 1.20 = 48.00. A test prices against whatever configuration it builds, with no mock to set up.

## Each step is given the configuration {#each-step-is-given-the-configuration}

`flatMap` runs the first reader, passes its result to the next step, and runs that step with the same environment. Nothing reads the configuration until `run` is called, and a reader keeps no result: each `run` calls its functions again, with the environment it is given.

```mermaid
flowchart TB
    accTitle: How one run of toPay uses its configuration
    accDescr: Running toPay with the standard configuration gives that configuration to both steps. The delivery step reads the threshold and the delivery charge, and gives 4.95. The VAT step receives that charge as its input, reads the VAT rate, and gives 53.94.
    run["toPay(order).run(STANDARD)"]
    config["the PricingConfig STANDARD"]
    delivery["delivery(40.00)<br/>reads the threshold and the charge"]
    vat["withVat(40.00 + 4.95)<br/>reads the VAT rate"]
    result["53.94"]
    run --> config
    config --> delivery
    config --> vat
    delivery -- "4.95" --> vat
    vat --> result
    classDef domain fill:#a6d189,stroke:#40a02b,color:#232634
    classDef step fill:#8caaee,stroke:#1e66f5,color:#232634
    class run,delivery,vat step
    class config,result domain
```

The configuration goes to every step, while each result goes only to the step after it. `Reader.ask()` gives the environment itself, for a step that needs the whole of it.

## On a `ReaderPath` {#on-a-readerpath}

Application code holds a reader on a `ReaderPath`, its Effect Path, and `Path.reader(reader)` gives you one. It chains with `via`, combines with `zipWith`, and adds `local`, which runs a reader against a configuration changed for that reader alone. A quote can then show what a member pays, with delivery free whatever the order costs:

```java
  // The configuration a member sees: the same, with delivery free on every order
  static PricingConfig forMembers(PricingConfig config) {
    return new PricingConfig(config.vatRate(), config.deliveryCharge(), BigDecimal.ZERO);
  }

  static ReaderPath<PricingConfig, String> quote(Order order) {
    ReaderPath<PricingConfig, BigDecimal> usual = Path.reader(toPay(order));
    ReaderPath<PricingConfig, BigDecimal> members = usual.local(ReaderBook::forMembers);
    return usual.zipWith(members, (pay, memberPays) -> pay + ", or " + memberPays + " for members");
  }

```

```java
    String quoted = quote(LAMP_ORDER).run(STANDARD);
```

`quoted` is `"53.94, or 48.00 for members"`. The standard configuration is passed once: `usual` reads it as it is, and `members` reads it through `forMembers`. `local` also adapts a reader written for a part of a larger environment, such as a `PricingConfig` inside the application's settings. `magnify` does the same from a `Getter` or a `FocusPath`, as [Local Environment Modification](../effect/advanced_effects.md#local-environment-modification) shows.

## Creating instances {#creating-instances}

`Reader.of(function)` wraps a function of the environment, `Reader.ask()` gives the environment itself, and `Reader.constant(value)` gives a value whatever the environment holds. `Reader.unit()` gives [`Unit`](../hkts/core-concepts.md#unit), and `Reader.fromConsumer(action)` runs an action with the environment and gives `Unit`. On a Path, `Path.reader(reader)`, `Path.ask()`, `Path.asks(function)` and `Path.readerPure(value)` start a `ReaderPath`. A `Reader` from these factories always gives a value, as [Values, never null](../hkts/core-concepts.md#values-never-null) sets out. `constant(null)` throws at the call, and a function given to `of` or `map` that returns `null` fails the reader when it runs. `Reader` is a functional interface, so a lambda written as one directly has no such check: build a reader with `Reader.of`.

## Methods {#methods}

| Method | What it does |
|---|---|
| `Reader.of(function)` | Wraps a function of the environment |
| `Reader.ask()` | Gives the environment itself |
| `Reader.constant(value)` | Gives `value`, whatever the environment |
| `Reader.unit()` | Gives `Unit`, whatever the environment |
| `Reader.fromConsumer(action)` | Runs `action` with the environment, and gives `Unit` |
| `map(f)` | Builds a reader whose result is changed by `f` |
| `flatMap(f)` | Builds a reader that runs the next step on this one's result, with the same environment |
| `asUnit()` | Keeps the computation and replaces the result with `Unit` |
| `run(environment)` | Runs the reader with `environment` and gives its result |
| `readerPath.via(f)`, `zipWith(other, f)` | Chain or combine `ReaderPath` steps over one environment |
| `readerPath.local(f)` | Runs the path against the environment `f` gives |
| `readerPath.magnify(getter)` | Runs the path against the part of a larger environment the `Getter` or `FocusPath` reads |
| `readerPath.toReader()` | Gives the `Reader` the path holds |

## Working with Kind {#working-with-kind}

Generic code, written once for any container, takes `Reader` through its `Monad` instance, from `Instances.monad(reader())`. The environment type is part of the witness, `ReaderKind.Witness<R>`, so one instance serves readers of one environment type. You widen a `Reader` to pass it in, run the result with `READER.runReader`, and `READER.narrow(kind)` gives the `Reader` back. These are the extra imports:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.reader;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.reader.ReaderKindHelper.READER;

import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Kind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Monad;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Instances;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.reader.ReaderKind;
```

```java
    Monad<ReaderKind.Witness<PricingConfig>> pricing = Instances.monad(reader());

    Kind<ReaderKind.Witness<PricingConfig>, BigDecimal> lamp = READER.widen(toPay(LAMP_ORDER));
    Kind<ReaderKind.Witness<PricingConfig>, String> label =
        pricing.map(total -> "pay " + total, lamp);

    String ran = READER.runReader(label, STANDARD);
```

`ran` is `"pay 53.94"`. Widening allocates nothing: `Reader` implements `ReaderKind` itself, so `widen` checks for `null` and returns the same object. `Reader` has a `Selective` instance too, `ReaderSelective.instance()`, an object of its own, and *Reader at a glance*, at the head of the page, lists it. Code that reads an environment through any monad, not only `Reader`, takes a `MonadReader`, as [Reading settings with `MonadReader`](../functional/for_mtl.md#reading-configuration-with-monadreader) shows.

## When to use `Reader` {#when-to-use-reader}

| You need | Use |
|---|---|
| Steps that read one environment, supplied once when the program runs | `Reader`, or [ReaderPath](../effect/advanced_effects.md#readerpath-the-environment-you-inherit) |
| A value the request binds, which threads forked for that request should see | [Context](context_scoped.md) |
| An environment, together with failure or asynchronous work | [ConfigContext](../effect/effect_contexts_config.md), or [ReaderT](../transformers/readert_transformer.md) |
| Steps that each change a value the next step reads | [State](state_monad.md) |
| One or two methods that need the setting | A parameter: a reader earns its place when many steps share the environment |

~~~admonish info title="Hands-On Learning"
Practise threading configuration with `Reader`, in Exercise 3 of [Tutorial 07: Real World](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/tutorial/coretypes/Tutorial07_RealWorld.java) (6 exercises), and `magnify` in Exercise 5 of [Tutorial 05: Optic-Polymorphic Zoom and Magnify](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/tutorial/expression/Tutorial05_ZoomAndMagnify.java) (6 exercises).
~~~

**Next:** [Context](context_scoped.md)

<!-- description: Read a request's trace id in Java with Context and ScopedValue: bind it around the run, see which forked threads inherit it, and default an unbound key. -->

# Context: Reading Scoped Values

_Read what the request bound, such as its trace id, without passing it through every call._

<!-- source: Matt Hooper (Richard Dreyfuss), Jaws (Universal Pictures, 1975), directed by Steven Spielberg, screenplay by Peter Benchley and Carl Gottlieb; the morgue scene, where Hooper rejects the ruling that Chrissie Watkins died in a boating accident; wording checked against the IMDb quotes page and the clip.cafe transcript of the scene -->
> _"This was no boat accident."_
>
> — Hooper, *Jaws*

Hooper will not accept the coroner's ruling: something did this, and he means to name it. A value read from a `ThreadLocal` was put there by something too, but the method's signature never says what. A method that returns a `Context<String, A>` says in its type that it reads a `String` the caller bound.

~~~admonish abstract title="Context at a glance"
| | |
|---|---|
| **Models** | A computation that reads a value bound to a `ScopedValue` when it runs |
| **Replaces** | A `ThreadLocal`, set at the start of a request and cleared at the end |
| **Effect Path** | None: `toVTask()` gives a [VTask](vtask_monad.md), and `Path.vtaskPath(task)` a [`VTaskPath`](../effect/path_vtask.md) |
| **Instances** | `Functor`, `Applicative`, `Monad` |
| **Choose it when** | The value belongs to the request, and the threads forked for it should see it; otherwise [Reader](reader_monad.md), which is given its environment when it runs, or compare every type in [Choosing a Type](supported-types.md#choosing-a-type) |
~~~

~~~admonish info title="What You'll Learn"
- Say what a `ScopedValue` changes about request context kept in a `ThreadLocal`
- Read a trace id with `Context.asks`, and bind it with `ScopedValue.where` around running the task, not building it
- Say which threads see a binding: those `Par` and `Scope` fork do, and the one `VTask.timeout` starts does not
- Read keys of different types together, and give a default for a key nobody bound
- Use `Context` in generic code through its `Monad` instance
~~~

~~~admonish example title="See Example Code"
**The code on this page is [ContextBook.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/context/ContextBook.java)**: the page includes it, so the build compiles and runs it. [ContextBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/context/ContextBookTest.java) holds what the page says each method does.
~~~

---

## From `ThreadLocal` to `ScopedValue` {#from-threadlocal-to-scopedvalue}

A request handler that keeps its trace id in a `ThreadLocal` must set it at the start and remember to clear it at the end. In between, any code it calls can set it to something else. [JEP 506](https://openjdk.org/jeps/506), which made `ScopedValue` final in Java 25, names three flaws of a `ThreadLocal`. The table sets each beside what a `ScopedValue`, bound for the length of one call, does instead:

| | `ThreadLocal` | `ScopedValue` |
|---|---|---|
| Changing the value | Any code that can reach it can `set` it, at any time | A nested call can bind a new value, and the outer value is back once that call returns |
| How long it lasts | Until something calls `remove`, or the thread ends | Until the call that bound it returns |
| Child threads | `InheritableThreadLocal` copies the values into each new thread, which costs every one storage of its own | The threads a `StructuredTaskScope` forks share the bindings of the thread that opened it |

`Context<R, A>` reads a `ScopedValue<R>` and gives an `A`. Like [Reader](reader_monad.md), it needs a value that no method passes it. Unlike `Reader`, it finds that value in the bindings of the thread that runs it. Calling `get()` on the `ScopedValue` reads the value too. What a `Context` adds is that the read shows in its type, waits until the context runs, and composes with `map`, `flatMap` and `recover`.

## Reading a trace id {#reading-a-trace-id}

The code on this page uses these imports, with `Order` from the order records the [Optics](../optics/ch_intro.md) chapter introduces:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import java.time.Duration;
import java.util.Locale;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.Order;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.context.Context;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.context.RequestContext;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.trymonad.Try;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.vtask.Par;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.vtask.VTask;
```

`RequestContext.TRACE_ID` is one of the keys the library defines, listed under [Pre-built keys](#pre-built-keys). It is a `public static final ScopedValue<String>`, made with `ScopedValue.newInstance()`, and you declare a key of your own the same way. `ScopedValue` is in `java.lang`, so it needs no import. `ORDER` is an order Ada has paid for, and an audit line about it reads the trace id:

```java
  // The trace id comes from the binding, not from a parameter
  static Context<String, String> auditLine(Order order, String event) {
    return Context.asks(
        RequestContext.TRACE_ID,
        traceId -> "[%s] %s for %s".formatted(traceId, event, order.customer().name()));
  }

```

`ScopedValue.where` binds a value, and the context runs inside the call that follows:

```java
    Context<String, String> paid = auditLine(ORDER, "payment taken");

    String line = ScopedValue.where(RequestContext.TRACE_ID, "req-7").call(paid::run);
```

`line` is `"[req-7] payment taken for Ada"`. Building `paid` reads nothing, and the binding lasts only while `call` runs. Inside a nested `where`, the inner value hides the outer one until that call returns. A test binds whatever trace id it needs in the same way, with nothing to mock.

## Bind around the run {#bind-around-the-run}

`toVTask()` turns a context into a [VTask](vtask_monad.md): a task that runs only when something runs it, and that `Par` can fork. `runSafe()` runs a task and holds its outcome in a [Try](try_monad.md), a `Success` with the value or a `Failure` with the exception. A binding made while a task is built has ended before the task reads it:

```java
  // The binding ends when call returns, before anything runs the task it built
  static VTask<String> boundWhileBuilding(Order order, String traceId) {
    return ScopedValue.where(RequestContext.TRACE_ID, traceId)
        .call(() -> auditLine(order, "payment taken").toVTask());
  }

  // The binding lasts while the task runs, so the task reads it
  static Try<String> boundWhileRunning(Order order, String traceId) {
    VTask<String> task = auditLine(order, "payment taken").toVTask();
    return ScopedValue.where(RequestContext.TRACE_ID, traceId).call(task::runSafe);
  }

```

```java
    Try<String> tooEarly = boundWhileBuilding(ORDER, "req-7").runSafe();
    // Failure(java.util.NoSuchElementException: ScopedValue not bound)
    Try<String> whileRunning = boundWhileRunning(ORDER, "req-7");
    // Success([req-7] payment taken for Ada)
```

`boundWhileBuilding` returns a task built inside a binding that has since ended. Running it fails with `NoSuchElementException`, which `ScopedValue.get` throws for a key that is not bound. Inside another binding of `TRACE_ID`, it would read that one instead, and nothing would warn you. Put the `where` at the edge of the application, around the call that runs the task, as `boundWhileRunning` does.

## Which threads see a binding {#which-threads-see-a-binding}

A binding belongs to the thread that runs the task, and to the threads a `StructuredTaskScope` forks from it. [`Par`](vtask_monad.md#parallel-composition-with-par) and [`Scope`](vtask_scope.md) open their scope on the thread that runs the task, so the tasks they fork see its bindings. `VTask.timeout` runs its task on a new virtual thread, which does not. `Par` and `Scope` are built on Java 25's structured concurrency API, a preview feature, so code that names them needs `--enable-preview`, as [On a `VTaskPath`](vtask_monad.md#on-a-vtaskpath) explains.

```java
    VTask<String> both =
        Par.map2(
            paid.toVTask(),
            auditLine(ORDER, "receipt sent").toVTask(),
            (first, second) -> first + "; " + second);
    VTask<String> timed = paid.toVTask().timeout(Duration.ofSeconds(30));

    Try<String> forked = ScopedValue.where(RequestContext.TRACE_ID, "req-7").call(both::runSafe);
    // Success([req-7] payment taken for Ada; [req-7] receipt sent for Ada)
    Try<String> ownThread =
        ScopedValue.where(RequestContext.TRACE_ID, "req-7").call(timed::runSafe);
    // Failure(java.util.NoSuchElementException: ScopedValue not bound)
```

```mermaid
flowchart TB
    accTitle: Which threads see a ScopedValue binding
    accDescr: A call binds TRACE_ID to req-7 and runs a task. The thread running the task sees req-7. Par, Scope and parEvalMap fork their tasks in a structured scope opened on that thread, so each fork sees req-7 too. VTask.timeout starts a virtual thread of its own, where TRACE_ID is not bound.
    bind["ScopedValue.where(TRACE_ID, req-7)<br/>.call(task::runSafe)"]
    running["the thread running the task<br/>sees req-7"]
    forking["Par, Scope, parEvalMap<br/>fork in a StructuredTaskScope"]
    fork1["fork: sees req-7"]
    fork2["fork: sees req-7"]
    own["VTask.timeout<br/>starts a thread of its own"]
    unbound["that thread: TRACE_ID not bound"]
    bind --> running
    running --> forking
    forking --> fork1
    forking --> fork2
    running --> own
    own --> unbound
    classDef domain fill:#a6d189,stroke:#40a02b,color:#232634
    classDef step fill:#8caaee,stroke:#1e66f5,color:#232634
    classDef error fill:#e78284,stroke:#d20f39,color:#232634
    class bind,forking,own step
    class running,fork1,fork2 domain
    class unbound error
```

The diagram shows the binding reaching every thread forked by a structured scope opened on the running thread. It stops at a thread an operator starts by itself, even one that opens a scope of its own there. [`VStreamPar`](vstream_parallel.md) runs a stream's tasks at the same time, and its operators fall on both sides:

| Operator | Its tasks see the caller's bindings |
|---|---|
| `Par`: `map2`, `map3`, `zip`, `zip3`, `all`, `traverse`, `race` | Yes |
| `Scope`: `allSucceed`, `anySucceed`, `firstComplete`, `accumulating`, then `join`, with or without the scope's own `timeout` | Yes |
| `VStreamPar.parEvalMap` | Yes |
| `VTask.timeout`, `detachOnCancel` and `runAsync` | No: each runs its task on a thread of its own |
| `VStreamPar.parEvalMapUnordered` | No: it runs its tasks, and reads the source, on threads of its own |
| `VStreamPar.merge` of two or more streams | No: it reads each stream on a thread of its own |
| `VTaskPath.timeout` and `withTimeout`, and `Resilience.builder(task).withTimeout(duration)` | No: they run the task through `VTask.timeout` |
| `CircuitBreaker.protect`, and `VTaskPath.withCircuitBreaker` | No: each call runs under the breaker's `callTimeout`, through `VTask.timeout` |

To carry a value onto a thread that does not see it, read it on the thread that runs the task, then bind it again inside the task that `timeout` runs:

```java
  // Read the trace id on the thread that runs the task, then bind it again inside the timed task,
  // which runs on a thread of its own. Only TRACE_ID is carried, so a step that reads another key
  // needs that key bound again too
  static VTask<String> timedWithTrace(Context<String, String> step, Duration limit) {
    return Context.ask(RequestContext.TRACE_ID)
        .toVTask()
        .flatMap(
            traceId ->
                VTask.of(() -> ScopedValue.where(RequestContext.TRACE_ID, traceId).call(step::run))
                    .timeout(limit));
  }

```

```java
    VTask<String> carried = timedWithTrace(paid, Duration.ofSeconds(30));

    Try<String> timedAndTraced =
        ScopedValue.where(RequestContext.TRACE_ID, "req-7").call(carried::runSafe);
    // Success([req-7] payment taken for Ada)
```

`timedWithTrace` carries `TRACE_ID` alone. A step that reads another key needs that key read and bound again in the same way.

## Several keys {#several-keys}

`R` in `Context<R, A>` is the type of the value a chain reads, and every step of a chain reads the same type. `TRACE_ID` and `CORRELATION_ID` are both `String` keys, so they share a chain. A step that reads `LOCALE`, a `Locale` key, does not join a chain that reads `String`, and javac reports that the type variable "has incompatible equality constraints String,Locale":

<!-- verify:rejects "has incompatible equality constraints" -->
```java
import java.util.Locale;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.context.Context;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.context.RequestContext;

Context<String, String> both =
    Context.ask(RequestContext.TRACE_ID)
        .flatMap(traceId -> Context.asks(RequestContext.LOCALE, Locale::toLanguageTag));
```

Turn each read into a `VTask` with `toVTask()`, and chain those:

```java
  // TRACE_ID holds a String and LOCALE a Locale, so the two reads meet in a VTask
  static VTask<String> confirmation(Order order) {
    return Context.ask(RequestContext.TRACE_ID)
        .toVTask()
        .flatMap(
            traceId ->
                Context.asks(RequestContext.LOCALE, Locale::toLanguageTag)
                    .toVTask()
                    .map(
                        language ->
                            "[%s] confirmation for %s in %s"
                                .formatted(traceId, order.customer().name(), language)));
  }

```

`where` calls chain, binding several keys for one call:

```java
    VTask<String> confirm = confirmation(ORDER);

    Try<String> confirmed =
        ScopedValue.where(RequestContext.TRACE_ID, "req-7")
            .where(RequestContext.LOCALE, Locale.UK)
            .call(confirm::runSafe);
    // Success([req-7] confirmation for Ada in en-GB)
```

## A key nobody bound {#a-key-nobody-bound}

Reading a key that is not bound fails. `run` throws the `NoSuchElementException`, and `runSafe()` on the task from `toVTask()` holds it as a `Failure`. `recover` gives a default in its place:

```java
    Try<String> nobodyBound = paid.toVTask().runSafe();
    // Failure(java.util.NoSuchElementException: ScopedValue not bound)
    String withDefault = Context.ask(RequestContext.TRACE_ID).recover(error -> "no trace").run();
```

`withDefault` is `"no trace"`. `toMaybe()` runs the context at once and gives `Nothing` for any failure. A key bound to `null` fails too: `ask` fails with a `NullPointerException` when it runs. Read a key that may hold `null` with `asks(key, Maybe::fromNullable)`, which gives [`Maybe`](maybe_monad.md)'s `Nothing` for it. `RequestContext.getTraceIdOrDefault(fallback)` reads the trace id directly, with a fallback.

## Pre-built keys {#pre-built-keys}

The library defines the keys a request usually carries, in two classes:

| Class | Keys | Helpers |
|---|---|---|
| `RequestContext` | `TRACE_ID`, `CORRELATION_ID`, `LOCALE`, `TENANT_ID`, `REQUEST_TIME`, `DEADLINE` | `generateTraceId()`, `getTraceIdOrDefault(fallback)` and the other defaults, `isDeadlineExceeded()` |
| `SecurityContext` | `PRINCIPAL`, `ROLES`, `PERMISSIONS`, `AUTH_TOKEN`, `SESSION_ID` | `Context` values such as `requireAuthenticated()`, `principalIfPresent()`, `hasRole(role)` and `requireRole(role)` |

[RequestContext Patterns](../effect/context_request.md) shows each request key in use, including [structured logging](../effect/context_request.md#mdc-integration-for-structured-logging) from scoped values. [SecurityContext Patterns](../effect/context_security.md) shows the security checks.

## Creating instances {#creating-instances}

`Context.ask(key)` reads a key, and `Context.asks(key, f)` reads it and applies `f`. `Context.succeed(value)` gives a value without reading anything. `Context.fail(error)` fails when it runs, though an `Error` is thrown at the call. It wraps a checked exception in a `ContextException`, so `run` declares none. `recover` and `recoverWith` are handed the original exception, while `runSafe()` on the task from `toVTask()` holds the `ContextException`. `Context.map2` and `map3` combine two or three contexts over one key type. A `Context` always gives a value, as [Values, never null](../hkts/core-concepts.md#values-never-null) sets out. `succeed(null)` throws at the call, and a step that returns `null` fails the run, with a message naming the function, such as `f must not return null`.

## Methods {#methods}

| Method | What it does |
|---|---|
| `Context.ask(key)`, `asks(key, f)` | Read the value bound to `key`, as it is or through `f` |
| `Context.succeed(value)`, `fail(error)` | Give `value`, or fail with `error`, reading nothing |
| `Context.map2(a, b, f)`, `map3(a, b, c, f)` | Combine contexts over one key type |
| `map(f)`, `flatMap(f)` | Change the result, or run a next step that reads the same key type |
| `recover(f)`, `recoverWith(f)`, `mapError(f)` | Replace a failure with a value or another context, or change the failure |
| `run()` | Reads the bindings of the current thread and gives the result, or throws |
| `toVTask()` | Gives a `VTask` that runs the context on the thread that runs it |
| `toMaybe()` | Runs the context at once, and gives `Nothing` for any failure |
| `asUnit()` | Keeps the computation and replaces the result with `Unit` |

## Working with Kind {#working-with-kind}

Generic code, written once for any container, takes `Context` through its `Monad` instance, from `Instances.monad(context())`. The key type is part of the witness, `ContextKind.Witness<R>`, so one instance serves contexts that read one key type. You widen a `Context` to pass it in, run the result with `CONTEXT.runContext` inside the binding, and `CONTEXT.narrow(kind)` gives the `Context` back. These are the extra imports:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.context.ContextKindHelper.CONTEXT;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.context;

import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Kind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Monad;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.context.ContextKind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Instances;
```

```java
    Monad<ContextKind.Witness<String>> traced = Instances.monad(context());

    Kind<ContextKind.Witness<String>, String> taken = CONTEXT.widen(paid);
    Kind<ContextKind.Witness<String>, String> sent =
        CONTEXT.widen(auditLine(ORDER, "receipt sent"));
    Kind<ContextKind.Witness<String>, String> together =
        traced.map2(taken, sent, (first, second) -> first + "; " + second);

    String ran =
        ScopedValue.where(RequestContext.TRACE_ID, "req-7")
            .call(() -> CONTEXT.runContext(together));
```

`ran` is `"[req-7] payment taken for Ada; [req-7] receipt sent for Ada"`. The instance's `map2` joins the two reads, and nothing reads the trace id until `runContext` runs them. The `Monad` helpers, such as `peek` and `flatMapIfOrElse`, work on it too, as [Helpers built on `flatMap` and `map`](../functional/monad.md#helpers) shows. Widening allocates nothing: `Context` implements `ContextKind` itself, so `widen` checks for `null` and returns the same object.

## When to use `Context` {#when-to-use-context}

| You need | Use |
|---|---|
| A value the request binds, read deep in the call stack and by the threads forked for it | `Context` |
| One read, in code that already runs inside the binding | `RequestContext.TRACE_ID.get()`, or your own key's `get()` |
| One environment, given to the program when it runs | [Reader](reader_monad.md) |
| To choose between `Context` and `ConfigContext`, the Effect chapter's form of `Reader` | [Context vs ConfigContext](../effect/context_vs_config.md) |
| The trace id in a library that reads a `ThreadLocal`, such as a logging MDC | Copy the scoped values into it at the edge, as [Bridging to SLF4J MDC](../effect/context_request.md#bridging-to-slf4j-mdc) shows |

~~~admonish info title="Hands-On Learning"
Practise reading and binding scoped values in [Tutorial 01: Context Basics](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/tutorial/context/Tutorial01_ContextBasics.java) (11 exercises).
~~~

**Next:** [State](state_monad.md)

<!-- Source: monads/state_monad.md -->

<!-- description: Update a value step by step in Java with State: add to a basket within a limit, run the steps from any basket, and sequence a bank account's month. -->

# State: Updating a Value Step by Step

_Each step is handed the state, and returns its result with the state it leaves for the next step._

<!-- source: Rich Hickey, "Simple Made Easy", a keynote at Strange Loop, 20 September 2011, given again at QCon London 2012; the slide title "State is Never Simple", whose first bullet is "Complects value and time", checked against the QCon London 2012 slide deck (RichHickey_SimpleMadeEasy.pdf), and set here in sentence case -->
> _"State is never simple."_
>
> — Rich Hickey, *Simple Made Easy*

Hickey's charge is against the variable, which braids a value together with time: whoever reads it must know what changed it, and when. A `State` keeps no variable. Each step is handed one state and gives the next, and the order of the steps is the only time there is.

~~~admonish abstract title="State at a glance"
| | |
|---|---|
| **Models** | A computation that is handed a state, such as a basket, and gives a result with the state it leaves |
| **Replaces** | A field that each method changes, or methods that take a value and return a pair of the result and the new value |
| **Effect Path** | [`WithStatePath`](../effect/advanced_effects.md#withstatepath-computation-with-memory) |
| **Instances** | `Functor`, `Applicative`, `Monad` |
| **Choose it when** | Each step works from what the steps before it left; otherwise [Reader](reader_monad.md), whose environment no step changes, or compare every type in [Choosing a Type](supported-types.md#choosing-a-type) |
~~~

~~~admonish info title="What You'll Learn"
- Add to a basket with steps that each give a result and the basket they leave
- Say what each step of a `State` chain is handed, and what it hands on
- Run the same steps from any basket, so a test starts from whatever basket it builds
- Combine steps on a `WithStatePath`, and sequence a month of banking with `For`
- Use `State` in generic code through its `Monad` instance
~~~

~~~admonish example title="See Example Code"
**The code on this page is [StateBook.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/state/StateBook.java) and [BankBook.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/state/BankBook.java)**: the page includes them, so the build compiles and runs them. [StateBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/state/StateBookTest.java) and [BankBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/state/BankBookTest.java) hold what the page says each method does.
~~~

---

## Adding to a basket {#adding-to-a-basket}

A customer's basket changes as they shop, and some steps report something too: whether a line went in, or what the basket comes to. Keep the basket in a field, and every method that changes it shares that field. A method's result then depends on what ran before it, which its signature never says. Pass the basket in and return a pair instead, and every caller must unpack the pair and hand the new basket to the next call. A `State<S, A>` is that function, from a state `S` to a result `A` and the next state, with the handing on done for you.

The code on this page uses these imports, with `LineItem` from the order records the [Optics](../optics/ch_intro.md) chapter introduces, and `Basket` from this chapter's examples:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import java.math.BigDecimal;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.stream.Stream;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.foundations.Basket;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.LineItem;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.effect.Path;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.effect.WithStatePath;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.state.State;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.state.StateTuple;
```

`Basket` is a record of the examples' own:

```java
/** The lines a customer has chosen so far. Adding a line gives a new basket. */
public record Basket(List<LineItem> lines) {

  public static final Basket EMPTY = new Basket(List.of());

  public Basket {
    lines = List.copyOf(lines);
  }

  public Basket add(LineItem line) {
    return new Basket(Stream.concat(lines.stream(), Stream.of(line)).toList());
  }

  /** Each line's price times its quantity, summed. */
  public BigDecimal total() {
    return lines.stream()
        .map(line -> line.price().multiply(BigDecimal.valueOf(line.quantity())))
        .reduce(BigDecimal.ZERO, BigDecimal::add);
  }
}
```

`Basket.add` gives a new basket, and leaves the one it was called on as it was. `State` makes no copy of the state it hands on. Keep the state an immutable value, as `Basket` is, since a step that changed it in place would change the caller's object too.

The step `add` puts a line in the basket if the total stays within `LIMIT`, which is 100.00, and gives `true`. Otherwise it gives `false`, and hands on the basket it was given. A `StateTuple` holds a step's result and the state it leaves:

```java
  // Adds the line if the basket stays within the limit, and says whether it did
  static State<Basket, Boolean> add(LineItem line) {
    return State.of(
        basket -> {
          Basket bigger = basket.add(line);
          return bigger.total().compareTo(LIMIT) <= 0
              ? new StateTuple<>(true, bigger)
              : new StateTuple<>(false, basket);
        });
  }

```

`LAMP` is one lamp at 40.00, `SOFA` one sofa at 450.00, and `BULBS` four bulbs at 2.50 each. Add the lamp, then the sofa:

```java
    State<Basket, Boolean> lampThenSofa = add(LAMP).flatMap(_ -> add(SOFA));

    StateTuple<Basket, Boolean> after = lampThenSofa.run(Basket.EMPTY);
    boolean sofaAdded = after.value();
    // false
    BigDecimal total = after.state().total();
    // 40.00

    BigDecimal fromBulbs = lampThenSofa.run(new Basket(List.of(BULBS))).state().total();
    // 50.00
```

The lamp goes in. The sofa would take the basket to 490.00, so `add(SOFA)` gives `false` and leaves the basket holding only the lamp. A `State` holds no basket of its own, so run the same steps from a basket of bulbs and they start from that one. The lamp joins the bulbs, the sofa is refused again, and the basket comes to 50.00. A test builds the basket it needs and runs the steps from it.

## Each step is handed the basket {#each-step-is-handed-the-basket}

Nothing runs until `run` is called. Then `flatMap` runs `add(LAMP)` on the basket it was given, and passes the result to `_ -> add(SOFA)`. The step that function returns runs on the basket `add(LAMP)` left.

```mermaid
flowchart TB
    accTitle: How one run of lampThenSofa hands the basket on
    accDescr: Running lampThenSofa from the empty basket hands that basket to add(LAMP), which gives true and a basket holding the lamp. The result true goes to the function _ -> add(SOFA), which gives the step add(SOFA). That step is handed the basket holding the lamp. The sofa would take it over the limit, so add(SOFA) gives false and the same basket, which is what the run gives.
    run["lampThenSofa.run(Basket.EMPTY)"]
    lamp["add(LAMP)<br/>40.00 is within the limit"]
    pick["_ -> add(SOFA)"]
    sofa["add(SOFA)<br/>490.00 would be over it"]
    result["false, with the basket holding the lamp"]
    run -- "the empty basket" --> lamp
    lamp -- "true" --> pick
    pick -- "gives the step add(SOFA)" --> sofa
    lamp -- "the basket holding the lamp" --> sofa
    sofa -- "false, with the same basket" --> result
    classDef domain fill:#a6d189,stroke:#40a02b,color:#232634
    classDef step fill:#8caaee,stroke:#1e66f5,color:#232634
    class run,lamp,pick,sofa step
    class result domain
```

The basket goes from each step straight to the next, while each result goes to the function that picks the step after it. Each `run` starts again from the basket it is given, so running a `State` twice does its work twice.

## A step for each line {#a-step-for-each-line}

A loop can build the chain. Each `flatMap` is handed the refusals so far, and the step it returns runs on the basket the steps before it left. `map` adds the line's SKU to the refusals when `add` gives `false`:

```java
  // One step for each line: each is handed the basket the step before it left
  static State<Basket, List<String>> addAll(List<LineItem> lines) {
    State<Basket, List<String>> refused = State.pure(List.of());
    for (LineItem line : lines) {
      refused =
          refused.flatMap(
              soFar ->
                  add(line)
                      .map(
                          added ->
                              added
                                  ? soFar
                                  : Stream.concat(soFar.stream(), Stream.of(line.sku())).toList()));
    }
    return refused;
  }

```

```java
    StateTuple<Basket, List<String>> trip = addAll(List.of(LAMP, SOFA, BULBS)).run(Basket.EMPTY);
    List<String> refused = trip.value();
    // [SOFA]
    BigDecimal tripTotal = trip.state().total();
    // 50.00
```

A long list makes a long chain. `run` works through the chain in a loop, not a stack frame for each step, so a chain of 100,000 steps runs on an ordinary thread. That holds for `map` as well as `flatMap`, and for the `WithStatePath` methods the next section shows.

## On a `WithStatePath` {#on-a-withstatepath}

Application code holds a `State` on a `WithStatePath`, its Effect Path, and `Path.state(state)` gives you one. It chains with `via`, runs one path after another with `then`, and combines with `zipWith`, which runs the second path on the state the first one left. Checkout reads the total from the basket the trip leaves:

```java
  // zipWith runs the second path on the basket the first one left
  static WithStatePath<Basket, String> checkout(List<LineItem> lines) {
    return Path.state(addAll(lines))
        .zipWith(
            WithStatePath.inspect(Basket::total),
            (refused, total) ->
                refused.isEmpty() ? "pay " + total : "pay " + total + ", refused " + refused);
  }

```

```java
    String receipt = checkout(List.of(LAMP, SOFA, BULBS)).evalState(Basket.EMPTY);
```

`receipt` is `"pay 50.00, refused [SOFA]"`. `evalState` gives only the result, `execState` only the final state, and `run` the `StateTuple` with both.

## A State always gives a value {#a-state-always-gives-a-value}

A `StateTuple` always holds a result and a state, so `State.pure(null)`, `State.set(null)` and a `StateTuple` built with `null` throw at the call. A function given to `map` or `inspect` that returns `null` fails the run with `f must not return null`, and one given to `modify` fails it with `StateTuple.construction state cannot be null`. A step with nothing to report gives [`Unit`](../hkts/core-concepts.md#unit), as `set` and `modify` do. `State.of` does not check what your function returns, so it must never return `null`. [Values, never null](../hkts/core-concepts.md#values-never-null) sets out the rule every type follows.

## Creating instances {#creating-instances}

`State.of(function)` wraps a function from a state to a `StateTuple`, and `State.pure(value)` gives a value and leaves the state as it is. `State.get()` gives the state itself, and `State.inspect(f)` gives what `f` reads from it. `State.set(state)` replaces it, and `State.modify(f)` changes it, and both give `Unit`. `new StateTuple<>(value, state)` takes the result first, but `StateTuple.of(state, value)` takes the state first. On a Path, `Path.state(state)`, `Path.statePure(value)`, `Path.getState()`, `Path.setState(state)` and `Path.modifyState(f)` start a `WithStatePath`, and `WithStatePath.inspect(f)` starts one that reads part of the state.

## Methods {#methods}

| Method | What it does |
|---|---|
| `State.of(function)` | Wraps a function from a state to a `StateTuple` |
| `State.pure(value)` | Gives `value`, and leaves the state as it is |
| `State.get()` | Gives the state |
| `State.inspect(f)` | Gives what `f` reads from the state |
| `State.set(state)` | Replaces the state, and gives `Unit` |
| `State.modify(f)` | Changes the state with `f`, and gives `Unit` |
| `map(f)` | Builds a `State` whose result is changed by `f` |
| `flatMap(f)` | Builds a `State` that runs the step `f` picks from this one's result, on the state this one left |
| `run(state)` | Runs from `state`, and gives the `StateTuple` of the result and the final state |
| `withStatePath.via(f)`, `then(next)`, `zipWith(other, f)` | Chain, sequence or combine `WithStatePath` steps over one state |
| `withStatePath.evalState(state)`, `execState(state)`, `run(state)` | Give the result, the final state, or both |
| `withStatePath.toState()` | Gives the `State` the path holds |

## Working with Kind {#working-with-kind}

Generic code, written once for any container, takes `State` through its `Monad` instance, from `Instances.monad(state())`. The state type is part of the witness, `StateKind.Witness<S>`, so one instance serves steps over one state type. You widen a `State` to pass it in, and run the result with `STATE.runState`, `STATE.evalState` or `STATE.execState`. `STATE.narrow(kind)` gives the `State` back. These are the extra imports, with those the bank account in the next section uses:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.state;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.state.StateKindHelper.STATE;

import java.util.Objects;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Kind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Monad;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Unit;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.expression.For;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Instances;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.state.StateKind;
```

```java
    Monad<StateKind.Witness<Basket>> baskets = Instances.monad(state());

    Kind<StateKind.Witness<Basket>, Boolean> lamp = STATE.widen(add(LAMP));
    Kind<StateKind.Witness<Basket>, String> label =
        baskets.map(added -> added ? "added" : "refused", lamp);

    String ran = STATE.evalState(label, Basket.EMPTY);
```

`ran` is `"added"`. Widening allocates nothing: every `State` is already a `StateKind`, so `widen` checks for `null` and returns the same object. `STATE.get()`, `STATE.set(state)`, `STATE.modify(f)` and `STATE.inspect(f)` build a widened step directly. Code that changes a state through any monad, not only `State`, takes a `MonadState`, as [Changing a basket with `MonadState`](../functional/for_mtl.md#threading-state-with-monadstate) shows. [ForState](../functional/forstate_comprehension.md) names each step's result, and updates the state through optics.

### A month in a bank account {#a-month-in-a-bank-account}

What the `Monad` instance adds is [`For`](../functional/for_comprehension.md), which lists a chain of steps in order rather than nesting them. A bank account shows it. An `Account` holds its balance and every transaction so far, with the refused ones:

```java
  enum TransactionType {
    OPENING_BALANCE,
    DEPOSIT,
    WITHDRAWAL,
    REJECTED_DEPOSIT,
    REJECTED_WITHDRAWAL
  }

  record Transaction(TransactionType type, BigDecimal amount, String description) {}

  // The balance, and every transaction so far, including those refused
  record Account(BigDecimal balance, List<Transaction> history) {

    Account {
      Objects.requireNonNull(balance, "balance");
      history = List.copyOf(history);
    }

    static Account opened(BigDecimal balance) {
      return new Account(
          balance,
          List.of(new Transaction(TransactionType.OPENING_BALANCE, balance, "Opening balance")));
    }

    Account addTransaction(Transaction transaction) {
      return new Account(balance, Stream.concat(history.stream(), Stream.of(transaction)).toList());
    }

    Account withBalance(BigDecimal newBalance) {
      return new Account(newBalance, history);
    }
  }

```

A deposit only changes the account, so it is a `State.modify`, and gives `Unit`. A withdrawal also says whether it was paid:

```java
  // A deposit changes the account and has nothing to report, so it gives Unit
  static State<Account, Unit> deposit(String description, BigDecimal amount) {
    return State.modify(
        account ->
            amount.signum() <= 0
                ? account.addTransaction(
                    new Transaction(TransactionType.REJECTED_DEPOSIT, amount, description))
                : account
                    .withBalance(account.balance().add(amount))
                    .addTransaction(new Transaction(TransactionType.DEPOSIT, amount, description)));
  }

  // A withdrawal reports whether it was paid, as well as changing the account
  static State<Account, Boolean> withdraw(String description, BigDecimal amount) {
    return State.of(
        account -> {
          if (amount.signum() <= 0 || account.balance().compareTo(amount) < 0) {
            Transaction refused =
                new Transaction(TransactionType.REJECTED_WITHDRAWAL, amount, description);
            return new StateTuple<>(false, account.addTransaction(refused));
          }
          Transaction paid = new Transaction(TransactionType.WITHDRAWAL, amount, description);
          return new StateTuple<>(
              true, account.withBalance(account.balance().subtract(amount)).addTransaction(paid));
        });
  }

```

`For` sequences the month over the `State` monad, so each step is widened with `STATE.widen`. Each `from` is given the results so far, and the step it returns runs on the account the step before it left. `yield` sees every step's result:

```java
  static Kind<StateKind.Witness<Account>, String> month() {
    Monad<StateKind.Witness<Account>> accounts = Instances.monad(state());

    return For.from(accounts, STATE.widen(deposit("Salary", new BigDecimal("20.00"))))
        .from(_ -> STATE.widen(withdraw("Bill payment", new BigDecimal("50.00"))))
        .from(_ -> STATE.widen(withdraw("Groceries", new BigDecimal("70.00"))))
        .from(_ -> STATE.widen(withdraw("Cinema", new BigDecimal("12.00"))))
        .yield(
            (_, bill, groceries, cinema) ->
                "bill paid: %s, groceries paid: %s, cinema paid: %s"
                    .formatted(bill, groceries, cinema));
  }

```

```java
    StateTuple<Account, String> after =
        STATE.runState(month(), Account.opened(new BigDecimal("100.00")));

    String report = after.value();
    BigDecimal balance = after.state().balance();
    // 0.00
    int transactions = after.state().history().size();
    // 5
```

`report` is `"bill paid: true, groceries paid: true, cinema paid: false"`. The salary takes the opening 100.00 to 120.00, and the bill and the groceries take it to 0.00. The cinema is refused and recorded as refused, so the history holds five transactions: the opening balance, the salary, two payments and the refusal. The account records no time, so the same month gives the same history on every run. A transaction that needs a time can read it from a [`TimeSource`](io_monad.md#reading-the-clock) passed to the step, which a test fixes.

## When to use `State` {#when-to-use-state}

| You need | Use |
|---|---|
| Steps that each work from what the step before them left | `State`, or [WithStatePath](../effect/advanced_effects.md#withstatepath-computation-with-memory) |
| Steps that read one environment, which no step changes | [Reader](reader_monad.md) |
| A log that steps add to, and read only at the end | [Writer](writer_monad.md) |
| State together with failure or asynchronous work | [StateT](../transformers/statet_transformer.md), or [MutableContext](../effect/effect_contexts_mutable.md) |
| A loop inside one method that updates a local variable | The local variable: nothing outside the method sees it change |

~~~admonish info title="Hands-On Learning"
Practise `MonadState` in Exercises 5 to 8 of [Tutorial 04: Polymorphic Capabilities](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/tutorial/transformers/Tutorial04_PolymorphicCapabilities.java) (14 exercises), and state updated through optics in [Tutorial 01: ForState Basics](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/tutorial/expression/Tutorial01_ForStateBasics.java) (13 exercises).
~~~

**Next:** [Writer](writer_monad.md)

<!-- description: Keep a log beside a result in Java with Writer: build an order's audit log step by step, choose how logs join with a Monoid, and chain steps on a WriterPath. -->

# Writer: Keeping a Log Beside the Result

_Each step returns its result with what it has to log, and the log's `Monoid` joins the entries._

~~~admonish abstract title="Writer at a glance"
| | |
|---|---|
| **Models** | A result together with a log, such as audit entries, that each step adds to |
| **Replaces** | A list passed to every method that logs, or a logger whose output a test has to capture |
| **Effect Path** | [`WriterPath`](../effect/advanced_effects.md#writerpath-accumulating-output) |
| **Instances** | `Functor`, `Applicative`, `Monad`, `Bifunctor` |
| **Choose it when** | Steps add to a log that you read once they are done; otherwise [State](state_monad.md), whose steps also read what they keep, or compare every type in [Choosing a Type](supported-types.md#choosing-a-type) |
~~~

~~~admonish info title="What You'll Learn"
- Build an order's audit log from steps that each return a result and the entries they add
- Say how `flatMap` joins two steps' logs, and why a `Writer` has done its work once it is built
- Choose the `Monoid` that says what an empty log is and how two logs join
- Chain steps on a `WriterPath`, which holds the `Monoid` for you
- Use `Writer` in generic code through its `Monad` instance
~~~

~~~admonish example title="See Example Code"
**The code on this page is [WriterBook.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/writer/WriterBook.java)**: the page includes it, so the build compiles and runs it. [WriterBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/writer/WriterBookTest.java) holds what the page says each method does.
~~~

---

## An audit log for an order {#an-audit-log-for-an-order}

Paying for an order and shipping it should each leave an entry in the order's audit log. Pass a list to every method that logs, and the log shows nowhere in what a method returns. Return a record of the order and its entries instead, and every caller joins the entries by hand. A `Writer<W, A>` is that record, a result `A` together with a log `W`, and `flatMap` does the joining.

The code on this page uses these imports, with `Order` and `OrderLenses` from the order records the [Optics](../optics/ch_intro.md) chapter introduces, and `AuditEntry` from this chapter's examples:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import java.util.List;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.foundations.AuditEntry;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.Order;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.OrderLenses;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.OrderStatus;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Monoid;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Monoids;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Unit;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.effect.Path;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.effect.WriterPath;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.writer.Writer;
```

`AuditEntry` is a record of the examples' own:

```java
/** One thing that happened to an order, kept for the audit log. */
public record AuditEntry(UUID orderId, String event) {

  public AuditEntry {
    Objects.requireNonNull(orderId, "orderId");
    Objects.requireNonNull(event, "event");
  }
}
```

A [`Monoid`](../functional/semigroup_and_monoid.md) says how logs behave. Its `empty()` is the log before any step, and its `combine` joins two logs, like the identity and the accumulator you pass to `Stream.reduce`. The log here is a list of entries, so `AUDIT` is the list monoid:

```java
  // The empty log is an empty list, and two logs join into one list
  static final Monoid<List<AuditEntry>> AUDIT = Monoids.list();

```

`pay` and `ship` each return the order with its new status, and the entry they add. `emailCustomer` here only logs that the email went, so it is a `tell`: a log entry with [`Unit`](../hkts/core-concepts.md#unit) as its result:

```java
  // Each step returns the order it changed, with the entry it adds to the log
  static Writer<List<AuditEntry>, Order> pay(Order order) {
    return Writer.of(
        List.of(new AuditEntry(order.id(), "paid")),
        OrderLenses.status().set(OrderStatus.PAID, order));
  }

  static Writer<List<AuditEntry>, Order> ship(Order order) {
    return Writer.of(
        List.of(new AuditEntry(order.id(), "shipped")),
        OrderLenses.status().set(OrderStatus.SHIPPED, order));
  }

  // A step with only something to log is a tell, and gives Unit
  static Writer<List<AuditEntry>, Unit> emailCustomer(Order order) {
    return Writer.tell(List.of(new AuditEntry(order.id(), "customer emailed")));
  }

```

`fulfil` joins the three. Writer's own `flatMap` is given `AUDIT`, so it knows how to join the logs, and `map(_ -> shipped)` keeps the shipped order as the result after the email:

```java
  // Writer's own flatMap is given the Monoid that joins the two logs
  static Writer<List<AuditEntry>, Order> fulfil(Order order) {
    return pay(order)
        .flatMap(AUDIT, WriterBook::ship)
        .flatMap(AUDIT, shipped -> emailCustomer(shipped).map(_ -> shipped));
  }

```

`NEW_ORDER` is an order for one lamp, not yet paid for. Fulfil it, then read the result with `run()` and the log with `exec()`:

```java
    Writer<List<AuditEntry>, Order> fulfilled = fulfil(NEW_ORDER);

    Order shipped = fulfilled.run();
    List<String> events = fulfilled.exec().stream().map(AuditEntry::event).toList();
    // [paid, shipped, customer emailed]
```

`shipped` is the order with the status `SHIPPED`, and the log holds three entries in the order the steps ran. No method took the log as a parameter, and a test reads it from the value `fulfil` returns.

## Each step's log joins the log before it {#each-steps-log-joins-the-log-before-it}

`flatMap` calls the next step with this step's result, then joins the two logs with the `Monoid`, the earlier log first.

```mermaid
flowchart TB
    accTitle: How fulfil joins the three steps' logs
    accDescr: pay gives the paid order and a log of one entry, paid. flatMap calls ship with the paid order, and AUDIT joins the two logs into paid then shipped. The second flatMap calls emailCustomer with the shipped order, and AUDIT joins its entry on the end, so fulfil gives the shipped order with all three entries in order.
    pay["pay(order)<br/>logs paid"]
    ship["ship(paid)<br/>logs shipped"]
    email["emailCustomer(shipped)<br/>logs customer emailed"]
    join1["the log after ship<br/>paid, shipped"]
    join2["the log fulfil gives<br/>paid, shipped, customer emailed"]
    pay -. "the paid order" .-> ship
    ship -. "the shipped order" .-> email
    pay --> join1
    ship --> join1
    join1 --> join2
    email --> join2
    classDef domain fill:#a6d189,stroke:#40a02b,color:#232634
    classDef step fill:#8caaee,stroke:#1e66f5,color:#232634
    class pay,ship,email step
    class join1,join2 domain
```

The order goes from each step to the next, on the dotted lines, while `AUDIT` joins each step's entries onto the log so far. A `Writer` is a value, not a description of work to run later: `flatMap` calls the next step at once. So `fulfil` has done its work when it returns, and `run()` only reads the result it holds. A chain built in a loop runs without growing the stack, but a step that calls itself through `flatMap` recurses as any Java method does. With `Monoids.list()`, each join copies the log so far, so for a chain of thousands of steps the time grows with the square of the log's length. For a log that long, keep a count or a summary instead, or write each entry out as it happens.

## Choosing the log's `Monoid` {#choosing-the-logs-monoid}

The `Monoid` decides what the log is. Choose another, and the steps keep another kind of log:

| The log | The `Monoid` | The empty log | Two logs join into |
|---|---|---|---|
| A list of entries | `Monoids.list()` | an empty list | the first list's entries, then the second's |
| Text | `Monoids.string()` | `""` | the first string, then the second |
| A count | `Monoids.integerAddition()` | `0` | their sum |
| One of your own | `Monoid.of(empty, combine)` | `empty` | what `combine` gives |

[Semigroup and Monoid](../functional/semigroup_and_monoid.md#ready-made-monoids) lists the ready-made monoids, and shows [a monoid of your own](../functional/semigroup_and_monoid.md#a-monoid-of-your-own).

## On a `WriterPath` {#on-a-writerpath}

Application code holds a `Writer` on a `WriterPath`, its Effect Path, and `Path.writer(writer, monoid)` gives you one. The path holds the `Monoid` and joins the logs itself, so `via` is given only the next step. Each step is still put on a path with `Path.writer(step, AUDIT)`, but `via` joins the logs with the path's own `Monoid`:

```java
  // A WriterPath holds the Monoid, so via is given only the next step
  static WriterPath<List<AuditEntry>, Order> fulfilOnPath(Order order) {
    return Path.writer(pay(order), AUDIT)
        .via(paid -> Path.writer(ship(paid), AUDIT))
        .via(shipped -> Path.writer(emailCustomer(shipped), AUDIT).map(_ -> shipped));
  }

```

```java
    WriterPath<List<AuditEntry>, Order> onPath = fulfilOnPath(NEW_ORDER);

    boolean sameAsWriter = onPath.run().equals(fulfil(NEW_ORDER));
    // true
```

The path gives the same `Writer` as `fulfil`. `run()` gives that `Writer`, `value()` the result and `written()` the log.

## A Writer always holds a log and a value {#a-writer-always-holds-a-log-and-a-value}

A `Writer` refuses a `null` log or result, so `Writer.of(null, value)`, `Writer.of(log, null)` and `Writer.tell(null)` throw at the call. Because a `Writer` does its work when it is built, a function given to `map` that returns `null` throws at the `map` call too, with `f must not return null`. A function given to `mapWritten` that returns `null` throws at the call with `Writer.construction log cannot be null`. [Values, never null](../hkts/core-concepts.md#values-never-null) sets out the rule every type follows.

## Creating instances {#creating-instances}

`Writer.of(log, value)` pairs a log with a result, and `Writer.value(monoid, value)` starts with the monoid's empty log. `Writer.tell(log)` logs with `Unit` as its result. On a Path, `Path.writer(writer, monoid)`, `Path.writerPure(value, monoid)` and `Path.tell(log, monoid)` start a `WriterPath`.

## Methods {#methods}

| Method | What it does |
|---|---|
| `Writer.of(log, value)` | Pairs `log` with `value` |
| `Writer.value(monoid, value)` | Gives `value` with the monoid's empty log |
| `Writer.tell(log)` | Logs `log`, and gives `Unit` |
| `map(f)` | Changes the result with `f`, and leaves the log as it is |
| `flatMap(monoid, f)` | Calls the step `f` gives for this result, and joins its log onto this one with `monoid` |
| `mapWritten(f)` | Changes the log with `f`, and leaves the result as it is |
| `bimap(onLog, onValue)` | Changes the log and the result together |
| `run()`, `exec()` | Give the result, or the log |
| `writerPath.via(f)`, `then(next)`, `zipWith(other, f)` | Chain, sequence or combine `WriterPath` steps, joining their logs with the first path's entries first |
| `writerPath.censor(f)` | Rewrites the whole log with `f` |
| `writerPath.run()`, `value()`, `written()` | Give the `Writer`, the result, or the log |

## Working with Kind {#working-with-kind}

Generic code, written once for any container, takes `Writer` through its `Monad` instance, from `Instances.writer(monoid)`. The instance needs the `Monoid` to join logs, so it takes one, and the log type is part of the witness, `WriterKind.Witness<W>`. You widen a `Writer` to pass it in. `WRITER.run` and `WRITER.exec` read the result and the log from what comes back, and `WRITER.runWriter` or `WRITER.narrow` gives the `Writer` itself. These are the extra imports:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.writer.WriterKindHelper.WRITER;

import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Kind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Monad;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Instances;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.writer.WriterKind;
```

```java
    Monad<WriterKind.Witness<List<AuditEntry>>> audited = Instances.writer(AUDIT);

    Kind<WriterKind.Witness<List<AuditEntry>>, Order> paid = WRITER.widen(pay(NEW_ORDER));
    Kind<WriterKind.Witness<List<AuditEntry>>, Order> thenShipped =
        audited.flatMap(order -> WRITER.widen(ship(order)), paid);

    int entries = WRITER.exec(thenShipped).size();
    // 2
```

Widening allocates nothing: every `Writer` is already a `WriterKind`, so `widen` checks for `null` and returns the same object. `Writer` is also a `Bifunctor`, over the log and the result: `WriterBifunctor.INSTANCE` takes a `Writer` widened with `WRITER.widen2`, and *Writer at a glance*, at the head of the page, lists it. Code that keeps a log through any monad, not only `Writer`, takes a `MonadWriter`, as [Keeping a log with `MonadWriter`](../functional/for_mtl.md#accumulating-output-with-monadwriter) shows.

## When to use `Writer` {#when-to-use-writer}

| You need | Use |
|---|---|
| Steps that add to a log, read once they are done | `Writer`, or [WriterPath](../effect/advanced_effects.md#writerpath-accumulating-output) |
| Steps that also read and change what they keep | [State](state_monad.md) |
| Log lines written out as they happen, to the console or a file | A logger, or a step in [IO](io_monad.md) |
| A log together with failure or asynchronous work | [WriterT](../transformers/writert_transformer.md) |
| A count or a sum beside the result | `Writer` with `Monoids.integerAddition()`, or `Monoids.bigDecimalAddition()` for money |
| A log kept inside one method | A local list: nothing outside the method sees it |

~~~admonish info title="Hands-On Learning"
Practise `MonadWriter`'s `tell` and `censor` in Exercises 9 to 11 of [Tutorial 04: Polymorphic Capabilities](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/tutorial/transformers/Tutorial04_PolymorphicCapabilities.java) (14 exercises).
~~~
