**Next:** [List](list_monad.md)

<!-- Source: monads/list_monad.md -->

<!-- description: Compute with several results in Java with List: every way to ship an order within a budget, every variant of a product, and pairing by position. -->

# List: Every Result of Every Branch

_Each step can give several results, and the list keeps them all, in order._

~~~admonish abstract title="List at a glance"
| | |
|---|---|
| **Models** | A computation with several results, such as every shipping option or every product variant |
| **Replaces** | Nothing: it is Java's own type |
| **Effect Path** | [`NonDetPath`](../glossary/effect-paths.md#nondetpath) for every combination, or [`ListPath`](../glossary/effect-paths.md#listpath), whose `zipWith` pairs by position |
| **Instances** | `Functor`, `Applicative`, `Selective`, `Monad`, `Alternative`, `MonadZero`, `Foldable`, `Traverse` |
| **Choose it when** | Nested loops build every combination, and you keep them all in memory; otherwise [Stream](stream_monad.md), which makes each element as it is read and can be read only once, or compare every type in [Choosing a Type](supported-types.md#choosing-a-type) |
~~~

~~~admonish info title="What You'll Learn"
- Find every way to ship an order by letting each courier branch into its services
- Drop a branch by giving no results for it, and filter what is left
- Choose between every combination, from `NonDetPath`, and pairing by position, from `ListPath`
- Predict what `of`, `map` and `flatMap` do with `null`, on a Path and in generic code
- Use `List` in generic code through its `MonadZero` instance
~~~

~~~admonish example title="See Example Code"
**The code on this page is [ListBook.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/list/ListBook.java)**: the page includes it, so the build compiles and runs it. [ListBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/list/ListBookTest.java) holds what the page says each method does.
~~~

---

## Every way to ship an order {#every-way-to-ship-an-order}

An order can go with either of two couriers, and each courier offers several services. To find every service within a budget, plain Java nests a loop over the services inside a loop over the couriers, and adds each match to a list. With streams it is `COURIERS.stream().flatMap(courier -> servicesOf(courier).stream())`, then a filter. A list used as a computation does the same: each step gives a list of results, and the next step runs once for each of them. Its Path adds a `zipWith` that gives every combination, and conversions to the other Paths.

~~~admonish warning title="Two Paths over a list"
`Path.list(list)` gives a `NonDetPath`, short for non-deterministic: it keeps every possible result. `Path.listPath(list)` gives a `ListPath`. Their `map` and `via` agree. Only `zipWith` and `zipWith3` differ: a `NonDetPath` gives every combination, and a `ListPath` pairs by position. Start with `NonDetPath`, which matches the `List` instance's `ap`.
~~~

The code on this page uses these imports:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import java.math.BigDecimal;
import java.util.List;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.effect.NonDetPath;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.effect.Path;
```

Each courier's services, with their prices:

```java
  // A courier's service, and what it charges
  record Service(String courier, String name, BigDecimal price) {}

  static final List<String> COURIERS = List.of("Parcelforce", "DPD");

  // Each courier offers several services
  static List<Service> servicesOf(String courier) {
    return switch (courier) {
      case "Parcelforce" ->
          List.of(
              new Service(courier, "standard", new BigDecimal("4.99")),
              new Service(courier, "express", new BigDecimal("9.99")));
      case "DPD" ->
          List.of(
              new Service(courier, "next day", new BigDecimal("7.49")),
              new Service(courier, "timed", new BigDecimal("12.99")));
      default -> List.of();
    };
  }

```

`Path.list(list)` gives a `NonDetPath`. Its `via` runs the next step for each element and joins every result into one list, and `filter` keeps those that pass:

```java
  // Every service from every courier, and only those within the budget
  static NonDetPath<Service> optionsWithin(BigDecimal budget) {
    return Path.list(COURIERS)
        .via(courier -> Path.list(servicesOf(courier)))
        .filter(service -> service.price().compareTo(budget) <= 0);
  }

```

```java
    List<String> underTen =
        optionsWithin(new BigDecimal("10.00")).map(s -> s.courier() + " " + s.name()).run();
    // [Parcelforce standard, Parcelforce express, DPD next day]
```

## Each result branches {#each-result-branches}

`via` gives the next step each result in turn, and keeps the results in order: all of the first courier's services, then all of the second's.

```mermaid
flowchart TB
    accTitle: How optionsWithin branches on couriers and services
    accDescr: The couriers Parcelforce and DPD each branch into their services through via. Parcelforce gives standard at 4.99 and express at 9.99, and DPD gives next day at 7.49 and timed at 12.99. The filter drops timed, which is over 10.00, and the three services left come back in order in one list.
    couriers["COURIERS<br/>via servicesOf"]
    pf["Parcelforce"]
    dpd["DPD"]
    standard["standard<br/>4.99"]
    express["express<br/>9.99"]
    nextDay["next day<br/>7.49"]
    timed["timed<br/>12.99, dropped"]
    result["filter keeps three<br/>in one list, in order"]
    couriers --> pf
    couriers --> dpd
    pf --> standard
    pf --> express
    dpd --> nextDay
    dpd --> timed
    standard --> result
    express --> result
    nextDay --> result
    classDef domain fill:#a6d189,stroke:#40a02b,color:#232634
    classDef error fill:#e78284,stroke:#d20f39,color:#232634
    class couriers,pf,dpd,standard,express,nextDay,result domain
    class timed error
```

Each courier branches into its services, `filter` drops the one over the budget, and the rest are joined into one list, in order. A step that gives an empty list, such as `Path.listEmpty()`, drops its branch the same way, since it adds nothing to the result.

## Every combination, or position by position {#every-combination-or-position-by-position}

`zipWith` combines two lists, and the two Paths combine them differently. On a `NonDetPath` it pairs each element of the first list with each element of the second, so two colours and two shades give four variants:

```java
    // Every colour with every shade
    List<String> variants =
        Path.list("black", "brass")
            .zipWith(Path.list("linen", "glass"), (colour, shade) -> colour + "/" + shade)
            .run();
    // [black/linen, black/glass, brass/linen, brass/glass]
```

On a `ListPath`, from `Path.listPath(list)`, it pairs the elements by position, and the shorter list sets the length:

```java
    // Each line with the day it is dispatched, position by position
    List<String> dispatched =
        Path.listPath("LAMP", "BULB", "SHADE")
            .zipWith(Path.listPath("Monday", "Tuesday"), (sku, day) -> sku + " on " + day)
            .run();
    // [LAMP on Monday, BULB on Tuesday]
```

`SHADE`, the third line, has no day, so it drops out. `zipWith3` differs between the two Paths in the same way.

## A list holds what a Java list holds {#a-list-holds-what-a-java-list-holds}

The Paths refuse `null`. `Path.list` and `Path.listPath` throw `NullPointerException` for a list that holds one, and so do `map` and `via` when their function returns `null`. The instance over `Kind` follows Java's `List`, which holds `null` if you put one there. Its `map` keeps a `null` that a function returns, and its `of(null)` gives an empty list, as `Optional`'s and `Stream`'s do. A `flatMap` function must return a list, and one that returns `null` throws `KindUnwrapException`. [Where null is read or held](../hkts/core-concepts.md#where-null-is-read-or-held) sets out the rules for each type.

## Creating instances {#creating-instances}

`Path.list(list)` and `Path.list(a, b, c)` start a `NonDetPath`, `Path.listPure(value)` one with a single element, and `Path.listEmpty()` an empty one. `Path.listPath(...)`, `Path.listPathPure(value)`, `Path.listPathEmpty()` and `Path.listPathRange(from, to)` start a `ListPath`. `toNonDetPath()` turns a `ListPath` into a `NonDetPath`, and `toListPath()` turns a `NonDetPath` back into a `ListPath`.

## Methods {#methods}

| Method | What it does |
|---|---|
| `nonDetPath.via(f)`, `listPath.via(f)` | Run `f` for each element, and join every result into one list |
| `map(f)`, `filter(p)` | Change each element, or keep those that pass |
| `nonDetPath.zipWith(other, f)`, `zipWith3(b, c, f)` | Combine every element with every element of the others |
| `listPath.zipWith(other, f)`, `zipWith3(b, c, f)` | Combine elements by position, up to the shortest list |
| `take(n)`, `drop(n)`, `takeWhile(p)`, `dropWhile(p)` | Keep or skip a prefix |
| `foldLeft(initial, f)`, `foldMap(monoid, f)` | Fold the elements into one value |
| `headOption()`, `lastOption()`, `get(index)`, `size()`, `isEmpty()` | Read the list |
| `run()` | Gives the `List` |

## Working with Kind {#working-with-kind}

Generic code, written once for any container, takes `List` through its [`MonadZero`](../functional/monad_zero.md) instance, from `Instances.monadZero(list())`. Its `flatMap` is `via`, and its `ap` applies every function to every value, as a `NonDetPath`'s `zipWith` does. `zero()` is the empty list, and the [`Alternative`](../functional/alternative.md) from `Instances.alternative(list())` joins two lists with `orElse`. These are the extra imports:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.list;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.list.ListKindHelper.LIST;

import java.util.function.Function;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Kind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.MonadZero;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Instances;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.list.ListKind;
```

```java
    MonadZero<ListKind.Witness> lists = Instances.monadZero(list());

    Kind<ListKind.Witness, String> couriers = LIST.widen(COURIERS);
    Function<String, Kind<ListKind.Witness, Service>> services =
        courier -> LIST.widen(servicesOf(courier));

    int offered = LIST.narrow(lists.flatMap(services, couriers)).size();
    // 4
```

`List` is a JDK type, so `widen` wraps it in a small holder, and `narrow` gives back the same list. `ListTraverse.INSTANCE` traverses a list with any `Applicative`, as [Foldable and Traverse](../functional/foldable_and_traverse.md) shows.

## When to use `List` {#when-to-use-list}

| You need | Use |
|---|---|
| Every result of several branching choices | `List`, or [`NonDetPath`](../glossary/effect-paths.md#nondetpath) |
| Two lists paired element by element | `ListPath`'s [`zipWith`](#every-combination-or-position-by-position) |
| A long or endless sequence, read once as it is produced | [Stream](stream_monad.md), or [VStream](vstream.md) when producing an element does I/O |
| A list that must hold at least one element | [NonEmptyList](nonemptylist_monad.md) |
| A loop over one list in one method | A `for` loop, or `stream()`: nothing else needs the structure |

~~~admonish info title="Hands-On Learning"
Practise `List`'s `map` in Exercise 3 of [Tutorial 02: Functor Mapping](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/tutorial/coretypes/Tutorial02_FunctorMapping.java) (7 exercises), and its `flatMap` in Exercise 6 of [Tutorial 04: Monad Chaining](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/tutorial/coretypes/Tutorial04_MonadChaining.java) (8 exercises).
~~~

---

**Next:** [Stream](stream_monad.md)

<!-- Source: monads/stream_monad.md -->

<!-- description: Use Java's Stream with Higher-Kinded-J: rerun a sequence on a StreamPath, pass an endless Stream to generic code, read it once, and number it with StreamOps. -->

# Stream: Java's Stream in Generic Code

_Keep Java's own `Stream`, with an Effect Path you can run more than once, and instances for generic code._

~~~admonish abstract title="Stream at a glance"
| | |
|---|---|
| **Models** | A sequence that makes each element as it is read, in Java's own type |
| **Replaces** | Nothing: it is Java's own type |
| **Effect Path** | [`StreamPath`](../glossary/effect-paths.md#streampath), which reads a finite stream into memory so it can run again, or [takes a supplier](#on-a-streampath) for an endless one |
| **Instances** | `Functor`, `Applicative`, `Monad`, `Alternative`, `MonadZero`, `Foldable`, `Traverse` |
| **Choose it when** | A method you call already returns a `Stream`; otherwise [List](list_monad.md), which can be read again, or compare every type in [Choosing a Type](supported-types.md#choosing-a-type) |
~~~

~~~admonish info title="What You'll Learn"
- Run the same sequence more than once on a `StreamPath`, even an endless one
- Pass an endless `Stream` to code written once for any container, making only the elements you take
- Say what single use means for a `Kind` that holds a stream, and which operation reads it early
- Number and combine streams with `StreamOps`
- Fold a stream through `StreamTraverse`
~~~

~~~admonish example title="See Example Code"
**The code on this page is [StreamBook.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/stream/StreamBook.java)**: the page includes it, so the build compiles and runs it. [StreamBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/stream/StreamBookTest.java) holds what the page says each method does.
~~~

---

## Java's own type {#javas-own-type}

`java.util.stream.Stream` makes each element only when something reads it, so a stream can run without end and still be read in part. Higher-Kinded-J does not replace it. It gives `Stream` an Effect Path, `StreamPath`, for application code, and a `MonadZero` instance, so that a method written once for any container accepts a stream too. A Java `Stream` can be read only once; a `StreamPath` can be run again.

The code on this page uses these imports:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import java.util.List;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.effect.Path;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.effect.StreamPath;
```

## On a `StreamPath` {#on-a-streampath}

`Path.streamIterate(seed, f)` builds a fresh stream each time the path runs. So `take` limits an endless one, and the same invoices come back on every run:

```java
    StreamPath<String> nextInvoices =
        Path.streamIterate(1001, n -> n + 1).take(3).map(n -> "INV-" + n);

    List<String> printed = nextInvoices.toList();
    List<String> printedAgain = nextInvoices.toList();
    boolean same = printed.equals(printedAgain);
    // true
```

`Path.stream(stream)` reads the whole stream when it is called, so that stream must be finite, and the path can then be run again. `StreamPath.generate(supplier)` and `StreamPath.fromSupplier(supplier)` build a fresh stream on each run, as `streamIterate` does. A `StreamPath`'s `zipWith` gives every combination of two paths' elements, as a [`NonDetPath`](list_monad.md#every-combination-or-position-by-position)'s does, and unlike `StreamOps.zip`. It reads both paths whole on each run, so `take` from an endless path first.

## Invoice numbers without end {#invoice-numbers-without-end}

Code written once for any container takes a stream widened to a `Kind<StreamKind.Witness, A>`. The [Foundations](../hkts/foundations_intro.md) introduction's "Which level am I working at?" tip says when you need that. The stream keeps its laziness through the instance's `map` and `flatMap`. These are the extra imports, with `LineItem` from the order records the [Optics](../optics/ch_intro.md) chapter introduces:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.stream;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.stream.StreamKindHelper.STREAM;

import java.util.stream.Stream;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.LineItem;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Kind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.MonadZero;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Monoids;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Instances;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.stream.StreamKind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.stream.StreamOps;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.stream.StreamTraverse;
```

Invoice numbers can come from a stream with no end:

```java
  // Invoice numbers from 1001 on, without end: none is made until the stream is read
  static Kind<StreamKind.Witness, Integer> invoiceNumbers() {
    return STREAM.widen(Stream.iterate(1001, n -> n + 1));
  }

```

`map` adds the prefix to each number as it is read, and `StreamOps.take` stops after three:

```java
    MonadZero<StreamKind.Witness> streams = Instances.monadZero(stream());

    Kind<StreamKind.Witness, String> invoices = streams.map(n -> "INV-" + n, invoiceNumbers());
    List<String> firstThree = StreamOps.toList(StreamOps.take(3, invoices));
    // [INV-1001, INV-1002, INV-1003]
```

Nothing is made until `toList` reads the stream, and then only three invoice numbers are. `flatMap` keeps the laziness too, so it reads an endless stream only as far as the consumer needs.

`ap` is the exception. Each function in its first stream needs every value in the second, so `ap` reads the whole second stream when it is called, and with an endless second stream it never returns.

## A stream is read once {#a-stream-is-read-once}

`ORDER_LINES` holds the lines of two orders:

```java
  // The lines of two orders: a lamp and four bulbs, then a shade
  static final List<List<LineItem>> ORDER_LINES =
      List.of(
          List.of(
              new LineItem("LAMP", 1, new BigDecimal("40.00")),
              new LineItem("BULB", 4, new BigDecimal("2.50"))),
          List.of(new LineItem("SHADE", 1, new BigDecimal("15.00"))));

```

`flatMap` turns each order's lines into a stream of their SKUs, and joins those streams into one:

```java
    Kind<StreamKind.Witness, List<LineItem>> orders = STREAM.widen(ORDER_LINES.stream());

    Kind<StreamKind.Witness, String> skus =
        streams.flatMap(lines -> STREAM.widen(lines.stream().map(LineItem::sku)), orders);
    List<String> picked = StreamOps.toList(skus);
    // [LAMP, BULB, SHADE]
```

A Java `Stream` can be used only once, and a `Kind` that holds one holds that one stream. Passing `skus` to any further operation, `map` as much as `toList`, throws `IllegalStateException`: `stream has already been operated upon or closed`. So build a fresh stream for each use, keep the elements in a [List](list_monad.md), or use a [`StreamPath`](#on-a-streampath).

## Numbering and combining with `StreamOps` {#numbering-and-combining-with-streamops}

`StreamOps` gives the common operations over a widened stream. `zipWithIndex` pairs each element with its position, counting from 0, which numbers a pick list:

```java
    Kind<StreamKind.Witness, String> numbered =
        streams.map(
            pair -> (pair._1() + 1) + ". " + pair._2(),
            StreamOps.zipWithIndex(StreamOps.fromArray("LAMP", "BULB", "SHADE")));
    List<String> pickList = StreamOps.toList(numbered);
    // [1. LAMP, 2. BULB, 3. SHADE]
```

`zip` pairs two streams element by element, and `concat` puts one after another. The Methods table lists the rest.

## Creating instances {#creating-instances}

`STREAM.widen(stream)` gives a `Kind` that holds a `Stream`, and `StreamOps.fromArray(...)`, `fromIterable(iterable)`, `range(from, to)` and `rangeClosed(from, to)` start one. The instance's `of(value)` gives a stream of one element, `of(null)` an empty stream, as `Optional`'s and `List`'s do, and `zero()` an empty stream. On a Path, `Path.stream(stream)`, `Path.streamFromList(list)`, `Path.streamPure(value)`, `Path.streamEmpty()` and `Path.streamIterate(seed, f)` start a `StreamPath`.

## Methods {#methods}

| Method | What it does |
|---|---|
| `streams.map(f, stream)`, `streams.flatMap(f, stream)` | Change each element, or join the stream `f` gives for each, as the stream is read |
| `streams.ap(functions, values)` | Applies every function to every value, reading every value at the call |
| `StreamOps.take(n, stream)`, `drop(n, stream)`, `filter(p, stream)` | Narrow the stream |
| `StreamOps.zip(a, b, f)`, `zipWithIndex(stream)`, `concat(a, b)` | Pair by position, number, or join streams |
| `StreamOps.toList(stream)`, `toSet(stream)`, `forEach(action, stream)` | Read the stream |
| `StreamOps.tap(action, stream)` | Runs `action` on each element as it passes |
| `streamPath.map(f)`, `via(f)`, `filter(p)`, `take(n)` | Build the next path |
| `streamPath.zipWith(other, f)` | Combines every element with every element of `other`, reading both whole |
| `streamPath.toList()`, `run()` | Give the elements as a `List`, or a fresh `Stream` |

## Working with Kind {#working-with-kind}

Generic code takes `Stream` through its [`MonadZero`](../functional/monad_zero.md) instance, from `Instances.monadZero(stream())`. `Stream` is a JDK type, so `STREAM.widen` wraps it in a small holder, and `STREAM.narrow` gives the stream back. The [`Alternative`](../functional/alternative.md) from `Instances.alternative(stream())` puts one stream after another with `orElse`. `StreamTraverse.INSTANCE` is its `Traverse`, and so its `Foldable`, and reads the stream to fold or traverse it:

```java
    Kind<StreamKind.Witness, Integer> quantities =
        STREAM.widen(ORDER_LINES.stream().flatMap(List::stream).map(LineItem::quantity));

    int items = StreamTraverse.INSTANCE.foldMap(Monoids.integerAddition(), q -> q, quantities);
    // 6
```

The two orders hold six items: a lamp, four bulbs and a shade.

## When to use `Stream` {#when-to-use-stream}

| You need | Use |
|---|---|
| A Java `Stream` handed to code written over `Kind` | `Stream`, through `Instances.monadZero(stream())` |
| A sequence that is run more than once | A [`StreamPath`](#on-a-streampath), or a [List](list_monad.md) |
| Elements produced by I/O, on virtual threads | [VStream](vstream.md) |
| Every combination of several choices, held in memory | [List](list_monad.md) |
| One pass over a stream in one method | The stream's own methods: nothing else needs the `Kind` |

---

**Previous:** [List](list_monad.md)
**Next:** [Identity](identity.md)

<!-- Source: monads/identity.md -->

<!-- description: Run code written for any monad with no effect in Java with Id: a step that cannot fail, a delivery charge tested with Id and run with IO, and a StateT over Id. -->

# Identity: The Monad That Adds Nothing

_An `Id` holds a value and adds nothing, so code written for any monad can run with no effect._

~~~admonish abstract title="Identity at a glance"
| | |
|---|---|
| **Models** | A value and nothing else: no failure, no absence, no delay |
| **Replaces** | A plain value, where code written for any monad needs one |
| **Effect Path** | [`IdPath`](../glossary/effect-paths.md#idpath), a step that cannot fail |
| **Instances** | `Functor`, `Applicative`, `Selective`, `Monad`, `Foldable`, `Traverse` |
| **Choose it when** | You run code written for any monad with no effect, in a test or as a transformer's inner monad; otherwise a plain value, or compare every type in [Choosing a Type](supported-types.md#choosing-a-type) |
~~~

~~~admonish info title="What You'll Learn"
- Hold a step that cannot fail on an `IdPath`, and join it to a chain that can
- Test code written for any monad by running it with `Id`
- Run a transformer over `Id`, and choose between it and the plain type
- Predict what `Id` does with `null`, which `IdPath` refuses
- Use `Id` in generic code through its `Monad` and `Traverse` instances
~~~

~~~admonish example title="See Example Code"
**The code on this page is [IdentityBook.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/identity/IdentityBook.java)**: the page includes it, so the build compiles and runs it. [IdentityBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/identity/IdentityBookTest.java) holds what the page says each method does.
~~~

---

## The monad that adds nothing {#the-monad-that-adds-nothing}

Code written for any monad takes a `Monad<F>` and values of type `Kind<F, A>`, and never says which monad `F` is. Something must fill `F` when you want no effect at all, such as a test that checks only the arithmetic, or a transformer whose inner monad has nothing to do. `Id` fills it. An `Id<A>` holds one value, its `map` and `flatMap` apply the function to that value, and nothing else happens. On a Path it is an `IdPath`, a step that cannot fail.

The code on this page uses these imports:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.id.IdKindHelper.ID;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.id;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.io;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.io.IOKindHelper.IO_OP;

import java.math.BigDecimal;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.foundations.Basket;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.LineItem;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Kind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Monad;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.TypeArity;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.WitnessArity;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.effect.EitherPath;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.effect.IdPath;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.effect.Path;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.id.Id;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.id.IdKind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Instances;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.io.IO;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.io.IOKind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.state.StateTuple;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.state_t.StateT;
```

The basket and its lines are the ones the [State](state_monad.md#adding-to-a-basket) page fills: a lamp at 40.00, four bulbs at 2.50, a sofa at 450.00, and a limit of 100.00. The basket's total cannot fail, so it is an `IdPath`. `get()` gives the value, and `toEitherPath()` makes it the first step of a chain that can fail:

```java
    // A step that cannot fail, on the Path that adds no effect
    IdPath<BigDecimal> subtotal = Path.id(Basket.EMPTY.add(LAMP).add(BULBS)).map(Basket::total);

    BigDecimal total = subtotal.get();
    // 50.00

    // It joins a chain that can fail as the first step of an EitherPath
    EitherPath<String, BigDecimal> withinLimit =
        subtotal
            .<String>toEitherPath()
            .via(t -> t.compareTo(LIMIT) <= 0 ? Path.right(t) : Path.left("over the limit"));
```

`toMaybePath()` starts a chain that can be empty in the same way.

## Testing code written for any monad {#testing-code-written-for-any-monad}

This method adds a delivery charge to a total under 50.00. It is written for any monad, so the service can run it in [IO](io_monad.md) and a test in `Id`. Its bound, `F extends WitnessArity<TypeArity.Unary>`, says only that `F` holds one type, as [The WitnessArity bound](../hkts/core-concepts.md#the-witnessarity-bound) explains:

```java
  static final BigDecimal DELIVERY = new BigDecimal("4.99");
  static final BigDecimal FREE_FROM = new BigDecimal("50.00");

  // Written once for any monad: F might be IO in the service and Id in a test
  static <F extends WitnessArity<TypeArity.Unary>> Kind<F, BigDecimal> withDelivery(
      Monad<F> monad, Kind<F, BigDecimal> subtotal) {
    return monad.map(
        total -> total.compareTo(FREE_FROM) >= 0 ? total : total.add(DELIVERY), subtotal);
  }

```

In a test, `Id` runs it with nothing around the value, so the test reads the total with `ID.narrow(...).value()`:

```java
    // In a test, Id runs the arithmetic with no effect around it
    Monad<IdKind.Witness> ids = Instances.monad(id());

    BigDecimal small = ID.narrow(withDelivery(ids, Id.of(new BigDecimal("12.50")))).value();
    // 17.49
    BigDecimal large = ID.narrow(withDelivery(ids, Id.of(new BigDecimal("60.00")))).value();
    // 60.00
```

The service passes `IO`, and the same method adds the charge when the `IO` runs:

```java
    // In the service, the subtotal is read when the IO runs, and so is the charge added
    Kind<IOKind.Witness, BigDecimal> subtotalLater =
        IO_OP.widen(IO.delay(() -> new BigDecimal("12.50")));
    IO<BigDecimal> charged = IO_OP.narrow(withDelivery(Instances.monad(io()), subtotalLater));

    BigDecimal served = charged.unsafeRunSync();
    // 17.49
```

## A transformer over `Id` {#a-transformer-over-id}

A transformer takes the monad it wraps as a parameter: `StateT<S, F, A>` threads a state through steps in `F`. With `Id` as `F` it adds nothing beyond the state, so a `StateT<Basket, IdKind.Witness, Boolean>` runs as the State page's `State<Basket, Boolean>` does. Here is that page's basket step, written as a `StateT` over `Id`:

```java
  // The State page's step, as a StateT over Id: adds the line if the basket stays within the limit
  static StateT<Basket, IdKind.Witness, Boolean> add(LineItem line) {
    return StateT.create(
        basket -> {
          Basket bigger = basket.add(line);
          return Id.of(
              bigger.total().compareTo(LIMIT) <= 0
                  ? new StateTuple<>(true, bigger)
                  : new StateTuple<>(false, basket));
        });
  }

```

```java
    // runStateT gives an Id holding the result and the basket the step leaves
    StateTuple<Basket, Boolean> after = ID.narrow(add(SOFA).runStateT(Basket.EMPTY)).value();

    // The sofa would take the basket past the limit, so the step leaves it out
    boolean added = after.value();
    // false
```

The library ships `State`, `Reader` and `Writer` as types of their own, which are shorter to write than a transformer over `Id`. Reach for the transformer when the code is written for any inner monad, and a test or a caller fixes that monad to `Id`.

## `Id` holds a null {#id-holds-a-null}

A traversal's `modify` runs through `Id`, so `Id` holds whatever an element holds. `Id.of(null)` holds the null, and the monad's `map` keeps a `null` that its function returns. `IdPath` refuses one: `Path.id(null)` throws `NullPointerException`, and so does `Path.idPath` given an `Id` that holds `null`. [Values, never null](../hkts/core-concepts.md#values-never-null) sets out the rule for each type.

## Creating instances {#creating-instances}

`Id.of(value)` wraps a value, and `Instances.monad(id()).of(value)` does the same through the instance. `Path.id(value)` starts an `IdPath`, and `Path.idPath(id)` turns an `Id` into one.

## Methods {#methods}

| Method | What it does |
|---|---|
| `id.value()` | Gives the value |
| `id.map(f)`, `id.flatMap(f)` | Apply `f` to the value; `flatMap`'s function returns an `Id` |
| `idPath.map(f)`, `via(f)`, `zipWith(other, f)` | The same on the Path; `via`'s function returns an `IdPath` |
| `idPath.get()`, `run()` | Give the value, or the `Id` |
| `idPath.toEitherPath()`, `toMaybePath()` | Start a chain that can fail, or be empty, with the value |

## Working with Kind {#working-with-kind}

Generic code takes `Id` through its `Monad`, from `Instances.monad(id())`, which is `IdMonad.instance()`. `IdTraverse.INSTANCE` traverses an `Id` with any `Applicative`, and is its `Foldable` too, and `IdSelective.instance()` is its `Selective`. `Id` implements its own `Kind`, so `Id.of(value)` needs no `widen`, and `ID.narrow` gives the `Id` back, as [Testing code written for any monad](#testing-code-written-for-any-monad) shows.

## When to use `Id` {#when-to-use-id}

| You need | Use |
|---|---|
| A test of code written for any monad, with nothing to set up | `Id` |
| A transformer's inner monad, when the code is written for any | `Id`, as in `StateT<S, IdKind.Witness, A>` |
| A state, an environment or a log threaded through steps | [State](state_monad.md), [Reader](reader_monad.md) or [Writer](writer_monad.md) |
| A step that cannot fail, in a chain of Paths | [`IdPath`](../glossary/effect-paths.md#idpath) |
| A value in ordinary code | The value itself: `Id` adds nothing |

~~~admonish info title="Hands-On Learning"
Run transformers over `Id` in [Tutorial 04: Polymorphic Capabilities](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/tutorial/transformers/Tutorial04_PolymorphicCapabilities.java) (14 exercises).
~~~

---

**Previous:** [Stream](stream_monad.md)
**Next:** [Trampoline](trampoline_monad.md)

<!-- Source: monads/trampoline_monad.md -->

<!-- description: Run deep recursion in Java without overflowing the stack using Trampoline: a sum a million calls deep, two functions that call each other, and a long list traversed under IO. -->

# Trampoline: Deep Recursion in a Loop

_Hold each step as data and run them in one loop, so deep recursion costs heap, not stack._

~~~admonish abstract title="Trampoline at a glance"
| | |
|---|---|
| **Models** | A recursive computation held as steps, which `run()` works through one at a time |
| **Replaces** | A recursive method that overflows the stack, or a loop that keeps its own stack of work |
| **Effect Path** | [`TrampolinePath`](../glossary/effect-paths.md#trampolinepath) |
| **Instances** | `Functor`, `Applicative`, `Monad` |
| **Choose it when** | Recursion can go thousands of calls deep, or two functions call each other; otherwise keep the plain recursion or loop you have, or compare every type in [Choosing a Type](supported-types.md#choosing-a-type) |
~~~

~~~admonish info title="What You'll Learn"
- Explain why a recursive sum overflows the stack, and what sets the depth it reaches
- Hold the same recursion as steps on a `TrampolinePath`, and run it a million calls deep
- Turn each kind of call into a step: a base case, a tail call, and a call whose result you combine
- Run two functions that call each other in one loop
- Traverse a long list under `IO` without nesting a call for each element
~~~

~~~admonish example title="See Example Code"
**The code on this page is [TrampolineBook.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/trampoline/TrampolineBook.java)**: the page includes it, so the build compiles and runs it. [TrampolineBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/trampoline/TrampolineBookTest.java) holds what the page says each method does.
~~~

---

## A sum that overflows {#a-sum-that-overflows}

The sums on this page use plain numbers, since the depth of the recursion is what matters. The code on this page uses these imports:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.io;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.io.IOKindHelper.IO_OP;

import java.util.List;
import java.util.stream.IntStream;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Kind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.effect.Path;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.effect.TrampolinePath;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Instances;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.io.IO;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.io.IOKind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.trampoline.Trampoline;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.trampoline.TrampolineUtils;
```

Each call to this sum waits for the deeper call it makes to return, so each holds a frame on the thread's stack:

```java
  // Each call waits for the deeper call it makes, so each adds a frame to the stack
  static long sum(long n) {
    if (n <= 0) {
      return 0;
    }
    return n + sum(n - 1);
  }

```

`sum(1_000)` gives `500500`, but `sum(1_000_000)` throws `StackOverflowError`. A thread's stack has a fixed size, 1 MB by default: 5,000 of these frames fit, and 100,000 do not. Where between the two it overflows depends on whether the JIT has compiled the method yet. The JVM never reuses a frame for a call in tail position, so rewriting the method as a tail call does not help. You can catch the error, but the sum is lost.

## The same sum, held as steps {#the-same-sum-held-as-steps}

A `TrampolinePath` holds the recursion instead of making it. `Path.trampolineDefer` holds the deeper call as a step to take later, and `map` holds what to do with that call's result:

```java
  // The same sum, held as steps: defer the deeper call, then map its result
  static TrampolinePath<Long> sumPath(long n) {
    if (n <= 0) {
      return Path.trampolineDone(0L);
    }
    return Path.trampolineDefer(() -> sumPath(n - 1)).map(deeper -> n + deeper);
  }

```

`run()` works through the steps in one loop. Each pending addition waits on a list on the heap, so the stack stays flat:

```java
    long total = sumPath(1_000_000).run();
    // 500000500000
```

The name comes from the loop. Each step bounces back to it with the next step to take, where a plain recursion would go one frame deeper:

<pre class="hkj-ascii-diagram" role="img" aria-label="On the left, plain recursion: sum(5) calls sum(4), down to sum(0), and each call stays on the stack until the one it made returns, so the stack grows with n and the additions happen as the calls return, giving 15. On the right, the trampoline: run takes a deferred step for sum(5), which hands back a deferred step for sum(4) and an addition to make later, and so on down to sum(0), which is done with 0. The loop keeps the pending additions on a list on the heap, then applies them, 0 + 1 + 2 + 3 + 4 + 5, giving 15. The stack stays flat.">
  Plain recursion                     On a trampoline

  sum(5)                              run():
    sum(4)                              +--> sum(5): defer sum(4), then add 5
      sum(3)                            |      |
        sum(2)                          |      v
          sum(1)                        +--> sum(4): defer sum(3), then add 4
            sum(0)                      |      |
            return 0                    |      v
          return 1                      +--> ...
        return 3                        |      |
      return 6                          |      v
    return 10                           +--> sum(0) is done: 0
  return 15                             then the held additions:
                                        0 + 1 + 2 + 3 + 4 + 5 = 15

  Stack grows with n                  Stack stays flat; additions wait on the heap
</pre>

~~~admonish tip title="Influence: Recursion, Thunks and Trampolines"
Higher-Kinded-J's trampoline grew out of Scott Logic's blog post [Recursion, Thunks and Trampolines with Java and Scala](https://blog.scottlogic.com/2025/05/02/recursion-thunks-trampolines-with-java-and-scala.html). The post builds the same idea from two cases, `Done` for a result and `More` for a step still to take, run by one loop. Higher-Kinded-J adds a third case, `FlatMap`, for sequencing, and an instance, so a `Trampoline` takes part in code written for any monad.
~~~

## Each kind of call, as a step {#each-kind-of-call-as-a-step}

A recursive method becomes a trampolined one call by call:

| In the recursive method | On a trampoline |
|---|---|
| The return type `T` | `Trampoline<T>`, or `TrampolinePath<T>` |
| A base case, `return value` | `Trampoline.done(value)` |
| A tail call, `return f(x)` | `Trampoline.defer(() -> f(x))` |
| A call whose result you combine, `return n + f(x)` | `Trampoline.defer(() -> f(x)).map(r -> n + r)` |
| Two calls, `return f(x) + f(y)` | `Trampoline.defer(() -> f(x)).flatMap(a -> f(y).map(b -> a + b))` |

`map` and `flatMap` are what keep a combined call safe: `defer` alone would leave the addition outside the trampoline. When the running total can travel with the call, a tail call needs only `defer`:

```java
  // A tail call: the running total travels with the call, so only defer is needed
  static Trampoline<Long> sumTail(long n, long total) {
    if (n <= 0) {
      return Trampoline.done(total);
    }
    return Trampoline.defer(() -> sumTail(n - 1, total + n));
  }

```

```java
    long sameTotal = sumTail(1_000_000, 0).run();
    // 500000500000
```

## Two functions that call each other {#two-functions-that-call-each-other}

Two functions that call each other overflow the stack as fast as one deep function. They are awkward to merge into one loop by hand, since the loop must track which function it is in. On a trampoline, each stays as it is written, and defers to the other:

```java
  // Two functions that call each other, each kept as it is written
  static Trampoline<Boolean> isEven(long n) {
    return n == 0 ? Trampoline.done(true) : Trampoline.defer(() -> isOdd(n - 1));
  }

  static Trampoline<Boolean> isOdd(long n) {
    return n == 0 ? Trampoline.done(false) : Trampoline.defer(() -> isEven(n - 1));
  }

```

```java
    boolean even = isEven(1_000_001).run();
    // false
```

## A long list under `IO` {#core-components}

`traverse` runs a function that returns an effect on each element of a list, and gathers the results into one effect holding a list, as [Foldable and Traverse](../functional/foldable_and_traverse.md) explains. Under a lazy applicative such as `IO`, a traverse that combines the elements one after another nests one call per element, so running the result for a long list can overflow. `TrampolineUtils.traverseListStackSafe` combines them as a balanced tree instead, and `sequenceStackSafe` does the same for a list of effects:

```java
    List<Integer> lines = IntStream.rangeClosed(1, 100_000).boxed().toList();

    // Combines the IO steps as a balanced tree, so the nesting grows with the log of the length
    Kind<IOKind.Witness, List<String>> labelled =
        TrampolineUtils.traverseListStackSafe(
            lines, n -> IO_OP.widen(IO.delay(() -> "line " + n)), Instances.monad(io()));

    int labels = IO_OP.narrow(labelled).unsafeRunSync().size();
    // 100000
```

They sit beside `Trampoline` in its package, but need no trampoline, since the tree's nesting grows with the log of the list's length.

## A trampoline always gives a value {#a-trampoline-always-gives-a-value}

Nothing runs until `run()`. A trampoline always gives a value, so `done(null)` throws `NullPointerException`, as [A Task Always Gives a Value](vtask_monad.md#a-task-always-gives-a-value) sets out for every lazy type.

## Creating instances {#creating-instances}

`Path.trampolineDone(value)` and `Path.trampolineDefer(supplier)` start a `TrampolinePath`, and `Path.trampoline(trampoline)` wraps a `Trampoline`. `Trampoline.done(value)` and `Trampoline.defer(supplier)` build a `Trampoline` directly.

## Methods {#methods}

| Method | What it does |
|---|---|
| `defer(supplier)` | Holds a step to take later |
| `map(f)`, `flatMap(f)` | Hold what to do with the result; on a `TrampolinePath`, `flatMap` is `via` |
| `zipWith(other, f)` | Combines two trampolines' results, on a `TrampolinePath` |
| `run()` | Works through every step in a loop, and gives the result |
| `toTrampoline()`, `toIOPath()`, `toLazyPath()` | Turn a `TrampolinePath` into a `Trampoline`, an `IOPath` or a `LazyPath` |

## Working with Kind {#working-with-kind}

Generic code takes `Trampoline` through its `Monad`, from `Instances.monad(trampoline())`, which is `TrampolineMonad.INSTANCE`. Its `flatMap` holds the step as `Trampoline.flatMap` does, so a deep chain built through the instance runs in the loop too. `TRAMPOLINE.widen` and `TRAMPOLINE.narrow`, from `TrampolineKindHelper`, convert between a `Trampoline` and its `Kind`.

## When to use `Trampoline` {#when-to-use-trampoline}

| You need | Use |
|---|---|
| Recursion that can go thousands of calls deep, such as a walk over a deep tree | `Trampoline`, or [`TrampolinePath`](../glossary/effect-paths.md#trampolinepath) |
| Two functions that call each other | `Trampoline`: each stays as it is written |
| A traverse of a long list under `IO` or another lazy applicative | `TrampolineUtils` |
| Recursion that stays a few hundred calls deep | Plain recursion |
| A loop that is already a loop | The loop: a trampoline adds an object per step |

**Benchmarks:** `TrampolineBenchmark` measures building and running trampolines, and deep recursion against plain recursion. [Benchmarks & Performance](../benchmarks.md) says how to run it.

---

**Previous:** [Identity](identity.md)
**Next:** [Free](free_monad.md)

<!-- Source: monads/free_monad.md -->

<!-- description: Hold a program as data in Java with Free: an order program that cancels the order when a line costs too much, run against an in-memory store and audited into a Writer. -->

# Free: Programs as Data

_Write the program once, as instructions, and choose what each one does when you run it._

<!-- source: Rúnar Bjarnason, "Constraints Liberate, Liberties Constrain", a talk at Scala World, Lake District, 20 to 22 September 2015; the title checked against the ScalaWorld 2015 recap deck (speakerdeck.com/ajantis/scalaworld-2015-recap), and set here in sentence case -->
> _"Constraints liberate, liberties constrain."_
>
> — Rúnar Bjarnason, *Constraints Liberate, Liberties Constrain*

Bjarnason's maxim cuts both ways on this page. A `Free` program is held to its instructions, so you are free to choose what each one does when it runs. But each step is also free to depend on the one before, and a step that waits on a result cannot be seen until that result exists.

~~~admonish abstract title="Free at a glance"
| | |
|---|---|
| **Models** | A program held as data: instructions that an interpreter runs later, in a monad it chooses |
| **Replaces** | Calls to an injected service, made directly from business code |
| **Effect Path** | [`FreePath`](../glossary/effect-paths.md#freepath) |
| **Instances** | `Functor`, `Applicative`, `Monad` |
| **Choose it when** | One program must run in several ways, such as live, in a test and as an audit; otherwise call the service, see [Free Applicative](free_applicative.md) for a program you can inspect whole, or compare every type in [Choosing a Type](supported-types.md#choosing-a-type) |
~~~

~~~admonish info title="What You'll Learn"
- Build an order program from instructions held as data, with nothing run yet
- Run the same program against an in-memory store and as an audit, by choosing the interpreter
- Predict what `foldMap` keeps of each instruction's value, and what still deepens the stack
- Inspect a program before it runs, and explain why only its first instruction shows
- Choose between `Free` and Tagless Final
~~~

~~~admonish example title="See Example Code"
**The code on this page is [FreeBook.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/free/FreeBook.java)**: the page includes it, so the build compiles and runs it. [FreeBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/free/FreeBookTest.java) holds what the page says each method does.
~~~

~~~admonish tip title="Where Free fits"
Most code never builds a `Free` by hand. [`@EffectAlgebra`](../effect/effect_handlers.md#defining-effects) generates the constructors and the interpreter base class for an instruction set, [`@ComposeEffects`](../effect/effect_handlers.md#composing-effects) combines several, and the [Effect Handlers](../effect/effect_handlers_intro.md) chapter shows the whole pattern. This page shows what those programs are made of.
~~~

---

## Instructions held as data {#instructions-held-as-data}

An order program prices a line, and cancels the order when the line costs more than a limit. Call an injected `OrderService` and a test can pass a fake, but each call runs the moment it is made, and nothing can look at the program first. A `Free` program holds the steps as instructions instead, which an interpreter runs later: a live interpreter calls the service, a test interpreter works against memory, and an audit interpreter records what it was asked. The instructions are the `OrderOp` records from [Applicative, Selective or Monad?](../functional/abstraction_levels.md):

```java
/**
 * The order service's instructions, held as data so that a program built from them can be inspected
 * before an interpreter runs it. {@code @EffectAlgebra} generates the {@code Kind} encoding, the
 * {@code Functor}, the interpreter base class and the {@code Free} constructors.
 */
@NullMarked
@EffectAlgebra
public sealed interface OrderOp<A> permits OrderOp.PriceOf, OrderOp.Cancel {

  /** Maps a function over the result type. */
  <B> OrderOp<B> mapK(Function<? super A, ? extends B> f);

  /**
   * Looks up the price of a SKU.
   *
   * @param sku the SKU to price
   * @param k continuation from the price to {@code A}
   * @param <A> the result type
   */
  record PriceOf<A>(String sku, Function<BigDecimal, A> k) implements OrderOp<A> {
    @Override
    public <B> OrderOp<B> mapK(Function<? super A, ? extends B> f) {
      return new PriceOf<>(sku, k.andThen(f));
    }
  }

  /**
   * Cancels an order.
   *
   * @param orderId the order to cancel
   * @param k continuation from {@link Unit} to {@code A}
   * @param <A> the result type
   */
  record Cancel<A>(UUID orderId, Function<Unit, A> k) implements OrderOp<A> {
    @Override
    public <B> OrderOp<B> mapK(Function<? super A, ? extends B> f) {
      return new Cancel<>(orderId, k.andThen(f));
    }
  }
}
```

Each record carries a continuation `k`, from the instruction's result to whatever the program does next. `@EffectAlgebra` generates the rest: `OrderOpKind` and `OrderOpKindHelper`, its encoding as a `Kind`; `OrderOpOps`, with a `Free` constructor for each record; a `Functor`; and `OrderOpInterpreter`, the base class an interpreter extends. The code on this page uses these imports:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.free.FreeKindHelper.FREE;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.id.IdKindHelper.ID;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.id;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.writer.WriterKindHelper.WRITER;

import java.math.BigDecimal;
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.UUID;
import java.util.function.Function;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.foundations.AuditEntry;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.foundations.OrderOp;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.foundations.OrderOpFunctor;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.foundations.OrderOpInterpreter;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.foundations.OrderOpKind;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.foundations.OrderOpOps;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Functor;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Kind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Monoids;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Unit;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.effect.FreePath;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.effect.Path;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.free.Free;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.free.FreeFactory;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.free.FreeKind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.free.FreeMonad;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.free.ProgramAnalyser;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.free.ProgramAnalysis;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.id.Id;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.id.IdKind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Instances;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.writer.Writer;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.writer.WriterKind;
```

`Path.free` puts each generated constructor on a `FreePath`:

```java
  static final Functor<OrderOpKind.Witness> ORDER_OPS = OrderOpFunctor.instance();

  // Each step is one instruction, held as data: nothing is priced or cancelled yet.
  // Function.identity() is the continuation k: the step's result is the instruction's own.
  static FreePath<OrderOpKind.Witness, BigDecimal> priceOf(String sku) {
    return Path.free(OrderOpOps.priceOf(sku, Function.identity()), ORDER_OPS);
  }

  static FreePath<OrderOpKind.Witness, Unit> cancel(UUID orderId) {
    return Path.free(OrderOpOps.cancel(orderId, Function.identity()), ORDER_OPS);
  }

```

The program chains them with `via`. The second step needs the price, so which step it is depends on what the first gives:

```java
  // What the program decided about a line
  record Decision(String sku, BigDecimal price, boolean cancelled) {}

  // Cancels the order if the line costs more than the limit: the second step needs the price
  static FreePath<OrderOpKind.Witness, Decision> cancelIfOver(
      UUID orderId, String sku, BigDecimal limit) {
    return priceOf(sku)
        .via(
            price ->
                price.compareTo(limit) > 0
                    ? cancel(orderId).map(_ -> new Decision(sku, price, true))
                    : Path.freePure(new Decision(sku, price, false), ORDER_OPS));
  }

```

Nothing has been priced or cancelled. `cancelIfOver` gives a description of the two steps, which runs only when an interpreter runs it, and the same description can run many times.

## One program, two interpreters {#one-program-two-interpreters}

An interpreter says what each instruction does, in a monad it chooses. It extends `OrderOpInterpreter<M>`, which is a [`Natural`](../functional/natural_transformation.md) from `OrderOp` to `M`, with one method for each record, and each method hands its result to the record's `k`. This one runs in [`Id`](identity.md), against a catalogue and a list of cancelled orders. Its `CATALOGUE` prices a `LAMP` at 40.00 and a `BULB` at 2.50:

```java
  // Runs each instruction against a catalogue and a list of cancelled orders
  static final class InMemoryOrders extends OrderOpInterpreter<IdKind.Witness> {
    final List<UUID> cancelled = new ArrayList<>();

    @Override
    protected <A> Kind<IdKind.Witness, A> handlePriceOf(OrderOp.PriceOf<A> op) {
      return ID.widen(Id.of(op.k().apply(CATALOGUE.get(op.sku()))));
    }

    @Override
    protected <A> Kind<IdKind.Witness, A> handleCancel(OrderOp.Cancel<A> op) {
      cancelled.add(op.orderId());
      return ID.widen(Id.of(op.k().apply(Unit.INSTANCE)));
    }
  }

```

`foldMap` runs the program with it. `LIMIT` is 30.00, so the lamp's line cancels the order:

```java
    InMemoryOrders store = new InMemoryOrders();

    Kind<IdKind.Witness, Decision> ran =
        cancelIfOver(ORDER, "LAMP", LIMIT).foldMap(store, Instances.monad(id())).runKind();

    Decision lamp = ID.narrow(ran).value();
    // Decision[sku=LAMP, price=40.00, cancelled=true]

    boolean cancelled = store.cancelled.contains(ORDER);
    // true
```

`foldMap` takes one instruction at a time. The interpreter runs it, and the program's continuation, given the result, says what comes next:

```mermaid
%%{init: {"sequence": {"actorMargin": 20, "diagramMarginX": 10}}}%%
sequenceDiagram
    accTitle: How foldMap runs cancelIfOver with the in-memory interpreter
    accDescr: foldMap takes the program's first instruction, PriceOf LAMP, and hands it to the interpreter, which gives Id of 40.00. foldMap hands the price to the program's continuation, which compares it with the limit and gives the next instruction, Cancel. The interpreter records the cancellation and gives Id of Unit. foldMap hands that to the continuation, which gives the program's result, a Decision that the order was cancelled.
    participant P as cancelIfOver
    participant F as foldMap
    participant I as InMemoryOrders
    P-->>F: PriceOf LAMP
    F->>I: PriceOf LAMP
    I-->>F: Id(40.00)
    F->>P: continue with 40.00
    P-->>F: Cancel the order
    F->>I: Cancel the order
    Note over I: records the order
    I-->>F: Id(Unit)
    F->>P: continue with Unit
    P-->>F: the Decision, cancelled
```

A second interpreter, `AuditedOrders`, cancels nothing. It records each instruction as an entry in a [Writer](writer_monad.md)'s audit log:

```java
  // Keeps an audit entry for each instruction in a Writer's log, and cancels nothing
  static final class AuditedOrders
      extends OrderOpInterpreter<WriterKind.Witness<List<AuditEntry>>> {
    private final UUID orderId;

    AuditedOrders(UUID orderId) {
      this.orderId = orderId;
    }

    @Override
    protected <A> Kind<WriterKind.Witness<List<AuditEntry>>, A> handlePriceOf(
        OrderOp.PriceOf<A> op) {
      BigDecimal price = CATALOGUE.get(op.sku());
      AuditEntry entry = new AuditEntry(orderId, "priced " + op.sku() + " at " + price);
      return WRITER.widen(Writer.of(List.of(entry), op.k().apply(price)));
    }

    @Override
    protected <A> Kind<WriterKind.Witness<List<AuditEntry>>, A> handleCancel(OrderOp.Cancel<A> op) {
      AuditEntry entry = new AuditEntry(op.orderId(), "would cancel");
      return WRITER.widen(Writer.of(List.of(entry), op.k().apply(Unit.INSTANCE)));
    }
  }

```

```java
    Writer<List<AuditEntry>, Decision> audited =
        WRITER.narrow(
            cancelIfOver(ORDER, "LAMP", LIMIT)
                .foldMap(new AuditedOrders(ORDER), Instances.writer(Monoids.list()))
                .runKind());

    List<String> events = audited.log().stream().map(AuditEntry::event).toList();
    // [priced LAMP at 40.00, would cancel]
```

`Free.foldMap` also takes a raw `Function` from instruction to target. Prefer a `Natural`, such as a generated interpreter, which states that each instruction's result type comes through unchanged.

## What `foldMap` keeps {#what-foldmap-keeps}

`foldMap` hands each instruction's whole value to the target monad. The audit's log holds both entries because the `Writer` keeps every entry it is given, and in the same way a `List` target keeps every result, and an `EitherOrBoth` every warning. A deep program runs in `Id`, `Either`, `Writer`, `EitherOrBoth`, `State` or `IO` without overflowing the stack.

Two things still deepen the stack. An instruction that gives several results, such as a `List` of two, runs the rest of the program once for each. A `Reader` nests one call for each instruction when you run it.

## A program always gives a value {#a-program-always-gives-a-value}

`Free.pure(null)` and `Path.freePure(null, functor)` throw at the call, and a `map` function that returns `null` fails the program when it is interpreted. [A Task Always Gives a Value](vtask_monad.md#a-task-always-gives-a-value) sets out the rule every lazy type follows.

## What can be seen before it runs {#what-can-be-seen-before-it-runs}

A program held as data can be inspected before it runs. `ProgramAnalyser` counts what it can see:

```java
    ProgramAnalysis analysis = ProgramAnalyser.analyse(cancelIfOver(ORDER, "LAMP", LIMIT).toFree());

    // How many instructions the analysis can see, and whether a function hides any more
    int seen = analysis.suspendCount();
    // 1
    boolean hidden = analysis.hasOpaqueRegions();
    // true
```

Only the first instruction shows. The cancel waits inside the function that `via` was given, and no analysis can see into a function without running it. A program whose steps do not depend on one another can be seen whole: [Free Applicative](free_applicative.md) holds one, and [Applicative, Selective or Monad?](../functional/abstraction_levels.md) prices the same `OrderOp` instructions that way. [Program Analysis](../effect/effect_handlers.md#program-analysis) lists what `ProgramAnalyser` counts.

## `Free` or Tagless Final {#free-or-tagless-final}

Tagless Final reaches the same goals, several interpreters and tests without a live service, without building programs as data. The program is a method written against an interface of operations, generic over the monad, and each interpreter implements the interface.

| | `Free` | Tagless Final |
|---|---|---|
| A program is | A value, which you can hold, pass around and run more than once | A method, which runs when it is called with an interpreter |
| Seen before it runs | Up to the first instruction whose result the rest needs | By running it with an interpreter that records instead of acting |
| Cost per step | Higher: each instruction is interpreted | Lower: each call runs directly |
| Code to write | An instruction set, which `@EffectAlgebra` encodes, and each interpreter | An interface, and each implementation |

Choose `Free` when the program itself must be a value: to pass it around, or to move it into a larger instruction set with [EitherF](eitherf.md). It also runs a deeply recursive program in a strict target such as `Id`, where `foldMap`'s loop keeps the stack flat. Choose Tagless Final when only the interpreters matter.

## Creating instances {#creating-instances}

A generated constructor such as `OrderOpOps.priceOf(sku, k)` lifts one instruction into a `Free`, and `Free.liftF(fa, functor)` lifts any. `Free.pure(value)` holds a result with no instruction. `Path.free(free, functor)`, `Path.freeLift(fa, functor)` and `Path.freePure(value, functor)` start a `FreePath`. An instruction set without a `Functor` of its own can take one from [Coyoneda](coyoneda.md).

## Methods {#methods}

| Method | What it does |
|---|---|
| `freePath.via(f)`, `map(f)` | Chain the next step on the result, or change the result |
| `zipWith(other, f)` | Combine two programs' results |
| `handleError(type, handler)` | Recover a failure of that type in the target monad, when the target is a `MonadError` |
| `recover(f)` | Recover any `Throwable` failure the same way |
| `foldMap(interpreter, monad)` | Run the program, giving a `GenericPath` in the target monad |
| `toFree()` | Gives the `Free` |
| `free.foldMap(interpreter, monad)` | Run a `Free`, giving a `Kind` in the target monad |
| `Free.translate(free, natural, functor)` | Move a program to another instruction set, as the [EitherF](eitherf.md) page shows |

## Working with Kind {#working-with-kind}

A `Free` is already its own `Kind`, so `FREE.widen` and `FREE.narrow`, from `FreeKindHelper`, are casts. Generic code takes it through a `FreeMonad`, built with `new FreeMonad<>()`, whose `map` and `flatMap` build the program as `Free`'s own do:

```java
    FreeMonad<OrderOpKind.Witness> frees = new FreeMonad<>();

    // A Free is already its own Kind, so widen and narrow are casts
    Kind<FreeKind.Witness<OrderOpKind.Witness>, BigDecimal> lampPrice =
        FREE.widen(OrderOpOps.priceOf("LAMP", Function.identity()));
    Kind<FreeKind.Witness<OrderOpKind.Witness>, BigDecimal> withDelivery =
        frees.map(price -> price.add(new BigDecimal("4.99")), lampPrice);

    BigDecimal charged =
        ID.narrow(FREE.narrow(withDelivery).foldMap(new InMemoryOrders(), Instances.monad(id())))
            .value();
    // 44.99
```

`Free.pure` at the start of a chain gives Java nothing to infer the instruction type from, so javac refuses this, with "no instance(s) of type variable(s) B exist":

<!-- verify:rejects "no instance(s) of type variable(s) B exist" -->
```java
Free<OrderOpKind.Witness, BigDecimal> fee =
    Free.pure(new BigDecimal("40.00")).map(price -> price.multiply(new BigDecimal("0.10")));
```

A `FreeFactory` fixes the instruction type once:

```java
    // A factory fixes the instruction type once, so Java can infer it along the chain
    FreeFactory<OrderOpKind.Witness> orders = FreeFactory.of();

    Free<OrderOpKind.Witness, BigDecimal> fee =
        orders.pure(new BigDecimal("40.00")).map(price -> price.multiply(new BigDecimal("0.10")));
```

A `FreePath` needs no factory, since `Path.freePure(value, functor)` takes the instruction type from its `Functor`.

## When to use `Free` {#when-to-use-free}

| You need | Use |
|---|---|
| One program run live, in tests and as an audit | `Free`, through [`@EffectAlgebra`](../effect/effect_handlers.md) |
| A program inspected whole before it runs | [Free Applicative](free_applicative.md) |
| Several instruction sets in one program | [EitherF](eitherf.md), or `@ComposeEffects` |
| One side effect, run one way | [IO](io_monad.md), or a direct call |
| A hot loop | Direct code: each instruction costs an interpretation |

~~~admonish info title="Hands-On Learning"
Practise the pattern in [Tutorial 01: Effect Algebra Basics](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/tutorial/effecthandlers/Tutorial01_EffectAlgebraBasics.java) (5 exercises) and [Tutorial 02: Multiple Interpreters](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/tutorial/effecthandlers/Tutorial02_MultipleInterpreters.java) (3 exercises), from the [Effect Handlers](../tutorials/effecthandlers/ch_intro.md) journey.
~~~

**Benchmarks:** `FreeBenchmark` measures building and interpreting `Free` programs, and `FreeEffectBenchmark` the cost of composing several instruction sets. [Benchmarks & Performance](../benchmarks.md) says how to run them.

---

**Previous:** [Trampoline](trampoline_monad.md)
**Next:** [Free Applicative](free_applicative.md)

<!-- Source: monads/free_applicative.md -->

<!-- description: Hold a program of independent steps as data in Java with Free Applicative: read an order's prices before they run, fetch them in one call, report every unknown SKU. -->

# Free Applicative: Programs You Can Read Whole

_No step waits on another, so the whole program is known before any of it runs._

~~~admonish abstract title="Free Applicative at a glance"
| | |
|---|---|
| **Models** | A program of instructions that do not depend on one another, held as data and known whole before it runs |
| **Replaces** | Independent calls made one at a time, when you want to count, check or batch them first |
| **Effect Path** | [`FreeApPath`](../glossary/effect-paths.md#freeappath) |
| **Instances** | `Functor`, `Applicative` |
| **Choose it when** | No step needs the result of another; otherwise [Free](free_monad.md), or compare every type in [Choosing a Type](supported-types.md#choosing-a-type) |
~~~

~~~admonish info title="What You'll Learn"
- Build an order's prices as one program whose instructions are all known before it runs
- Count a program's instructions and list what it will ask for, without running it
- Price every line with one catalogue call, by reading the program first
- Report every unknown SKU at once, by interpreting into `Validated`
- Choose between Free Applicative and Free, and hold one inside the other
~~~

~~~admonish example title="See Example Code"
**The code on this page is [FreeApplicativeBook.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/freeap/FreeApplicativeBook.java)**: the page includes it, so the build compiles and runs it. [FreeApplicativeBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/freeap/FreeApplicativeBookTest.java) holds what the page says each method does.
~~~

~~~admonish tip title="Where Free Applicative fits"
[`@EffectAlgebra`](../effect/effect_handlers.md#defining-effects) generates an instruction set's encoding, its `Functor` and its interpreter base class, and those serve a Free Applicative program as they serve a [Free](free_monad.md) one. Its generated constructors build `Free` programs only, so a `FreeAp` program lifts each instruction itself, as this page's `priceOf` does. [FreeApPath](../effect/path_freeap.md) documents the Path's operations; this page shows what independent steps buy you.
~~~

---

## Steps that do not depend on one another {#steps-that-do-not-depend-on-one-another}

Before checkout, the order service prices each line of a basket again from today's catalogue. In a [Free](free_monad.md) program each step may wait on the result of the one before, so an analysis [sees only as far as the first step that waits](free_monad.md#what-can-be-seen-before-it-runs). No line's price waits on another's, so the basket can be held as a Free Applicative program instead. Its steps are combined with `zipWith`, which gives neither step the other's result, and so every instruction is known before the program runs.

The instructions are the `OrderOp` records the [Free](free_monad.md#instructions-held-as-data) page uses. Each carries a continuation `k`, from the instruction's result to what the program does next, and `@EffectAlgebra` generates `OrderOpKind`, `OrderOpFunctor` and `OrderOpInterpreter` from them:

```java
/**
 * The order service's instructions, held as data so that a program built from them can be inspected
 * before an interpreter runs it. {@code @EffectAlgebra} generates the {@code Kind} encoding, the
 * {@code Functor}, the interpreter base class and the {@code Free} constructors.
 */
@NullMarked
@EffectAlgebra
public sealed interface OrderOp<A> permits OrderOp.PriceOf, OrderOp.Cancel {

  /** Maps a function over the result type. */
  <B> OrderOp<B> mapK(Function<? super A, ? extends B> f);

  /**
   * Looks up the price of a SKU.
   *
   * @param sku the SKU to price
   * @param k continuation from the price to {@code A}
   * @param <A> the result type
   */
  record PriceOf<A>(String sku, Function<BigDecimal, A> k) implements OrderOp<A> {
    @Override
    public <B> OrderOp<B> mapK(Function<? super A, ? extends B> f) {
      return new PriceOf<>(sku, k.andThen(f));
    }
  }

  /**
   * Cancels an order.
   *
   * @param orderId the order to cancel
   * @param k continuation from {@link Unit} to {@code A}
   * @param <A> the result type
   */
  record Cancel<A>(UUID orderId, Function<Unit, A> k) implements OrderOp<A> {
    @Override
    public <B> OrderOp<B> mapK(Function<? super A, ? extends B> f) {
      return new Cancel<>(orderId, k.andThen(f));
    }
  }
}
```

The code on this page uses these imports:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import static org.higherkindedj.example.book.foundations.OrderOpKindHelper.ORDER_OP;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.constant.ConstKindHelper.CONST;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.free_ap.FreeApKindHelper.FREE_AP;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.id.IdKindHelper.ID;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.id;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.validated.ValidatedKindHelper.VALIDATED;

import java.math.BigDecimal;
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.Map;
import java.util.UUID;
import java.util.function.Function;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.foundations.OrderOp;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.foundations.OrderOpFunctor;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.foundations.OrderOpInterpreter;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.foundations.OrderOpKind;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.foundations.OrderOpOps;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.LineItem;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Functor;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Kind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Monoids;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Semigroups;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Unit;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.constant.Const;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.constant.ConstKind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.effect.FreeApPath;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.effect.FreePath;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.effect.Path;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.free.Free;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.free.ProgramAnalyser;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.free.ProgramAnalysis;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.free_ap.FreeAp;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.free_ap.FreeApAnalyzer;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.free_ap.FreeApApplicative;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.free_ap.FreeApKind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.id.Id;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.id.IdKind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Instances;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.validated.Validated;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.validated.ValidatedKind;
```

`Path.freeApLift` puts one instruction on a `FreeApPath`. `Function.identity()` is its continuation, which hands the price straight back as the step's result:

```java
  static final Functor<OrderOpKind.Witness> ORDER_OPS = OrderOpFunctor.instance();

  // One instruction, held as data: nothing is priced until an interpreter runs it
  static FreeApPath<OrderOpKind.Witness, BigDecimal> priceOf(String sku) {
    return Path.freeApLift(
        ORDER_OP.widen(new OrderOp.PriceOf<>(sku, Function.identity())), ORDER_OPS);
  }

```

A line's total is its price times its quantity, and the basket's total adds the lines together with `zipWith`:

```java
  // A line at today's price: the price times the quantity
  static FreeApPath<OrderOpKind.Witness, BigDecimal> lineTotal(LineItem line) {
    return priceOf(line.sku()).map(price -> price.multiply(BigDecimal.valueOf(line.quantity())));
  }

  // Every line priced again; no line's price waits on another's
  static FreeApPath<OrderOpKind.Witness, BigDecimal> basketTotal(List<LineItem> lines) {
    return lines.stream()
        .map(FreeApplicativeBook::lineTotal)
        .reduce(
            Path.freeApPure(BigDecimal.ZERO, ORDER_OPS), (a, b) -> a.zipWith(b, BigDecimal::add));
  }

```

Nothing has been priced. `BASKET` holds one lamp and four bulbs, added last week at 38.00 and 2.40 each. The catalogue now prices a `LAMP` at 40.00 and a `BULB` at 2.50, so the basket comes to 50.00.

## Reading a program before it runs {#reading-a-program-before-it-runs}

An analysis is an interpreter too. This one turns each instruction into the SKU it would price, held in a [Const](const_type.md), which carries a value through the program and ignores what each step would give:

```java
  // An analysis is an interpreter too: each instruction gives the SKU it would price, in a Const
  static final class SkusToPrice extends OrderOpInterpreter<ConstKind.Witness<List<String>>> {
    @Override
    protected <A> Kind<ConstKind.Witness<List<String>>, A> handlePriceOf(OrderOp.PriceOf<A> op) {
      return CONST.widen(new Const<>(List.of(op.sku())));
    }

    @Override
    protected <A> Kind<ConstKind.Witness<List<String>>, A> handleCancel(OrderOp.Cancel<A> op) {
      return CONST.widen(new Const<>(List.of()));
    }
  }

```

`FreeApAnalyzer` counts the instructions, and `analyseWith` runs the analysis, joining the lists with `Monoids.list()`, a [Monoid](../functional/semigroup_and_monoid.md) that appends:

```java
    FreeAp<OrderOpKind.Witness, BigDecimal> program = basketTotal(BASKET).toFreeAp();

    int instructions = FreeApAnalyzer.countOperations(program);
    // 2

    List<String> skus = FreeApAnalyzer.analyseWith(program, new SkusToPrice(), Monoids.list());
    // [LAMP, BULB]
```

Nothing has been priced yet. A program of any length can be read: a basket of ten thousand lines is counted, analysed and run without overflowing the stack.

## One catalogue call for the whole basket {#one-catalogue-call-for-the-whole-basket}

Priced one line at a time, a basket of ten lines makes ten calls to the catalogue service: the N+1 problem, as when a loop of `findById` calls could have been one `IN (...)` query. Read first, it makes one call. `priceInOneCall` lists the SKUs, asks the catalogue for all of them at once, and then runs the program with an interpreter that prices from what came back:

```java
  // The catalogue service prices many SKUs in one call
  interface Catalogue {
    Map<String, BigDecimal> pricesFor(List<String> skus);
  }

  // Prices each instruction from prices already fetched, and records any cancellation
  static final class FetchedPrices extends OrderOpInterpreter<IdKind.Witness> {
    private final Map<String, BigDecimal> prices;
    final List<UUID> cancelled = new ArrayList<>();

    FetchedPrices(Map<String, BigDecimal> prices) {
      this.prices = prices;
    }

    @Override
    protected <A> Kind<IdKind.Witness, A> handlePriceOf(OrderOp.PriceOf<A> op) {
      return ID.widen(Id.of(op.k().apply(prices.get(op.sku()))));
    }

    @Override
    protected <A> Kind<IdKind.Witness, A> handleCancel(OrderOp.Cancel<A> op) {
      cancelled.add(op.orderId());
      return ID.widen(Id.of(op.k().apply(Unit.INSTANCE)));
    }
  }

  // Reads the SKUs first, asks the catalogue once, then runs the program
  static BigDecimal priceInOneCall(
      FreeApPath<OrderOpKind.Witness, BigDecimal> program, Catalogue catalogue) {
    List<String> skus =
        FreeApAnalyzer.analyseWith(program.toFreeAp(), new SkusToPrice(), Monoids.list());
    FetchedPrices fetched = new FetchedPrices(catalogue.pricesFor(skus));
    return ID.narrow(program.foldMap(fetched, Instances.monad(id())).runKind()).value();
  }

```

A stand-in catalogue records each call it is given, so the run can count them:

```java
    // A stand-in catalogue that records each call, and prices from CATALOGUE
    List<List<String>> calls = new ArrayList<>();
    Catalogue catalogue =
        requested -> {
          calls.add(requested);
          return CATALOGUE;
        };

    BigDecimal total = priceInOneCall(basketTotal(BASKET), catalogue);
    // 50.00

    int catalogueCalls = calls.size();
    // 1
```

`foldMap` hands the instructions to the interpreter in the order `zipWith` and `map2` wrote them, here the lamp and then the bulbs.

## Every unknown SKU at once {#every-unknown-sku-at-once}

An interpreter into [`Validated`](validated_monad.md) gives each price, or a failure naming the SKU the catalogue does not know:

```java
  // Gives a price, or a failure naming the SKU the catalogue does not know
  static final class CheckedPrices extends OrderOpInterpreter<ValidatedKind.Witness<List<String>>> {
    @Override
    protected <A> Kind<ValidatedKind.Witness<List<String>>, A> handlePriceOf(
        OrderOp.PriceOf<A> op) {
      BigDecimal price = CATALOGUE.get(op.sku());
      return price == null
          ? VALIDATED.widen(Validated.invalid(List.of("no price for " + op.sku())))
          : VALIDATED.widen(Validated.valid(op.k().apply(price)));
    }

    @Override
    protected <A> Kind<ValidatedKind.Witness<List<String>>, A> handleCancel(OrderOp.Cancel<A> op) {
      return VALIDATED.widen(Validated.valid(op.k().apply(Unit.INSTANCE)));
    }
  }

```

This basket holds two SKUs the catalogue does not know. `Validated`'s `ap` keeps the failures of both sides, so the run reports both:

```java
    List<LineItem> withUnknowns =
        List.of(
            new LineItem("RUG", 1, new BigDecimal("120.00")),
            new LineItem("LAMP", 1, new BigDecimal("38.00")),
            new LineItem("VASE", 2, new BigDecimal("15.00")));

    Validated<List<String>, BigDecimal> checked =
        VALIDATED.narrow(
            basketTotal(withUnknowns)
                .foldMap(new CheckedPrices(), Instances.validated(Semigroups.list()))
                .runKind());

    List<String> problems = checked.getError();
    // [no price for RUG, no price for VASE]
```

The same lines as a `Free` program, interpreted the same way, report only the rug. Each of its steps waits on the one before, and `Validated`'s `flatMap` has no result to hand on after a failure.

## Free or Free Applicative {#free-or-free-applicative}

Ask whether a step needs the result of one before it. If it does, the program is a [Free](free_monad.md) one. If none does, it can be a Free Applicative one, and it gains what this page has shown:

| | `Free` | Free Applicative |
|---|---|---|
| A step can | Wait on the result before it, and choose what comes next | Not see any other step's result |
| Known before it runs | Up to the first step that waits on a result | Every instruction |
| Built with | `via` | `zipWith` and `map` |
| Run in | A `Monad` | An `Applicative`, such as `Validated`, which keeps every failure, or `Const`, which reads the program |

Independent instructions can also run at the same time. Interpreted into `CompletableFuture`, whose `ap` combines futures already running, an interpreter that starts each price with `supplyAsync` prices the lamp and the bulbs at once.
