## Inside a Free program {#inside-a-free-program}

A real program often has both kinds of step: the basket's prices, which are independent, then a decision that needs their total. `Free.Ap` holds a Free Applicative program as one step of a `Free` one:

```java
  // The basket's prices as one step of a Free program; the next step needs the total
  static FreePath<OrderOpKind.Witness, Boolean> cancelIfTotalOver(
      UUID orderId, List<LineItem> lines, BigDecimal limit) {
    FreePath<OrderOpKind.Witness, BigDecimal> total =
        Path.free(new Free.Ap<>(basketTotal(lines).toFreeAp()), ORDER_OPS);
    return total.via(
        sum ->
            sum.compareTo(limit) > 0
                ? Path.free(OrderOpOps.cancel(orderId, Function.identity()), ORDER_OPS)
                    .map(_ -> true)
                : Path.freePure(false, ORDER_OPS));
  }

```

`LIMIT` is 30.00, and the basket comes to 50.00:

```java
    FetchedPrices store = new FetchedPrices(CATALOGUE);

    boolean cancelled =
        ID.narrow(
                cancelIfTotalOver(ORDER, BASKET, LIMIT)
                    .foldMap(store, Instances.monad(id()))
                    .runKind())
            .value();
    // true

    ProgramAnalysis analysis =
        ProgramAnalyser.analyse(cancelIfTotalOver(ORDER, BASKET, LIMIT).toFree());

    // ProgramAnalyser counts the basket's FreeAp as one parallel scope, and does not look inside
    int basketSteps = analysis.parallelScopes();
    // 1
```

`ProgramAnalyser` counts the basket as one step, a parallel scope, and does not look inside it. Read the basket with `FreeApAnalyzer` before you wrap it.

## A program always gives a value {#a-program-always-gives-a-value}

`Path.freeApPure(null, functor)` and `FreeAp.pure(null)` throw at the call, and a `map` or `zipWith` function that returns `null` fails the program when it is interpreted. [A Task Always Gives a Value](vtask_monad.md#a-task-always-gives-a-value) sets out the rule every lazy type follows.

## Creating instances {#creating-instances}

`Path.freeApLift(fa, functor)` lifts one instruction onto a `FreeApPath`, `Path.freeApPure(value, functor)` holds a result with no instruction, and `Path.freeAp(freeAp, functor)` puts a `FreeAp` on a Path. Without a Path, `FreeAp.lift(fa)` and `FreeAp.pure(value)` build a `FreeAp` directly, and need no `Functor`.

## Methods {#methods}

On a `FreeApPath`:

| Method | What it does |
|---|---|
| `map(f)` | Change the result |
| `zipWith(other, f)`, `zipWith3(b, c, f)` | Combine programs whose steps do not see one another's results |
| `foldMap(interpreter, monad)` | Run the program, giving a `GenericPath` in the target monad |
| `foldMapKind(interpreter, applicative)` | Run the program in any `Applicative`, giving a `Kind` |
| `toFreeAp()` | Get the `FreeAp` |

On a `FreeAp`, and with `FreeApAnalyzer`:

| Method | What it does |
|---|---|
| `map2(other, f)`, `ap(ff)` | Combine two programs |
| `foldMap(interpreter, applicative)` | Run the program, giving a `Kind`; `analyse(...)` is the same call, named for an analysis |
| `retract(applicative)` | Run a program whose instructions are already an `Applicative`, such as `IO`, with no interpreter |
| `FreeApAnalyzer.countOperations(program)` | Count the instructions |
| `FreeApAnalyzer.collectOperations(program)`, `groupByType(program, narrow)` | List the instructions, or count them by record type |
| `FreeApAnalyzer.containsOperation(program, test)` | Ask whether any instruction passes a test |
| `FreeApAnalyzer.analyseWith(program, analysis, monoid)` | Run an analysis into `Const`, joining its values with the monoid |

## Working with Kind {#working-with-kind}

`FREE_AP.widen` and `FREE_AP.narrow`, from `FreeApKindHelper`, convert between a `FreeAp` and its `Kind`. Generic code takes it through `FreeApApplicative.instance()`, whose `map2` builds the program as `FreeAp`'s own does:

```java
    FreeApApplicative<OrderOpKind.Witness> applicative = FreeApApplicative.instance();

    // A FreeAp is already its own Kind, so widen and narrow are casts
    Kind<FreeApKind.Witness<OrderOpKind.Witness>, BigDecimal> lamp =
        FREE_AP.widen(priceOf("LAMP").toFreeAp());
    Kind<FreeApKind.Witness<OrderOpKind.Witness>, BigDecimal> bulb =
        FREE_AP.widen(priceOf("BULB").toFreeAp());

    Kind<FreeApKind.Witness<OrderOpKind.Witness>, BigDecimal> both =
        applicative.map2(lamp, bulb, BigDecimal::add);

    BigDecimal lampAndBulb =
        ID.narrow(FREE_AP.narrow(both).foldMap(new FetchedPrices(CATALOGUE), Instances.monad(id())))
            .value();
    // 42.50
```

`FreeApApplicative` has no `flatMap`: a Free Applicative program is an `Applicative` and never a `Monad`, which is what keeps every instruction in view.

## When to use Free Applicative {#when-to-use-free-applicative}

| You need | Use |
|---|---|
| Independent lookups counted, checked or batched before they run | Free Applicative |
| Every failure among independent checks | Free Applicative run in `Validated`, or [Validated](validated_monad.md) directly |
| A step that needs another's result | [Free](free_monad.md), holding a Free Applicative step where the steps are independent |
| Several instruction sets in one program | [EitherF](eitherf.md) |
| Independent work run once, one way | [VTask](vtask_monad.md)'s `Par`, or a direct call |

~~~admonish info title="Hands-On Learning"
Practise building programs in [Tutorial 10: Free Applicative](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/tutorial/coretypes/Tutorial10_FreeApplicative.java) (6 exercises), and reading them in [Tutorial 11: Static Analysis](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/tutorial/coretypes/Tutorial11_StaticAnalysis.java) (10 exercises), from the [Advanced Patterns](../tutorials/coretypes/advanced_journey.md) journey.
~~~

---

**Previous:** [Free](free_monad.md)
**Next:** [EitherF](eitherf.md)

<!-- Source: monads/eitherf.md -->

<!-- description: Join two instruction sets in one Java program with EitherF: an order program that cancels and tells the customer, run by one interpreter per set. -->

# EitherF: Composing Effect Vocabularies

_One program, two instruction sets, and each instruction sent to the interpreter for its own set._

<!-- source: E. M. Forster, Howards End (1910), Chapter XXII; text checked against Project Gutenberg eBook #2946 -->
> _"Only connect! That was the whole of her sermon."_
>
> — E. M. Forster, *Howards End* (Chapter XXII)

Margaret Schlegel's sermon is meant for Henry Wilcox, who keeps the two halves of his life apart. Two instruction sets kept apart cannot share a program either. `EitherF` connects them: one program uses both, and each instruction still reaches the interpreter for its own set.

~~~admonish abstract title="EitherF at a glance"
| | |
|---|---|
| **Models** | One instruction from either of two instruction sets |
| **Replaces** | One interface grown to hold every service's operations, or a dispatcher written by hand |
| **Effect Path** | None: a program over the pair is a [`FreePath`](../glossary/effect-paths.md#freepath) |
| **Instances** | `Functor` |
| **Choose it when** | One program needs instructions from two or more sets, which [`@ComposeEffects`](../effect/effect_handlers.md#composing-effects) joins for you; otherwise [Free](free_monad.md) over one set, or compare every type in [Choosing a Type](supported-types.md#choosing-a-type) |
~~~

~~~admonish info title="What You'll Learn"
- Join two instruction sets so that one program can use both
- Run the program with an interpreter for each set, combined into one
- Read what `@ComposeEffects` generates: an `Inject` for each set and the pair's `Functor`
- Move a program written for one set into the pair without changing it
- Nest a third and a fourth instruction set
~~~

~~~admonish example title="See Example Code"
**The code on this page is [EitherFBook.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/eitherf/EitherFBook.java)**, with [OrderEffects.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/eitherf/OrderEffects.java): the page includes them, so the build compiles and runs them. [EitherFBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/eitherf/EitherFBookTest.java) holds what the page says each method does.
~~~

~~~admonish tip title="Where EitherF fits"
For most code, `EitherF` is an implementation detail. [`@ComposeEffects`](../effect/effect_handlers.md#composing-effects) generates what joins the sets, and the [Payment Processing](../examples/payment_processing.md) example composes four of them. Knowing what that code is made of helps when you read a type error, or join sets by hand.
~~~

---

## Two instruction sets, one program {#two-instruction-sets-one-program}

The order service's [Free](free_monad.md) program prices a line and cancels the order when the line costs too much. Now it must also tell the customer why. Notices are a concern of their own, with their own interpreter: an outbox in a test, a mail service when the service runs. They are a second instruction set beside `OrderOp`:

```java
/**
 * The order service's notices to customers, held as data beside {@link OrderOp}, so that one
 * program can use both instruction sets. {@code @EffectAlgebra} generates the {@code Kind}
 * encoding, the {@code Functor}, the interpreter base class and the {@code Free} constructors.
 */
@NullMarked
@EffectAlgebra
public sealed interface NoticeOp<A> permits NoticeOp.Send {

  /** Maps a function over the result type. */
  <B> NoticeOp<B> mapK(Function<? super A, ? extends B> f);

  /**
   * Sends the customer who placed an order a notice about it.
   *
   * @param orderId the order the notice is about
   * @param text what the notice says
   * @param k continuation from {@link Unit} to {@code A}
   * @param <A> the result type
   */
  record Send<A>(UUID orderId, String text, Function<Unit, A> k) implements NoticeOp<A> {
    @Override
    public <B> NoticeOp<B> mapK(Function<? super A, ? extends B> f) {
      return new Send<>(orderId, text, k.andThen(f));
    }
  }
}
```

A `Free<OrderOpKind.Witness, A>` holds `OrderOp` instructions only, and a program over `NoticeOp` has another type, so `via` cannot chain one onto the other. Adding a `Send` record to `OrderOp` would mix the two concerns, and every `OrderOp` interpreter would have to handle notices. The code on this page uses these imports:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import static org.higherkindedj.example.book.foundations.NoticeOpKindHelper.NOTICE_OP;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.eitherf.EitherFKindHelper.EITHERF;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.id.IdKindHelper.ID;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.id;

import java.math.BigDecimal;
import java.util.ArrayList;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.Map;
import java.util.UUID;
import java.util.function.Function;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.foundations.NoticeOp;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.foundations.NoticeOpFunctor;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.foundations.NoticeOpInterpreter;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.foundations.NoticeOpKind;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.foundations.NoticeOpOps;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.foundations.OrderOp;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.foundations.OrderOpFunctor;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.foundations.OrderOpInterpreter;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.foundations.OrderOpKind;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.foundations.OrderOpOps;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Kind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Natural;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Unit;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.effect.FreePath;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.effect.Path;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.eitherf.EitherF;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.eitherf.EitherFFunctor;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.eitherf.EitherFKind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.eitherf.Interpreters;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.free.Free;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.id.Id;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.id.IdKind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.inject.Inject;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.inject.InjectInstances;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Instances;
```

## An instruction from either set {#an-instruction-from-either-set}

`Either<L, R>` holds a value that is an `L` or an `R`. `EitherF<F, G, A>` holds an instruction that is from the set `F` or the set `G`, each giving an `A`: `Left` wraps an instruction from `F`, and `Right` one from `G`. The `F` in the name says it holds a type constructor's value, as `Kind<F, A>` does. A program over the pair holds instructions from both sets, and running it sends each to the interpreter for its side:

```mermaid
flowchart TB
    accTitle: How one interpreter for the pair dispatches an instruction
    accDescr: An instruction in the pair is an EitherF. A Left holds an OrderOp instruction, which goes to the InMemoryOrders interpreter. A Right holds a NoticeOp instruction, which goes to the Outbox interpreter.
    I["an instruction in the pair"]:::domain --> D{"Left or Right?"}:::decision
    D -->|Left| O["an OrderOp instruction"]:::domain
    D -->|Right| N["a NoticeOp instruction"]:::domain
    O --> IO["InMemoryOrders"]:::step
    N --> OB["Outbox"]:::step
    classDef domain fill:#a6d189,stroke:#40a02b,color:#232634
    classDef step fill:#8caaee,stroke:#1e66f5,color:#232634
    classDef decision fill:#e5c890,stroke:#df8e1d,color:#232634
```

## Joining the sets {#joining-the-sets}

`@ComposeEffects` on a record that names the sets generates what the pair needs:

```java
/**
 * The order service's two instruction sets as one. {@code @ComposeEffects} generates {@code
 * OrderEffectsSupport}, with an {@code Inject} for each set, the pair's {@code Functor} and a
 * {@code BoundSet} to hold each set's constructors.
 */
@NullMarked
@ComposeEffects
public record OrderEffects(Class<OrderOp<?>> orders, Class<NoticeOp<?>> notices) {}
```

The generated `OrderEffectsSupport`, in the record's own package, wires the pair. Its `functor` gives the pair's `Functor`, which `Free` needs, and its `injectOrders` and `injectNotices` say which side each set takes. Each set's own `@EffectAlgebra` generates a `boundTo`, which gives that set's constructors bound to its side. The pair's witness is `EitherFKind.Witness<OrderOpKind.Witness, NoticeOpKind.Witness>`:

```java
  // The pair's Functor maps an instruction from either set
  static final EitherFFunctor<OrderOpKind.Witness, NoticeOpKind.Witness> ORDERS_AND_NOTICES =
      OrderEffectsSupport.functor(OrderOpFunctor.instance(), NoticeOpFunctor.instance());

  // Each set's generated constructors, bound to its side of the pair
  static final OrderOpOps.Bound<EitherFKind.Witness<OrderOpKind.Witness, NoticeOpKind.Witness>>
      ORDERS = OrderOpOps.boundTo(OrderEffectsSupport.injectOrders(), ORDERS_AND_NOTICES);
  static final NoticeOpOps.Bound<EitherFKind.Witness<OrderOpKind.Witness, NoticeOpKind.Witness>>
      NOTICES = NoticeOpOps.boundTo(OrderEffectsSupport.injectNotices(), ORDERS_AND_NOTICES);

```

`ORDERS.priceOf` and `NOTICES.send` give `Free` programs over the pair, so `via` chains them. The program cancels the order and tells the customer, or does neither:

```java
  // Cancels the order if the line costs more than the limit, and tells the customer why
  static FreePath<EitherFKind.Witness<OrderOpKind.Witness, NoticeOpKind.Witness>, Boolean>
      cancelAndTell(UUID orderId, String sku, BigDecimal limit) {
    return Path.free(ORDERS.priceOf(sku, Function.identity()), ORDERS_AND_NOTICES)
        .via(
            price ->
                price.compareTo(limit) <= 0
                    ? Path.freePure(false, ORDERS_AND_NOTICES)
                    : Path.free(ORDERS.cancel(orderId, Function.identity()), ORDERS_AND_NOTICES)
                        .then(
                            () ->
                                Path.free(
                                    NOTICES.send(
                                        orderId,
                                        "Cancelled: " + sku + " costs " + price,
                                        Function.identity()),
                                    ORDERS_AND_NOTICES))
                        .map(_ -> true));
  }

```

## One interpreter for the pair {#one-interpreter-for-the-pair}

Each set keeps an interpreter of its own, written as [on the Free page](free_monad.md#one-program-two-interpreters). `InMemoryOrders` prices from a catalogue that holds a `LAMP` at 40.00, and records each cancellation. `Outbox` keeps each notice instead of sending it:

```java
  // Runs each order instruction against a catalogue and a list of cancelled orders
  static final class InMemoryOrders extends OrderOpInterpreter<IdKind.Witness> {
    final List<UUID> cancelled = new ArrayList<>();

    @Override
    protected <A> Kind<IdKind.Witness, A> handlePriceOf(OrderOp.PriceOf<A> op) {
      return ID.widen(Id.of(op.k().apply(CATALOGUE.get(op.sku()))));
    }

    @Override
    protected <A> Kind<IdKind.Witness, A> handleCancel(OrderOp.Cancel<A> op) {
      cancelled.add(op.orderId());
      return ID.widen(Id.of(op.k().apply(Unit.INSTANCE)));
    }
  }

  // Keeps each notice in an outbox instead of sending it
  static final class Outbox extends NoticeOpInterpreter<IdKind.Witness> {
    final List<String> sent = new ArrayList<>();

    @Override
    protected <A> Kind<IdKind.Witness, A> handleSend(NoticeOp.Send<A> op) {
      sent.add(op.text());
      return ID.widen(Id.of(op.k().apply(Unit.INSTANCE)));
    }
  }

```

`Interpreters.combine` joins them into one interpreter for the pair, which hands a `Left` to the first and a `Right` to the second. `LIMIT` is 30.00:

```java
    InMemoryOrders orders = new InMemoryOrders();
    Outbox outbox = new Outbox();

    // One interpreter for the pair: each instruction goes to the one for its set
    Natural<EitherFKind.Witness<OrderOpKind.Witness, NoticeOpKind.Witness>, IdKind.Witness> both =
        Interpreters.combine(orders, outbox);

    boolean cancelled =
        ID.narrow(
                cancelAndTell(ORDER, "LAMP", LIMIT).foldMap(both, Instances.monad(id())).runKind())
            .value();
    // true

    List<String> notices = outbox.sent;
    // [Cancelled: LAMP costs 40.00]
```

Within the limit, as for a `BULB`, the program cancels nothing and sends nothing.

## What the generated code is {#what-the-generated-code-is}

An `Inject<F, G>` puts an instruction from the set `F` into the larger set `G`. `OrderEffectsSupport`'s two are the library's `injectLeft` and `injectRight`. Written by hand, they draw the [HKJ checker](../tooling/compile_checks.md)'s `migration-nudge` warning, which points you to `@ComposeEffects`:

```java
  // What OrderEffectsSupport's two Injects are: orders go on the left, notices on the right
  @SuppressWarnings("migration-nudge") // shows what @ComposeEffects generates
  static final Inject<
          OrderOpKind.Witness, EitherFKind.Witness<OrderOpKind.Witness, NoticeOpKind.Witness>>
      INTO_ORDERS = InjectInstances.injectLeft();

  @SuppressWarnings("migration-nudge") // shows what @ComposeEffects generates
  static final Inject<
          NoticeOpKind.Witness, EitherFKind.Witness<OrderOpKind.Witness, NoticeOpKind.Witness>>
      INTO_NOTICES = InjectInstances.injectRight();

```

An injected instruction is an `EitherF`, as [Working with Kind](#working-with-kind) shows.

## Three sets and more {#three-sets-and-more}

A pair holds two sets. A third nests on the right, and so does a fourth, which is as many as `@ComposeEffects` and `Interpreters.combine` take:

<pre class="hkj-ascii-diagram" role="img" aria-label="How EitherF nests as sets are added. Two sets are EitherF of F and G. Three are EitherF of F and an EitherF of G and H. Four nest once more on the right.">
Two sets:    EitherF&lt;F, G&gt;
Three sets:  EitherF&lt;F, EitherF&lt;G, H&gt;&gt;
Four sets:   EitherF&lt;F, EitherF&lt;G, EitherF&lt;H, I&gt;&gt;&gt;
</pre>

`InjectInstances.injectRightThen(inner)` reaches into the nesting: `injectRightThen(injectLeft())` puts an instruction from `G` into the three-set nesting, as a `Right` holding a `Left`. `@ComposeEffects` writes each of these for you.

## Moving a program into the pair {#moving-a-program-into-the-pair}

A program written for `OrderOp` alone, before notices existed, joins the pair with `Free.translate`, which passes each instruction through a [natural transformation](../functional/natural_transformation.md). An `Inject`'s method is one:

```java
    // Written for OrderOp alone, before notices existed
    FreePath<OrderOpKind.Witness, BigDecimal> lampAndBulb =
        Path.free(OrderOpOps.priceOf("LAMP", Function.identity()), OrderOpFunctor.instance())
            .zipWith(
                Path.free(
                    OrderOpOps.priceOf("BULB", Function.identity()), OrderOpFunctor.instance()),
                BigDecimal::add);

    // Each instruction moves to the left of the pair; the program is otherwise unchanged
    Free<EitherFKind.Witness<OrderOpKind.Witness, NoticeOpKind.Witness>, BigDecimal> joined =
        Free.translate(lampAndBulb.toFree(), INTO_ORDERS::inject, ORDERS_AND_NOTICES);

    BigDecimal total =
        ID.narrow(
                joined.foldMap(
                    Interpreters.combine(new InMemoryOrders(), new Outbox()),
                    Instances.monad(id())))
            .value();
    // 42.50
```

Each bound constructor works the same way: `ORDERS.priceOf` builds the `OrderOp` program and translates it into the pair.

## An instruction is never null {#an-instruction-is-never-null}

`EitherF.left(null)` and `EitherF.right(null)` throw at the call, so an instruction in the pair always holds one from a set. Its result follows the rule of the `Free` program that holds it: [a program always gives a value](free_monad.md#a-program-always-gives-a-value).

## Creating instances {#creating-instances}

`@ComposeEffects` generates the `Inject`s and the `Functor`, and each set's generated `boundTo` gives its constructors in the pair. By hand, `InjectInstances.injectLeft()`, `injectRight()` and `injectRightThen(inner)` give the `Inject`s, `EitherFFunctor.of(left, right)` the `Functor`, and `EitherF.left(fa)` and `EitherF.right(ga)` an instruction.

## Methods {#methods}

| Method | What it does |
|---|---|
| `EitherF.left(fa)`, `EitherF.right(ga)` | Hold an instruction from the left set, or from the right one |
| `eitherF.fold(onLeft, onRight)` | Act on the instruction from whichever side holds it |
| `inject.inject(fa)` | Put an instruction from one set into a larger one |
| `Interpreters.combine(f, g)`, up to four | Join one interpreter per set into one for the nesting |
| `EitherFFunctor.of(left, right)` | The pair's `Functor`, built from each set's |
| `Free.translate(program, natural, functor)` | Move a program to another instruction set |
| `XOps.boundTo(inject, functor)` | A set's generated constructors, building programs in the larger set |

## Working with Kind {#working-with-kind}

An `EitherF` is already its own `Kind`, so `EITHERF.widen` and `EITHERF.narrow`, from `EitherFKindHelper`, are casts. An injected instruction narrows to one, and `fold` says which side holds it:

```java
    // An instruction in the pair is an EitherF: Left holds an order instruction, Right a notice
    EitherF<OrderOpKind.Witness, NoticeOpKind.Witness, Unit> held =
        EITHERF.narrow(
            INTO_NOTICES.inject(
                NOTICE_OP.widen(new NoticeOp.Send<>(ORDER, "Shipped", Function.identity()))));

    // fold acts on whichever side holds the instruction
    boolean aNotice = held.fold(_ -> false, _ -> true);
    // true
```

Its only instance is `EitherFFunctor`, which maps through the instruction on whichever side holds it, using that set's `Functor`. That is all a `Free` program needs of its instruction set: `EitherF` has no `Applicative` or `Monad`, because the program is the monad.

## When to use `EitherF` {#when-to-use-eitherf}

| You need | Use |
|---|---|
| One program over two or more instruction sets | `@ComposeEffects`, which generates the `EitherF` nesting |
| To read a type error in that program, or to join sets by hand | This page's `Inject`, `EitherF` and `Interpreters.combine` |
| One instruction set | [Free](free_monad.md) on its own |
| Two services called directly, run one way | A direct call to each |

~~~admonish info title="Hands-On Learning"
Practise joining instruction sets in [Tutorial 04: Combining Effects with EitherF](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/tutorial/effecthandlers/Tutorial04_CombiningEffects.java) (3 exercises), from the [Effect Handlers](../tutorials/effecthandlers/ch_intro.md) journey.
~~~

**Benchmarks:** `FreeEffectBenchmark` measures what dispatching through `EitherF` and `Interpreters.combine` costs beside a program over one instruction set. [Benchmarks & Performance](../benchmarks.md) says how to run it.

---

**Previous:** [Free Applicative](free_applicative.md)
**Next:** [Coyoneda](coyoneda.md)

<!-- Source: monads/coyoneda.md -->

<!-- description: Hold every map in Java with Coyoneda: apply three maps to an order's lines in one walk, and give a warehouse's instruction set the Functor it lacks. -->

# Coyoneda: Maps Held for One Pass

_Hold every `map` for one pass, and give an instruction set the `Functor` it lacks._

<!-- source: Mark Twain, "The Late Benjamin Franklin" (written about 1870), collected in Sketches New and Old (1875); the maxim heads the sketch, signed "B. F."; text checked against Project Gutenberg eBook #3189 -->
> _"Never put off till to-morrow what you can do day after to-morrow just as well."_
>
> — Mark Twain, *The Late Benjamin Franklin*

Twain signs the maxim with Franklin's initials, in a sketch that mocks Franklin's maxims. A Coyoneda takes it as advice: every `map` is written down and put off. Only when you lower it is the work done, all of it in one pass.

~~~admonish abstract title="Coyoneda at a glance"
| | |
|---|---|
| **Models** | A value of `F` with the `map` calls made on it held back, to be applied in one pass |
| **Replaces** | A `Functor` written by hand for an instruction set, or a chain of `map` calls that each walk the structure |
| **Effect Path** | None |
| **Instances** | `Functor` |
| **Choose it when** | An instruction set has no `Functor` of its own; otherwise use the `Functor` that [`@EffectAlgebra`](../effect/effect_handlers.md#defining-effects) generates, or compare every type in [Choosing a Type](supported-types.md#choosing-a-type) |
~~~

~~~admonish info title="What You'll Learn"
- Hold three maps on a list, and apply them in one walk
- Give an instruction set with no `Functor` one, so that a `Free` program can hold it
- Run that program with `Coyoneda.interpreter`, or one held value with `foldMap`
- Choose between Coyoneda and a `Functor` of the set's own, such as the one `@EffectAlgebra` generates
~~~

~~~admonish example title="See Example Code"
**The code on this page is [CoyonedaBook.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/coyoneda/CoyonedaBook.java)**: the page includes it, so the build compiles and runs it. [CoyonedaBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/coyoneda/CoyonedaBookTest.java) holds what the page says each method does.
~~~

~~~admonish tip title="Where Coyoneda fits"
An instruction set from [`@EffectAlgebra`](../effect/effect_handlers.md#defining-effects) gets a generated `Functor`, built on its records' `mapK`, which extends each record's continuation: the function from the instruction's result to what comes next. So it never needs Coyoneda. Coyoneda is for a set you write yourself whose records carry no continuation, and for holding many maps on a structure that is costly to walk.
~~~

---

## Three maps, one walk {#maps-held-for-one-pass}

A Java `Stream` applies each element's maps in one pass. `List`'s `map`, like the `map` of most types with a `Functor`, does not. It walks the list and builds a new one, so three maps in a row make three walks and two lists that are thrown away. Coyoneda gives any such type the stream's single pass. It holds the list, and each `map` adds its function to the ones it holds without applying anything. `lower` hands them all to the list's `Functor` as one `map`, which passes each element through the functions in turn:

```mermaid
flowchart TB
    accTitle: How a Coyoneda holds three maps and applies them in one pass
    accDescr: lift wraps the basket's list of lines in a Coyoneda that holds no functions. Three calls to map add three functions to it, and apply none. lower hands the three to the List Functor as one map, which walks the list once and gives the list of prices.
    L["the basket's lines"]:::domain -->|lift| C0["Coyoneda: the list, no functions"]:::domain
    C0 -->|"map, map, map"| C3["Coyoneda: the list, three functions held"]:::domain
    C3 -->|"lower: one walk"| R["the new prices"]:::domain
    classDef domain fill:#a6d189,stroke:#40a02b,color:#232634
```

The code on this page uses these imports:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.coyoneda.CoyonedaKindHelper.COYONEDA;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.id.IdKindHelper.ID;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.list.ListKindHelper.LIST;

import java.math.BigDecimal;
import java.math.RoundingMode;
import java.util.HashMap;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.Map;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.LineItem;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Kind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Natural;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.TypeArity;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.WitnessArity;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.coyoneda.Coyoneda;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.coyoneda.CoyonedaFunctor;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.coyoneda.CoyonedaKind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.effect.FreePath;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.effect.Path;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.id.Id;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.id.IdKind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.id.IdMonad;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.list.ListKind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.list.ListMonad;
```

The list here stands in for a structure that is costly to walk, such as a large tree or a page of results from a remote store. The basket holds a lamp at 40.00 and four bulbs at 2.50 each, and `VAT` is 1.20, so each line's price with VAT is 48.00 for the lamp and 3.00 for a bulb:

```java
    // Three maps, held as one: nothing is applied yet
    Coyoneda<ListKind.Witness, BigDecimal> withVat =
        Coyoneda.lift(LIST.widen(BASKET))
            .map(LineItem::price)
            .map(price -> price.multiply(VAT))
            .map(price -> price.setScale(2, RoundingMode.HALF_EVEN));

    // Lowering applies all three to each line, in one walk over the list, with List's Functor
    // (ListMonad.INSTANCE is List's Monad, and so its Functor too)
    List<BigDecimal> prices = LIST.narrow(withVat.lower(ListMonad.INSTANCE));
    // [48.00, 3.00]
```

Through `List`'s own `map`, the same three functions walk the list three times; through Coyoneda, once. The held functions are applied in a loop, so a chain of a hundred thousand maps is lowered without overflowing the stack.

## An instruction set with no Functor {#an-instruction-set-with-no-functor}

A [Free](free_monad.md) program needs a `Functor` for its instructions, to put the rest of the program after each one. `OrderOp`'s records carry a continuation `k`, so `@EffectAlgebra` builds that `Functor` from `mapK`. The warehouse's instruction set cannot have one, because each record fixes its own result type and carries nothing for a `map` to extend:

```java
  // The warehouse's instructions. Each record fixes its own result type and carries no
  // continuation, so there is nothing a map could change: StockOp can have no Functor.
  sealed interface StockOp<A> extends Kind<StockOp.Witness, A> {
    final class Witness implements WitnessArity<TypeArity.Unary> {
      private Witness() {}
    }

    // How many of a SKU the warehouse holds
    record Available(String sku) implements StockOp<Integer> {}

    // Sets a quantity aside for an order, if the warehouse holds that many
    record Reserve(String sku, int quantity) implements StockOp<Boolean> {}
  }

```

Lifted into a Coyoneda, an instruction gets the `Functor` it lacks: `CoyonedaFunctor` holds each map instead of applying it. A `FreePath` over `CoyonedaKind.Witness<StockOp.Witness>` then holds the warehouse's instructions:

```java
  // Coyoneda gives any instruction set a Functor, so Free can hold StockOp's instructions
  static final CoyonedaFunctor<StockOp.Witness> STOCK = CoyonedaFunctor.instance();

  static <A> FreePath<CoyonedaKind.Witness<StockOp.Witness>, A> step(StockOp<A> op) {
    return Path.freeLift(COYONEDA.lift(op), STOCK);
  }

  // Reserves a line if the warehouse holds enough of its SKU: the second step needs the count
  static FreePath<CoyonedaKind.Witness<StockOp.Witness>, Boolean> reserveLine(LineItem line) {
    return step(new StockOp.Available(line.sku()))
        .via(
            held ->
                held >= line.quantity()
                    ? step(new StockOp.Reserve(line.sku(), line.quantity()))
                    : Path.freePure(false, STOCK));
  }

```

## Running a program over Coyoneda {#running-a-program-over-coyoneda}

The interpreter is written for `StockOp` alone, as a [`Natural`](../functional/natural_transformation.md) with a case for each record. Its warehouse holds three lamps and two bulbs:

```java
  // Runs each instruction against stock held in memory
  static final class Warehouse implements Natural<StockOp.Witness, IdKind.Witness> {
    final Map<String, Integer> levels = new HashMap<>(Map.of("LAMP", 3, "BULB", 2));

    @Override
    @SuppressWarnings("unchecked") // each record fixes A; the switch cannot say so
    public <A> Kind<IdKind.Witness, A> apply(Kind<StockOp.Witness, A> fa) {
      Object result =
          switch ((StockOp<A>) fa) {
            case StockOp.Available available -> levels.getOrDefault(available.sku(), 0);
            case StockOp.Reserve reserve -> {
              int held = levels.getOrDefault(reserve.sku(), 0);
              if (held < reserve.quantity()) {
                yield false;
              }
              levels.put(reserve.sku(), held - reserve.quantity());
              yield true;
            }
          };
      return (Kind<IdKind.Witness, A>) ID.widen(Id.of(result));
    }
  }

```

`Coyoneda.interpreter(warehouse, functor)` runs each instruction with it, and then applies the maps the Coyoneda held, with `Id`'s `Functor`:

```java
    Warehouse warehouse = new Warehouse();
    IdMonad id = IdMonad.instance();

    // Coyoneda.interpreter runs each instruction with the warehouse, then applies its held maps
    boolean lampReserved =
        ID.narrow(
                reserveLine(BASKET.get(0))
                    .foldMap(Coyoneda.interpreter(warehouse, id), id)
                    .runKind())
            .value();
    // true

    boolean bulbsReserved =
        ID.narrow(
                reserveLine(BASKET.get(1))
                    .foldMap(Coyoneda.interpreter(warehouse, id), id)
                    .runKind())
            .value();
    // false
```

The basket's second line wants four bulbs and the warehouse holds two, so nothing is set aside, and the warehouse's count is unchanged. One held value runs through an interpreter the same way, with `foldMap`:

```java
    // One held value runs through an interpreter with foldMap, and needs no Functor for StockOp
    Coyoneda<StockOp.Witness, Boolean> enoughForTwo =
        Coyoneda.lift(new StockOp.Available("LAMP")).map(count -> count >= 2);

    boolean enough = ID.narrow(enoughForTwo.foldMap(new Warehouse(), id)).value();
    // true
```

## Why it is called Coyoneda {#the-yoneda-lemma}

Coyoneda is named for the co-Yoneda lemma: for a `Functor` `F`, a `Coyoneda<F, A>` and a `Kind<F, A>` hold the same information. `lift` and `lower` turn one into the other, and lowering a lifted list gives back the list. So holding the maps loses nothing: it only decides when they run.

## A held value is never null {#a-held-value-is-never-null}

`Coyoneda.lift(null)` and `Coyoneda.apply(null, f)` throw at the call. A held function runs only when you lower or interpret, through the target's own `map`, so a null it returns meets that type's rule, which [Values, never null](../hkts/core-concepts.md#values-never-null) sets out. [`Id`](identity.md), for one, keeps the null as its value.

## Creating instances {#creating-instances}

`Coyoneda.lift(fa)` holds a value with no functions, and `Coyoneda.apply(fx, f)` holds it with one. `COYONEDA.lift(fa)`, from `CoyonedaKindHelper`, gives the same, already widened to a `Kind` for a `Free` program.

## Methods {#methods}

| Method | What it does |
|---|---|
| `coyoneda.map(f)` | Hold one more function, applied after the others |
| `lower(functor)` | Apply the held functions with `F`'s own `Functor`, in one `map` |
| `foldMap(natural, functor)` | Run the held value through an interpreter into `G`, then apply the held functions with `G`'s `Functor` |
| `Coyoneda.interpreter(natural, functor)` | An interpreter for a program whose instructions are Coyonedas |
| `underlying()` | The value held, before any function |

## Working with Kind {#working-with-kind}

`COYONEDA.widen` and `COYONEDA.narrow`, from `CoyonedaKindHelper`, convert between a `Coyoneda` and its `Kind`. Its one instance, `CoyonedaFunctor`, works for any `F`, because its `map` only holds the function:

```java
    CoyonedaFunctor<ListKind.Witness> functor = CoyonedaFunctor.instance();

    // A Coyoneda is already its own Kind, so widen and narrow are casts
    Kind<CoyonedaKind.Witness<ListKind.Witness>, Integer> quantities =
        COYONEDA.widen(Coyoneda.lift(LIST.widen(BASKET)).map(LineItem::quantity));
    Kind<CoyonedaKind.Witness<ListKind.Witness>, Integer> doubled =
        functor.map(quantity -> quantity * 2, quantities);

    List<Integer> lowered = LIST.narrow(COYONEDA.narrow(doubled).lower(ListMonad.INSTANCE));
    // [2, 8]
```

Coyoneda has no `Applicative` or `Monad`: combining two held values needs `F`'s own.

## When to use `Coyoneda` {#when-to-use-coyoneda}

| You need | Use |
|---|---|
| A `Free` program over an instruction set whose records carry no continuation | Coyoneda |
| A `Free` program over an `@EffectAlgebra` instruction set | Its generated `Functor`, with no Coyoneda |
| Many maps on a structure that is costly to walk | Coyoneda, lowered once |
| A few maps on a `List` or an `Optional` | Their own `map` |
| A pipeline over a Java `Stream` | The stream, which already applies each element's maps in one pass |

~~~admonish info title="Hands-On Learning"
Practise lifting, mapping and lowering in [Tutorial 09: Coyoneda](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/tutorial/coretypes/Tutorial09_Coyoneda.java) (5 exercises), from the [Advanced Patterns](../tutorials/coretypes/advanced_journey.md) journey.
~~~

**Next:** [Const](const_type.md)

<!-- description: Read with code written to build, in Java with Const: one traversal of an order's lines rebuilds them in Id and adds them up in Const. -->

# Const: The Value That Map Leaves Alone

_A `Const<C, A>` holds a `C` and no `A`, so code written to build an `A` reads a `C` instead._

<!-- source: Verbal Kint, The Usual Suspects (1995), directed by Bryan Singer, screenplay by Christopher McQuarrie; wording checked against Wikiquote's The Usual Suspects page -->
> _"The greatest trick the Devil ever pulled was convincing the world he didn't exist."_
>
> — Verbal Kint, *The Usual Suspects*

Verbal Kint is talking about Keyser Söze, a name that runs through every story he tells. The `A` in `Const<C, A>` is that kind of name: it is in every signature, and no object holds it.

~~~admonish abstract title="Const at a glance"
| | |
|---|---|
| **Models** | A value of type `C`, carried through code that expects an `A` it never holds |
| **Replaces** | A second loop written beside a traversal, to sum, count or collect what it visits |
| **Effect Path** | None |
| **Instances** | `Functor`, `Applicative`, `Bifunctor` |
| **Choose it when** | Code written for any `Applicative`, such as a traversal or a [Free Applicative](free_applicative.md) program, should read rather than build; otherwise [Id](identity.md), which rebuilds, or compare every type in [Choosing a Type](supported-types.md#choosing-a-type) |
~~~

~~~admonish info title="What You'll Learn"
- Hold a value whose second type is only a type, which every map leaves alone
- Run one traversal two ways: in `Id` to rebuild an order's lines, and in `Const` to add them up
- Explain why `Const`'s `Applicative` needs a `Monoid`, and what it combines
- Recognise `Const` behind a traversal's `foldMap`, a lens's read and a Free Applicative program's analysis
~~~

~~~admonish example title="See Example Code"
**The code on this page is [ConstBook.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/main/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/constant/ConstBook.java)**: the page includes it, so the build compiles and runs it. [ConstBookTest.java](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/example/book/foundations/constant/ConstBookTest.java) holds what the page says each method does.
~~~

~~~admonish tip title="Where Const fits"
You rarely build a `Const` yourself. A traversal's `foldMap`, `getAll` and `exists` run the traversal in a `Const` of their own, and `FreeApAnalyzer.analyseWith` runs a program in this one. This page shows the idea they share, for when you write code over any `Applicative` and want it to read.
~~~

---

## A value, and a type that holds nothing {#a-value-and-a-type-that-holds-nothing}

A `Const<C, A>` holds one value, of type `C`. Its `A` is a [phantom type](../glossary/type-system.md#phantom-type): it appears in the type and is stored nowhere. Mapping the `A` therefore has nothing to change. The code on this page uses these imports:

<!-- verify -->
```java
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.constant.ConstKindHelper.CONST;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.id.IdKindHelper.ID;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Witnesses.id;
import static org.higherkindedj.hkt.list.ListKindHelper.LIST;

import java.math.BigDecimal;
import java.math.RoundingMode;
import java.util.List;
import java.util.function.Function;
import org.higherkindedj.example.book.optics.cast.LineItem;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Applicative;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Kind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Kind2;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.Monoids;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.TypeArity;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.WitnessArity;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.constant.Const;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.constant.ConstApplicative;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.constant.ConstBifunctor;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.constant.ConstKind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.constant.ConstKind2;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.id.Id;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.instances.Instances;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.list.ListKind;
import org.higherkindedj.hkt.list.ListTraverse;
```

```java
    // A Const<BigDecimal, LineItem> holds a BigDecimal, and no LineItem at all
    Const<BigDecimal, LineItem> total = new Const<>(new BigDecimal("50.00"));

    // Mapping the LineItem changes only the type: there is no LineItem to map
    Const<BigDecimal, String> relabelled = total.mapSecond(LineItem::sku);

    BigDecimal unchanged = relabelled.value();
    // 50.00
```

`mapSecond` never calls its function, and the `Functor`'s `map` leaves the value as it is too.

## One traversal, two jobs {#one-traversal-two-jobs}

[Foldable and Traverse](../functional/foldable_and_traverse.md) wrote a traversal once and ran it with `Validated` and with `Either`. `eachLine` is written the same way, for any `Applicative`: it visits every line, and rebuilds the list inside `F`:

```java
  // Written once, for any Applicative: visits every line, and rebuilds the list in F
  static <F extends WitnessArity<TypeArity.Unary>>
      Kind<F, Kind<ListKind.Witness, LineItem>> eachLine(
          Applicative<F> applicative,
          Function<LineItem, Kind<F, LineItem>> visit,
          List<LineItem> lines) {
    return ListTraverse.INSTANCE.traverse(applicative, visit, LIST.widen(lines));
  }

```

Run in [`Id`](identity.md), it rebuilds the lines. The basket holds a lamp at 40.00 and four bulbs at 2.50 each, and `VAT` is 1.20:

```java
    // Run in Id, the traversal rebuilds the list: every line gets its price with VAT
    Kind<ListKind.Witness, LineItem> repriced =
        ID.narrow(
                eachLine(
                    Instances.applicative(id()), line -> ID.widen(Id.of(withVat(line))), BASKET))
            .value();

    List<BigDecimal> prices = LIST.narrow(repriced).stream().map(LineItem::price).toList();
    // [48.00, 3.00]
```

Run in `Const`, the same traversal builds nothing, as a `reduce` over the same loop would. Each visit gives a `Const` holding the line's total before VAT, and the `Applicative` adds the totals with `Monoids.bigDecimalAddition()`, a [Monoid](../functional/semigroup_and_monoid.md) that sums money exactly: 40.00 + 4 × 2.50. The list it would have rebuilt is only a type:

```java
    // Run in Const, the same traversal builds nothing: the monoid adds the totals before VAT
    ConstApplicative<BigDecimal> summing = new ConstApplicative<>(Monoids.bigDecimalAddition());

    Kind<ConstKind.Witness<BigDecimal>, Kind<ListKind.Witness, LineItem>> visited =
        eachLine(summing, line -> CONST.widen(new Const<>(lineTotal(line))), BASKET);

    BigDecimal basketTotal = CONST.narrow(visited).value();
    // 50.00
```

## Why the Applicative needs a Monoid {#why-the-applicative-needs-a-monoid}

An `Applicative`'s `of` must give a value with nothing in it, and its `ap` must combine two. A `Const` has only its `C` to do that with, so `ConstApplicative` takes a `Monoid<C>`. `of` gives the monoid's empty value, so an empty basket comes to zero. `ap` combines the two sides' values, the left one first, so a list monoid collects what each line gives in the order of the lines. There is no `flatMap`: a next step would need the `A`, and there is none.

## Const behind folds, reads and analyses {#const-behind-folds-reads-and-analyses}

The library uses the same idea in two places:

- A [traversal's `foldMap`](../optics/folds.md#step-4-aggregation-with-foldmap-and-monoids), and the `getAll` and `exists` built on it, run the traversal in a constant functor. A Focus path's `foldMap` uses this `Const`; a plain `Traversal`'s fold uses the optics module's own copy of it, which behaves the same.
- [Free Applicative](free_applicative.md#reading-a-program-before-it-runs) analyses a program the same way, with an interpreter that gives each instruction's SKU in a `Const`.

The idea also explains a lens. A lens written once for any `Functor`, in the van Laarhoven style, reads its focus when it runs in `Const`, and sets it when it runs in `Id`. A lens's `modifyF` run in `Const` gives the focus and rebuilds nothing.

## A Const always holds a value {#a-const-always-holds-a-value}

`new Const<>(null)` throws `NullPointerException`, and so do `mapFirst` and `bimap` when their function returns `null`. A traversal's fold therefore fails at the element whose function returned `null`, rather than passing the `null` to its monoid. [Values, never null](../hkts/core-concepts.md#values-never-null) sets out the rule.

## Creating instances {#creating-instances}

`new Const<>(value)` holds a value, and an applicative's `of(anything)` gives one holding the monoid's empty value. `new ConstApplicative<>(monoid)` builds the `Applicative` for a `Monoid<C>`, since `Const` has no `Instances` entry: its instance depends on the monoid you choose.

## Methods {#methods}

| Method | What it does |
|---|---|
| `constant.value()` | Get the value held |
| `mapFirst(f)` | Change the value |
| `mapSecond(g)` | Change only the phantom type; `g` is never called |
| `bimap(f, g)` | Both at once; only `f` touches the value |
| `new ConstApplicative<>(monoid)` | The `Applicative` that combines values with the monoid |
| `ConstBifunctor.INSTANCE` | The `Bifunctor`, over `ConstKind2` |

## Working with Kind {#working-with-kind}

A `Const` is already its own `Kind` twice over. As a `Kind<ConstKind.Witness<C>, A>`, with the value's type fixed, it takes `ConstApplicative`; `CONST.widen` and `CONST.narrow` are casts. As a `Kind2<ConstKind2.Witness, C, A>` it takes `ConstBifunctor`, through `CONST.widen2` and `CONST.narrow2`:

```java
    ConstBifunctor bifunctor = ConstBifunctor.INSTANCE;

    // first changes the value; second changes only the type
    Kind2<ConstKind2.Witness, BigDecimal, LineItem> held =
        CONST.widen2(new Const<>(new BigDecimal("50.00")));
    Kind2<ConstKind2.Witness, BigDecimal, String> rounded =
        bifunctor.second(
            LineItem::sku, bifunctor.first(sum -> sum.setScale(0, RoundingMode.HALF_EVEN), held));

    BigDecimal whole = CONST.narrow2(rounded).value();
    // 50
```

## When to use `Const` {#when-to-use-const}

| You need | Use |
|---|---|
| To sum, count or collect what code written for any `Applicative` visits | `Const` with the matching monoid |
| To read through a traversal or a Focus path | Its `foldMap`, `getAll` or `exists`, which use `Const` for you |
| To read a Free Applicative program before it runs | `FreeApAnalyzer.analyseWith` |
| Two values that both hold data | A record |
| One value with no effect | [Id](identity.md) |

---

**Previous:** [Coyoneda](coyoneda.md)
**Next:** [Supported Types](supported-types.md)

<!-- Source: monads/supported-types.md -->

<!-- description: Find the Higher-Kinded-J type for the Java you write today, such as Maybe for a method that may return null, and see which type classes each type supports. -->

# Supported Types

_Find the type for the code you write today, and see which type classes each type supports._

## Choosing a Type {#choosing-a-type}

Find the code you write today, or the Java type it already gives you, in the first column. Application code holds the type beside it through the [Effect Path](../effect/ch_intro.md) in the last column, where it has one.

| Your code today | Type | Effect Path |
|---|---|---|
| **Failure** | | |
| A method that throws a checked exception | [Either](either_monad.md) | [`EitherPath`](../effect/path_either.md) |
| Error messages collected in a `List`, or Bean Validation's `Set<ConstraintViolation>` | [Validated](validated_monad.md) | [`ValidationPath`](../effect/path_validation.md) |
| A `try`/`catch` block around a call that throws | [Try](try_monad.md) | [`TryPath`](../effect/path_try.md) |
| A result record holding a value and a list of warnings | [EitherOrBoth](either_or_both_monad.md) | [`EitherOrBothPath`](../effect/path_either_or_both.md) |
| A `List` checked with `isEmpty()` before `getFirst()` | [NonEmptyList](nonemptylist_monad.md) | None: a Path holds it, as [`ValidationPath`](../effect/path_validation.md) holds its errors |
| **Absence** | | |
| A method that may return `null` | [Maybe](maybe_monad.md) | [`MaybePath`](../effect/path_maybe.md) |
| A method you call already returns an `Optional` | [Optional](optional_monad.md) | [`OptionalPath`](../effect/path_optional.md) |
| **Deferred** | | |
| A method that performs its side effect as soon as it is called | [IO](io_monad.md) | [`IOPath`](../effect/path_io.md) |
| A field filled in on first use, behind a `null` check | [Lazy](lazy_monad.md) | `LazyPath`, shown in [On a `LazyPath`](lazy_monad.md#on-a-lazypath) |
| An API gives you a future, or the work is already running on another thread | [CompletableFuture](cf_monad.md) | `CompletableFuturePath`, shown in [On a `CompletableFuturePath`](cf_monad.md#on-a-completablefuturepath) |
| **Virtual threads** | | |
| A `Callable` submitted to an `ExecutorService`, and the `Future` you wait on | [VTask](vtask_monad.md) | [`VTaskPath`](../effect/path_vtask.md), and [`VResultPath`](../effect/path_vresult.md) for a typed error |
| A loop over a paged or blocking source, or a Java `Stream` or `Iterator` built over one | [VStream](vstream.md) | [`VStreamPath`](../effect/path_vstream.md) |
| **Environment and state** | | |
| Settings injected into a service, or passed down through every method that needs them | [Reader](reader_monad.md) | [`ReaderPath`](../effect/advanced_effects.md#readerpath-the-environment-you-inherit) |
| A `ThreadLocal`, set at the start of a request and cleared at the end | [Context](context_scoped.md) | None: `toVTask()` gives a [VTask](vtask_monad.md), and `Path.vtaskPath(task)` a [`VTaskPath`](../effect/path_vtask.md) |
| A field that each method changes, or methods that take a value and return a pair of the result and the new value | [State](state_monad.md) | [`WithStatePath`](../effect/advanced_effects.md#withstatepath-computation-with-memory) |
| A list passed to every method that logs, or a logger whose output a test has to capture | [Writer](writer_monad.md) | [`WriterPath`](../effect/advanced_effects.md#writerpath-accumulating-output) |
| **Collections** | | |
| Nested loops build every combination, and you keep them all in memory | [List](list_monad.md) | [`NonDetPath`](../glossary/effect-paths.md#nondetpath) for every combination, or [`ListPath`](../glossary/effect-paths.md#listpath), whose `zipWith` pairs by position |
| A method you call already returns a `Stream` | [Stream](stream_monad.md) | [`StreamPath`](../glossary/effect-paths.md#streampath), which reads a finite stream into memory so it can run again, or [takes a supplier](stream_monad.md#on-a-streampath) for an endless one |
| **Programs as data** | | |
| A plain value, where code written for any monad needs one | [Identity](identity.md) | [`IdPath`](../glossary/effect-paths.md#idpath), a step that cannot fail |
| A recursive method that overflows the stack, or a loop that keeps its own stack of work | [Trampoline](trampoline_monad.md) | [`TrampolinePath`](../glossary/effect-paths.md#trampolinepath) |
| Calls to an injected service, made directly from business code | [Free](free_monad.md) | [`FreePath`](../glossary/effect-paths.md#freepath) |
| Independent calls made one at a time, when you want to count, check or batch them first | [Free Applicative](free_applicative.md) | [`FreeApPath`](../glossary/effect-paths.md#freeappath) |
| One interface grown to hold every service's operations, or a dispatcher written by hand | [EitherF](eitherf.md) | None: a program over the pair is a [`FreePath`](../glossary/effect-paths.md#freepath) |
| A `Functor` written by hand for an instruction set, or a chain of `map` calls that each walk the structure | [Coyoneda](coyoneda.md) | None |
| A second loop written beside a traversal, to sum, count or collect what it visits | [Const](const_type.md) | None |

Each type's page opens with a card that names a neighbouring type for when that type does not fit.

An Effect Path can hold two effects at once: [`VResultPath`](../effect/path_vresult.md), for one, is a deferred task that fails with a typed error. Where no Path holds the pair you need, a monad transformer adds one effect to another, as [Other types with instances](#other-types-with-instances) shows.

## What each type supports {#what-each-type-supports}

| Type | Type classes |
|---|---|
| [`Either<L, R>`](either_monad.md) | `Functor`, `Applicative`, `Selective`, `Monad`, `MonadError<L>`, `Foldable`, `Traverse`, `Bifunctor` |
| [`Validated<E, A>`](validated_monad.md) | `Functor`, `Applicative`, `Selective`, `Monad`, `MonadError<E>`, `Foldable`, `Traverse`, `Bifunctor` |
| [`Try<T>`](try_monad.md) | `Functor`, `Applicative`, `Monad`, `MonadError<Throwable>`, `Foldable`, `Traverse` |
| [`EitherOrBoth<L, R>`](either_or_both_monad.md) | `Functor`, `Applicative`, `Monad`, `Foldable`, `Traverse`, `Bifunctor` |
| [`NonEmptyList<A>`](nonemptylist_monad.md) | `Functor`, `Applicative`, `Monad`, `Foldable`, `Traverse` |
| [`Maybe<T>`](maybe_monad.md) | `Functor`, `Applicative`, `Selective`, `Monad`, `MonadError<Unit>`, `Alternative`, `MonadZero`, `Foldable`, `Traverse` |
| [`Optional<T>`](optional_monad.md) | `Functor`, `Applicative`, `Selective`, `Monad`, `MonadError<Unit>`, `Alternative`, `MonadZero`, `Foldable`, `Traverse` |
| [`IO<A>`](io_monad.md) | `Functor`, `Applicative`, `Selective`, `Monad` |
| [`Lazy<A>`](lazy_monad.md) | `Functor`, `Applicative`, `Monad` |
| [`CompletableFuture<T>`](cf_monad.md) | `Functor`, `Applicative`, `Monad`, `MonadError<Throwable>` |
| [`VTask<A>`](vtask_monad.md) | `Functor`, `Applicative`, `Monad`, `MonadError<Throwable>` |
| [`VStream<A>`](vstream.md) | `Functor`, `Applicative`, `Monad`, `Alternative`, `Foldable`, `Traverse` |
| [`Reader<R, A>`](reader_monad.md) | `Functor`, `Applicative`, `Selective`, `Monad` |
| [`Context<R, A>`](context_scoped.md) | `Functor`, `Applicative`, `Monad` |
| [`State<S, A>`](state_monad.md) | `Functor`, `Applicative`, `Monad` |
| [`Writer<W, A>`](writer_monad.md) | `Functor`, `Applicative`, `Monad`, `Bifunctor` |
| [`List<E>`](list_monad.md) | `Functor`, `Applicative`, `Selective`, `Monad`, `Alternative`, `MonadZero`, `Foldable`, `Traverse` |
| [`Stream<T>`](stream_monad.md) | `Functor`, `Applicative`, `Monad`, `Alternative`, `MonadZero`, `Foldable`, `Traverse` |
| [`Id<A>`](identity.md) | `Functor`, `Applicative`, `Selective`, `Monad`, `Foldable`, `Traverse` |
| [`Trampoline<A>`](trampoline_monad.md) | `Functor`, `Applicative`, `Monad` |
| [`Free<F, A>`](free_monad.md) | `Functor`, `Applicative`, `Monad` |
| [`FreeAp<F, A>`](free_applicative.md) | `Functor`, `Applicative` |
| [`EitherF<F, G, A>`](eitherf.md) | `Functor` |
| [`Coyoneda<F, A>`](coyoneda.md) | `Functor` |
| [`Const<C, A>`](const_type.md) | `Functor`, `Applicative`, `Bifunctor` |

A type class, such as `Functor` or `Traverse`, names operations that work on any type with an instance of it, so a row tells you which generic code accepts that type. [Type Classes](../functional/ch_intro.md) says what each one lets you call. Every row lists the classes in the same order, and includes each class a listed one extends, as [the hierarchy](../functional/ch_intro.md#the-hierarchy) shows. `MonadError<…>` names the error its instance raises and recovers from: the type's own `L` or `E`, a `Throwable`, or `Unit`, the bare absence of `Maybe` and `Optional`.

Two rows that match can still behave differently, because behaviour belongs to the instance: `Either`'s `ap` stops at the first error, and `Validated`'s keeps every one. A test reads the table from the library's classes, so it lists exactly the instances of these type classes the library has. [`Semigroup` and `Monoid`](../functional/semigroup_and_monoid.md), which take a plain type rather than a container, are left out. Some instances take arguments. `Validated`'s and `EitherOrBoth`'s take a `Semigroup` to join two errors, `Writer`'s and `Const`'s a `Monoid` to join two logs or two values, and `EitherF`'s a `Functor` for each side.

## Other types with instances {#other-types-with-instances}

A monad transformer, such as `EitherT`, adds one effect to a monad you choose, so its instance takes that monad's own, and `WriterT`'s a `Monoid` as well. `ReaderT`'s `MonadReader<…>`, `StateT`'s `MonadState<…>` and `WriterT`'s `MonadWriter<…>` name the environment, state or output each gives access to, and [MTL Capabilities](../transformers/mtl_capabilities.md) shows code written against them. `Tuple2` is a pair whose two sides [Bifunctor](../functional/bifunctor.md) maps, and `Function` a function whose input and output [Profunctor](../functional/profunctor.md) adapts. None of them has a Core Types page, so each row links the page that shows it.

| Type | Type classes |
|---|---|
| [`EitherT<F, L, R>`](../transformers/eithert_transformer.md) | `Functor`, `Applicative`, `Monad`, `MonadError<L>` |
| [`OptionalT<F, A>`](../transformers/optionalt_transformer.md) | `Functor`, `Applicative`, `Monad`, `MonadError<Unit>` |
| [`MaybeT<F, A>`](../transformers/maybet_transformer.md) | `Functor`, `Applicative`, `Monad`, `MonadError<Unit>` |
| [`ReaderT<F, R_ENV, A>`](../transformers/readert_transformer.md) | `Functor`, `Applicative`, `Monad`, `MonadReader<R_ENV>` |
| [`StateT<S, F, A>`](../transformers/statet_transformer.md) | `Functor`, `Applicative`, `Monad`, `MonadState<S>` |
| [`WriterT<F, W, A>`](../transformers/writert_transformer.md) | `Functor`, `Applicative`, `Monad`, `MonadWriter<W>` |
| [`Tuple2<A, B>`](../functional/bifunctor.md) | `Bifunctor` |
| [`Function<T, R>`](../functional/profunctor.md) | `Profunctor` |

Java has no higher-kinded types, so generic code reaches every type on this page through the same encoding, which [How the Encoding Works](../hkts/core-concepts.md#the-pieces-each-type-provides) shows.

![Two cartoon toys, one sweeping an arm towards the horizon while the other looks on, worried, captioned "Monads, monads, monads everywhere"](../images/monads_everywhere.webp)

~~~admonish info title="Hands-On Learning"
Practise with concrete types in [Tutorial 06: Concrete Types](https://github.com/higher-kinded-j/higher-kinded-j/blob/main/hkj-examples/src/test/java/org/higherkindedj/tutorial/coretypes/Tutorial06_ConcreteTypes.java) (7 exercises).
~~~

<!-- description: Every Higher-Kinded-J type class at a glance, from Functor and Monad to Traverse, with its key methods, plus every ready-made Semigroup and Monoid. -->

# Quick Reference

_Choose a type class, look up its key methods and page, and find a ready-made `Semigroup` or `Monoid`._

## Which type class? {#which-type-class}

| You want to | Reach for |
|---|---|
| Change each value inside, and keep the container | [`Functor`](../functional/functor.md) |
| Validate several fields and report every error, with `Validated` | [`Applicative`](../functional/applicative.md) |
| Run an effect only when an earlier result calls for it, with every branch named up front | [`Selective`](../functional/selective.md) |
| Make a call that needs the last call's result | [`Monad`](../functional/monad.md) |
| Fail with a typed error, and recover from it | [`MonadError`](../functional/monad_error.md) |
| Try a cache, then the database | [`Alternative`](../functional/alternative.md) |
| Keep only the values that pass a test, inside a `For` | [`MonadZero`](../functional/monad_zero.md) |
| Sum or count a list, with a `Monoid` | [`Foldable`](../functional/foldable_and_traverse.md) |
| Look up every id, failing if any is missing | [`Traverse`](../functional/foldable_and_traverse.md) |

[Applicative, Selective or Monad?](../functional/abstraction_levels.md) decides between `Applicative`, `Selective` and `Monad`. Behaviour belongs to the instance, not the type class: `Either`'s `ap` stops at the first error, while `Validated`'s joins them all with its `Semigroup`.

## Type classes over a container {#type-classes-over-a-container}

These work on a container held as a `Kind`, as [How the Encoding Works](core-concepts.md) explains, and [the `Instances` facade](../functional/instances_facade.md) gives you one for a type. On an [Effect Path](../effect/ch_intro.md), the same operations are methods of the Path itself.

| Type class | Key methods | What it gives you | Page |
|---|---|---|---|
| `Functor` | `map` | Change the value inside, and keep the container | [Functor](../functional/functor.md) |
| `Applicative` | `of`, `ap`, `map2` to `map5` | Put a value in the container, and combine steps that do not depend on one another | [Applicative](../functional/applicative.md) |
| `Selective` | `select`, `branch`, `ifS`, `whenS` | Run an effect only when an earlier result calls for it, with every branch named up front | [Selective](../functional/selective.md) |
| `Monad` | `flatMap`, `flatMap2` to `flatMap5`, `as`, `peek` | Build the next step from the last result | [Monad](../functional/monad.md) |
| `MonadError` | `raiseError`, `handleErrorWith`, `handleError`, `recover`, `recoverWith` | Fail with a typed error, and recover from it | [MonadError](../functional/monad_error.md) |
| `Alternative` | `empty`, `orElse`, `orElseAll`, `guard` | Fall back to a second container when the first is empty (`Maybe`), or join the two (`List`) | [Alternative](../functional/alternative.md) |
| `MonadZero` | `zero`, `filter` | Drop a value that fails a test, as the `when` step of a [`For`](../functional/for_comprehension.md) does | [MonadZero](../functional/monad_zero.md) |
| `Foldable` | `foldMap` | Reduce a structure to one plain value, with a `Monoid` | [Foldable and Traverse](../functional/foldable_and_traverse.md) |
| `Traverse` | `traverse`, `sequenceA` | Run an effect on every element, and gather the results in one container | [Foldable and Traverse](../functional/foldable_and_traverse.md) |
| `MonadReader` | `ask`, `asks`, `reader`, `local` | Read an environment | [MTL Capabilities](../transformers/mtl_capabilities.md) |
| `MonadState` | `get`, `gets`, `inspect`, `put`, `modify` | Read and replace a state | [MTL Capabilities](../transformers/mtl_capabilities.md) |
| `MonadWriter` | `tell`, `listen`, `listens`, `pass`, `censor` | Add to an output kept beside the result | [MTL Capabilities](../transformers/mtl_capabilities.md) |

Argument order varies: `map(f, fa)` and `flatMap(f, ma)` take the function first, while `map2(fa, fb, f)` and `handleErrorWith(ma, handler)` take it last. Each linked page, apart from MTL Capabilities, states its type class's laws, and [the hierarchy](../functional/ch_intro.md#the-hierarchy) shows which type class extends which, drawn from `hkj-api` itself. [Lifting the Hood](lifting_the_hood.md#what-this-means-in-practice) says what a call through the encoding costs.

## Type classes over a two-sided type {#type-classes-over-a-two-sided-type}

These work on a type with two parameters, held as a `Kind2`.

| Type class | Key methods | What it gives you | Page |
|---|---|---|---|
| `Bifunctor` | `bimap`, `first`, `second` | Change either side of a two-sided type, such as `Either` or `Tuple2` | [Bifunctor](../functional/bifunctor.md) |
| `Profunctor` | `dimap`, `lmap`, `rmap` | Adapt a function's input and its output | [Profunctor](../functional/profunctor.md) |

## Type classes over a plain type {#type-classes-over-a-plain-type}

These take plain values, such as two `String`s, rather than a `Kind`.

| Type class | Key methods | What it gives you | Page |
|---|---|---|---|
| `Semigroup` | `combine` | Join two values of one type, where the grouping never changes the result | [Semigroup and Monoid](../functional/semigroup_and_monoid.md) |
| `Monoid` | `empty`, `combine`, `combineAll`, `combineN`, `of` | A `Semigroup` with an empty value, so that it can fold nothing at all | [Semigroup and Monoid](../functional/semigroup_and_monoid.md) |

## Ready-made Semigroups and Monoids {#ready-made-semigroups-and-monoids}

A `Semigroup` joins two values:

| Factory | Joins |
|---|---|
| `Semigroups.list()` | Two lists, end to end, into a new list |
| `Semigroups.set()` | Two sets, by union |
| `Semigroups.string()` | Two strings, end to end |
| `Semigroups.string(delimiter)` | Two strings, with the delimiter between them |
| `Semigroups.first()` | Two values, keeping the first |
| `Semigroups.last()` | Two values, keeping the second |

A `Monoid` adds an empty value, which changes nothing it is joined to, and which a fold of an empty structure gives:

| Factory | Joins | Empty value |
|---|---|---|
| `Monoids.list()` | Lists, end to end | An empty list |
| `Monoids.set()` | Sets, by union | An empty set |
| `Monoids.string()` | Strings, end to end | `""` |
| `Monoids.integerAddition()` | `Integer`s, by `+` | `0` |
| `Monoids.integerMultiplication()` | `Integer`s, by `*` | `1` |
| `Monoids.longAddition()` | `Long`s, by `+` | `0L` |
| `Monoids.longMultiplication()` | `Long`s, by `*` | `1L` |
| `Monoids.doubleAddition()` | `Double`s, by `+`, which rounds | `0.0` |
| `Monoids.doubleMultiplication()` | `Double`s, by `*`, which rounds | `1.0` |
| `Monoids.bigDecimalAddition()` | `BigDecimal`s, by `add`, exactly: use it for money | `BigDecimal.ZERO` |
| `Monoids.booleanAnd()` | `Boolean`s, by `&&` | `true` |
| `Monoids.booleanOr()` | `Boolean`s, by `\|\|` | `false` |
| `Monoids.firstOptional()` | `Optional`s, keeping the first present one | `Optional.empty()` |
| `Monoids.lastOptional()` | `Optional`s, keeping the last present one | `Optional.empty()` |
| `Monoids.maximum()` | `Optional`s, keeping the larger value | `Optional.empty()` |
| `Monoids.maximum(comparator)` | `Optional`s, keeping the larger value by the comparator | `Optional.empty()` |
| `Monoids.minimum()` | `Optional`s, keeping the smaller value | `Optional.empty()` |
| `Monoids.minimum(comparator)` | `Optional`s, keeping the smaller value by the comparator | `Optional.empty()` |
| `Monoids.update()` | [`Update`](../functional/semigroup_and_monoid.md#composing-updates)s, applying the first and then the second | `Update.identity()` |

`Monoid.of(empty, combine)` makes your own from an empty value and a `Semigroup`, as [Semigroup and Monoid](../functional/semigroup_and_monoid.md) does to count the units of each SKU.

---

**Previous:** [Supported Types](../monads/supported-types.md)
